PREFACE 
This Report of the Comptroller and Auditor 
General of India for the year ended 31 March 
2023 has been prepared for submission to the 
Governor of Sikkim under Article 151 of the 
Constitution of India.  
This Report contains significant results of 
Performance Audit  and Compliance Audit  
(including Subject Specific Compliance 
Audit) of the departments of the Government 
of Sikkim under Social, Economic, Revenue, 
General Sectors & Local Bodies and Public 
Sector Undertakings.  
The cases mentioned in the Report are those 
which came to notice in test audit during the 
period 2022-23, as well as those which came 
to notice in earlier years, but could not be 
dealt with in the previous reports. Matters 
relating to the period subsequent to 20 22-23 
have also been included appropriately in the 
Report. 
The audits have been conducted in conformity 
with the Auditing Standards issued by the 
Comptroller and Auditor General of India.

PREFACE
This Report of the Comptroller and Auditor General 
of India for the year ended 31 March 2023 has been 
prepared for submission to the Governor of Sikkim 
under Article 151 of the Constitution of India. 
This Report contains significant results of 
Performance Audit and Compliance Audit 
(including Subject Specific Compliance Audit) of 
the departments of the Government of Sikkim under 
Social, Economic, Revenue, General Sectors & 
Local Bodies and Public Sector Undertakings. 
The cases mentioned in the Report are those which 
came to notice in test audit during the period 2022-23, 
as well as those which came to notice in earlier 
years, but could not be dealt with in the previous 
reports. Matters relating to the period subsequent 
to 2022-23 have also been included appropriately 
in the Report.
The audits have been conducted in conformity with 
the Auditing Standards issued by the Comptroller 
and Auditor General of India.

OVERVIEW

OVERVIEW
This Audit Report consists of eight chapters, which deal with Social, Economic, 
Economic (Public Sector Undertakings), Revenue, General Sectors and Local Bodies. 
Chapter VIII deals with Follow up action on earlier Audit, reports and response of 
departments to recommendations of the Public Account Committee. 
This Report also includes three Performance Audits viz. Performance Audit on Building 
and Other Construction Workers Welfare Cess, Implementation of Pradhan Mantri 
Gram Sadak Yojana and Solid Waste Management in Urban Local Bodies, four Subject 
Specific Compliance Audits (SSCA) on Public Health Infrastructure and Management 
of Health Services, Department oversight on GST payment and return filing, Swadesh 
Darshan Scheme and Efficacy of implementation of 74th Constitutional Amendment Act, 
in Sikkim. The Report was forwarded to the Secretary of the Departments concerned 
with a request to furnish replies to the observations. Replies received have been suitably 
incorporated in the Report. 
SOCIAL SECTOR 
The Chapter on Social Sector consists of one Performance Audit and one Subject 
Specific Compliance Audit. 
PERFORMANCE AUDIT 
BUILDING AND OTHER CONSTRUCTION WORKERS WELFARE CESS
Government of India (GoI) enacted the Building and Other Construction Workers’ 
(Regulation of Employment and Conditions of Service) Act, 1996  (BOCW Act) and 
Building and Other Construction Workers Welfare Cess Act, 1996 (Cess Act). The Acts 
aimed to provide safety, health and welfare measures for the benefit of building and 
other construction (BOC) workers through levy of cess.
Government of Sikkim (GoS) notified (August 2010) the Sikkim Building and Other 
Construction Workers (SBOCW) Rules, 2010. The collection of BOCW cess was fixed 
at the rate of one per cent on the cost of construction incurred by the employers.
Audit observations
The Board did not prepare annual budget estimates upto 2021-22.  In the absence of an 
annual budget, the Board could not effectively plan and manage its financial resources to 
support the implementation of various programmes and projects to promote the welfare 
of BOC workers.
(Paragraph 2.3.8.2)
Audit observed that during 2017-18 to 2021-22, the Board had invested ₹ 73.44 crore in 
non-nationalised banks (IDBI Bank, Yes Bank, Axis Bank, ICICI Bank, HDFC Bank, 
etc.), which was in contravention to the directions of the Supreme Court and the SBOCW 
rules, 2010.
(Paragraph 2.3.8.3)

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
vi
The Board incurred administrative expenditure ranging from 3.48 per cent and 65.93 per 
cent during the period from 2017-18 to 2021-22, with five per cent being the maximum 
limit.
(Paragraph 2.3.8.4)
The Board had not maintained details of workers viz., name, contact numbers, Certificate 
for age proof and address proof, Bank details, family details for nomination, etc. which 
were required to be submitted by the employer. The Board also failed to produce to 
Audit year-wise list of registered workers for the period 2017-22. 
(Paragraph 2.3.10.1)
The establishment register maintained by the Board only included manual entries of 
those establishments or employers who had applied for registration, leaving many 
unregistered entities outside its purview. The Board did not conduct any inspection 
to assess eligibility for registration of establishments carrying out works where 10 or 
more workers were employed by the employer, towards ensuring their registration. 
Due to non-identification of all unregistered establishments and non-maintenance of 
a comprehensive database for capturing details of all establishments or employers, the 
Board not only could not ensure implementation of welfare benefits for all BOC workers 
but also could not assess and collect the actual cess receivable from these establishments.
(Paragraph 2.3.10.2)
The system of providing Universal Access Number/Unique Identification Number  
to construction workers was not introduced by the Board during the period of audit  
(2017-22).
(Paragraph 2.3.10.5)
Audit scrutiny showed that none of the employers carrying out building or other 
construction works under the Board’s record had furnished returns showing the details 
of workers entitled to be registered as well as those who left the service during the 
preceding month and particulars of the establishment, during 2017-22. 
(Paragraph 2.3.10.6)
The State had not notified the building plans approval authority for collection of advance 
cess from the estimate of the construction of the building plan. Consequently, in none 
of the private construction works was applicable advance cess collected and received 
by the Board. In addition, the Board had not made necessary efforts to register eligible 
private establishments.
(Paragraph 2.3.11.4)
There were significant delays in releasing financial assistance for all schemes. In seven 
cases, financial assistance was not released to the beneficiaries and there was no valid 
explanation by the Board for the non-release. 
(Paragraph 2.3.13.1)
The Board provided financial assistance to 7,500 beneficiaries by depositing the amount 
to the employer’s account. The Board released ₹ 1.50 crore to 44 employers for onward 
transfer of financial assistance to their 7,500 workers (March and April 2020). Analysis 
of UCs revealed that out of 6,911 beneficiaries, in 1,129 cases financial assistance 

Overview
vii
was extended through hand receipts. Thus, due to non-transfer of the funds directly 
to the beneficiaries account through DBT, lack of confirmation as to whether financial 
assistance was received by the beneficiaries, the disbursement of financial assistance 
amounting to ₹ 1.38 crore remained irregular and doubtful.
(Paragraph 2.3.13.4)
Despite provision of annual meeting of the State Advisory Committee, only five meetings 
were held till date (April 2023) since its constitution in the year 2010. Further, during 
the period 2017-22, only two meetings  were held against the stipulated number  of five 
meetings.
(Paragraph 2.3.15.1)
Recommendations:
	Online registration mechanism for BOC workers with complete details (name, 
contact number, nominees, bank details, identification documents, subscription fee, 
etc.) to be initiated by the Board at the earliest. Further, the Board should ensure 
that all construction works and workers involved are registered under the Board. 
The Board should maintain a comprehensive database of such registered workers.
	The Board may ensure that Assessment Officers and Cess collectors are made 
accountable for ensuring that cess is duly assessed, realised and remitted to the 
Board.
	The Government may authorise the advance collection of cess from Pvt. Buildings 
and construction works, through the building plan-approving authorities i.e., 
urban/ local bodies.
	The Government should ensure investing the Cess fund in nationalised banks only.
	The Government may ensure mandatory provision of financial assistance through 
Direct Benefit Transfer mode only.
SUBJECT SPECIFIC COMPLIANCE AUDIT 
PUBLIC HEALTH INFRASTRUCTURE AND MANAGEMENT OF HEALTH 
SERVICES 
The public healthcare delivery system in India is organised at three levels – primary, 
secondary, and tertiary. The vast network of Primary Health Sub-Centres (SCs), Primary 
Health Centres (PHCs), Urban Primary Health Centres (UPHCs) and Community Health 
Centres (CHCs) form the primary tier of public healthcare delivery system for rural and 
urban population. District Hospitals (DHs) provide secondary-level healthcare services 
for rural and urban populations. These hospitals handle the treatment and management of 
diseases or medical conditions that require specialised care. Tertiary healthcare involves 
providing advanced and super-specialty services to be provided by medical institutions 
in urban areas, which are well equipped with sophisticated diagnostic and investigative 
facilities. 
Audit Observations
The State Government had not formulated any policy or strategy in line with the National 
Health Policy 2017 (NHP 17) till March 2023, to determine the size of budget to be 
allocated to the Health Sector, either as percentage of the overall State budget or the 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
viii
GSDP. The State did not achieve the NHP stipulated eight per cent budgetary allocation 
for health sector in any of the five years during the period 2017-22.
(Paragraph 2.4.22.1)
The budget allocation/ expenditure on maintenance of hospitals, health centres and other 
buildings was on a decreasing trend since 2019-20 despite addition of five new health-
care facilities and increase in bed capacity by 770 units over the period 2017-22.
 (Paragraph 2.4.22.4)
The newly constructed PHC at Chipsu was lying unused, rendering an expenditure of ₹ 
6.23 crore idle. 
(Paragraph 2.4.22.5)
DHs ( Singtam, Geyzing) had no General Surgery Department to undertake major 
surgeries, etc. The Rhenock CHC also did not have blood storage facility as required 
under the IPHS. Further, there was no surgeon posted in the CHC therefore OT facility 
could not provide required services and was used to provide only basic first aid and 
minor surgical procedures like suturing, dressing of minor injuries, wounds, abscess, 
etc.
(Paragraph 2.4.23.2)
In the PHCs, one to two additional medical officers beyond the IPHS norms were 
provided in three out of the five sampled PHCs, which indicated a positive trend.  
However, there was excess deployment of Staff nurses in the sampled PHCs ranging 
from five to 20 against the norm of three staff nurses for a PHC.  Rhenock CHC did 
not have any specialist. The CHC also did not have medical Superintendent, Public 
Health Specialist and Public Health Nurse, as required under the IPHS. Shortages and 
excesses of specialists and other manpower were noticed in several Departments in the 
DHs. Thus, the deployment of specialists, medical officers, nurses and paramedics in the 
two DHs was haphazard and non-uniform. This resulted in non-availability of adequate 
services in Departments with shortage of manpower and under-utilisation of manpower 
in Departments which were overstaffed.
 (Paragraph 2.4.23.3)
The two DHs sampled for Audit (Singtam & Geyzing) had only 40 out of the 84 prescribed 
pathological services and tests. While Singtam DH did not have Electrocardiogram 
(ECG) facility, the Geyzing DH had only one out of the three prescribed ophthalmological 
services. None of the sampled DHs, CHC and the PHCs had any system for obtaining 
quality assurance for the pathological tests despite the IPHS stipulation for ensuring 
external monitoring through performance evaluation and external quality assessment of 
laboratories. 
(Paragraph 2.4.23.8)
Stillbirth rate varied between 0 per cent and 2.35 per cent in the sampled DHs, CHC 
and PHCs during 2017-22. Geyzing DH registered highest stillbirth rate of 2.35 per cent 
followed by Rinchengpong PHC at 1.72 per cent.
(Paragraph 2.4.23.13)

Overview
ix
DG sets provided were functional in only two PHCs ( Sombaria and Pakyong) out of 
the sampled five PHCs. Singtam DH witnessed frequent water shortages due to absence 
of a dedicated supply line of water and absence of dedicated maintenance plan and 
manpower to ensure regular upkeep of the water supply system.  
(Paragraph 2.4.24.4)
Virology lab for conduct of Reverse Transcription-Polymerase Chain Reaction  (RT-
PCR) test was established in the State only in one hospital, the New STNM Super 
Specialty Hospital, Gangtok during 2020-21 to conduct RT-PCR test. None of the two 
DHs had functioning virology labs as of March 2023 for testing Covid-19. 
(Paragraph 2.4.26.1)
The Covid Health Centre at the State Institute of Capacity Building at Karfectar was, 
lying idle and in locked condition and was not being used for any purpose. No manpower 
was deputed in the facility and neither were any patients treated upto September 2023.
(Paragraph 2.4.26.2)
Out of the 108 ventilators received by the State from various sources, 41 ventilators 
were issued to the New STNM Hospital, 51 ventilators were lying idle in the Store and 
the whereabouts of 16 ventilators were not available in records produced to Audit.
(Paragraph 2.4.26.3)
The oxygen plants in the State had been established without ensuring availability of 
supporting equipment like DG sets and without ensuring prompt repairs due to which 
the plants either remained idle or functioned only intermittently. Due to this fact, the 
Department continued using bottled oxygen purchased from Government funds for in-
patients.  
(Paragraph 2.4.26.4)  
As regards performance on the Sustainable Development Goal 3 on Good Health and 
Well Being, Sikkim ranked sixth among NE States with a score of 62, although Sikkim’s 
score moved upwards from 52 to 62 during 2018-19 to 2020-21. 
(Paragraph 2.4.27.3)
One of the important Indicators in which Sikkim lagged glaringly behind other States 
was the extremely high suicide rate which was more than 300 per cent of the national 
average. 
(Paragraph 2.4.27.4)
Recommendations:
	The State may endeavour to increase its allocation to and expenditure on the Health 
Sector to bring it at par with the target under the National Health Policy 2017 with 
a view to provide all essential specialist services as well as desirable services in the 
DHs and CHCs of the State. 
	The Department may establish proper policy for procurement of medicines and 
consumables to avoid unwarranted and infructuous expenditure.

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
x
	The Department may take initiative to make good the shortage of essential equipment 
and ensure availability of required specialist services and blood storage facility in 
the OTs of the DHs and CHCs. 
	Specialist services viz., Surgery, Medicine, Obstetrics & Gynaecology, Paediatrics 
and Anaesthesia, etc. may be introduced in the CHCs in the State as required under 
IPHS norms. 
	Government may take steps to set up new PHSCs, PHCs and CHCs to mitigate 
shortages of CHCs, PHCs and PHSCs in the two districts of the State to ensure 
availability of proper healthcare facilities near the place of residence of people of 
those areas.  
	The Department may take steps to identify reasons for high stillbirth rates in the 
State and take corrective measures to reduce the rate.
	The Department may make RTPCR cum Virology labs functional in the DHs. 
	The Government/Department should prepare an action plan for utilising the newly 
constructed 50 bedded Covid Hospital constructed at Karfectar at the earliest.
	The Department may ensure regular repair and upkeep of the Oxygen generating 
plants with a view to ensure their utility. 
	The Department may take stock of the idle ventilators and concentrators in the 
stores and ensure their usage or disposal, as appropriate.  
	Government should take appropriate action towards regular monitoring and 
supervision of the health and family welfare programmes with a view to ensure 
achieving the SDG 3 targets. 
	Government may take serious note to ascertain the reasons for the high suicide rate 
and deaths due to road accidents in the state and make concerted efforts to contain 
the same.
ECONOMIC SECTOR 
The Chapter on Economic Sector consists of one Performance Audit and one Compliance 
Audit Paragraph. 
PERFORMANCE AUDIT 
IMPLEMENTATION OF THE PRADHAN MANTRI GRAM SADAK YOJANA 
(PMGSY)
Government of India (GoI) launched the centrally sponsored ‘ Pradhan Mantri Gram 
Sadak Yojana’ (PMGSY) in December 2000 for providing all-weather road connectivity 
to all eligible unconnected habitations in rural areas with a population of 500 persons 
and above in plain areas and 250 persons in hilly areas. Besides, upgradation of existing 
rural roads was also to be carried out under PMGSY . Further, as the Programme 
unfolded, a dire need to consolidate the entire rural road network by upgradation of 
selected Through Routes and some Major Rural Links (MRLs) was felt and accordingly 
a new intervention was evolved in May 2013 namely PMGSY-II. PMGSY–II envisages 

Overview
xi
consolidation of the existing Rural Road Network by covering upgradation of selected 
rural roads based on their economic potential and their role in facilitating the growth of 
rural market centres and rural hubs. 
In Sikkim, total expenditure of ₹ 1131.72 crore (Scheme ₹ 1086.82 crore, administrative 
₹ 16.44 crore, and Maintenance ₹ 28.46 crore) was incurred during 2017-23 on 
construction, upgradation, and maintenance of rural roads under PMGSY . During the period 
covered, 433 projects (Roads-178, Bridges-97, upgradation-34 and Maintenance-124) 
were either ongoing or completed or under maintenance in the said period.
Audit Observations
	In Sikkim, there were a total 773 habitations eligible for connectivity under PMGSY . 
As of 31  March 2023, 225 eligible habitations had been connected. With 548 
habitations remaining to be connected, audit noticed that 44 ineligible habitations 
were provided with connectivity. 
(Paragraph 3.3.8.2)
	There were instances of works where SRDDA had prepared DPR in an adhoc 
manner. Out of 46 sampled PMGSY-I roads, 21 roads were executed in such a 
manner that part of the road works were executed at a different location as against the 
original work site sanctioned by National Rural Infrastructure Development Agency 
(NRIDA). Further, out of 21 road works, connectivity to the targeted habitations by 
construction of five roads had been achieved without exhausting sanctioned length 
of these roads and remaining length was executed at other habitations which were 
already connected by another road, resulting in avoidable expenditure of ₹ 8.27 
crore.
(Paragraph 3.3.8.3)
	37 delayed works were under execution during audit period, which were sanctioned 
during 2007-08 to 2021-22. RDD had attributed the delay to the contractors and 
had issued notices to them, however, LD amounting to ₹ 17.21 crore had not been 
levied on the contractors.
(Paragraph 3.3.9.2)
	There were 275 works were in execution, out of which four works were completed 
in time, and remaining works were delayed ranging from upto one year to more 
than 10 years during the period 2017 to 2023.
(Paragraph 3.3.10.1)
	In three out of 46 sampled works, landowners had initially provided NOC for 
acquisition of their land for roads. However, subsequently the landowners refused 
to part their land during execution of work due to various reasons including non-
payment of land compensation, SRRDA was forced to stall the work, and execution 
of 580 metres of Stage II works was abandoned due to land dispute.
(Paragraph 3.3.10.2)
	On scrutiny of records and JPV of 45 road works (December 2022 to February 
2023) out of 55 sampled roads (PMGSY I - 46 and PMGSY-II-9), it was noticed 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
xii
that the road length of 1570 meter in four roads was lesser than the sanctioned 
length, however, SRRDA had paid the contractor for the full sanctioned length.
(Paragraph 3.3.10.5)
	Audit scrutinised total 113 sampled works (Roads-55, Bridges-24 and 
Maintenance-34) out of 433 works and found that the contractors did not provide the 
required insurance cover for work execution period as well as for five years (defect 
liability period) after completion of construction for all works. In the absence of 
insurance coverage by the contractors, SRRDA remained at risk of bearing financial 
burden of damages caused to roads /bridges, personal injury/death due to accidents 
during execution.  
(Paragraph 3.3.10.10)
	NQM carried out a total 559 inspections where 14 per cent works were graded as 
unsatisfactory and 12 per cent works required improvement. Similarly, SQM had 
carried out 1507 inspections where four per cent works were graded as unsatisfactory 
and two per cent works as requiring improvement. However, there was no record of 
action taken by SRRDA to rectify the work.
(Paragraph 3.3.11.1)
	No records relating to minutes of meetings of the State Level Standing Committee 
held during the period 2017-2023 against the requirement of 24 meetings to be 
conducted, were available with SRRDA. This indicated lack of monitoring of 
PMGSY .
(Paragraph 3.3.11.3)
Recommendations:
	Responsibility of the officials concerned may be fixed for selecting ineligible 
habitations, preparing deficient DPRs and executing unapproved portions of works.
	SRRDA may monitor the progress of road works with reference to the prescribed 
milestones and quality and stringent action be initiated against defaulting 
contractors to avoid delay in execution. 
	SRRDA may ensure that the roads built are in compliance with the quality 
specifications as specified in the guidelines and may take necessary rectificatory 
measures on the recommendations of NQM and SQM.
	SRRDA may streamline the existing mechanism for routine maintenance of PMGSY 
roads and bridges such that the roads and bridges remain serviceable through out 
their service life, without deterioration.
COMPLIANCE AUDIT 
Rural Development Department erroneously paid labour cess amounting to ₹ 4.32 crore 
to the contractor as the estimate included labour cess and the rate quoted by the contractor 
was inclusive of all taxes (including labour cess). This resulted in unwarranted payment 
amounting to ₹ 4.32 crore. Further, RDD had also paid labour cess amounting to  
₹ 0.38 crore on inadmissible items, which was avoidable.

Overview
xiii
Recommendation: The Department should take urgent action to recover ₹4.70 crore 
from the contractor. The State Government needs to fix responsibility on the officials 
concerned for such a lapse. 
(Paragraph 3.4)
ECONOMIC SECTOR
 (PUBLIC SECTOR UNDERTAKINGS) 
As on 31 March 2023, the State of Sikkim had 17 working PSUs (13 working Government 
Companies and four working Statutory Corporations) under the audit purview of the 
Comptroller and Auditor General of India (C&AG). During the period 2022-23, the 
investment of the State Government (capital and long-term loans) in 17 PSUs amounted 
to ₹ 82.65 crore consisting of 67.31 per cent  (₹ 55.63 crore) towards capital and 
32.69 per cent (₹ 27.02 crore) towards long term loans.
(Paragraphs 4.1.1 and 4.1.2.1)
The State’s budgetary support to PSUs increased from Nil (2020-21) to ₹31.29 crore 
(equity: ₹ 6.30 crore; loans: ₹ 24.99 crore) during 2022-23. As per the information 
furnished by the PSUs, during 2022-23, the State Government provided grants 
amounting to ₹ 66.35 crore to five PSUs namely Temi Tea, SC, ST & OBC Development 
Corporation, Namchi Smart City Limited, Gangtok Smart City Development Limited 
and Sikkim Industrial Development & Investment Corporation. 
(Paragraph 4.1.4)
As on 30 September 2022, 58 accounts of 14 PSUs had not been finalised. 
(Paragraph 4.1.6)
As per the latest finalised accounts of PSUs as on 30 September 2023, the aggregate 
paid-up capital and accumulated losses of 17 working PSUs were ₹3,716.86 crore 
and (-) ₹1,149.09 crore respectively.
(Paragraphs 4.1.8.2)
REVENUE SECTOR 
The Chapter on Revenue Sector contains one Subject Specific Compliance Audit. This 
Chapter also gives an overview of revenue receipts which showed that during the period 
2022-23, the revenue raised by the State Government (₹ 2473.37 crore) was 31 per cent 
of the total revenue receipts. The balance 69 per cent of the receipts during 2022-23 was 
from Government of India. 
(Paragraph 5.1.1)
	The analysis of arrears of revenue as on 31 March 2023 showed that ₹ 454.91 crore, 
of which, ₹138.28 crore was outstanding for more than five years.
(Paragraph 5.3)

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
xiv
SUBJECT SPECIFIC COMPLIANCE AUDIT 
“OVERSIGHT ON GST PAYMENTS AND RETURNS FILING” FOR THE 
YEAR 2017-18
Subject Specific Compliance Audit on transitional credits under GST in Sikkim was 
taken up with a view to seeking an assurance on whether the mechanism envisaged by 
the Department for selection and verification of transitional credit claims was adequate 
and effective (System issues) and whether the transitional credits carried over by the 
individual taxpayers into GST regime were valid and admissible (Compliance issues).
Audit Observations
Audit observed that out of total 10940 taxpayers, only four taxpayers were scrutinised 
by the Commercial Taxes Division, Finance Department, Government of Sikkim and 
ASMT-10 were issued during the period from 2017-18 to 2020-21. Further, the modalities 
of scrutiny adopted by the CTD, the basis for selection of taxpayers for scrutiny, the 
checks that were done in the application itself, any data analytics methods adopted and 
reports of Directorate General of Analytics and Risk Management (DGARM) received, 
if any, and action taken thereon were called for (September 2022) in Audit, but the CTD 
had not responded till date (November 2023). Further, Audit observed that out of the 
fourteen taxpayers selected for detailed audit, three had filed GSTR-1 return(s) after a 
delay of more than six months, three had filed GSTR-3B returns after a delay of more 
than six months and two taxpayers had not filed GSTR-9 and GSTR-9C till July 2023. 
Yet no action for cancellation of their registration was seen to have been initiated by the 
CTD (November 2023).
(Paragraph 5.12.6.1)
Audit selected a sample of 93 cases (18 from Industries Sector, 46 from Service Sector 
and 29 from Trading Sector) from amongst the top deviations/inconsistencies in each of 
the 12 parameters for the year 2017-18. The audit queries were issued to the respective 
Sectors on 19 May 2022. Audit checks in these cases were limited to verifying the 
CTD’s action on the identified deviations/mismatches. Out of 93 queries issued, 
responses were received in 38 cases and no response was received (November 2023) in 
55 cases. Further, out of total 93 inconsistencies/mismatches pointed out by Audit, the 
CTD provided responses in case of 38 inconsistencies/mismatches amounting to ₹ 10.52 
crore. Out of those 38 replies, Audit noticed deviations from the provisions of the SGST 
Act in 22 cases involving amount of ₹ 3.71 crore. Out of 22 compliance deviations, the 
CTD accepted audit observations and made recoveries in four cases with tax effect of ₹ 
0.24 crore and in 18 cases, the replies were not acceptable to Audit. Out of 38 cases, in 
16 cases, the Department’s reply was acceptable to Audit, which includes 4 cases of data 
entry errors and 12 cases under other valid explanations.
(Paragraph 5.12.6.2)
Recommendations:
After evaluation of records furnished and analysis of data available in the State system, 
the following recommendations are made:

Overview
xv
	The CTD may put in place proper mechanism for scrutiny of returns to ensure 
compliance of rules pertaining to returns filing and tax payments by all taxpayers. 
The CTD may prepare a Standard Operating Procedure for manual scrutiny of 
returns in line with one prepared by CBIC, for use till automation of scrutiny is 
completed.
	CTD may establish mechanism to regularly identify late-filers and non-filers and 
take necessary steps to recover tax, interest and late fee from them.
	CTD may establish mechanism to regularly identify taxpayers who have not filed 
GST returns for more than six months and take necessary steps to recover applicable 
tax, interest and late fee from them and cancel the registrations.
	CTD may urgently pursue the 55 inconsistencies/deviations pointed out by Audit 
for which responses have not been provided and intimate the results there-of.
	The CTD may conduct similar data analysis (as conducted by Audit for Centralised/ 
limited Audit) of GST returns and other related data of all the taxpayers under State 
jurisdiction to identify inconsistencies and take necessary steps to recover dues 
from the taxpayers wherever applicable. 
GENERAL SECTOR 
The Chapter on General Sector contains one Subject Specific Compliance Audit. 
SUBJECT SPECIFIC COMPLIANCE AUDIT 
SWADESH DARSHAN SCHEME
Swadesh Darshan is the flagship scheme of Ministry of Tourism (MoT) of Government 
of India (GoI) for development of tourism infrastructure in the country. MoT launched 
this Central Sector Scheme in January 2015. The objective of the scheme was to develop 
tourist circuits and promote cultural and heritage value of the country. The MoT approved 
two projects Swadesh Darshan-I (SD-I) and Swadesh Darshan-II (SDII) under North-
East Circuit for the state of Sikkim. The entry point defined in SDI was at Rangpo and 
exit point was at Singtam. In respect of Swadesh Darshan -II the entry point was at 
Singtam and exit point at Melli. A Subject Specific Compliance Audit on SD, brought 
out the following significant findings:
Audit Observations
Identification of the tourist circuit and the destinations is a vital component of the 
scheme. The tourist circuits/destinations were to be identified by MoT in consultations 
with the stakeholders and States based on a pre-dominant tourism theme, considering 
certain factors. However, DPR prepared by the department did not have any reference 
to consultations. Such deficiencies in planning affected the overall execution and 
implementation of the projects resulting in instances of delay in completion of the 
projects, non-utilisation of infrastructure and improper utilisation of infrastructure 
created.
(Paragraph 6.3.8.1)

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
xvi
The GoI released ₹ 187.95 crore to the State Government for SD-I & SD-II. The 
department carried out 127 works under 35 selected components, out of which 126 
works were handed over to the Assets cell of the Department by the Engineering cell 
after completion of the works for leasing out and one asset (Development of Adventure 
Tourism with International Standard Zip line from Hathipailay to Aritar Lake) was not 
handed over to asset cell. However, out of 127 assets, only 19 assets were being utilised 
for the purposes for which they were created, 77 were lying unutilised till the date of 
audit and 31 were being used for other unintended purposes. This led to infructuous 
expenditure of ₹ 17.68 crore and idling of assets valuing ₹ 18.98 crore. 
(Paragraph 6.3.9)
As per guidelines/revised guidelines, improvement/investment in assets/structures 
owned by the private entities were not admissible under the schemes. TCAD submitted 
the land availability certificate along with the DPR to MoT stating that all lands were 
free from encumbrance to accommodate all features of the project and the lands were 
in possession of the Department. However, tourist infrastructure in nine destinations 
was constructed in the land belonging to either Monastery or temple, which affected the 
State’s ownership of the project/assets as well as its access to revenue from the project.
(Paragraph 6.3.9.1)
Projects under Swadesh Darshan-I and II were sanctioned by the GoI on 30.06.2015 and 
16.09.2016 respectively. However, there was an inordinate delay in awarding tenders for 
SD-I and II. The delay in finalisation of tenders ranged from 139 days to 419 days (SD-
I) and 90 days to 243 days (SD-II) from the due date of award of tender (three months 
from the date of sanction) due to belated process for tendering, retendering due to non-
fulfillment of tendered conditions, etc.
(Paragraph 6.3.9.2)
None of the 35 components under 21 destinations (SD-I: 13 and SD-II 8) selected by 
audit were completed within the stipulated time schedule. Works were delayed ranging 
from four months to 37 months from the stipulated date of completion.
(Paragraph 6.3.9.3)
According to Paragraph 2 (20) of the Sikkim Public Works Code 2009, Contingencies 
(Works) when used in respect of the accounts of a work, indicate incidental expenses of 
a sundry nature, which cannot be classified under any Sub-head or sub-work but pertain 
to the work. However, the Department had incurred ₹ 1.85 crore on procurement of ten 
vehicles and on items which were not related to the schemes/works under SD, which 
was irregular. 
(Paragraph 6.3.10.4)
As per revised guidelines of the scheme 2017, the States UT Administration shall 
appoint a State Level Monitoring Committee for timely implementation of the project. 
The purpose of the committee is to provide feedback on the progress of implementation 
to GoI regularly. Accordingly, the Department constituted a monitoring committee 
consisting of six members headed by Chief Engineer as Chairman on 10.02.2016, 
however after a lapse of seven months of issue of sanctioned letter. However, no records 

Overview
xvii
were made available at the department to indicate that SLMC met regularly after its 
formation. In absence of monitoring mechanism, delay in completion of works ranging 
from four months to 37 months, could not be avoided. 
(Paragraph 6.3.11.1)
Recommendations:
	 Department should ensure that before selection of a project, proper survey, 
feasibility studies and consultation with shareholders are ensured. DPR should 
spell out clearly the reasons for selection of a project. No deviation from approved 
DPR shall be permitted unless absolutely necessary.
	 Department shall make every effort to ensure that the assets created under Swadesh 
Darshan I and Swadesh Darshan II are utilised for the purpose for which they 
were created.
	 Department shall ensure that a proper monitoring system may be developed to 
capture key data pertaining to tourist footfall, employment generation for effective 
implementation of the scheme.
	 The issue of construction of infrastructure at Monastery and Temple premises may 
be taken up with GoI for regularisation.
LOCAL BODIES
The Chapter on Local Bodies contains one Performance Audit and one Subject Specific 
Compliance Audit. 
PERFORMANCE AUDIT 
SOLID WASTE MANAGEMENT (SWM)
Government of Sikkim (GoS), in the State Policy on Solid Waste Management Strategy 
notified in April 2019 has envisioned to establish a ‘Zero Waste System’ for Solid Waste 
Management in the state by the year 2030 with a mission to enable the community to 
visualise experience and own up utilisation of resources along with conservation of 
environment for sustainable development.
Audit Observations
Even four years after the policy initiative was approved in 2019, as of June 2023, only 
two (out of seven) ULBs had decentralised solid waste processing facilities at Mangan 
Nagar Panchayat (MNP) and Gangtok Municipal Corporation (GMC).
(Paragraph 7.3.9.1)
The data on waste generated, as submitted by ULBs was based on estimation without 
assessing the quantity and composition of waste generated through sample collection.
(Paragraph 7.3.9.3)
The test checked ULBs had not carried out assessment of the financial viability of SWM 
services on a stand-alone basis to ensure their sustainability. While the user charges 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
xviii
were not realised in full by two ULBs (GyNP and MNP), the ULBs were dependent on 
government grants for SWM services to the extent of 35 to 94 per cent as only 6 to 65 
per cent of expenditure on SWM services was met from own source of revenue (user 
charges/ outsourced contract fees). 
(Paragraph 7.3.10.2 &7.3.10.3)
Two ULBs (GMC and NJNP) had outsourced the services of door-to-door collection of 
waste and transportation up to landfill/ dumpsite to garbage contractors. The garbage 
contractors had not complied with the terms and conditions of the contract towards bearing 
cost of fuel, minor repair and maintenance of garbage truck provided to contractors 
for rendering SWM services (collection and transportation). Resultantly, GMC and 
NJNP incurred expenditure of ₹ 1.30 crore towards fuel, repair and maintenance of 
garbage truck during 2022-23, leading to avoidable expenditure and undue favour to the 
contractors.
(Paragraph 7.3.10.4 & 7.3.10.5)
Joint physical verification showed that the source segregation of waste was not being 
carried out by majority of the households. The households bring in waste in single bin 
without segregating, dump the unsegregated waste in the garbage truck, which did not 
have separate compartment for dry and wet waste, thereby indicating lack of efforts at 
segregation.
(Paragraph 7.3.11.3)
Vehicles used for door-to-door collection of waste were uncovered (open top) and non- 
compartmentalised (no separate compartment for carrying wet and dry waste). Further, 
staff deployed for door-to-door collection were not found to be using protective gears.
(Paragraph 7.3.11.4)
The performance of the ULBs regarding processing of waste was very minimal, ranging 
between 17 and 34.51 per cent of waste generation, due to limited number of processing 
units (51.80 tpd capacity), majority (50 tpd) of which (at Martam and Gangtok) were 
non-functional from November 2021 to January 2023 and again from June 2023. A 
total of 20.2 (out of 56.55) TPD was processed during 2021-22 by four test checked 
ULBs. However, no facility to convert waste to energy by applying technology such as 
incineration, gasification, pyrolysis, or any other technology was available with ULBs. 
Waste to energy plant was not set up in any of test checked ULBs during 2018-23.
(Paragraph 7.3.11.6)
ULBs did not have sanitary landfill sites for disposal of solid waste, except for Gangtok 
town, operated by GMC. Three (out of 7) ULBs (NNP, NJNP and GyNP) used dumping 
yard at Sipchu in West Sikkim for disposal of waste. 
(Paragraph 7.3.12)
The only sanitary landfill (SLF) at Martam, initially designed for 25 years (up to 2040) 
for catering to only one ULB (Gangtok MC), was catering to four ULBs as of March 
2023. Due to excessive dumping of mixed waste, coupled with very limited processing 

Overview
xix
of waste, the shelf life  of landfill would end within two years (2025), requiring urgent 
action by the State Government.
(Paragraph 7.3.12.1)
The monitoring by UDD and ULB was lax as direction of State Level Committee (SLC) 
and National Green Tribunal (NGT) was not acted upon as of June 2023 with regard to 
source segregation and safe disposal as unsegregated waste (mixed waste) was collected 
and transported by the garbage vehicles and the landfill/ dumpsite continued to receive 
mixed waste instead of inert waste. 
(Paragraph 7.3.14.1 & 7.3.14.2)
Recommendations:
	 The Municipal Solid Waste Management Plan should be drawn up duly assessing 
the quantity and composition of waste for scientific management of increased 
quantum of waste.
	 The department should take immediate steps to increase the user fee and ensure 
collection of user fee from all concerned to augment the revenue base of ULB.
	 The source segregation of wastes should be strictly implemented and vehicles 
having compartments must be used for waste collection and transportation to 
ensure that the segregated waste is not mixed up with other waste.
	 The processing of waste should be increased to ensure only inert waste (up to 20 
per cent of total waste generated) reaches the landfill to avoid unnecessary burden 
on landfill.
	 Monitoring should be strengthened to ensure full compliance to NGT orders, 
advisories issued by State Level Advisory Board (SLAB) and SPCB to ensure 
efficacious implementation of SWM Rules.
SUBJECT SPECIFIC COMPLIANCE AUDIT 
EFFICACY OF IMPLEMENTATION OF 74 TH CONSTITUTIONAL 
AMENDMENT ACT IN SIKKIM
Audit Observations
	 The 74th CAA introduced Articles 243Q to 243ZG relating to municipalities. State 
Governments were required to incorporate these provisions in the respective 
Municipalities Acts. The provisions of the 74 th CAA had been appropriately 
incorporated in the Sikkim Municipalities Act, 2007 by the State Legislature. 
However, legal provisions were not backed by decisive actions leading to 
compromise in attaining the spirit of the 74th CAA.
(Paragraph 7.4.4)
	 Government of Sikkim through amendments to ‘the Sikkim Municipalities 
Act, 2007’ transferred (June 2010) six (out of 18) functions to ULBs in 2012. 
However, the ULBs were solely responsible for one function, had limited role in 
five functions and had no role in remaining 12 functions. In absence of transfer 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
xx
of all important functions, these functions continued to be executed by various 
departments of State Government without involving ULBs.
(Paragraph 7.4.4.1)
	The State Government had accepted 13 (out of 20) recommendations pertaining to 
3rd to 5th SFCs for the ULBs. Out of 13 accepted recommendations, no action was 
initiated on 8 recommendations as of November 2023 and due to non-compliance 
of accepted recommendations, the ULBs could not enhance their revenue base, 
especially the revenue from property tax.
(Paragraph 7.4.4.2)
Recommendations:
	 State Government should empower ULBs to function as institutions of self-
government by devolving all the 18 functions enlisted in the 12 th Schedule of the 
Constitution devolved to ULBs urgently.
	 The State Government should empower the ULBs to generate maximum possible 
revenue, especially through early finalisation of modalities of property tax and 
levy of other fees/taxes independently by ULBs to augment the resource base of 
ULBs and enable them to function as institutions of self-governance.
	 The State Government should initiate efforts to empower ULBs by ensuring 
compliance with accepted recommendations of various SFCs.
FOLLOW UP OF AUDIT OBSERV ATIONS
	 As on 31 March 2023 PAC had completed discussions of Audit Reports for the 
year up to 2014-15 and discussions on Audit Report 2015-16 were in progress. 
Recommendations on the Audit Reports for the year up to 2014-15 had been 
issued by the PAC.

were pending for settlement as of March 2023.  The large pendency of IRs indicates

Offices and Heads of Departments as pointed out by audit through IRs.

and Veterinary Services, State Disaster Relief Fund and District Collectors where 
28 IRs and 76 paragraphs were discussed, out of which 14 IRs and 51 paragraphs 
were settled. 
(Paragraph 8.5)

CHAPTER I
 INTRODUCTION

CHAPTER I
INTRODUCTION
1.1 About this Report
This Report of the Comptroller and Auditor General of India (C&AG) relates to matters 
arising from performance audits and test audit of transactions of various departments of 
Government of Sikkim pertaining to Social, Economic, Economic (PSUs), Revenue and 
General Sectors and Local Bodies.
The primary purpose of this Report is to bring to the notice of the State Legislature, 
significant results of audit. Auditing standards require that the materiality level for 
reporting should be commensurate with the nature, volume and magnitude of transactions. 
The findings of audit are expected to enable the Executive to take corrective action, 
to frame appropriate policies as well as to issue directives that will lead to improved 
financial management and contribute to better governance. 
This Chapter in addition to explaining the planning and coverage of audit, provides 
a synopsis of significant instances of non-compliance with applicable laws, rules, 
regulations, various orders and instructions issued by competent authorities. 
1.2 Historical Background and Authority for Audit 
Sikkim is the 22 nd State of the Indian Union, having merged with the Indian Union on 
26 April 1975.  Initially, Sikkim Cell was created under the charge of Joint Director, in 
the Office of the Director of Audit, (Central), Kolkata, to discharge the accounting and 
auditing functions for the State of Sikkim. A full-fledged office as an independent unit 
started functioning in Gangtok w.e.f 10 December 1981. Consequent to restructuring of 
cadres in the Indian Audit and Accounts Department on 01 March 1984, this Office was 
bifurcated into two offices, viz., Accounts & Entitlement Office (A&E) and Audit Office. 
Each office is headed by the Accountant General.  
Under the directions of the C&AG, the Office of the Accountant General (Audit), Sikkim 
conducts audit of Government departments, Public Sector Undertakings, Autonomous 
Bodies and other Institutions 1 under Social, Economic, Revenue and General Sectors, 
and Local Bodies which are spread all over the State. The Accountant General (Audit) 
is assisted by a Deputy Accountant General.
The authority for audit is derived from Article 149 and 151 of the Constitution of India 
and the Comptroller and Auditor General’s (Duties, Powers and Conditions of Service) 
Act, 1971 (C & AG’s (DPC) Act).  Under Section 13 of the C & AG’s (DPC) Act the Office 
of the Accountant General (Audit) has been entrusted with the audit of all expenditure 
incurred from the Consolidated Fund of the Government of Sikkim.  This office conducts 
audit of revenue receipts of the Government of Sikkim under Section 16 of the C&AG’s 
(DPC) Act.  The office of Accountant General (Audit) conducts supplementary audit 
of the accounts of four State Government companies under Section 143 (6) (a) of the 
Companies Act 2013, as these companies are registered under the Companies Act 2013. 
The Companies Act 1956 as well as Companies Act 2013 have not been extended to 
1 Audit of World Bank assisted projects, Asian Development Bank assisted projects etc.

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
2
the State of Sikkim. Therefore, other companies of the State Government, (except the 
above four companies) are registered under State’s own “Registration of Companies 
Act, Sikkim 1961”. Audit of these companies are entrusted to the C&AG of India by the 
State Government from time to time. 
This office is also responsible for audit of accounts of autonomous bodies and authorities 
falling under Sections 14, 15, 19 (2), 19 (3) and 20 (1) of C&AG’s (DPC) Act.  The 
C&AG prescribes the principles and methodologies for various audits in the Auditing 
Standards and the Regulations on Audit and Accounts (Amendments), 2020.  
1.3 Planning and conduct of Audit
Audit process commences with the assessment of risk of the departments based on the 
expenditure incurred, criticality/complexity of activities, priority accorded for the activity 
by the Government, level of delegated financial powers, assessment of internal controls, 
concerns of stakeholders, previous audit findings, etc. Based on this risk assessment, 
frequency and extent of audit are decided and an annual audit plan is formulated to 
conduct audit. 
After completion of each audit, Inspection Report (IR) containing audit findings is issued 
to the Head of the unit with a request to furnish replies within one month of receipt of 
the IR. Wherever replies are received, audit findings are either settled or further action 
for compliance is advised. Significant audit observations pointed out in these IRs, which 
require attention at the highest level in the Government, are processed for inclusion in 
the Audit Reports which are submitted to the Governor of Sikkim under Article 151 of 
the Constitution of India for being laid in the State Legislature.
Audit Plan is prepared in such a way that it fits into the long term and short-term 
goals of audit in consonance with the overall “Vision and Mission” of the Supreme 
Audit Institution of India.  It is prepared after carrying out risk assessment and 
keeping in view the available manpower. Elements of the Audit Quality Management 
Framework (AQMF) viz., materiality, inputs from V oucher Level Computerisation 
(VLC), financial size of the units, data from various e-Governance initiatives taken by 
government, flagship programmes undertaken by auditees, press criticism/ electronic 
media coverage, expected audit impact and continuous improvement based on past 
experience, etc. are taken into account to the extent possible while framing out the plan. 
A sector wise analysis of government spending, investment policy of the government in 
infrastructure development, industrialisation and socio-economic activities along with 
due consideration of possible audit impact is taken into account in prioritising auditee 
units for preparing the audit plan.
Considering the availability of resources, focus has been given in areas of high financial 
risk than to thinly spreading out resources throughout Government activities. This would 
have better socio-economic impact and add value to governance.  
Out of 1015 auditee units, 266 units were planned for audit during 2022-23 after carrying 
out risk assessment and keeping in view the available human resources, of which 211 
units were actually audited during 2022-23.

2.2 Planning and conduct of audit 
Audit process starts with the assessment of risks faced by various departments of the 
Government. The assessment is based on expenditure incurred, criticality/complexity of 
activities, level of delegated financial powers, assessment of overall internal controls, etc.
After completion of audit of each unit on a test check basis, Inspection Reports (IRs) 
containing audit findings are issued to the heads of the departments. The departments are 
to furnish replies to the audit findings within one month of receipt of the IRs. Whenever 
replies are received, audit findings are either settled based on reply/action taken or 
further action is required by the audited entities for compliance. Some of the important 
audit observations arising out of the IRs are processed for inclusion in the Audit Reports. 
The Audit Reports are submitted to the Governor of the State under Article 151 of the 
Constitution of India for laying on the table of the Legislature.
Test audits were conducted involving expenditure of ₹725.01 crore (including expenditure 
of ₹ 464.37 crore of previous years) during 2022-23, of the State Government under 
Social Sector. The details of year-wise break-up are given in Appendix 2.1.
This Chapter contains one Performance Audit and one Subject Specific Compliance 
Audit.
PERFORMANCE AUDIT
SIKKIM BUILDING AND OTHER CONSTRUCTION WORKERS WELFARE 
BOARD
2.3  Performance Audit on Building and Other Construction Workers Welfare 
Cess
2.3.1  Introduction
Government of India (GoI) enacted the Building and Other Construction Workers’ 
(Regulation of Employment and Conditions of Service) Act, 1996  (BOCW Act) and 

Chapter II: Social Sector
7
Building and Other Construction Workers Welfare Cess Act in 1996  (Cess Act). The 
Acts aimed to provide safety, health, and welfare measures for the benefit of building 
and other construction workers (BOC) through levy/collection of cess.
The Act, inter alia , mandated the constitution of a Building and Other Construction 
Workers’ Welfare Board and framing of rules by every State Government to exercise 
the powers conferred under the Act for the welfare of BOC Workers. The provision of 
the Act applies to “every establishment 2 which employs or had employed on any day 
of the preceding twelve months, ten or more building workers in any building or other 
construction work”. Further, the Act provides that every building worker between the 
age group of 18 to 60  years who was not a member of any welfare fund established 
under any law and had completed ninety days of service during the preceding twelve 
months as a construction worker in the State could be registered as a ‘beneficiary’. 
Government of Sikkim (GoS) notified 3 (August 2010) the Sikkim Building and Other 
Construction Workers (Regulation of Employment and Conditions of Service) (SBOCW) 
Rules, 2010 wherein Rule 263 envisages constitution of fund by the name “The Sikkim 
Building and Other construction Workers Welfare Fund”.  The collection of BOCW 
cess was fixed at the rate of one per cent on the cost of construction incurred by the 
employers. 
The Sikkim Building & Other Construction Workers Welfare Board (SBOCWB) was 
constituted vide State Government notification dated 26 February 20104.
Rule 259 of the SBOCW Rules, 2010 enshrined the powers, duties, and functions of the 
Board as under:
The Board shall be responsible for- 
a) all matters connected with the administration of the fund and grant of benefits to the 
beneficiaries under the Act and Rules made thereof;
b) laying down policies for the deposits of the amount of the fund;
c) submission of an annual budget to the Government on the activities of the Board; 
d) submission of an annual report to the Government on the activities of the Board;
e) proper maintenance of accounts;
f) annual audit of accounts of the Board following the provisions of the Act;
g) collection of contributions to the fund; 
h) launching of prosecution for and on behalf of the Board;
i) speedy settlement of claims and sanction of advances and other benefits;
j) proper and timely recovery of any amount due to the Board, and;
k) submission of information to the Government on such matters as the government 
may refer to it, from time to time.
2 Establishment means any establishment belonging to, or under the control of, Government, anybody 
corporate or firm, an individual or association or other body of individuals which or who employs 
building workers in any building or other construction work; and includes an establishment belonging 
to a contractor but does not include an individual who employs such workers in any building or 
construction work in relation to his own residence, the total cost of such construction not being more 
than ₹ 10 lakh.
3  8/DL dated 31 August 2010.
4 No. 13 DL dated 26 February 2010.

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
8
GoI had recommended (October 2020) the consolidation of existing labour laws through 
simplification, rationalisation, and amalgamation into four labour codes focusing on 
Wages, Industrial Relations, Social Security, and Occupational Safety, Health, and 
Working Conditions. The implementation of these Labour Codes was contingent 
upon notification by the Central Government, which would lead to the repeal of all 
existing Labour relating Acts and Rules, including the BOCW RECS and Cess Act. 
Further, GoI directed (November 2020) all State Governments to formulate rules for the 
implementation of these Labor Codes.
Subsequently, the Government of Sikkim (GoS) notified the Wages (January 
2022), Industrial (April 2022), and Social Security (September 2022) Rules for the 
implementation of the respective codes. However, the Occupational Safety, Health, and 
Working Conditions rules was yet to be notified by the Govt of India. Consequently, 
the performance audit conducted during the period from April 2017 to March 2022 was 
conducted on the implementation of existing BOCW (RECS) and Cess Act 1996, which 
remained in force in Sikkim.
2.3.2 Organisational set-up
The SBOCWB is headed by a Chairperson who is the Minister in charge of the Labour 
Department (LD), GoS. Apart from representatives from GoS and member representatives 
from various establishments, the Secretary to the Government of Sikkim, LD works 
as a Chief Executive Officer who is assisted by the Chief Inspector (Joint Labour 
Commissioner, LD). At the district level, all Deputy/Assistant Labour Commissioners 
and Labour Inspectors were designated (June 2009) as Registering Officers and Cess 
collectors for registration of construction workers as well as for establishments engaging 
the workers and collecting of cess from these establishments respectively. The Board 
had not appointed a full-time Secretary for discharging regular function of the Board. 
Since the inception, the Secretary, LD was carrying out the duties of the Board. The 
Board had appointed 15 employees, however, all the staff were appointed against lower 
scales (assistant cess collector, driver, peons, chowkidars, etc.), who had very little role 
in the functioning of the Board. Further, the Board had not formulated any sanctioned 
strength for its operations till the date of audit (April 2023).

2.3.3 Audit Objectives 
The PA was conducted to get a reasonable assurance that; 
➢ the rules notified by the Government under the Act are consistent with the spirit of 
both BOCW Act 1996 and Cess Act 1996, 
➢ there was an effective system for the registration of establishments and 
beneficiaries, 
➢ the cess assessment, cess collection and transfer of collected cess to the fund were 
efficient, 
➢ the Government prescribed appropriate health and safety norms and could ensure 
an environment of compliance with those norms by employers. 
➢ the administration and utilisation of funds on implementation of welfare scheme 
by the Board was efficient and effective and as per Act and rules framed by the 
State Government.

2.3.5 Audit Methodology
Performance Audit (PA) commenced with an Entry Conference (September 2022) with the 
Labour Department at the level of the Secretary to the Government and Chief Executive 
Officer of the Board  wherein audit objectives, scope, criteria and methodology were 
explained. Audit findings were discussed in an Exit Conference (June 2023)  with the 
Secretary and Chief Executive Officer of BOCW Board. In the exit meeting, Secretary 
stated that things may have gone wrong in some of the areas of implementation of Acts 
and Rules by the Boards but functioning of the Board is in the process of improvement 
and there is scope for improvement. Secretary assured that with the co-operation of 
Audit office, Board’s functioning with regard to quantum of cess collection, registration 
mechanism, utilisation of fund for the welfare of the BOC Workers etc. would be 
improved a lot in the next coming years. Secretary also stated that finding of audit will 
be taken as guidance and also requested that finding of audit may be taken in positive 
direction rather than negative action of the Board.
2.3.6 Audit Criteria
Audit findings were benchmarked against the criteria derived from the following sources: 
	Building and Other Construction Workers (Regulation of Employment and 
Conditions of Service) Act, 1996 (BOCW Act); 
	Building and Other Construction Workers Welfare Cess Act 1996 (Cess Act); 
	Building & Other Construction Workers (RECS) Rules 1998;
	Sikkim Building & Other Construction (RECS) Rules 2010;
	Workmen Compensation Act 1923;
	Sikkim Financial Rules, 1979;
	Guidelines, Circulars, Notifications and various Orders issued by GoI/ State; 
Government from time to time;
	Judgments of Hon’ble Supreme Court of India.

2.3.8	 Audit	findings
2.3.8.1 Financial Management
As per Rule 263 of the SBOCW (RECS) Rule 2010 the Welfare Board fund constitutes 
grants or loans or advances, if any, made to the Board by the Central Government, all 
contributions paid by the beneficiaries and all sums received by the Board under the Cess 
Act, 1996 and Rules made thereunder may be credited. The main source of the Fund was 
the cess levied and collected from employers who undertake construction works. The 
cess was levied at the rate of one per cent of the cost of construction. Cess was required 
to be spent for the welfare of building and other construction workers on schemes like 

Chapter II: Social Sector

pension, maternity benefits, disability pension, payment for funeral assistance, medical 
assistance, financial assistance for education and marriage of children and accidents, etc.

2017-18 to 2021-22. As such, Audit could not verify the authenticity of the figures 
reflected in the statement of the Annual Accounts.
2.3.8.2 Non-preparation of Annual Budget
Section 25 of the BOCW Act, provides that the Board shall prepare during each financial 
year its budget for the next financial year, showing the estimated receipts and expenditure 
of the Board and forward the same to State Government and GOI.
Further, Rule 259 (1C) of the SBOCW Rules  stipulates the submission of the annual 
budget on activities of the Board to the Government.
Audit observed that the Board did not prepare annual budget estimates upto 2021-22.  
In the absence of an annual budget, the Board could not effectively plan and manage its 
financial resources to support the implementation of various programmes and projects 
to promote the welfare of BOC workers.

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
12
2.3.8.3 Investment of Welfare Fund in non-nationalised Banks
As per the direction of the Supreme Court (SC)  issued in Writ Petition No. 38 of 
2006 (March 2018), the funds available with the Welfare Boards which have not been 
disbursed or are not likely to be disbursed within a short period should be properly 
invested in nationalised banks only.
Further, section 280 of SBOCW RECS Rules, 2010 specifies investment of cess funds in 
Scheduled banks and Securities under clause (a) to (d) of section 20 of the Indian Trust 
Act, 1882.
Audit observed that during 2017-18 to 2021-22, the Board had invested ₹ 73.44 crore 
in non-nationalised banks11, which was in contravention to the above directions of SC 
and the SBOCW rules, 2010. However, as of March 2022, out of the total investment 
of ₹ 149.41 crore12 the Welfare fund, the Board invested 71.35 per cent of the fund i.e., 
₹ 106.60 crore in non-nationalised banks with only 29 per cent of the investment i.e., 
₹ 42.80 crore having been invested in nationalised banks.
Further, investing welfare funds in non-nationalised banks puts the fund at high risk. 
Since, a welfare fund comprises fee collected for the most vulnerable segment of 
unorganised labour whose works are characterised by their inherent risk to life and 
limbs, complete protection and security of such fund must be ensured by the State for 
keeping it risk-free during any unpredictable financial crisis. 
2.3.8.4 Excess Administrative Expenditure
Section 24 (3) of the BOCW (RECS) Act, 1996  states that administrative expenses of 
the Board in any financial year should not exceed five per cent of the total expenditure 
incurred during the year. However, the Board incurred administrative expenditure which 
exceeded the norms of such expenditure and ranged between 3.48 per cent and 65.93 per 
cent of total expenditure as detailed in Table 2.4.

It was observed that the administrative expenses exceeded their permissible limit during

Objective 1: Whether the rules notified by the Government under the Act are 
consistent with the spirit of both Acts?
2.3.9  Planning and Implementation of Act
2.3.9.1  Disparity between Act and Government Notification
The Cess Act of 1996, stipulated that proceeds of the cess collected should be paid to the 
Board by the local authority or the State Government collecting the cess after deducting 
cost of collection of cess not exceeding one per cent  of the amount collected. This 
included the cess collected in respect of Government works as well as Private works 
where cess may be collected in advance by the plan-approving authority.
However, the notification issued (September 2010)  by the State Government did not 
include any clause for advance cess collection by building planning authority and one 
per cent collection charges for the service rendered. Further, the notification included 
cess deduction towards construction works of the State Government Departments and it 
did not include the provision of cess deduction from Private constructions (Paragraph 
2.3.11.4). Thus, there was a disparity between the provisions of the Cess Act and the 
State Government notification.
Objective 2: Whether there was an effective system for the registration of 
establishments and beneficiaries?
2.3.10   Implementation of Acts & Rules
2.3.10.1 Registration of BOC Workers
Section 12 of the BOCW Act  and Rule 264 of SBOCW Rules  outline the eligibility 
criteria for registration of a BOC Worker as a beneficiary under the Act. The provisions 
state that any building worker who is between 18 and 60 years of age and had engaged 
in building or construction work for at least 90 days in the preceding 12 months is 
eligible for registration. The registration was a one-time process based on which a 
building worker registered as a beneficiary becomes entitled to the benefits provided by 
the Board from its welfare fund.
Rule 265 of SBCOW Rules, 2010 envisages that an application (Form XXVII) should 
be submitted by the beneficiary to the Beneficiary Registering Officer authorised by the 
Board, with the application form to contain the name of the builder worker, father’s name, 
date of birth, permanent address, marital status, name of the bank, etc. accompanied by 
three passport size photographs and attested copies of the school certificate and birth 
certificate. Thereafter, (Rule 266) on receipt of the application, Registering Officer shall 
register the name of the worker as beneficiary under the Act, provided the applicant has 
complied with all the requirements as laid down in the Rules. Further, Rule 266(4) of 
the SBCOW Rules specifies filing of nomination Form XXXI by the building workers.
Moreover, Section 16 of the BOCW RECS Act 1996 read with Rule 267 (1) of Sikkim 
BOCW Rules 2010 stipulates that a beneficiary of the fund shall contribute to the fund at 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
14
₹ 20 per month. This contribution shall be remitted once in 3 months in any of the banks 
specified by the Board in the district in which the member resides.
Further, GoI direction on Model Welfare Scheme envisages that the authority responsible 
for registration of migrant workers must share information with the respective State 
Welfare Board, so that their registration/renewal/delivery of welfare benefits is monitored 
by the Boards of the source States.
Audit observed that the Board had not maintained details of workers viz., name, contact 
numbers, Certificate for age proof and address proof, Bank details, family details for 
nomination, etc. which were required to be submitted by the employer on the above-
prescribed forms. It was only during the audit that the Board provided (31 October 
2022) a list of 5,278 registered workers 13 by considering the contribution/subscription 
fees (September 2022) received from various employers. The Board also could not 
produce a year-wise list of registered workers for the period 2017-22. In the absence of 
such vital data, audit could not ascertain the eligibility of registered workers, yearwise 
trends of registration of workers and could not verify details such as number of workers 
working in the State, since when working, employers under whom they were working, 
etc. in the State.
The Board did not conduct any inspections of the newly registered establishments to 
ensure that eligible workers get registered as ‘beneficiaries’. Also, there was no initiative 
taken by the Board to enrol new BOC workers by conducting awareness programmes as 
elaborated in Paragraph 2.3.13.3 nor was there any initiatives to simplify the process 
of registration for workers, such as through online registration systems. This was also 
evident from the fact that a significant number of construction workers were not found to 
be registered with the Board during JPV . The Board also did not maintain records i.e., the 
certificate of employment issued by the employer to the registered construction workers 
to ensure eligibility to become beneficiaries of BOCW welfare scheme notified by the 
Board. Neither had the Board maintained any records of registered migrant workers, 
nor had they initiated any provision for capturing the change in employer of existing 
registered workers.
Thus, the Board did not have a comprehensive database of the construction workers. 
The Board also could not make satisfactory efforts to enrol unregistered workers. In 
absence of such efforts, there was possibility that eligible workers remained outside the 
ambit of the Act.
2.3.10.2 Non-identification of unregistered establishments
Section 1 (4) of the BOCW Act states that the Act applies to every establishment which 
employs, or had employed on any day of the preceding twelve months, ten or more 
building workers in any building or other construction works.
Section 7 of the BOCW Act stipulates that every employer, undertaking construction 
work, would make an application to the registering officer for registration of the 
establishment within 60 days from the commencement of the work. Also, Chapter IV of 
the SBOCW Rules specifies the manner of registration of the establishments.
Further, Section 43 of the BOCW Act  inter alia stipulates that an Inspector may enter 
any premises or place where building or other construction works are carried on for 
the purpose of examining any records and require the production thereof for inspection 
13  East: 2067, South: 1340, North: 754 and West: 1117.

Chapter II: Social Sector
15
and examine any person whom he finds in any such premises or place and who, he has 
reasonable cause to believe, is a building worker employed therein.
It was observed that the Board had not made any concerted effort to identify and register 
the establishments or employers involved in construction works in Sikkim. There was no 
comprehensive database of either registered or unregistered establishments/ employers 
maintained by the Board. While the Board did maintain an establishment register, this 
only included manual entries 14 of those establishments or employers who had applied 
for registration, leaving many unregistered entities outside its purview. Scrutiny of the 
establishment register showed that during 2017-22, in selected two districts viz., East 
and South, the Board had registered 121 numbers (South 96 & East 25) of construction 
works which had applied for registration. The Monthly Progress Reports provided 
by two State Departments, viz., Building & Housing (BHD) and Urban Development  
Department (UDD) and two PSEs namely, Gangtok Smart City Development Limited 
(GSCDL) and National Highways and Infrastructure Development Corporation Limited 
(NHIDCL) showed a total of 349 construction works 15 being executed in the selected 
districts. Audit verified four construction sites16 along with department officials wherein, 
it was observed that all the inspected establishments fulfilled the criteria of having more 
than ten construction workers in their workforce. However, they were not registered 
with the Board. 
It was evident that the employers of the eligible establishments had not applied for 
registration from the Board prior to commencement of the work. Further, the Labour 
Inspectors had also not carried out any exercise to identify the establishments eligible for 
registration from these 349 establishments in the sampled districts. The Board restricted 
itself only to collecting one per cent of cess received from the State departments and 
contractors approaching the Board for obtaining cess clearance certificates. No formal 
mechanism such as inspection of establishments and verification of workers involved 
in BOC work, was introduced by the Board to ensure identification of prospective 
establishments as mandated under the Act.
The Board stated that it had only focused on registering Government contractors 
involved in various Government works and had not included the works which were 
executed by the department itself.  Further, the Board stated that the Act applies only to 
those establishments where ten or more building workers were employed. Hence, it was 
not applicable to register all the works executed by the Contractors and Departments.  
The reply was not acceptable as the Board did not conduct any inspection to assess 
eligibility for registration of establishments carrying out works where 10 or more 
workers were employed by the employer, towards ensuring their registration. 
Thus, due to non-identification of all unregistered establishments and non-maintenance 
of a comprehensive database for capturing details of all establishments or employers, the 
Board not only could not ensure implementation of welfare benefits for all BOC workers 
but also could not assess and collect the actual cess receivable from these establishments.
