2.3.10.3 Registration of ineligible beneficiaries led to irregular expenditure of ₹ 2.12 
crore
Section 2 (1) (d) of the BOCW Act defines a building or other construction work.  
Further, Section 2(e) of the Act defines a building worker as a person who is employed 
to do any skilled, semi-skilled or unskilled, manual, supervisory, technical or clerical 
work on hire or reward, whether the terms of employment be expressed or implied, in 
connection with any building or other construction work. No workers beyond the ambit 
of the Act should be included or registered under the class of BOC workers.
Scrutiny of records (2017- 22) of registered beneficiaries under the Board showed that 
222 tea plantation workers  from Temi Tea Estate (TTE) were registered by the Board 
as beneficiaries. It was found that the Board had transferred ₹ 1.56 crore  towards  
construction of a creche waiting shed and Badminton court to TTE (2018-19) which was 
not related to the scope of welfare of BOC workers. Further, the Board had also spent 
₹ 4.44 lakh17 as financial relief during COVID-19 (April 2020) and purchased welfare 
items to the tune of ₹ 51.70 lakh (October 2020) for distribution to TTE workers on eight 
occasions (2017-21).
The plantation workers were regular wage earners who were exclusively involved in 
growing and manufacturing tea and were not engaged in any construction activities. 
The plantation workers were governed by the Plantation Labour Act, 1951 and as such 
did not fall under the purview of the BOCW Act. Hence, the registration of 222 plantation 
workers was ineligible and they were not entitled for benefit from the BOCW Welfare 
fund.
Similarly, the Board had also registered workers of Roads and Bridges Department 
(RBD), appointed by the State’s Public Works Department (PWD). The inclusion of 
these workers contradicted the purpose of the BOCW Act, which is intended to protect 
vulnerable workers in temporary and unorganised sectors, as emphasised in the Supreme 
Court’s judgment (March 2018).
The matter of including Muster Roll workers of the PWD under SBOCWB was brought 
to the attention of the Law Department by the Board. The Law Department stated 
(September 2013) that the services of Muster Roll workers under PWD, despite being 
temporary, eventually become regularised by the State and hence could not be brought 
under the ambit of the BOCW Act. This information was communicated during the 14th 
Board Meeting (October 2013) where no decision was given. However, disregarding 
the clear direction from the Law Department, the Board in 32nd Board Meeting (August 
2018) decided to register RBD workers, including those on muster roll and one-family-
one-job basis, under the BOCW Act and transferred ₹ 35.92 lakh during COVID-19 
(March 2020) to the employers bank account for onward transmission to 1796 workers.
As the BOCW Act specifically aimed to meet the welfare of those categories of 
workers who are involved exclusively in Building or other construction works and are 
characterised by their casual nature and temporary relationship with their employer, the 
decision of the Board to incur expenditure for the welfare of tea plantation and PWD 
workers contravened the essence of the Act.
Thus, the expenditure of ₹ 2.48 crore18 from the BOCW Welfare Fund was irregular.
17 222 Nos. x ₹ 2000. 
18 ₹ 150 lakh +₹ 6 lakh + ₹4.44 lakh+ ₹51.70 lakh + ₹ 35.92 lakh = ₹ 247.92 lakh.

Chapter II: Social Sector
17
2.3.10.4 Identity Cards (ID Cards) for BOCW beneficiaries 
Section 12 of the BOCW Act  inter alia stipulates that every building worker who has 
completed either 18 years of age, b ut has not completed 60 years of age, and who has 
been engaged in any building or other construction work for not less than ninety days 
during the preceding twelve months shall be eligible for registration as a beneficiary 
under this Act. 
Section 13(I) of the BOCW Act  stipulates that the Board shall issue an identity card 
(ID) to every beneficiary with a photograph duly affixed thereon with enough space for 
entering the details of the building or other construction work done by him/her.
Similarly, Rule 266 (1) of SBOCW Rules  specifies that the Beneficiary Registering 
Officer after receiving the application under Rule 265 shall register the name of the 
worker as a beneficiary under the Act, if such applicant workman has complied with 
all the requirements as laid down in these rules and shall issue an ID card with his 
photograph duly affixed thereon in form XXVIII. Further, it is also stated in the Rule that 
Beneficiary Registering Officer shall maintain a register of ID card issued (Form XXIX).
Scrutiny of records (2017-22) showed that the Board had expended an amount of 
₹ 5.76 lakh19 (March 2019) towards the printing of 5,000 ID cards for issuing to BOC 
Workers. Records showed that these ID cards had been issued to the employers. However, 
there was no record that the BOC workers had received any ID cards. The matter was 
also discussed with the Board during an entry conference (September 2022) wherein it 
was informed that identity cards were issued in all four districts except East. However, 
the Board could not produce records of their issuance such as acknowledgement 
indicating receipt of IDs by the registered workers, register of issued IDs maintained by 
the registering officer, etc. Apart from the above, during the Joint Physical Verification 
of registered establishments and beneficiary survey in selected districts conducted by the 
audit team along with the Board Officials, none of the registered workers or beneficiaries 
could produce any ID card despite being registered with the Board.
The ID card serves as documentary proof that the worker had engaged in construction 
work for more than 90 days to be eligible for registration and availing benefits under 
the Act. However, non-issuance of ID cards was not only in contravention to the above 
Act & Rule but also raises doubts about the genuineness of BOC workers. Further, there 
were no records of receipt of ID cards maintained by the Board which made the entire 
expenditure of ₹ 5.76 lakh for payment against printing of ID cards doubtful.
2.3.10.5 Non-implementation of the Universal Access Number to the registered 
construction workers
Supreme Court’s (SC) Judgement (March 2018) referring to the previous Court hearing 
(September 2015) wherein Ministry of Labour and Employment, Government of India 
(MoL&E) stated introducing of Universal Access Number (UAN) to every construction 
worker, so that, if he or she migrates from one State to another, the benefit of registration 
does not get lost, nor does the construction workers need to get themselves registered in 
other State.
Further, according to the direction of SC (March 2018), the Director General, Labour 
Welfare, MoL&E instructed (July 2020) all the State Government and Union Territories 
19  M/s Milan Enterprise.

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
18
to comply with the Model Scheme for the welfare of BOC Workers  which included 
allotment of Unique Identification Numbers (UIN) to the registered BOC workers and 
uploading complete details on State web portal and National BOCW portal.
Similarly, Paragraph 6.2.2 of the direction received from the MoL & E (July 2020) on 
the implementation of the Mission Mode Project (MMP)by all State Welfare Boards 
(SWBs) mentioned issuing of online migration certificate (MC) to be developed and 
operationalised by the SWBs.
Audit observed that the system of providing UAN/UIN to construction workers was not 
introduced by the Board during the period of audit (2017-22). There were neither recorded 
reasons for non-commencement of UAN/UIN with the Board nor any compliance on the 
development of MC which had a scheduled implementation timeline of three months 
from the date of issue of the GoI letter (July 2020). Thus, due to noncompliance to the 
above directions, the Board could not provide continuity of already registered workers 
migrating from the state. This would adversely impact the portability of grant of welfare 
benefits to migrant workers (Paragraph 2.3.13.1).
2.3.10.6  Non-submission of returns by the registered establishments
As per Rule 268 of the SBOCW Rules, 2010  every employer shall before the 15 th day 
of every month submit returns showing the details of workers entitled to be registered 
as well as those who left the service during the preceding month and particulars of the 
establishment. Similarly, as per Rule 242 of the BOCW Rules 1998, every employer 
shall send annually a return relating to an establishment to the Registering Officer having 
jurisdiction ensuring it is received not later than 15 th February following each calendar 
year.
Audit scrutiny showed that none of the employers carrying out building or other 
construction works under the Board’s record had furnished requisite returns during 2017-
22. Resultantly, the Board had no information about the details of the workers entitled 
to be registered, information about the maximum number of building workers employed 
on any day during the year, the total number of days of engagement of building workers, 
etc.
2.3.10.7 Intimation of notice of commencement and completion of the building and 
other construction work
Rule 237 (1) of the SBOCW Rules 2010 stipulates that every employer shall, at least 
thirty days before the commencement of any building or other construction work under 
his control, sent or cause to be sent to the Inspector having jurisdiction, a written notice 
intimation of the actual date of the commencement, the probable date of completion and 
other such particulars as referred to in sub section (1) of section 46 of the Act relating to 
such building or other construction work in Form-IV , annexed to these rules.
Further, as per Section 48 of the BOCW Act, an employer who fails to give notice of the 
commencement of the building or other construction work, shall be punishable with 
imprisonment for a term that may extend to three months, or with a fine that may extend 
to two thousand rupees or both.
Audit observed from records made available that none of the registered establishments 
had intimated the actual date of commencement and probable date of completion as 
per the provision of the Act, ibid. Neither had the Board taken any steps to acquire 

Chapter II: Social Sector
19
information from the establishments nor was any penalty imposed on employers who 
failed to provide such notice.
Thus, non-obtaining of information from employers had led to the Board’s inability to 
conduct inspections and identify potential establishments for registration. Consequently, 
eligible beneficiaries have been deprived of the benefits of welfare schemes of the Board.
Objective 3: Whether cess assessment, cess collection and transfer of collected cess 
to the fund were efficient?
During the period 2017-18 to 2021-22, the Board had collected a total cess of ₹ 87.92 crore 
and accumulated interest of ₹ 31.50 crore as exhibited in Table 2.3. Audit noticed various 
deficiencies in collection of cess which includes non-implementation of cess collection 
from Private institutions, absence of well-functioning assessment mechanism and short 
realisation of cess, which has largely affected potential cess receivables in the State as 
elaborated in succeeding paragraphs.
2.3.11 Assessment and Collection of the Cess
2.3.11.1 Non-assessment of cess receivable
Section 4 (1) of the BOCW Act, 1996 stipulates that “Every employer shall furnish such 
return to such officer or authority, in such manner and at such time as may be prescribed”. 
Section 4 (2), further, states that “If any person carrying on building or other construction 
work, liable to pay the cess, fails to furnish any return under subsection (1), the officer or 
the authority shall give a notice requiring such person to furnish such return before such 
date as may be specified in the notice”.
Further, Section 5 of the Act specifies the manner of assessment of cess by the authority 
after making such inquiry, if he thinks fit, from the furnished returns vis-à-vis Section 
4 (1) of the Act and finally, passing of assessment order stipulating specified time on 
which cess shall be paid by the employer.
From records made available, Audit observed that the system of cess assessment and 
passing of assessment orders  not brought into effect although the State had appointed 
ALC/DLC as Assessing Officer (June 2009) for this purpose. Also, the Board did 
not ensure furnishing of returns from the Employers as envisaged in the above Act. 
On its part, the Board only considered the collection of one per cent cess from every 
construction work bill which was received from the employer for obtaining a Labour 
Clearance Certificate (LCC)20. 
Further, the inspection of work sites was also not conducted by any authorised officer 
for verifying the cost of construction. As a result, the overall cost of construction and 
cess to be realised could not be ascertained from scrutiny of records made available 
to Audit.  Thus, there was no scope for assessing the quantum of un-realised cess, 
penalty, or interest. The Board’s approach is fraught with the risk of short collection or 
nonrealisation of the cess which have been illustrated in the succeeding paragraphs.
2.3.11.2 Non-receipt of Cess fund ₹ 2.64 crore
Section 3 to 5 of the Cess Act, 1996 envisages the function of the Board on the collection 
of cess at the rate of one per cent of the cost of construction, furnishing return of the 
employer carrying BOC work, assessment of the cess from the furnished return and 
20  For release of the running bills and final bills, LCC is mandatory. 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
20
passing of assessment order specifying date within which the cess shall be paid by the 
employer. Similarly, Section 4 and 5 of the Cess Rule, 1998 stipulates the manner of cess 
collection and transfer of cess by the Government office, PSUs, and Local authorities 
within 30 days of its collection.
To implement Cess Act in Sikkim, the State vide notification dated 06 September 2010 
directed all the State Government Departments to deduct one per cent cess from the 
estimated cost of construction and forward the same vide Account payee cheque/ Bank 
draft to the Board. Further, during the submission of the work bill by the contractor/
establishment, a one per cent cess was to be deducted before the bills were cleared by 
the Departments.
The Board issues Labour Clearance Certificate (LCC) on deposit of one per cent cess by 
drawing a Bank draft in favour of the Secretary, BOCW from every work (Contractual/ 
departmental). Therefore, based on the submission of the LCC, the concerned State 
departments release the bills (RA/ Final) for their respective works. Thus, submission of 
LCC is the only proof of cess deposit and in its absence, the payment is not released by 
the State Pay and Accounts Office..  Thus, the issue of LCC based solely on receipt of 
cess from the employer was inadequate to ensure proper collection of cess from every 
registered project. The sample copy of LCC shown in the following image.
Image 2.1: Receipt of LCC Image 2.2: LCC Form
Based on the above, audit observed the following:
	The cess from three units 21 (State Department/Autonomous Bodies) was not 
deposited into the Boards Account. Test check of cess deposits from departmental 
vouchers, bank statements and the Board’s information showed that there was a 
short collection of cess of ₹ 2.64 crore22 from three units. Thus, due to a lack of cess 
assessment, the Board did not realise the actual cess receivable by it.
	Rule 12 of Cess Rules, 1998 stipulates imposing of penalty for non-payment or 
less payment of cess including the cess deducted at source within the date specified 
in the assessment order by an Assessing Officer not exceeding the amount of cess. 
However, due to the lack of an assessment order or specified date on which cess 
was to be paid by the establishment, the power to impose penalty in such cases is 
lost.

₹ 138.26 lakh, Building & Housing Department: ₹ 0.56 lakh.

21
	The State Government’s circular (Sept 2010) on implementation of the BOCW 
and Cess Act was only instruction for the State Departments, no such gazette 
notifications/circulars were issued for making cess deduction mandatory for all 
Autonomous Bodies and PSUs, where cess is to be collected as per the provisions 
of the Act. Further, the system of checks for cess deposits through LCC was only 
limited to work expenditure being routed through State PAO. Thus,  due to lack of 
an effective mechanism for cess assessment, inability to enforce cess collection, 
and absence of reconciliation for deposited cess, the Board could not collect actual 
cess receivable which led to a short-levy of cess of ₹ 2.64 crore.
2.3.11.3  Non-deposit/ non-crediting & non-presentation of Bank/ Demand drafts of 
cess -₹ 102.47 lakh
Section 3 of the Building and Other Construction Workers Welfare Cess (RECS) Act-
1996 stipulates levy and collection of cess, at such rate not exceeding two  per cent but 
not less than one per cent of the cost of construction incurred by an employer, as the 
Central Government, may, by notification in the Official Gazette, from time to time 
specify. Further, the Cess deducted at source by the implementing department/ agencies, 
etc. is deposited either by submitting through Cheque or by direct credit to the Board’s 
Bank account.
	Non deposit of cess received by the Board in the Bank Account
The Board did not reconcile the amount of collected cess recorded in the cess register with 
the Bank Statements to date (Jan 2023). It was observed that in one case ₹  81.42 lakh23 
was yet to be credited into Board’s Account despite the fact the DD was already received 
by the Board (October 2022).
	Loss of cess due to non-revalidation of cheques of ₹ 13.04 lakh
Audit scrutiny for the period 2017 to 2022 showed that in 13 cases ranging between 
560 months, amount of ₹ 13.04 lakh was lying pending to be deposited in the Board’s 
Account. It was noticed that the amount remained undeposited due to reasons such as 
blocked/suspended account number or expiry of validity of cheques.
	Non-presentation of Bank/Demand Drafts/misplacement/loss of cheque 
amounting to ₹ 8.01 lakh
Bank information/ statements showed that from April 2017 till November 2022, there 
were 113 cases of unsettled DDs of ₹ 6.19 lakh pending in the Bank’s suspense account. 
The reason behind the non-remittance of DDs not cleared was not furnished to audit 
(April 2023). It was also observed that the Board was unaware of the fact, that the 
said amount was pending settlement till the date of audit. Further, as the figure only 
represents cases during the audit period (2017-22), the possibility of such un-settled 
cases may not be ruled out since opening of the Board’s Account (2010). The Board does 
not have a monitoring system to check that whether all DDs received had been deposited 
into Board’s account. These DDs lying with the Board for more than three months need 
to be re-validated and deposited in the Board’s account.
In addition to the above, scrutiny of bank statements and re-validation of cheque records 
showed that two cheques of ₹ 1.82 lakh  relating to the period 2017-18 were yet to be 
deposited in the Board’s account. It was observed that these two cheques were misplaced 
by the bank during the process of outstation cheque clearing. The Board had requested 
23  Cheque No. 084018 dt. 21 October 2022.

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
22
(September 2017) the employer for the issue of fresh cheques of the same amount in lieu 
of the loss of two cheques. However, there were no records available with the Board to 
ascertain whether cheques of the said amount had been revalidated by the employer and 
had been deposited in the Board’s account.
The above cases resulted in loss of cess fund amounting to ₹ 8.01 lakh24.
2.3.11.4     Non-collection of advance cess from Private Constructions
The definition of establishment as per Section 2(a)(j) of the BOCW Act, 1996 specifies 
any establishment belonging to, or under the control of Government, corporate or firm, 
an individual or association or other body of individuals which or who employs building 
workers in any building or other construction work; and includes an establishment 
belonging to a contractor, but does not include an individual who employs such workers 
in any building or contraction work concerning his residence the total cost of such 
construction not being more than rupees ten lakh.  Also, Section 1 (4) of the BOCW Act 
states that the Act applies to every establishment which employs, or had employed on 
any day of the preceding twelve months, ten or more building workers in any buildings 
or other construction works.
Section 3(2) of the Cess Act, 1996 specifies a collection of one per cent cess from every 
employer in such manner and at such time, including deduction at source concerning a 
building or other construction work of a Government or of a public sector undertaking 
or advance collection through a local authority where approval of such building or other 
construction work by such local authority is required, as may be prescribed.
Further, as per Section 3(3) “the proceeds of the cess collected under sub-section (2) 
shall be paid by the local authority or the State Government collecting the cess to the 
Board after deducting the cost of collection of such cess not exceeding one per cent of 
the amount collected”.
The above Act stipulates the collection of advance cess by the building plan approval 
authority, which can be achieved by assessing the construction cost outlined  in 
the submitted building plans. The cost of construction of the building area could be 
determined using the plinth area rates set by the State Public Works Department (PWD). 
Subsequently, the planning authority should collect the applicable cess based on the 
calculated construction cost while approving these building plans.
During the period April 2021-December 2022, Gangtok Municipal Corporation (GMC) 
approved 419 building plans in respect of construction works executed by private parties. 
It was observed that the State had not notified the building plans approval authority 
for collection of advance cess from the estimate of construction of the building plan 
as envisaged in Section 3 (2) of the Cess Act. Consequently, in none of the private 
construction works, was applicable advance cess collected and received by the Board. 
Similarly, Urban Development Department (UDD) had approved 15 building plans (Non-
residential/ Commercial buildings) during 2019-22 without commensurate/ applicable 
cess being received by the Board. Also, the Board had also not ventured to register the 
eligible private establishments by conducting inspections as mentioned in preceding 
Paragraphs 2.3.10.1 and 2.3.10.2.
As per the provisions of the Act, the department could have empowered the Local 
Authorities (Municipal Corporation, Panchayats)/Urban Development authorities to 
24 ₹ 6.19 Lakh + ₹1.82 Lakh.

Chapter II: Social Sector
23
collect advance cess from cost of construction, while retaining one per cent  of cess 
collection fee, at the time of approval of building plans.
Thus, due to not designating GMC and UDD to collect cess for the Board and lack of 
monitoring by Board, potential cess collection from 434 Private establishments 25 was 
affected.
Objective 4: Whether Government prescribed, appropriate health and safety norms 
and could ensure an environment of compliance to those norms by the Employer?
2.3.12 Cases of Incident/ Accidents
2.3.12.1 Inspection of Building and other construction sites
According to Rule 286 of the SBOCW Rules 2010, an Inspector may, inter alia, examine 
construction site, place, or premises used for a building or other construction work. He 
may issue a show-cause notice or warning to employers regarding the safety, health 
or welfare of building workers provided under the Act or the Rules. Further, he may 
hold an enquiry into the cause of any accident or dangerous occurrence due to any 
operation connected with or incidental to such building or other construction work, or 
of non-compliance with any of provisions of the Act and Rules and give directions in 
this regard.
The Act also aimed at regulating the safety, health, welfare and other conditions of 
service of Building and Other Construction Workers through the Board in every State. 
Sections 32 to 34 of the Act and Rule 241 of the SBOCW Rules, 2010 made it mandatory 
for employers to ensure drinking water, latrines and urinals and accommodation for 
workers. Further, Rule 5 (9) of the SBOCW Rules, 2010 mandates maintenance of clean 
and hygienic condition of latrines and urinals by the employers.
It was observed that the Board (2017-22) had not conducted any inspections for ensuring 
health and safety measures despite incurring expenditure of ₹ 27.02 lakh for this purpose, 
as there were no records of conducted inspections i.e., inspection reports, inspection 
notes, etc. during the audit period.
Joint Physical Verification (JPV) of 16 construction sites (eight registered and eight 
unregistered establishments from two selected Districts) with the Board Officials showed 
instances of non-availability of basic amenities as detailed in Paragraph 2.3.16.
Objective 5: Whether administration and utilisation of funds on implementation of 
the welfare scheme by the Board were efficient and effective and as per Act and rules 
framed by the State Government?
2.3.13.1 Implementation of Welfare Schemes
Section 22 read with Section 24 (2) of the BOCW (RECS) Act 1996, stipulates that Cess 
collected was required to be spent for the welfare of construction workers on schemes, i.e. 
maternity benefits, pension, advances for purchase and construction of houses, disability 
pension, payments of financial assistance, medical assistance, financial assistance for 
education and marriage of children.
In compliance with the Act, the Government of Sikkim notified 22 welfare schemes  to 
be implemented by the Welfare Board. However, as per the direction of the Supreme

Court, (March 2018) the GoI framed a Model Welfare Scheme  which included life & 
disability care, Health and Maternity cover, Education to the wards of the BOC workers, 
Housing, Pension, Skill Development and awareness programmes. Accordingly, 
SBOCWB announced (June 2018) eight Financial Assistance (FA) programmes, which 
were revised from time to time. 
Scrutiny of records revealed that ₹ 22.84 crore26 was spent towards the implementation 
of welfare programs (Chart 2.2). Further, in terms of FA of the eight notified schemes, 
70 beneficiaries (2017-22) applied for FA. The Board had released ₹ 14.08 lakh against 
settlement of such claims (Chart 2.3).

The above table shows that there were significant delays in releasing FA for all schemes. 
The Board had not recorded the reasons in writing for delays and non-release of FA. The 
incomplete documentation indicates that the Board was not effective in implementing 
the FA schemes as envisaged in the BOCW Act and SBOCW Rules, which emphasise 
the provision of immediate assistance, particularly in cases of death and injury at the 
worksite.
Furthermore, it was observed that in seven cases, FA was not released to the beneficiaries 
without any valid explanation by the Board for the non-release. The nonrelease of FA 
deprived the eligible beneficiaries of the schemes benefits.
Rule 272 of the SBOCW (RECS) Rules 2010 stipulates that a registered beneficiary who 
has been working as a building worker for not less than 5 years after the registration as 
beneficiary under the rule shall on completion of 60 years of age be eligible for pension. 
The above rule further stipulates submission of application in Form XXXVII by the 
beneficiary to the Secretary of the Board or officer authorised by him for the purpose. 
Audit, however, observed that even after 13 years of framing the Rules (2010), there 
was not even a single case of disbursement of pension assistance till date (April 2023).
Scrutiny of records regarding the subscription paid by registered beneficiaries revealed 
that 41 workers were working in various establishments (West Sikkim) who had crossed 
60 years of age as detailed in Appendix 2.4. However, the Board did not identify such 
cases where a grant of a pension could have been rendered to an eligible beneficiary as 
envisaged in the Rule 272 of the SBOCWB Rules 2010
The fact that only 61 out of 5278 registered workers have availed the FA benefits during 
the period 2017-18 to 2021-22, refle cts ineffective implementation. At the same time, 
the corpus of the Board increased by 130.25 per cent28 from ₹ 65.70 crore (April 2017)

26
to ₹ 151.28 crore (March 2022) during the audit period (Table 2.3). This issue assumes 
significance as the schemes include financial assistance for medical purposes, education 
and hostel expenses for children, maternity benefits etc.
Further, considering the FA claim settlements during the period 2021-22, audit observed 
that only 21 claims were received and settled by the Board during the period.
2.3.13.2 Non-implementation of group insurance to BOC Workers
Section 22(d) of the BOCW (RECS) Act 1996 envisages the implementation of a Group 
Insurance scheme for registered workers. This had also been reiterated in the Model 
Welfare Scheme by GoI according to which the State Welfare Board should cover BOC 
workers between the ages 18 to 50 years  under Pradhan Mantri Jeevan Jyoti Bima 
Yojana (PMJJBY) and Pradhan Mantri Suraksha Bima Yojana (PMSBY).
The Board in its 38thmeeting (January 2020) discussed the implementation of the scheme, 
however, audit observed that no concrete steps had been taken to implement the scheme.
2.3.13.3  Lack of awareness among BOC Workers
The direction issued by the Supreme Court (18 January 2010),  inter alia,  stated that 
awareness should be built up, about the registration of building workers and about the 
benefits available under the Act. There should be an effective use of media, AIR and 
Doordarshan, for awareness programmes regarding the Act, the benefits available there 
and procedures for availing the benefits.
Further, under the directions (March 2018) of the Supreme Court, the MoL&E, GoI 
had prepared Model Scheme for BOC Workers and Action Plan (for Strengthening 
Implementation Machinery). This included awareness generation among BOC workers.
Audit revealed that the Board incurred an expenditure of ₹ 35.04 lakh 29  (2017-22) for 
organising various awareness programmes. However, records of conducted programmes 
were not produced to audit.
This is, further, substantiated by the fact that an extremely smaller number of beneficiaries 
(61 claims) had availed the scheme benefits (2017-22). It was also noticed during the 
beneficiary survey that 85 out of 158 selected workers were unaware of the Board and 
its projected welfare schemes, which was reflective of the alarmingly low figure of 
registered BOC workers i.e., 5,278 in the State (October 2022).
2.3.13.4 Financial Assistance during COVID-19
Ministry of Labour and Employment (MoL&E), GOI instructed30  all the States to aid and 
support workers against economic disruption during the COVID-19 pandemic. Further, 
the Minister of State (I/C), MoL&E requested 31 all the Chief Ministers of States for 
providing adequate relief immediately to construction workers by transferring adequate 
funds in the bank accounts of the construction workers, through Direct Benefit Transfer 
(DBT mode) from BOCW fund.
28 ₹ 151.28 crore (March 2022) - ₹ 65.70 (April 2017) /₹ 65.70 *100.

31 DO letter No. dated 24 March 2020.

Chapter II: Social Sector
27
Initially, the Board decided to provide financial aid at the rate of ₹ 2,000/- each to 7,836 
registered construction workers32 and incurred an expenditure of ₹ 1.57 crore. However, 
based on the compilation of the latest position of registered workers collected from the 
employers/establishments, the Board provided FA to 7,500  beneficiaries by depositing 
the FA amount to the employer’s account. Accordingly, the Board released ₹ 1.50 crore33 
 to the 44 employers for onward transfer of the FA to their 7,500 workers (March and 
April 2020).
Scrutiny of the Board’s records (January 2021) of Covid FA revealed the following:
	During the time of sanctioning of funds, there was a change in the number of 
registered workers from 7,836 to 7,500 within the same month (March 2020). This 
proves that the Board did not maintain the details of registered workers (Name, 
ID Number, Adhar number, bank details, etc.) and largely relied on the figures 
of registered workers taken from employers and field offices during the time of 
FA disbursal. Since the Board did not have credible records of registered workers 
during that period, audit was unable to verify the actual receipt of the extended FA 
disbursed to the beneficiaries. Further, the possibility of eligible workers being left 
out from the assistance scheme during the pandemic cannot be ruled out in audit.
	The Board had not accounted for the monthly subscription paid by the registered 
workers to ascertain whether the beneficiaries are eligible/ ineligible for receiving 
the benefits. There were no such records during the time of disbursal, which could 
substantiate that the 7,500 beneficiaries to whom FA was to be provided were 
registered with the Board at that time. This was further corroborated by the fact 
that the transfer of ₹ 1.50 crore was made to the employers’ (44 employers) bank 
accounts instead of transferring directly to beneficiaries’ accounts (DBT), which was 
in contravention to MoL&Es’ direction. Further, out of the 44 employers to whom 
the FA was disbursed, four employers34 returned the unutilised fund of ₹ 11.60 lakh 
owing to non-availability of workers. Thus, as per the information provided by 
these employers, out of the FA release of ₹ 1.50 crore to 7,500 beneficiaries, only 
6,911 beneficiaries (₹ 1.38 crore) received the benefit of FA.
	Analysis of UCs (Appendix 2.5 & 2.6) revealed that out of the 6,911 beneficiaries, 
in 1,129 cases the FA was extended through hand receipts while in 5,782 cases the 
payment was made to the beneficiaries by bank transfer through an employer. As 
such, to confirm the actual receipt of FA money in the beneficiary accounts, the 
bank should in turn furnish Unique Transfer Receipts (UTRs) for every transaction. 
However, the Board did not obtain bank UTRs from the employers for confirming 
the actual receipt by the beneficiaries. In the absence of UTRs, audit could not 
verify if the FA amounts reached to the intended beneficiaries. Further, the transfer 
of money through hand receipts was in contravention of the GoI direction.
Thus, due to non-transfer of the funds directly to the beneficiaries account through DBT, 
lack of confirmation as to whether financial assistance was received by the beneficiaries, 
32 The Board replied that the decrease in number of workers to 5278 (October 2022) was mainly due 
to the occurrence of seasonal works and migratory workers in Sikkim. Further, the closure of Power 
Projects like Teesta Urja Limited and Gati etc. had contributed tremendous decrease in the construction 
workers in Sikkim.

the disbursement of financial assistance amounting to ₹ 1.38 crore remained irregular 
and doubtful. Further, due to the lack of any contact details of beneficiaries, audit was 
unable to provide assurance on the receipt of one-time Covid-19 FA by the beneficiaries.
2.3.13.5 Excess procurement of welfare items- ₹ 1.72 crore
Department of Labour, Government of Sikkim vide notification no. 06/DL dated 
12 March 2015 framed 22 schemes which included schemes for distribution of Welfare 
items i.e., Blankets, boots, shoes, tools, etc. for the BOC workers. The State framed the 
above scheme in the exercise of the power conferred under clause (h) of section 22 (1) 
of the BOCW Act, 1996. Accordingly, the Board during the period 2017-18 to 2018-19 
decided to implement distribution of welfare items on numerous occasions and the same 
was approved during the Board meetings.
Audit observed that during 2017-18 to 2019-20, on four occasions, the Board incurred 
an expenditure of ₹ 8.91 crore for the purchase of welfare  items35 for distribution to 
potential beneficiaries. It was, however, noticed that the Board had procured welfare 
items by estimating a higher number 36 of registered workers even though the active 
beneficiaries during that period were comparatively less in number (Appendix 2.7).  
Audit found that an excess of 5,121 welfare items was procured which had resulted in an 
unwarranted expenditure of ₹ 1.72 crore from the Welfare fund. Records indicating the 
availability/utilisation of these items were not made available to Audit. It was observed 
that the Board had not maintained the list of registered beneficiaries and had relied on 
the provisional figures of registered workers taken from the establishments at the time 
of scheme implementation. Further, the distribution of the excess items to ineligible 
persons cannot be ruled out.
Thus, the inability of the Board to assess the actual number of beneficiaries before 
procurement of welfare items and the lack of records indicating the existence of the 
excess purchased items resulted in a wasteful expenditure of ₹ 1.72 crore from the 
welfare fund.

₹ 1.17 crore and ₹ 0.44 crore respectively. As per GOIs’ direction, such construction 
was required to be at a central location, the building must be maintained properly and all 
amenities to be provided for the users.
The objective behind the construction of TCNH was to provide temporary accommodation 
for the BOC workers while moving to their respective destinations. On the other hand, 
the objective of PUC was to create a welfare centre (registration, health care services, 
insurance, etc.) for the BOC workers and their families. Further, the PUC was also 
meant to serve as a skill development centre for the residents of the locality.
Audit observed that the buildings were located 
in a high-altitude isolated area (6,000 ft.) which 
is approximately 11 km away from the main 
town. Since the location is situated on the way to 
Indo-China international Border at Nathula pass, 
special permits/passes are required for reaching 
the TCNH/PUC. Apparently, it would be very 
difficult for the general public, other than defense 
personnel, to travel to and from such a location. 
Despite GoI’s direction on condition of constructing the said building in a centralised 
location, the Board decided to construct the buildings in an isolated location without any 
basis or assessing the suitability of the selected location.
During Joint Physical Verification (October 2022) with Board officials, it was observed 
that no BOC worker had used the facilities since the completion of the buildings 
(February 2022). Verification of the transit camp entry register confirmed that no workers 
or visitors had visited the TCNH till the date of audit (October 2022).
It was also observed that the TCNH lacked basic amenities of providing water supply 
and kitchen facilities (utensils, gas, oven, etc.) within the building area despite the fact 
there were no shops or eateries anywhere near the location.
The flooring of the building was found to be deteriorating, possibly due to lack of 
maintenance.

Thus, due to improper planning and an imprudent decision of the Board, the objective 
of constructing both buildings remained defeated and resulted in unfruitful expenditure 
of ₹ 1.61 crore from the BOC welfare fund.
2.3.14.2 Irregular/doubtful utilsation of Welfare fund of ₹ 3.15 crore
Sections 22 and 24 (2) of the Act, 1996, mandated that at least 95 per cent of the fund 
should be utilised for the benefit of the construction workers while five per cent is to 
be used for administrative expenses. Under the direction of the Supreme Court (SC) of 
Image 2.4: Transit Camp Entry Register

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023

India (2015), the GoI reiterated (June 2016) that welfare funds should not be used for 
any other purpose other than for the welfare of construction workers and exclusively 
for their families. In addition to the above, as per the direction of SC (March 2018), the 
funds available with the Welfare Boards shall not be utilised by the State for any other 
expenditure of the State Government.
Audit observed that in contravention to the provision cited above, the Board incurred an 
expenditure of ₹ 3.15 crore (Table 2.6) on inadmissible/irregular expenses which were 
yet to be recouped to the welfare fund (March 2023).

2.3.15 Monitoring and Internal Control
There was no effective internal audit system in place on the Board. The absence of 
internal control in the Board resulted in the non-presentation of Demand drafts amounting 
to ₹6.19 lakh (Paragraph 2.3.11.3), non-preparation of the Annual budget (Paragraph 
2.3.8.2) and excess procurement of welfare items at ₹ 1.72 crore (Paragraph 2.3.13.5). 
The absence of monitoring and Internal Control in the Board is discussed in the following 
paragraphs.
2.3.15.1 State Advisory Committee
Section 4 of the BOCW Act stipulates that the State Government shall constitute a 
committee to be called the State Building and Other Construction Workers Advisory 
Committee (SAC) to advise the State Government on such matters arising out of the 
administration of this Act as may be referred to it. The SAC was to be reconstituted 
every three years and to meet at least once a year.
The SAC was constituted in February 2010  in the State and reconstituted in August 
2014, January 2018 and July 2019 respectively with the then Minister in charge of the 
LD as the Chairperson.
Audit observed that against 13 meetings, only five were held till date (April 2023) since 
its constitution in the year 2010. Further, during the period 2017-22, only two meetings 
were held against the stipulated number  of five meetings. In the absence of regular 
meetings, the Board could not obtain important Committee decisions which were 
necessary to advise the State Government for better administration of the BOCW Act.
2.3.15.2 Shortfall in Conducting Board Meetings
Rule 251 of the SBOCW Rules, stipulates that SBCOWWB shall ordinarily meet once 
in two months. Scrutiny revealed a substantial shortfall in number of the Board Meetings 

Chapter II: Social Sector
33
conducted during 2017-22 as depicted in Table 2.7.
Table 2.7: Number of meetings held by the Board during 2017-22
Year Normative number of meetings Number of meetings held

2019-20 6 4
2020-21 6 3
2021-22 6 0
Total 30 15
Source: Information furnished by the Board
From the above table, it could be seen that only 15 meetings were held against the 
required 30 meetings (50 per cent) and no meeting was held in 2021-22. The holding of 
the Board Meeting was vital for taking important decisions for the welfare of the BOC 
workers. Non-conducting/shortfall in the number of meetings was indicative of less 
seriousness on the part of the Board in monitoring and implementing various provisions 
of the Acts & Rules.
2.3.15.3  Non-conduct of Social Audit
Specific direction was given (March 2018) by the Hon’ble Supreme Court in response 
to a writ petition42 to conduct a Social Audit on the implementation of the Act so that in 
future there is better and more effective and meaningful implementation of the Act. The 
matter had been discussed in the Monitoring Committee meeting held (May 2018) in 
Delhi and included in the Model Welfare Scheme circulated by GOI for compliance by 
State/UT Governments.
The objective of the Social Audit was to visit ongoing construction sites, inform the 
workers about their entitlements to the Act, identify workers eligible but not registered 
and facilitate workers for filing grievances, etc.
Scrutiny, however, revealed that no Social Audit had been conducted by the Board till 
the date (May 2023).
The non-conduct of Social Audit indicated ineffectiveness in functioning of the Board 
towards better monitoring.
2.3.16	 Beneficiary	and	joint	physical	verification
Two districts, viz., East and South districts,  were sampled and selected for audit. A 
total of 200 BOC workers (100 workers from each district) and 16 Establishments were 
selected for the survey. The 16 establishments included eight registered establishments 
(four establishments from each district) and eight unregistered establishments with equal 
numbers from each district. Out of the 200 beneficiary workers, all 59 workers of the two 
districts who had availed the Financial Assistance scheme from the Board (57 from East 
District and two from South District) were selected for the survey. The Joint beneficiary 
survey was conducted by the audit team with a structured questionnaire designed to 
capture the perception of the beneficiaries about the BOCW Board, the schemes available 
from it and their grievances. The summary of the findings are as follows:

his family had not received FA (April 2023) despite the fact that his application 
was already forwarded to the Board by the District Field Officer as early as January 
2020.

2.3.18 Conclusion
The Sikkim Building and Other Construction Welfare Board was established to 
ensure the safety and welfare of construction workers. Audit observed deficiencies in 
the implementation of the BOCW Act and related rules in the state of Sikkim which 
included non identification of establishments for registration (Paragraph 2.3.10.2) , 
inability to assess and collect the cess (Paragraph 2.3.11.1 and 2.3.11.2), non-conduct 
of inspection at sites (Paragraph 2.3.12.1)  and improper implementation of welfare 
schemes (Paragraph 2.3.13.1). 
The Board had neither initiated a proper system of registering establishments/workers 
nor established a sound assessment mechanism for collecting cess. There was no 
mechanism for online registration of the establishments and workers as of date. As a 
result, the Board was unaware about the exact number of construction workers in the 
state as evidenced by the differing figures produced by the Board on different occasions. 
This also indicated that the Board was not aware of the exact quantum of cess due for 
collection and that the estimation of beneficiaries for various welfare schemes was not 
realistic. 
The Board did not have independent staff of its own, and its day-to-day activities were 
managed by the staff of the labour department itself, in addition to their normal work 
and duties. Audit observed that this had resulted in the improper implementation of the 
BOCW act. Critical activities such as inspection of establishments, ensuring submission 
of mandatory returns by employers and maintenance of records were not done properly 
by the Board. Further, Audit also noticed instances where cess funds were diverted to 
activities not related to BOCW.
Audit noticed that the expenditure on various welfare activities is very low as compared 
with the total accumulated cess funds available with the Board. This translated into 
very few beneficiaries receiving financial assistance from the Board. Further, there were 
instances of beneficiaries being provided financial assistance after a considerable delay. 
Audit also noticed that employers had failed to promptly report incidents/accidents 
which happened at the work sites and necessary action was not taken action by the 
Board in this regard. Apart from these, Audit further noticed that awareness regarding 
welfare schemes under the Act was low amongst the workers. 
Audit concludes that the SBOCW Board has remained ineffective in executing the 
provisions of the BOCW act for the welfare of construction workers of the State of 
Sikkim. 
2.3.19 Recommendations
For better and more effective implementation of the act, it is recommended that –
	The Board shall ensure the preparation and submission of the Annual Budget for 
approval of the Government.
	The Government should ensure that the Cess Fund is used exclusively for the welfare 
measures of the BOC workers only. The use of funds for administrative purposes 
should be within the permissible limit of five per cent of total expenditure.

Chapter II: Social Sector
37
	Online registration mechanism for BOC workers with complete details (name, 
contact number, nominees, bank details, identification documents, subscription fee, 
etc.) is to be initiated by the Board at the earliest. Further, the Board should ensure 
that all construction works and workers involved are registered under the Board. 
The Board should maintain a comprehensive database of such registered workers.
	The Board should institutionalise a mechanism to identify all employers and 
workers and ensure proper and timely collection of cess.
	The Board may ensure that Assessment Officers and Cess collectors are made 
accountable for ensuring that the cess is duly assessed, realised and remitted to the 
Board.
	The Government may authorise the advance collection of cess from Private 
Buildings and construction works, through the building plan-approving authorities 
i.e., urban/ local bodies.
	The Government should ensure investing the Cess fund in nationalised banks only.
	Transit accommodation/hostel/ labour shed/ night shelter for the BOC workers 
should be constructed at centralised locations to ensure better utilisation.
	The Government may ensure mandatory provision of financial assistance through 
Direct Benefit Transfer mode only.
HEALTH AND FAMILY WELFARE DEPARTMENT
2.4	 Subject	 Specific	Compliance	Audit	on	Public	Health	Infrastructure	and	
Management of Health Services
2.4.1  Introduction
The public healthcare delivery system in India is organised at three levels – primary, 
secondary, and tertiary. The vast network of Primary Health Sub-Centres (SCs), Primary 
Health Centres (PHCs), Urban Primary Health Centres (UPHCs) and Community Health 
Centres (CHCs) form the primary tier of public healthcare delivery system for rural and 
urban population. District Hospitals (DHs) provide secondary-level healthcare services 
for rural and urban population. These hospitals handle the treatment and management of 
diseases or medical conditions that require specialised care. Tertiary healthcare involves 
providing advanced and super-specialty services to be provided by medical institutions 
in urban areas, which are well-equipped with sophisticated diagnostic and investigative 
facilities. 
2.4.2 Overview of Public Healthcare facilities in Sikkim
Sikkim is the least populated and second smallest State in India with a population of 
6.83 lakh (2021-22). To provide universal healthcare services to the people, the State 
has a 1000 bedded Super Specialty Hospital at Gangtok, four District Hospitals, two 
Community Health Centres (CHCs), two Ayush Hospitals, one TB Hospital, one Covid 
Hospital, 24 Primary Health Centres (PHCs), two Urban Primary Health Centres 
(UPHCs) and 148 Public Health Sub-Centres (PHSCs). 
Analysis of the existence of health centres in the State at the primary and secondary 
levels vis-à-vis the norm laid down by the Indian Public Health Standards (IPHS) for a 
hilly/mountainous State revealed the availability of an adequate number of DHs4546
47 in the 
45 
46 
47 The IPHS lays down that for a district with a population of 10 lakh, the requirement of a DH would 
be of 300 beds. Hence, a DH with 100 beds would cater to a population of 3,33,333. Each district in 
Sikkim had population below the IPHS norm.

State. There was, however, a shortage of CHCs (6), PHCs (11) and PHSCs (83) in three 
districts48 of the State. Details are shown in Appendix 2.8.
2.4.3 Service availability in the DH, CHCs and PHCs in the State
The IPHS categorises service availability in the DHs, CHCs and PHCs as essential 
services (minimum assured services) and desirable services (services that a DH should 
aspire to achieve). 
Out of four DHs in the State, Namchi DH had all 12 essential services; Singtam and 
Geyzing DHs had 11 services and Mangan DH had eight services available. The detailed 
status of availability of essential services in four DHs of the State is highlighted in 
Appendix 2.9.
Two DHs (Namchi and Geyzing DH) out of four had functional ICUs for general patients 
while the other two DHs, Mangan and Singtam, did not have general ICU 49 facilities. 
Although Singtam DH had two ICU facilities, one for paediatric care and other for 
trauma care for road accident victims, both were non-functional due to lack of required 
paraphernalia and manpower to run the units. While Namchi DH and Mangan DH had 
dedicated ICUs for road trauma victims, Geyzing DH did not have a trauma care ICU. 
None of the four DHs had desirable services like forensic and endoscopy services, 
Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI), Electroencephalogram (EEG), Angiography and 
Echocardiography.  
The IPHS norms have categorised specialist services in general medicine, surgery, 
obstetrics and gynaecology, paediatrics, dental service and anaesthesia as essential 
services for Community Health Centres (CHC). The two CHCs in the State ( Rhenock 
CHC and Jorethang CHC) did not have the above specialty services and hence functioned 
as mere PHCs with additional beds.
2.4.4 Availability of Doctors, Nurses and Paramedics
The IPHS has prescribed manpower requirements for the DHs, CHCs and PHCs based 
on the service delivery norms of each health facility. The State Government had neither 
formulated manpower requirement norms for its hospitals and health centres nor notified 
sanctioned strength of human resources required for its health institutions. In the 
absence of notified sanctioned strength of manpower, audit examined the deployment of 
manpower in the health institutions with reference to the IPHS norms.
	Manpower in District Hospitals 
Audit noticed that the deployment of specialists, doctors, nurses and paramedics in the 
DHs was not as per IPHS norms. Detailed status of manpower in the DHs is given in 
Appendix 2.10.
●	 Mangan DH did not have any specialist posted in seven essential Departments 
and in addition there was a shortage of 11 medical officers, 21 nurses and five 
paramedics in the DH.  
●	 Singtam DH had a shortage of two General Surgery specialists, one Anaesthetist 
and one Radiologist.  
●	 Geyzing DH had a shortage of one Medicine Specialist, two General Surgeons, and 
six medical officers. 
48 East, West and South Districts. There was no shortage of health facilities in the North District. 
49 The ICUs in the DHs of the State are categorised as Paediatric ICU, Trauma ICU, Covid-19 ICU and 
general ICU for other ailments. 

Chapter II: Social Sector
39
Thus, several Departments of the DHs suffered a shortage of manpower vis-à-vis IPHS 
norms. 
 Human Resources in CHCs 
In terms of the IPHS, a CHC is required to have five essential services in the disciplines of 
general medicine, paediatrics, general surgery, obstetrics & gynaecology and anaesthesia. 
The two CHCs (Rhenock & Jorethang) in the State did not have any specialists posted 
in them. 
The CHCs also did not have any manpower in the essential positions of Medical 
Superintendent, Public Health Specialists and Public Health Nurse while there was 
excess manpower in dental Department (dentists) to the extent of 100 per cent (Rhenock, 
CHC), and 500 per cent (Jorethang, CHC) as compared to overall excess of 1283 per 
cent dentists in the State50 and excess staff nurses to the extent of 30 per cent (Rhenock, 
CHC) and 120 per cent (Jorethang, CHC) as compared to overall excess of 90 per cent 
staff nurses in the State and short deployment of eight paramedics in Jorethang CHC as 
compared to overall excess of 232 paramedics in the State.  Thus, while several essential 
positions in the CHC had no manpower posted in them, the deployment of dentists, 
nurses and paramedics in the CHCs was done without adhering to the norms. Details are 
given in Appendix 2.11. 
In the absence of specialists, the sampled CHC ( Rhenock) was compelled to refer 
668 patients during 2017-22 seeking specialist consultation and care to the District 
Hospital, Singtam or to the STNM Hospital Gangtok. This deprived sick people under 
the CHC of essential healthcare services expected from a CHC closer to their homes. 
	Human Resources in PHCs 
Detailed status of manpower position of 26 UPHC / PHCs in the State is shown in 
Appendix 2.12(a) & (b). 
Audit observed that 85 doctors, 188 nurses, 219 paramedics and a staggering 446 ‘other 
staff’51 were more than IPHS norms in the State’s PHCs during 2021-22. Further, the 
deployment of doctors, nurses, paramedics and ‘other staff’ was also not uniform across 
the PHCs but was extremely random ranging from one doctor in a PHC to 11 doctors 
in another PHC, four nurses in a PHC to 23 nurses in another PHC, one paramedic in 
a PHC to 21 paramedics in another and four ‘other staff’ in a PHC to 39 such staff in 
another PHC. Thus, at the PHC level, the deployment of manpower was  haphazard and 
did not adhere to the IPHS norms. 
2.4.5 Outpatient Department (OPD) Services in the District Hospitals
According to the IPHS, the District Hospitals are supposed to provide OPD services 
in General Medicines, General Surgery, Obstetrics & Gynaecology, Paediatrics, 
Ophthalmology, ENT, Orthopaedics, Psychiatry, Dental Care, etc. 
Audit noticed that the four District Hospitals in the State were providing OPD Services 
in 11 Departments according to availability of Specialist Doctors. There was maximum 
OPD footfall in the general medicine department followed by paediatrics, gynaecology 
& obstetrics, ophthalmology and dental departments as detailed in Appendix 2.13. 
Since there was no specialist service in the essential department of Surgery in Geyzing, 
Singtam and Mangan DHs, there was no footfall in the OPDs of this department in the 
50 4 DHs, 2 CHCs and 26 PHCs.
51 other staff includes safai karmachari, guard, driver, dhobi etc.

three DHs. However, in the department of Dermatology & Venereology, which was a 
desirable service, there was considerable footfall in the OPDs of two DHs (Namchi DH, 
Singtam DH) that had this service.
2.4.6 Availability of Beds in the District Hospitals
IPHS categories District Hospitals into five grades52 from Grade-I to Grade -V according 
to bed strength of each hospital. The four DHs of Sikkim were categorised as Grade-I 
with bed capacity of 100 beds each. Hospital beds were distributed among different wards 
viz., Medicine ward, Surgical Ward, Orthopaedic Ward, Paediatric Ward, Obstetrics and 
Gynaecology, Ophthalmology ward, etc. Separate wards for Maternal care and Child-
care were available in each District Hospital. The distribution of beds in the wards of the 
DHs was done depending upon the demand for each service from time to time. 
2.4.7 IPD Services in the District Hospitals
District Hospitals provide IPD Services in Medicine, Surgery, Maternity, Paediatrics, 
Nursery, Isolation related cases, Burn cases, Orthopaedics, Ophthalmology, Malaria and 
Infectious Disease-related cases.
The four District Hospitals of Sikkim provided IPD Services based on availability of 
Specialist Doctors with each Hospital. Maximum in-patients in the DHs pertained to 
general medicine, gynaecology, and paediatrics. In dermatology department in Singtam 
and Namchi DHs, which had this desirable service, there were no in-patients. Only Namchi 
DH had in-patients in Surgery Department as this DH had specialist service in Surgery. 
Mangan DH had in-patients in all four departments available in the DH (medicine, 
obstetrics & gynaecology, paediatrics and psychiatry). Details are given in Appendix 2.14.
2.4.8 Availability of Diagnostic Service in the District Hospitals
According to the IPHS, the District Hospital laboratory and other diagnostic services 
shall serve the purpose of public health and be able to perform all tests required to 
diagnose epidemics or important diseases.  The Laboratory services which are to be 
available in a District Hospital are i) Clinical Pathology ii) Pathology iii) Microbiology 
iv) Serology v) Biochemistry vi) Cardiac investigation vii) Ophthalmology viii) ENT ix) 
Radiology x) Endoscopy and xi) Respiratory function tests. 
Audit noticed that all four DHs in the State had facilities for i) Clinical Pathology ii) 
Pathology iii) Microbiology iv) Serology v) Biochemistry tests. However, tests like 
Stress Test, ECG under Cardiac Investigation, Endoscopy for ENT and Audiometry test, 
all kinds of Endoscopy and Pulmonary function tests under Respiratory investigation 
were not available in the DHs in the State. 
In respect of Radiology investigations, CT scan and MRI Services were not available in 
the DHs. 
2.4.9 Availability of Blood Bank and Blood Storage Facility
IPHS norms prescribe availability of blood bank in a DH and blood storage facility in 
every CHC as essential facility. Three DHs (Singtam, Geyzing and Namchi) in the State 
had functional Blood Banks while the fou rth DH ( Mangan) did not have a functional 
Blood Bank. The two CHCs in the State ( Rhenock and Jorethang) did not have any 
52 Grade -I DH with 100 beds, Grade-II with 200 beds, Grade-III with 300 beds, Grade-IV with 400 beds 
and Grade-V with 500 beds. 

Chapter II: Social Sector
41
Blood storage facility to cater to patients requiring blood transfusion. All the four DHs 
in the State had valid licenses for operating the Blood Banks issued by the Central 
License Approving Authority, Drugs and Cosmetics Cell, Health and Family Welfare 
Department, Government of Sikkim.
2.4.10 Dietary Service in the District Hospitals in the State
According to the IPHS, dietary service of a hospital is an important therapeutic tool. 
Apart from normal diets, diabetic, semi-solid diets and liquid diets shall also be available. 
Food shall be distributed in covered containers.  
The four DHs of the State provided seven types of diets free of cost for different 
category of patients viz., normal non-vegetarian diet, normal vegetarian diet, diabetic 
non-vegetarian diet, diabetic vegetarian diet, paediatric non-vegetarian diet, paediatric 
vegetarian diet and churned diet / full fluid diet for severe acute malnutrition for adult 
and children. Food was carried for distribution in covered containers. 
2.4.11 Hospital Linen Service in the District Hospitals in the State 
IPHS prescribes the number of different types of linen53 that are required for patient care 
services for DHs with different bed capacities in the category of 101 to 200, 201 to 300, 
and 301 to 500. The four DHs in Sikkim were categorised as 100 bedded hence the IPHS 
norm could not be strictly applied. 
Audit noticed that no norms had been laid down by the Department for the DHs for 
providing various types of linen, periodicity of changing linen for the patients and 
methodology for washing and cleaning of linen. The DHs adopted their own method as 
suitable to them for cleaning and washing of linen. While the Geyzing DH outsourced 
laundry services, the other three DHs ( Singtam, Namchi, and Mangan ) had regular 
appointed dhobis for washing hospital linen. Records relating to changing of soiled 
linens and issue of fresh linens were, however, not maintained by any of the DHs in 
the State. Joint Physical verification of the sampled DHs ( Singtam and Geyzing DH) 
revealed that the patients preferred to use their own linen rather than those provided by 
the Hospitals. Images below corroborate the observation. 
Image 2.14 & 2.15: Patients preferred using their own bedspreads, blankets, cloths etc. in the DHs

2.4.12 Ambulance Service 
In terms of the IPHS (Clause XVI Hospital Transport Services) norms, a District Hospital 
shall have well equipped Basic Life Support (BLS) and desirably one Advanced Life 
Support (ALS) ambulance. 
The NHM guidelines prescribe the availability of tele-ambulance services which can be 
availed by calling telephone numbers 108 and 102 for attending to patients of critical 
care, trauma (accident) victims etc., (108) and basic patient transport to cater to the 
needs of pregnant women, children etc. (102).  
Audit noticed that there were 13 ambulances in four DHs of the State (Singtam DH- four; 
Namchi DH- four; Geyzing DH – three & Mangan DH - two) . The DHs provided free 
ambulance service as referral transport to higher health facilities. The ambulances were 
provided with basic life support facility viz., Oxygen Cylinder, First Aid Box, trained 
paramedics, etc. as required under the IPHS norms. However, only one ambulance under 
Geyzing DH in West district was connected with 102 national ambulance service catering 
to pregnant women, sick infants and sterilisation cases while ‘108 Emergency Transport 
System’ service for all other medical emergencies was not available in the State.  
2.4.13 Good practices 
The following good practices were noticed in the District Hospitals of the State: 
➢ Management of Bio Medical Waste 
Biomedical waste management (BMW) is an integral part of infection control activities 
of the hospital. According to the Bio-Medical Waste Management Rules, 2016 (BMW 
Rules) hazardous, toxic and bio-medical waste has to be separated into 10 categories 
for the purpose of safe transportation to specific sites for specific treatment. Further, the 
BMW Rules inter alia stipulate the procedures for collection, handling, transportation, 
disposal and monitoring of the bio-medical Waste with clear roles for waste generators.
Audit noticed that all four DHs were segregating hospital wastes in separate-coloured 
bins, available at the point of generation of waste, particularly in the ward areas, OTs, 
etc. as per the BMW rules. Incinerators were available in all four DHs for burning of 
the BMW and deep burial pits (sharp pits) were available for burial of sharp objects 
calling for such burial. General solid wastes were collected by the concerned municipal 
authorities for disposal while recyclable items were handed over to agencies collecting 
such items. 
➢ Mortuary Service in the District Hospitals
IPHS provides that every District Hospital should have the facilities for keeping of dead 
bodies and conducting autopsy.
Audit noticed that all the DHs in the State had a mortuary service for keeping the dead 
bodies and conducting of autopsy as per norms. 
2.4.14 Status of Implementation of AB-PMJAY in Sikkim 
Ayushman Bharat- Pradhan Mantri Jan Aarogya Yojana ( AB-PMJAY) is a flagship 
health scheme of the GoI, launched (23 September 2018) to achieve Universal Health 
Coverage (UHC) as recommended in the National Health Policy, 2017. PM -JAY aims 
to provide health insurance cover of ₹ five lakh per family per year for secondary and 

Chapter II: Social Sector
43
tertiary care hospitalisation.  Under PMJAY , the States are free to choose the modalities 
of implementation of the Scheme.  State can implement the scheme through insurance 
company or directly through the Trust / Society or mixed model. Sikkim chose to 
implement the PMJAY Scheme in trust mode where a Government of India (GoI) 
empanelled Third Party Agency (TPA) was appointed to support implementation of the 
Scheme. 
The scheme was rolled out in the State on the same day it was launched nationally 
(23 September 2018).  State Empanelment Committee (SEC) was formed on 06 February 
2023 with the objective of providing requisite support and physical verification of the 
health facilities for their empanelment under the AB-PMJAY . District Implementation 
Units (DIUs) were simultaneously set up in four districts (East District, West District, 
North District and South District) with the objective of supporting implementation of 
the Scheme in each district included under the Scheme. Further, 801 Contact points were 
established at district, sub-division and block levels for registration of beneficiaries.  
All the empanelled hospitals also had beneficiary registration facilities. The State had 
empanelled 17 hospitals (16 public hospitals and one private hospital) as of March 2022.
On being pointed out about delay in establishment of SEC, the Department stated that 
the delay was due to existence of a standing Hospital Empanelment Committee since 
August 2018 which performed the functions of the SEC.
As per Socio Economic Caste Census (SECC) 2011, there were 1.39 lakh members 
of 39,791 distinct households in the State eligible for availing benefits under the AB-
PMJAY scheme. However, as of 31 March 2022, only 0.45 lakh (32 per cent) members 
of 14,966 (38 per cent) households had been registered. A total of 5198 beneficiaries 
had been admitted under the scheme in various hospitals of the State during 2018-22 
against whom claims amounting to ₹ 3.01 crore had been paid by Government to various 
hospitals during the period.
2.4.15 Operationalisation of Health and Wellness Centres (HWCs) 
Government of India announced (February 2018) creation of 1.50 lakh Health and Wellness 
Centres (HWCs) by transforming existing Sub Centres and Primary Health Centres 
as the base pillar of Ayushman Bharat. These centres would deliver Comprehensive 
Primary Health Care (CPHC) bringing healthcare closer to homes of people covering 
both maternal and child health services and non-communicable diseases, including free 
essential drugs and diagnostic services. 
In Sikkim, all 26 Primary Health Centres (PHCs/UPHCs) and 144 Public Health Sub 
Centres (PHSCs) have been transformed (March 2023) into Health and Wellness Centres 
(HWCs) as envisaged by the Government of India. A total of 5.17 lakh patients in the 
State had been served by the HWCs as of March 2023. 
2.4.16 Medical Colleges in the State 
There was no Government Medical college in the State as of March 2023.

and supervising implementation of the Health and Family Welfare Programmes. The

2.4.18 Audit objectives 
The Subject Specific Compliance Audit was conducted to ascertain whether: 
●	 Funds provided for Healthcare were adequate and utilised efficiently,  
●	 Adequate Healthcare infrastructure and services were available and properly 
managed, 
●	 Support services in the health facilities were available and adequate,  
●	 Assets created for medical emergencies like Covid-19 pandemic were utilised 
efficiently, and 
●	 Health and wellbeing of people improved as per the SDG 3. 
2.4.19 Audit criteria
The criteria for the Compliance audit have been derived from: 
●	 National Health Policy, 2017, 
●	 Indian Public Health Standards, 2012, 
●	 Assessors’ Guidebook for Quality Assurance for PHCs, CHCs and DHs, 
●	 State specific targets and National Indicators under the SDG, 
●	 Government of India/Government of Sikkim policies, norms, orders, circulars, and 
annual/administration reports, etc.

2.4.20 Scope and methodology 
This Audit covers the period 2017-22. Relevant records in the Health and Family 
Welfare Department at the State level, two District Hospitals (East and West Districts), 
two PHCs & one CHC in East District and 3 PHCs in West District were examined 54. 
Information was also obtained from the Home Department and State Disaster Relief 
Fund (Land Revenue & Disaster Management Department) relating to funds provided 
for handling Covid-19 pandemic. Audit was conducted during 2022-23 including joint 
physical verification of sampled units. 
Audit methodology included scrutiny of files/records, gathering of evidence by issue 
of audit enquiries and conducting joint physical verification (JPV) with departmental 
officials and obtaining photographic evidence in the sampled units. 
2.4.21 Sampling methodology 
A Performance Audit of Select District Hospitals (Geyzing DH, Singtam DH and STNM 
Hospital Gangtok) in Sikkim for the period from 2014-15 to 2018-19 was conducted 
during 2019-20 and the Report placed in the State Legislature on 26 March 2021.  
For this audit, conducted during 2022-23, the same two District Hospitals (Geyzing DH 
and Singtam DH), one CHC and two PHCs in East District and three PHCs in West 
District were selected using Sample random sampling without replacement (SRSWOR). 
There is no CHC in West District. 
2.4.22	 Audit	findings
Audit objective 1: Whether funds provided for healthcare were adequate and 
managed efficiently?
2.4.22.1 Health Sector Funding vis-à-vis National Health Policy
The State Government had not formulated any policy or strategy in line with the 
National Health Policy 2017 (NHP 17) till March 2023 to determine the size of budget 
to be allocated to the Health Sector, either as percentage of the overall State budget or 
the GSDP. The National Health Policy 2017 (NHP 17) envisaged raising Public Health 
Expenditure to more than eight per cent of the budget by 2020 and 2.5 per cent of GSDP 
by 2025.  
Audit studied the status of funding by the State Government for health sector for the 
period 2017-22. Findings are highlighted in the succeeding paragraphs:
The overall budget allotment and expenditure of the State Government and of the Health 
and Family Welfare Department during 2017-22 is given in Table 2.8.

The State did not achieve NHP 17 stipulated eight per cent budgetary allocation for 
health sector in any of the five years during the period 2017-22. The budgetary allocation 
for health ranged between 5.02 per cent and 7.26 per cent of the total State outlay on 
health. During 2017-22, average yearly expenditure on Health Sector was 7.07 per cent 
of the State’s yearly expenditure. Thus, the State was unable to meet the NHP stipulated 
eight per cent expenditure on health throughout the audit period of 2017-22. 
During 2017-22, the State’s expenditure on health sector ranged between 1.38 per cent 
and two per cent of the GSDP. 
Shortfall in the allocation/expenditure of funds for health sector as compared to the 
amount stipulated under NHP 17 resulted in inability to provide essential specialist 
services in the two CHCs of the State and some vital specialist services in the DHs of 
North, East and West districts like surgery, paediatrics, anaesthesia, ophthalmology, etc. 
while desirable services, which the CHCs and DHs were to aspire for, were missing in 
all the CHCs and DHs of the State. Short allocation of funds for the health sector also 
resulted in neglect of upkeep of the hospitals, health centres and other residential/ non-
residential buildings of the Department, as discussed in paragraph 2.4.22.2.

₹ 1989.03 crore (77 per cent) and capital expenditure ₹ 583.54 crore (23 per cent).

As can be seen above, 63 per cent of the revenue expenditure was incurred on salary and

cent on purchase of equipment and 13 per cent towards other items of expenditure like 
office expenses, TA, motor vehicle, repair/maintenance, minor works, etc.  
It was further observed that while 63 per cent of the revenue expenditure was incurred 
on salaries, a meagre share of 0.11 per cent of the revenue expenditure was spent on 
maintenance of the hospitals, health centres and other buildings indicating lower priority 
given for upkeep of the existing health infrastructure. Joint Physical verification (April

2.4.22.5 Inefficient / irregular utilisation of funds
a) Infructuous expenditure of ₹ 6.23 crore 
The Department took up (May 2016) the work ‘Construction of Tshangu PHC, Chipsu 
in East Sikkim’ at a cost of ₹ 6.23 crore sanctioned by Government of India (GoI) under 
the NHM.  The objective of the project was to provide a PHC facility in the area along 
with residential quarters for doctors and other staff.  The building was also proposed to 
serve as acclimatisation centre for pilgrims and tourists visiting Mansarovar.
The work stipulated to be completed within 24 months by May 2018 was completed in 
November 2019 after a delay of over a year and expenditure of ₹ 6.23 crore.  
Scrutiny of records revealed that while the provision in the SPW Manual55 provides for 
calling of open tenders for selection of contractors for undertaking any public work, 
the Department in contravention of the same, requested (May 2016) the area MLA 56 
to identify contractors for calling quotations to carry out the construction work. Out of 
three contractors recommended by the MLA, one contractor 57 was selected based on 
quotations obtained from them. Thus, the manner of allocation of work was irregular 
and against the SPW manual provisions. 
Further, the facility had neither been handed over till date (March 2023) to the concerned 
authority (CMO, East, Singtam DH) for its operation as a PHC nor was it being utilised 
for any other purpose like acclimatisation centre for pilgrims. There was also no record 
of posting of any manpower (doctors, paramedics, etc.) to the PHC nor provision of 
55 SPWM Para 11.1 - Wide publicity should be given to the NIT.  Tenders must be invited in the most open 
and transparent manner possible by advertisement in Sikkim Herald and other National and Local 
papers, and by notice pasted in public places and duly hosting in the Departmental Websites etc.

in East Sikkim’ at a cost of ₹ 6.23 crore sanctioned by Government of India (GoI) under 
the NHM.  The objective of the project was to provide a PHC facility in the area along

equipment for diagnostic and investigative purposes. Thus, the facility remained non-

Joint physical verification of the PHC building (26 August 2023) revealed that the building 
was locked and was lying unused and idle. Dust and debris had collected everywhere 
in the floors, tables, chairs, etc. due to prolonged idling of the facility. Damages and 
dampness were visible in the walls, doors, ceiling, etc. Images below corroborate the
