Power and water connection had not been provided to the building and neither was any 
equipment provided for diagnostic purposes. The facility also had not been provided with 
essential equipment and other paraphernalia for use of people requiring acclimatisation. 
The entire expenditure of ₹ 6.23 crore has so far been idling.
It is recommended that the PHC be repaired and made functional at the earliest.
b) Unwarranted excess expenditure of ₹ 2.93 crore on purchase of medicines/
consumables   
The Central Health Stores Organisation (CHSO) of the H&FW Department, Government 
of Sikkim is assigned with responsibility of procurement, storage and distribution of 
medicines and consumables for various health facilities of the State Government.  
The CHSO invited bids in July 2019 for procurement of medicines and consumables 
for the State’s Hospitals and Health Centres. After evaluation of the financial bids, the 
CHSO worked out (November 2019) the total cost of medicines and consumables to 
₹ 51.92 crore. The Finance Department approved sanction of ₹ four crore in December 
2019 and balance amount of ₹ 47.92 crore in February 2020. 
Audit noticed that after utilisation of the medicines and consumables purchased from the 
funds of ₹ four crore and based on fresh requirement from various health facilities, the 

Chapter II: Social Sector
51
CHSO re-worked (April 2020) the total cost of medicines and consumables to ₹ 40 crore 
on the basis of the tendered rates obtained earlier. 
However, the H&FW Department decided that the procurement of the medicines and 
consumables may be made from the SIMFED 58 on the plea that it was difficult to call 
fresh tenders due to the lock-down.  Audit noticed that Quotations from SIMFED were 
invited in June 2020 and supply orders for the medicines and consumables were issued 
to SIMFED in August 2020 and September 2020 respectively for ₹ 40 crore and the 
supplies were completed by March 2021.  
It may be mentioned that SIMFED had not been authorised by the State Government in 
its allocation of business to supply medicines and medical consumables to the H&FW 
Department. It was also unclear as to how the Department presumed SIMFED would be 
able to supply medicines and consumables at reasonable rates promptly during lockdown 
without calling for competitive bids. 
Thus, despite having valid tendered rates, the Department’s decision to procure 
medicines and consumables from SIMFED, despite SIMFED not being authorised to 
supply medical items, on the plea of inability to call fresh tenders due to lockdown, was 
irregular. 
Audit noticed that out of 237 items purchased from SIMFED, 19 items were purchased 
at rates equal to the tendered rates, 42 items at rates slightly lower than the tendered rates 
leading to saving of ₹ 27.39 lakh and 176 items at much higher rates leading to excess 
expenditure of ₹ 2.93 crore. Thus, there was net excess expenditure of ₹  2.66 crore 
from the public exchequer on purchase of medical items from SIMFED, despite the 
Department having valid lower tendered rates.
2.4.22.6 Conclusion
●	 The State had neither formulated its Health Policy nor did it adhere to the NHP 17 
stipulation of allocating adequate funds to the Health Sector. The State’s expenditure 
on health was below two per cent of the GSDP during 2017-22 and fell short of the 
NHP 17 stipulation during the period.  
●	 Shortfall in the allocation/expenditure of funds for health sector as compared to the 
NHP 17 resulted in inability to provide essential specialist services in the two CHCs 
of the State and some vital services in the DHs of North, East and West districts 
like surgery, paediatrics, anaesthesia, ophthalmology, etc. while desirable services, 
which the CHCs and DHs were to aspire for, were missing in all the CHCs and DHs 
of the State. 
●	 The meagre revenue expenditure of 0.11 per cent  for maintenance of health 
infrastructure negatively impacted the upkeep of infrastructure to the required 
standard.  
●	 Rather than increasing funds for repair and maintenance of infrastructure over time, 
the State Government kept downsizing funds, from ₹ 49.37 lakh during 2017-18 to 
₹40.43 lakh during 2021-22.  
●	 There were instances of unwarranted expenditure of ₹ 2.93 crore on procurement of 
medicines and consumables as well as infructuous expenditure of ₹ 6.23 crore on 
construction activities.
58 Sikkim State Cooperative Supply and Marketing Federation Limited (SIMFED). 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
52
2.4.22.7 Recommendations
i) The State may endeavour to increase its allocation to and expenditure on the Health 
Sector to bring it at par with the target under the National Health Policy 2017 with 
a view to provide all essential specialist services and also desirable services in the 
DHs and CHCs of the State. 
ii) The Department may take steps to increase funding for maintenance and upkeep of 
its physical infrastructure.
iii) The Department may take urgent steps to make the PHC at Chipsu, Tshangu 
functional.
iv) The Department may work out proper policy for procurement of medicines and 
consumables to avoid unwarranted and infructuous expenditure.
Audit objective 2: Whether healthcare infrastructure and services were available 
and properly managed?

Between the two sampled districts, East District had the highest shortage of CHCs (3),

benefits of proper healthcare facilities and services from qualified professionals near 
their place of residence.
2.4.23.2 Availability of Operation Theatre and Blood Storage facilities
District Hospital 
As per IPHS norms, Operation Theatre (OT) is an essential facility that a District 
Hospital and Community Health Centre (CHC) should have. Blood Storage unit should 
be in close proximity to the pathology department and at an accessible distance to OT 
and Emergency and Accident Departments. 
59 One PHSC per 3000 population; one PHC per 20000 population; one CHC per 80000 population. 

Chapter II: Social Sector
53
Audit noticed that in both the sampled DHs ( Singtam, Geyzing) there was no General 
Surgery Department to undertake major surgeries, etc. Hence the OTs in the DHs were 
functioning partially for addressing minor injuries and minor surgical procedures like 
suturing, dressing of minor cuts & wounds, abscess, etc. and could not function full- 
fledged as required for a District Hospital under the IPHS. 
Community Health Centre
The OT facility available in the Rhenock CHC sampled for audit had six equipment out 
of 11 required under the IPHS for a CHC-OT. The CHC also did not have blood storage 
facility as required under the IPHS. Further, there was no surgeon posted in the CHC 
therefore OT facility could not provide required services and was used to provide only 
basic first aid and minor surgical procedures like suturing, dressing of minor injuries, 
wounds, abscess, etc.

Audit noticed that for essential services in the PHCs, one to two additional medical 
officers beyond the IPHS norms were provided in three out of the five sampled PHCs 
which indicated a positive trend.  However, there was excess deployment of Staff nurses 
in the sampled PHCs ranging from five to 20 against the norm of three staff nurses for a 
PHC. Medical officer for AYUSH, desirable under the IPHS, was provided in one PHC 
(Soreng) while dental service was available in all the sampled PHCs. 
There being no dearth of essential manpower in the sampled PHCs in terms of the IPHS, 
the population covered by these PHCs could be expected to receive adequate medical 
care as required under a PHC. 
Community Health Centres (CHCs): CHCs are to provide routine and emergency care 
which include specialist services in Surgery, Medicine, Obstetrics and Gynaecology, 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
54
Paediatrics and Anaesthesia.  Besides, Dental and AYUSH services were also to be 
delivered.  
Audit noticed that the Rhenock CHC did not have any specialist. The CHC also did 
not have Medical Superintendent, Public Health Specialist and Public Health Nurse, 
as required under the IPHS. Non-availability of specialist doctors and other essential 
manpower in the CHC effectively reduced the functionality of the CHC to  a PHC with 
additional beds. Detailed analysis of availability of manpower in the sampled CHCs is 
highlighted in paragraph 2.4.4.
District Hospital: In terms of the IPHS, a District Hospital is expected to provide 
effective and affordable health care services, including specialist services, to both 
urban population (district headquarter town) and the rural population of the district. 
The essential services (minimum assured services) required to be provided by a 
DH, inter alia , comprised specialist services in general medicine, general surgery, 
obstetrics & gynaecology, paediatrics, emergency services, critical care /intensive care 
units, anaesthesia, ophthalmology, ENT, orthopaedics, radiology including imaging, 
psychiatry, geriatric, dental care and AYUSH.  
The availability of specialist services in the two DHs sampled for Audit is shown in 
Appendix 2.10. 
The following points were noticed during audit of the two sampled DHs: 
●	 The two sampled DHs had essential specialist services in all Departments except 
in General Surgery and Radiology Department. Absence of the two vital specialties 
in the DHs affected the DHs’ ability to handle cases calling for major surgeries 
and essential imaging diagnostic services required for almost all types of critical 
diseases. 
●	 Shortage and excess of specialists and other manpower were noticed in several 
Departments in the DHs. While Singtam DH had two excess specialists for General 
Medicine and five excess general medical officers, there was shortage of one 
medicine specialist and six medical officers in Geyzing DH. Both the DHs suffered 
from shortage of specialty services in Anaesthesia, which would affect operations 
by specialists.  
●	 There was excess deployment of 38 and 19 paramedics as well as nine and 13 
nurses in the Singtam and Geyzing DH, respectively, beyond the IPHS norm. The 
Geyzing DH also had excess deployment of 13 dentists beyond the IPHS norms. 
Thus, the deployment of specialists, medical officers, nurses and paramedics in the two 
DHs was haphazard and non-uniform. This resulted in non-availability of adequate 
services in Departments  with shortage of manpower and under-utilisation of manpower 
in Departments which were overstaffed. 
2.4.23.4 Outpatient Department Services in DHs, PHCs and CHCs
The Outpatient Department (OPD) is the first point of contact between patient and hospital 
staff.  An OPD or outpatient clinic is the part of a hospital designed for the treatment 
of out-patients i.e., people with health problems who visit the hospital for diagnosis or 
treatment, but do not at this time require a bed or to be admitted for overnight care.  It 
normally remains integrated with the in-patient services and is staffed by physicians and 
surgeons who also attend inpatients in the wards.  The treatment procedure followed in 
the Public Health Institutions in the State is depicted in Chart 2.7. 

Chapter II: Social Sector
55
Chart 2.7: Flow of OPD services in PHCs, CHCs & DHs 
Audit findings pertaining to OPD services like registration, consultation, waiting time 
and other basic OPD facilities/ services are discussed below. 
2.4.23.5 Registration facility for OPD 
The registration counter is the entry point of contact with the hospital for a patient and is 
an important component of the hospital for patients and their attendants. NHM Assessor 
guidebook (V ol-l) estimates 3-5 minutes per patient as the average time required for 
registration, which roughly works out to about 20 to 12 patients/hour per counter. 
The average daily patient load on a registration counter in the sampled health centres 
during 2021-22, is shown in Table 2.13.

average patient load per counter per hour during 2021-22 in the sampled DHs was 32 
to 41 patients per hour, in the CHC it was 15 patients and in the five PHCs the average 
patient load per hour ranged between two and 18 patients. Singtam DH had the highest 
patient load among the two DHs and Pakyong PHC the highest patient load per hour 
among the sampled PHCs. In the Singtam and Geyzing DHs, registration load exceeded 
the prescribed norm of 20 to 12 patients per hour, which made the waiting period for 
registration of patients in the two DHs longer than prescribed.

The OPDs in the DHs and CHCs were to be run by specialist doctors in terms of the 
IPHS.  The two sampled DHs had essential specialist services in all Departments except 
General Surgery and Radiology (both DHs) while the Rhenock CHC did not have any 
specialist services.  People seeking medical care under Rhenock CHC were thus deprived 
of the benefit of specialist services in the CHC and were forced to move to higher health 
facilities elsewhere.   
The patient load in all the health facilities declined during 2020-21 compared to the 
previous year, which could be due to the impact of Covid-19 pandemic and lockdown. 
AYUSH is categorised as essential service at the CHC level and desirable at the PHC 
level as per the IPHS norms. The facility was available in CHC Rhenock and in one PHC 
out of the five sampled PHCs. Dental facility was available in all five PHCs.  The State 
Government had neither formulated any norms or policies for posting AYUSH doctors/ 
specialists in the CHCs and PHCs nor adhered to the IPHS norms.  This resulted in non-
availability of specialist doctors in the CHCs and absence of AYUSH services in most 
PHCs.

2021-22 in the two DHs, one CHC and five sampled PHCs. The OPD cases across all

It is observed that the two DHs sampled for Audit ( Singtam & Geyzing) had only 40 
out of the 84 prescribed path ological services and tests. While Singtam DH did not 
have Electrocardiogram (ECG) facility, the Geyzing DH had only one out of the three 
prescribed ophthalmological services.  
Audit noticed that CHC Rhenock had only 18 out of the 35 prescribed clinical 
pathological services and tests and did not have ECG service while out of three prescribed 
ophthalmological tests, only one test was available. 
As regards the five PHCs sampled for audit, availability of Clinical Pathology services 
and other tests ranged between six and nine services out of the prescribed nine services. 
Non-availability of equipment and qualified technicians were the main reasons for 
absence of the desired level of services and the referred out cases as discussed in 
Paragraph 2.4.23.12.
Audit further noticed that none of the sampled DHs, CHC and the PHCs had any system 
for obtaining quality assurance for the pathological tests despite the IPHS stipulation 
for ensuring external monitoring through performance evaluation and external quality 
assessment of laboratories. The H&FW Department had not established any system or 
procedure for conducting quality checks and validation of pathological tests by any 
External Quality Testing Agency. 
It is recommended that the Department establish a system for quality checks and validation.
2.4.23.9 In-Patient Department (IPD) Services in the PHCs, CHCs and DHs
In terms of the IPHS, a District Hospital, inter alia , should have minimum assured 
specialist services (essential services) for the In-patient Department in general medicine, 
general surgery, obstetrics & gynaecology, paediatrics, emergency services, critical 
care / intensive care units, anaesthesia, ophthalmology, ENT, orthopaedics, radiology 
including imaging, psychiatry, geriatric, dental care and AYUSH. The two DHs 
sampled for Audit had significant number of specialist services available as discussed 
in Paragraph 2.4.23.3 (District Hospital) . Both the DHs, however, did not have the 
essential specialist service on General Surgery. Among the desirable services (eight), 
both the DHs had dialysis services available while Singtam DH had dermatology and 
venereology service. AYUSH facilities were available in both DHs. 
It is recommended that the Department may establish system of quality checks and 
validation of the pathological tests by external quality testing agencies.
2.4.23.10  Availability of in-patient services in the Community Health Centres
The Community Health Centres (CHCs) are the 30 bedded health facility centres in the 
State. In terms of the IPHS, CHCs should provide specialist in-patient service pertaining 
to General Medicine, General Surgery, Obstetrics & Gynaecology and Paediatrics.  
Audit observed that the required specialist services were not available in the sampled 
CHC (Rhenock). Absence of specialist services for General Medicine, Surgery, Obstetrics 
&Gynaecology and Paediatrics in the CHC led to 668 patients having maternal issues, 
child diseases and other issues to travel to the nearest District Hospital (Singtam DH) or 
the STNM Hospital, Gangtok seeking treatment. 
2.4.23.11  In-patient services in the Primary Health Centres
The Primary Health Centres (PHCs) are the 10 bedded health facility centres in the State 
which are managed by Medical Officers. Specialist services, which are categorised as 

Chapter II: Social Sector
59
‘desirable services’ by the IPHS for the PHCs, were not available in any of the sampled 
PHCs. General medicine service, Normal Vaginal Deliveries (NVD) with Anti and Post 
Natal Care and Immunisation services were found delivered by the sampled PHCs as 
envisaged in the IPHS. 
2.4.23.12  Referrals of patients to higher facilities for better treatment
Referral is a dynamic process, in which a health worker at one level of the health system, 
having insufficient resources (drugs, equipment, skills) to manage a clinical condition of 
a patient, seeks the help of a better facility at the same or higher level.  
Audit noticed that out of 3.25 lakh patients visiting the sampled health facilities, 
8344 patients were referred to the District and State Hospitals during 2017-18 to  
2021-22.  The detailed position is shown in Table 2.17.

Among the two DHs, maximum referred out cases of 2981 pertained to Geyzing DHand 
442 cases were related to maternity issues. Higher medical facilities for referral outside 
Geyzing were available either at Gangtok or Siliguri, both about 120 Kms away from 
Geyzing. This indicated that the referred out cases involved substantial journey distress 
and extra out of pocket expenditure for the patients.
The Singtam DH had 2552 referred out cases maximum of which related to maternity 
issues (1688). Singtam DH being closer to Gangtok (25 Kms), journey distress suffered by 
the referred-out cases would be comparatively lower. Among the PHCs, Rinchengpong 
PHC had the highest number of referred out cases of 919 (75 per cent) out of the total 
inpatient admission of 1219 patients. 
2.4.23.13 Maternal and Child Care
(i)  MMR and IMR
Maternal Mortality Rate (MMR) refers to the number of maternal deaths per 100,000 live 
births due to causes related to pregnancy or within 42 days of termination of pregnancy, 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
60
regardless of the site or duration of pregnancy.  Infant Mortality Rate (IMR) indicates 
the number of deaths of infants (under one year) per 1,000 live births. 
The All India MMR during 2014-16 stood at 130 per 100,000 live births which declined 
to 113 in 2016-18. The All India IMR which stood at 34 per 1000 live births in 2016 
came down to 28 in 2020. 
Trend of MMR and IMR in Sikkim during 2017-22 is shown in Table 2.18.

As the live births in Sikkim ranged between 6715 and 7268 during 2017-22, which 
was much below the level of one lakh live births per year, the MMR of Sikkim was not 
calculated.  The IMR of the State was on a declining trend which declined from 15 in 
2017-18 to nine in 2021-22, in comparison to the All-India average, which is positive 
for the State. 
(ii)    Antenatal Care
Antenatal care is the systemic supervision of women during pregnancy to monitor the 
progress of foetal growth and to ascertain the well-being of the mother and the foetus. A 
proper antenatal check-up provides necessary care to the mother and helps identify any 
complications of pregnancy such as anaemia, pre-eclampsia, hypertension, etc. 
Antenatal Care (ANC) check-up
As per instructions issued by the Maternal Health Division, Ministry of Health and 
Family Welfare, all pregnant women (PW) are required to be registered and minimum 
four Antenatal Care (ANC) check-ups are needed to be conducted. Each pregnant woman 
is given a unique ID while registering her name and details in the Reproductive and 
Child Health (RCH) Register in the facility and is given a Mother and Child Protection 
(MCP) card. All the investigations done and date of visit have to be recorded in the MCP 
card and the same is required to be updated in the RCH Register. All pregnant women 
who are registered with MCP card are to be given Iron Folic Acid (IFA) tablets and 
Calcium tablets compulsorily. IFA tablets (180) have been prescribed for six months 
during pregnancy and are to be continued for six months post-partum.
During the period 2017-18  to 2021-22, the position of mandated ANC check-up 
conducted in two sampled Districts was as given in the Table 2.19:

Audit noticed that the percentage of ANC carried out ranged between 56 to 72 per cent

	During the period 2017- 20, against 22263 and 8958 PWs registered for ANC, 
15695 (70 per cent) and 7717 (86 per cent) were registered within the first trimester 
of pregnancy in Singtam DH and Gyalshing DH respectively. There was shortfall 
of 30 and 14 per cent in registration of PWs in Singtam DH and Gyalshing DH 
respectively. 
	During the period covered by audit, 8289 and 1626 PW representing 37 and 
18 per cent did not receive minimum four ANC check-ups in Singtam DH and 
Gyalshing DH respectively during the pregnancy period.  
	Out of 22,263 and 8958 PWs registered for ANC in these two hospitals, 312 and 10 
PWs suffered from severe anaemia which was a major cause of maternal and infant 
mortality as per maternal mortality review.

(a) Trend of Stillbirths (State)

It can be seen from Table 2.22 that the rate of stillbirths during 2017-22 in the State

Discrepancy was however noticed between the figures of total live births, total deliveries 
vis-a-vis the total number of still births in the State, captured in the health management 
information system data bank (HMIS) of the Department, reflected in Tables 2.22 
and 2.23 . The difference between total deliveries and total live births should be the 
total number of stillbirths. This difference, however, did not match with the figures of 
stillbirths depicted for the State in the HMIS. This fact cast doubts on the efficacy and 
authenticity of the data capturing system in the Department for HMIS. The Department 
may ensure that robust data capturing systems are in place.
(b)  Trend of Stillbirths (sampled health institutions)
Audit also observed that stillbirth rate varied between 0 per cent and 2.35 per cent in 
the sampled DHs, CHC and PHCs during 2017-22 as shown in Table 2.23. Geyzing DH 
registered highest stillbirth rate of 2.35 per cent followed by Rinchengpong PHC at 1.72

2.4.23.14  Conclusion
●	 The shortage of PHSCs, PHCs and CHCs in the three districts of the State deprived 
the benefits of proper healthcare facilities from being provided to the rural population 
of the districts near their place of residence.  
●	 OTs in the sampled DHs Singtam & Geyzing suffered from lack of essential 
equipment as well as the specialist doctors in surgery. As a result, the OTs functioned 
only partially for addressing minor surgical procedures. OT in the Rhenock CHC 
also remained non-functional due to non-availability of surgeon and the blood 
storage facility.
●	 Absence of specialist services in the Rhenock CHC deprived sick people of essential 
healthcare services expected from a CHC closer to their homes. 
●	 Deployment of specialists, medical officers, nurses and paramedics in the two 
sampled DHs was done without considering either actual requirement or the IPHS 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
64
norm. This resulted in non-availability of services in some Departments while 
leading to underutilisation of manpower in Departments having excess staff. 
●	 The State was experiencing higher still birth rate (percentage) over and above the 
all India average.
●	 There were discrepancies in the figures of total live births, total deliveries and the 
total number of stillbirths captured in the health management information system 
(HMIS) of the Department indicating flaws in the process of data collection and 
data management.
2.3.23.15 Recommendations
i) The Department may take initiative to make good the shortage of essential equipment 
and ensure availability of required specialist services and blood storage facility in 
the OTs of the DHs and CHCs. 
ii) Specialist services viz., Surgery, Medicine, Obstetrics & Gynaecology, Paediatrics 
and Anaesthesia, etc. may be introduced in the CHCs in the State as required under 
the IPH norms. 
iii) Specialists, medical officers, nurses and paramedics in the sampled DHs (Singtam 
and Geyzing) may be redeployed based on IPH norms and actual requirement.
iv) Government may take steps to set up new PHSCs, PHCs and CHCs to mitigate the 
shortages (five CHCs, nine PHCs and 67 PHSCs) in the two districts of the State 
and to ensure availability of proper healthcare facilities near the place of residence 
of people of those areas. 
v) The Department may take steps to identify reasons for high stillbirth rates in the 
State and take corrective measures to reduce the rate.
vi) The Department may take steps to ensure robust data entry and validation 
processes.
Objective 3: Whether Support services in the health facilities were available and 
adequate
2.4.24 Other Support Services
The operational activities of a health facility comprise a wide variety of support services 
viz., management of linens, dietary management, ambulance service, sweeping and 
cleaning service, hospital security service, etc. Management of support services in the 
sampled health institutions is discussed in Paragraphs 2.4.24.1 to 2.4.24.6. 
2.4.24.1 Hospital linen services
As per IPHS, the number of linen (OT coats, bed sheets, bed covers, pillows, blankets, 
pillow covers) required in DHs, CHCs & PHCs has been quantified as per the bed 
strength of each health facility.
During physical verification (April 2023), use of hospital issued linens like bed sheets, 
blankets and pillows by the patients was found very rare.  There was no provision for 
providing patient-dresses to the in-patients.  Most of the patients preferred to use their 
own linens and blankets rather than that provided by the hospitals. Washing machines for 
cleaning the hospital linen were available, however, ironing facilities were not available 
in the sampled health facilities.

Chapter II: Social Sector
65
2.4.24.2 Good Practices in Staff Immunisation and Hospital Disinfection
 Staff	immunisation 
Periodic medical check-up and immunisation of staff is a part of the hospital infection 
control programme and to be followed by the DHs, CHCs and the PHCs as per norms. 
Audit noticed that the sampled health facilities provided Hepatitis B and Tetanus 
injections to all its health care workers and ensured immunisation of staff from infectious 
diseases.  
 Disinfection and sterilisation 
To prevent hospital acquired infections in patients, visitors and staff, the NHM Assessor’s 
Guidebook 2013 required each DH to frame a schedule of procedure to be followed 
by the health care facilities known as Standard Operating Procedures (SOP) for septic 
procedures, culture surveillance and determination of hospital acquired infections. The 
facility should also have standard procedures for processing disinfection and sterilisation 
of equipment and instruments.  
Audit observed that both DHs had functional Infection Control Committees headed by 
CMO of the respective DH and had SOPs for septic procedures, culture surveillance and 
determination of hospital acquired infections. The sampled hospitals adopted boiling, 
autoclaving, and chemical sterilisation processes for disinfection and sterilisation of 
hospital equipment and instruments. 
2.4.24.3 Disaster management and fire security
NHM Assessor’s Guidebook envisages that a Disaster Management Plan (DMP) be 
prepared for each health facility. Besides, disaster management training for hospital staff 
and conducting periodic mock drills in the hospitals is necessary. Standard Operating 
Procedures (SOPs) should be available, and a disaster management committee should 
be constituted. 
Audit noticed that the sampled hospitals had disaster management plan but had not 
developed SOPs on disaster management. Mock drills on disaster management and 
fire safety drills were conducted periodically. Though the sampled health facilities had 
adequate number of fire extinguishers, fire detection alarms were not available in any of 
the sampled health centres.  
2.4.24.4 Power back-up and water supply
Physical verification of the sampled units revealed (April 2023) availability of 24x7 
power supply including back-up arrangement in both the DHs. Besides, the DHs also 
had power inverters installed in the Emergency wards and OTs. However, in respect 
of the PHCs, the DG sets provided were functional in only two PHCs ( Sombaria and 
Pakyong) out of the sampled five PHCs. The DG set provided to the CHC Rhenock 
was also non-functional. The DG sets were non-functional due to lack of recharge or 
nonreplacement of the dead batteries used in the DG sets to start. 
Singtam DH had frequent water shortages due to absence of a dedicated supply line 
of water and absence of dedicated maintenance plan and manpower to ensure regular 
upkeep of the water supply system.  

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
66
2.4.24.5 Ambulance Service
In terms of the IPHS (Clause XVI Hospital Transport Services) norms, a District 
Hospital shall have well equipped Basic Life Support (BLS) and desirably one Advanced 
Life Support (ALS) ambulance. For a DH of bed capacity 101 to 200 beds (Clause-
XIII), availability of three ambulances was prescribed. The NHM guidelines prescribe 
availability of national ambulance services which can be availed by calling telephone 
numbers 108 and 102 for attending to patients of critical care, trauma, and accident 
victims etc. (108) and basic patient transport to cater to the needs of pregnant women, 
children etc. (102).  
Audit noticed that there were three standby ambulances in Geyzing DH for referral 
services in case of emergencies.  These ambulances were fitted with basic life support 
(BLS) system having oxygen support and suction machines. Besides, there were an 
additional seven ambulances in the PHCs of West District which could be availed 
through Call Centre No. 102 for pregnancy related cases.  
Singtam DH had four ambulances with BLS system. Apart from these four ambulances, 
there was at least one ambulance in each of the five PHCs and two ambulances in 
the Rhenock CHC under Singtam DH that were used for transporting patients. The 
ambulances under Singtam DH were, however, not connected with the 102 national 
ambulance service. 
For all other critical cases, 108 connectivity was however not available in the test 
checked health facilities. 
2.4.24.6 Evaluation of In-patient Services through Outcome Indicators
This paragraph presents an assessment of overall Health Indicators of the State and the 
IPD services provided during 2017-22 in the sampled health facilities. The Outcome 
Indicators (OIs) prescribed in IPHS guidelines are Bed Occupancy Rate (BOR), Referral 
out Rate (ROR), etc. Table 2.24 gives the categorisation and methodology of evaluating 
these standards. 
Table 2.24: Calculation of quality indicators
Type Quality Indicator Numerator Denominator
Productivity of

(per cent)

No. of days during the period 
Efficiency ROR 
(per 1000) 
Total No. of cases referred 
to higher facility X 1000 Total No. of admissions 
Source: IPHS 
The bed occupancy rate (BOR) is the average occupancy of hospital beds within a given 
period. It is an indicator of the productivity of the hospital services and is a measure of 
verifying whether the available infrastructure and processes are adequate for delivery of 
health services. In terms of the IPHS, the BOR of hospitals should be at least 80 per cent. 
Similarly, the referral out rate (ROR) of a hospital denotes the total number of cases 
referred to higher facilities out of the total admissions in the hospital during a given 
period, per 1000 new admissions.  
The BOR62 and ROR of the sampled health facilities during 2021-22 as worked out by 
audit is shown in Table 2.25.

is taken in the absence of complete data on the number of days each in-patient occupied a hospital 
bed.

2.4.24.7 Assessment of Performance
All the eight hospitals and health centres sampled for Audit had BOR ranging between 
eight to 78 per cent, which was below the IPHS norm of 80 to 100 per cent. Machong 
PHC had the lowest BOR of 8.13 while Rinchengpong PHC had the second lowest 
BOR of 20.04. This was despite these two PHCs having excess manpower (29 and 40) 
in various cadres. Pakyong PHC had highest BOR of 77.31 per cent and lowest referral 
out rate of 76 indicating relatively better patient care and better availability of services 
at that PHC.  All the health centres, excepting Pakyong PHC, had much higher ROR 
which indicated dearth of adequate facilities and services in those centres compelling 
the hospital authorities to refer critical and emergent cases for better medical service 
from elsewhere.  
2.4.24.8 Conclusion
Ambulance service under Call Centre No.102 for pregnancy related cases was available 
only in the West District in the State while the service under call centre No. 108 for other 
emergencies was not available in any of the test checked health facilities. 
2.4.24.9  Recommendations 
i) The Department may take immediate steps to provide dedicated water supply line 
to the Singtam District Hospital and ensure its regular maintenance.
ii) Ambulance service under Call Centre No.102 for pregnancy related cases and 108 
for other emergencies may be made available for all health facilities. 
Audit objective 4: Whether assets created for medical emergencies like Covid-19 
pandemic were utilised efficiently?

2.4.26.1 Virology lab (RT-PCR testing facilities)
Audit noticed that Virology lab for conduct of RT-PCR64 test was established in the State

Audit further noticed that none of the two DHs had functioning virology labs as of March

facility due to lack of adequate space for housing the equipment, want of manpower to 
conduct the tests and non-availability of bio-safety cabinets, etc. The RTPCR testing kits 
thus remained unused and in packed condition. The warranty period of six out of the 
seven components of the RTPCR kits had already expired in March 2023.

2.4.26.2  ICU facilities for Covid-19 patients
a) Singtam District Hospital- non- functioning 
of paediatric ICU and Trauma ICU
A six bedded paediatric ICU unit had been 
established in Singtam DH during December 2022 
under Emergency Covid Response Package (ECRP 
-II) funded under NHM with two ventilators. The 
Department, however, had not provided specific 
manpower and equipment/ accessories to run the 
unit and make it functional. During joint physical 
verification (April 2023) it was noticed that the 
unit remained idle and unused.
Image 2.25: Idle Paediatric ICU 
Singtam-DH
There was also a six bedded trauma ICU unit in the DH with four ventilators. This unit 
was also non-functional as surgery services were not available in the DH. The ventilators 
in the two ICU units had also not been tested for functionality and hence it was not 
known whether they were in working condition.

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
70
b)  Rhenock Community Health Centre-idle expenditure ₹ 31.18 lakh 
Funds of ₹ 34.49 lakh had been provided 
by the GoI under Emergency Covid 
Response Package (NHM, ECRP-II) for 
creating a twenty bedded pre-fabricated 
oxygen support unit for treating Covid 
affected patients under East District. 
A concrete unit on the terrace of the 
Rhenock CHC was created (December 
2022) from the funds at a cost of ₹ 
31.18 lakh. The facility, however, 
remained idle (April 2023) as it was yet to be provided with essential equipment like beds, 
ventilators, oxygen connection etc. 
c)  Idle Covid-Hospital at Karfectar
The Chief Minister inaugurated a 50 bedded 
Covid Health Centre at the State Institute of 
Capacity Building at Karfectar in August 2022 
equipped with Intensive Care Unit beds (17), 
High Care Unit beds (19), Bedside Monitors 
(25), Ventilators (13), Digital X-Ray (one), 
Jumbo Oxygen Cylinders (150), Defibrillator 
(one), ECG Machine (one), Arterial Blood Gas 
(ABG) Machine etc. The Covid Hospital was, 
however, lying idle and in locked condition 
and was not being used for any purpose. No manpower was deputed in the facility and 
neither were any patients treated since its creation. The Department / Government had 
also not taken any decision till date (September 2023) for using the facility as a regular 
hospital or any other allied purpose. Damage of the equipment and accessories provided 
to the hospital due to prolonged disuse could not be ruled out in audit.

2.4.26.4 Oxygen Generating Plants
The State had six Oxygen generation plants of total generation capacity of 3900 litres per 
minute (lpm). The Plants were installed during 2021-22 in the STNM Superspeciality 
Hospital and the four DHs. Four of the Oxygen plants had been provided under PM 
CARES, one as aid from the Turkey Government and one purchased by Department 
from funds provided by the NEC. Details are given in Table 2.30.
Table 2.30: Status of Oxygen Plants in the State
Sl
No 
Capacity of Plant  
(litres per minute) 
Date of in-
stallation 
Hospital where the plants were 
installed 
Source 
1 500 June 2021 STNM Hospital, Gangtok NEC Funded 
2 1000 22.06.2021 STNM Hospital, Gangtok PM CARES 
3 200 26.06.2021 Singtam DH, East Sikkim Turkey Aid 
4 200 28.06.2021 Geyzing DH, West Sikkim PM CARES 
5 1000 16.08.2021 Namchi DH, South Sikkim PM CARES 
6 1000 22.08.2021 Mangan DH, North Sikkim PM CARES 
Total 3900 
The status of functioning of the Oxygen plants installed in the Hospitals of the State is 
highlighted below: 
i) The 1000 lpm plant at Gangtok functioned intermittently due to maintenance issues.  
ii) The 200 lpm oxygen plant in Gyalshing DH was functional since 28 June 2021 
and had run 5913 hours as on 31 March 2023. The plant, however, was functioning 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
72
only when there was electricity as the DG set provided for the plant was non-
functional.  
iii) The 200 lpm Oxygen Plant in Singtam DH was idle for want of dedicated DG set to 
run the plant, very long starting period (> one hour) before oxygen could actually 
generate and flow to the patients. The Hospital therefore was still using bottled 
oxygen to cater to the needs of patients.  
iv) The plant installed (August 2021) in Mangan DH more than two years ago, remained 
idle due to low demand and non-provision of DG set to operate it. The Hospital thus 
had to continue using bottled Oxygen procured from the State budget. 
Thus, the oxygen plants in the State had been established without ensuring availability 
of supporting equipment like DG sets and without ensuring prompt repairs due to which 
the plants either remained idle or functioned only intermittently. Due to this fact the 
Department continued using bottled oxygen purchased from the Government funds for 
the in-patients.  
2.4.26.5 Non-reduction in consumption of bottled oxygen
Audit observed that even after installation of the oxygen generation units of 3900 lpm 
capacity during 2021-22, there was no reduction in the procurement of bottled oxygen, 
as depicted in Table 2.31 (excluding 2020-22 being pandemic affected years). 
Table	2.31:	Refilling	and	Expenditure	on	bottled	oxygen
Year No.		of	cycles	of	refilling	 Expenditure	on	refilling	(₹	in	lakh)	
2017-2018 3843 6.70
2018-2019 4037 10.67 
2019-2020 8000 33.06 
2020-2021 11156 45.53 
2021-2022 21020 93.39 
2022-2023 7869 39.40 
Total 55925 228.75
During the year 2021-22 when the oxygen plants had 
actually been installed in the State’s five hospitals, the 
Department procured maximum quantity of liquid oxygen 
valuing ₹ 93.39 lakh. Even after Covid period of 2021-22, 
the quantity of bottled oxygen purchased remained higher 
than the quantity purchased during 2017-19 prior to the 
Covid period. This was despite installation of the six 
oxygen plants of such high installed capacity of 3900 lpm 
and also despite 249 oxygen concentrators (Paragraph 
2.4.26.3) lying idle with the Department. Thus, installation 
of six oxygen plants of such high capacity of 3900 lpm 
besides existence of surplus oxygen concentrators with the Department did not help 
reduce the Department’s dependence on bottled oxygen as the oxygen plants remained 
inoperative while 249 oxygen concentrators languished in disuse in the Department’s 
stores. 
2.4.26.6 Unfruitful expenditure of ₹ 21.27 lakh on construction of shed for O2 Plant
The Government of India, Department of Health and Family Welfare, requested 
(November 2020) the State Government for sharing site readiness confirmation for 
Image 2.33: Idle Covid Hospital, 
Karfectar


Chapter II: Social Sector
73
installation of Oxygen Generating Plants in the State’s Hospitals. The plants were 
proposed to be provided by the Government of India free of cost. The State Health Society 
accordingly spent ₹ 21.27 lakh (April 2021) from the NHM funds for construction of a 
shed for housing the Oxygen Generating Plant in the STNM Hospital Complex. 
During joint physical verification (March 2023) it was noticed that the shed was 
constructed at a location away from the location of the manifold system for supply 
of oxygen to the hospital beds, with no approach road for ferrying the Oxygen plant 
equipment and other paraphernalia. The structure thus remained idle and unused 
resulting in an unfruitful expenditure of ₹ 21.27 lakh. 
2.4.26.7 Conclusion
●	 The State had established RTPCR testing facility only in the STNM Hospital at 
Gangtok. None of the district hospitals had functioning RTPCR facility during 
2020-22.  
●	 The 50 bedded Covid Hospital at Karfectar remained idle for more than a year 
since its creation in August 2022. The Department / Government had not taken any 
decision to utilise the building.
●	 Out of the six O 2 generating plants having total installed capacity of 3900 lpm set 
up during 2021-22 in the five hospitals in the State, two plants of 1200 lpm set 
up in Mangan and Singtam DH were lying idle due to non-provisioning of DG 
sets while one plant of 1000 lpm installed at the STNM Hospital functioned only 
intermittently.  
●	 A large number of Ventilators (51) and Oxygen concentrators (12) were lying idle 
and unused in the Departmental Stores and the Stores of STNM Hospital  
●	 Despite installation of the oxygen plants and idling of existing oxygen concentrators, 
the dependence on bottled oxygen in the hospitals had not reduced.   
2.4.26.8 Recommendations
i) The Department may make RTPCR cum Virology labs functional in the DHs. 
ii) The Department /Government should prepare an action plan for utilising the 
idle newly constructed 50 bedded Covid Hospital constructed at Karfectar at the 
earliest.
iii) The Department may ensure regular repair and upkeep of the Oxygen generating 
plants with a view to ensure their utility. 
iv) The Department may take stock of the idle ventilators and concentrators in the 
stores and ensure their usage or disposal, as appropriate.  
Audit Objective 3: Assess status of improvement of health and wellbeing of people of 
the State as envisaged under SDG Goal 3.
2.4.27.1 United Nations Sustainable Development Goals (Goal-3 Health and 
Wellbeing)
United Nations General Assembly adopted (September 2015) the 2030 Agenda for 
Sustainable Development which included 17 Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) 
with the objective of transforming the world into a better place for all people.  

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
74
Goal 3 of the SDG concern ‘Good Health and Well-being’ which is committed to ensure 
healthy lives and promote well-being for all at all ages. There are 13 sub-goals 66 under 
Goal 3. 
Government of India entrusted NITI Aayog  as nodal agency for helping India chart 
its quest for attaining the commitments under the SDGs. Findings of Audit relating to 
adoption and fulfilment of SDG 3 goals by the State are elaborated below.

as per the NITI Aayog Report on ‘SDG Index India and Dashboard of 2021, is depicted 
in Chart 2.8.

States viz percentage of children in the age group 9-11 months fully immunised and 
suicide rate and death due to road accidents.
2.4.27.6  Conclusion 
●	 The State was lagging behind in four health indicators in comparison to the National 
average as per the SDG India Index Dash-Board 2021– percentage of immunisation 
of children in the age group 9-11 years, suicide rate; death rate due to road accidents 
and total number of physicians, nurses and midwives per 10000 population.  
●	 The State fared poorly in respect of three health indicators as compared with the NE 
States viz percentage of children in the age group 9-11 months fully immunised and 
suicide rate and death due to road accidents.
●	 The State’s suicide rate was extremely high and was more than 300 per cent of the 
national average. This was a matter of serious concern for the State Government. 
2.4.27.7 Recommendations
i) Government should take appropriate action towards regular monitoring and 
supervision of the health and family welfare programmes with a view to ensure 
achieving the SDG 3 targets. 
ii) Government may take serious note to ascertain the reasons for the high suicide rate 
and deaths due to road accidents in the state and make concerted efforts to contain 
the same.

3.2 Planning and conduct of audit
Audit process starts with the assessment of risks faced by various departments based 
on expenditure incurred, criticality/complexity of activities, level of delegated financial 
powers, assessment of overall internal controls, etc.
After completion of audit of each unit on a test check basis, Inspection Reports (IRs) 
containing audit findings are issued to the heads of the departments. The departments 
are required to furnish replies to the audit findings within one month of receipt of the 
IRs. Whenever replies are received, audit findings are either settled based on reply/
action taken or further action is required by the audited entities for compliance. Some of 
the important audit observations arising out of these IRs are processed for inclusion in 
the Audit Reports. The Audit Reports are submitted to the Governor of the State under 
Article 151 of the Constitution of India for laying on the table of the Legislature for 
taking further appropriate action.
Test audits were conducted involving expenditure of ₹ 3,071.43 crore (including 
expenditure of ₹ 1,967.26 crore of previous years) during the year 2022-23 of the State 
Government under Economic Sector. The details of year-wise break-up are given in 
Appendix 3.1.

PERFORMANCE AUDIT
RURAL DEVELOPMENT DEPARTMENT
State Rural Roads Development Agency
3.3  Performance Audit on Implementation of the Pradhan Mantri Gram Sadak 
Yojana (PMGSY)
3.3.1 Introduction
Rural Road Connectivity, and its sustained availability, is a key component to Rural 
Development. Despite rural roads being a state subject and the responsibility of providing 
connectivity to all village/habitations resting with the state governments, the Government 
of India, acknowledging the expected socio-economic benefits to the rural population 
from rural roads and as a strategy for poverty alleviation and to overcome deficiencies in 
rural road planning, introduced (December 2000) a centrally sponsored scheme ‘Pradhan 
Mantri Gram Sadak Yojana’  (PMGSY)  to provide all-weather road 6667
68 connectivity 
including necessary bridges to eligible unconnected habitations in rural areas.
The objective of the PMGSY is to provide connectivity, by way of an ‘all-weather road’ 
to the eligible unconnected habitations (Census 2001) in the rural areas with a population 
of 500 persons and above in plain areas and 250 persons in hilly areas.
PMGSY-I was initially a 100 per cent centrally sponsored scheme, but from the year 
2015-16, the funding is shared between the Centre and the State as 90:10 for North 
Eastern and Himalayan States.
As the Programme unfolded, a dire need to consolidate the entire rural road network by 
upgradation69 of selected Through Routes 70 and some Major Rural Links (MRLs) was 
felt and accordingly a new intervention was evolved in May 2013 namely PMGSY-II. 
PMGSY–II envisages consolidation of the existing Rural Road Network by covering 
upgradation of selected rural roads based on their economic potential and their role in 
facilitating the growth of rural market centres and rural hubs.
National Rural Infrastructure Development Agency (NRIDA) was registered (January 
2002) as a society for extending technical and management support to the GoI in 
implementation of PMGSY . 
66 
67 
68  An All Weather Road is one which is negotiable during all weathers, with some permitted interruptions. 
Essentially this means that at cross-drainage structures, the duration of overflow or interruption at 
one stretch shall not exceed 12 hours for ODRs and 24 hours for VRs in hilly terrain, and 3 days in 
the case of roads in plain terrain. The total period of interruption during the year should not exceed 
10 days for ODRs and 15 days for VRs. The pavement should be negotiable during all-weathers, but 
this does not necessarily imply that it should be paved or sealed or blacktopped.
69 Upgradation  shall cover the improvement of surface drainage and constructing all missing cross-
drainage works to make the road all-weather, improvement the road pavement to standards required 
for the traffic, subject to condition and road geometrics to prescribed standards and improving road 
safety.
70 Through Routes  are the roads which collect traffic from several link roads or a long chain of 
Habitations and lead it to Marketing centres either directly or through the higher category roads i.e., 
the District Roads or the State or National Highway.

usually headed by a Superintending Engineer/ Executive Engineer.

3.3.3 Organisational set-up
The Ministry of Rural Development (MoRD), GOI has been entrusted with the task 
of organising the PMGSY programme at the National Level. The Rural Development 
Department (RDD) was the State Level Nodal Department with overall responsibility for 
implementation of PMGSY . The State Government created (August 2003) a state level 
autonomous agency - the State Rural Roads Development Agency (SRRDA) headed by 
Chief Executive Officer cum Secretary, RDD as the Nodal Officer. 
The Principal Chief Engineer, RDD as the Chief Empowered Officer of the scheme 
was supported by two Chief Engineers, Addl. Chief Engineers, Director of Accounts, 
Superintending Engineers, Divisional Engineers and Assistant Engineers and other 
subordinate staff. State Level Standing Committee 71 (SLSC) is headed by the Chief 
Secretary and comprised  the Additional Chief Secretary, Planning and Development 
Department; Secretary, RDD; Secretary, Roads and Bridges Department. The 
organisational set up of SRRDA is as given below:
Chart 3.3: Organisational structure of SRRDA

to the NRIDA for clearance by MoRD.

3.3.8 Planning  
The process of s election and execution of road  works under PMGSY is detailed in 
Chart 3.4. 
Chart 3.4 – Planning process under PMGSY I and II 
Chart 3.4 shows the process of selection of roads for execution under PMGSY I, where initially 
DRRP is prepared. From DRRP, Core Network and lastly CNCPL is prepared out of the Core 
Network before final selection of roads for execution. Further, A Comprehensive Upgradation 
cum Consolidation Priority List (CUCPL) is prepared out of DRRP which will act as the basis 
of all upgradation proposals under PMGSY II scheme. A separate DRRP has to be prepared by 
DRRP
•The District Rural Roads Plan (DRRP) is a long-term plan of road network in a district,
showing the existing road network, the habitations of various population size and roads
proposed for connecting the yet unconnected habitations to already connected
habitations/ all-weather roads in an economic and efficient way in terms of cost and
utility. It is also known as the Master Plan for Rural Roads for the district.
Core network
•Core Network (CN) : It directly flows from the DRRP. The rural road network
required for providing the ‘basic access’ to all villages/ habitations is termed as the
Core Network. Basic access is defined as one all-weather road access from each
village/ habitation to the nearby Market Centre or Rural Business Hub (RBH) and
essential social and economic services. A Core Network comprises Through Routes
and Link Routes. The Core Network is the only basis for selection of works under
PMGSY.
CNCPL
•Comprehensive New Connectivity Priority List (CNCPL) : Once the Core Network is
ready, the States are required to prepare CNCPL at Block and District level, of all
proposed road links under PMGSY (with road code, names of habitations being
connected with habitations code, population served and length). For a given Order of
Priority and a given planning unit, the listing can be on the basis of total population
benefited.
CUCPL
(PMGSY-II)
•Comprehensive Upgradation cum Consolidation Priority List (CUCPL)-For
upgradation of selected Thorough Routes and major rural road, PMGSY–II was
formulated to cover upgradation of existing selected rural roads based on their
economic potential and their role in facilitating the growth of rural market centres and
rural hubs. A CUCPL was to be prepared out of DRRP which will act as the basis of all
upgradation proposals.

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
86
Chart	3.5:	Process	flow	of	selection	and	approval	of	PMGSY	Roads
In the case of Hill States, PMGSY I works are to be executed in two stages; 
Stage I-consisting of formation cutting 73, slope stabilisation 74, protective works and 
drainage works and Stage II-consisting of Water Bound Macadam (WBM) layers and 
bituminous surface (Black topping) course. The second stage was to be taken up after 
two rainy seasons have elapsed to ensure adequate stabilisation of the side slopes. 
Audit observed shortcomings in the planning process as discussed in the succeeding 
paragraphs.
3.3.8.1 Selection of road projects
PMGSY Operations Manual vide Paragraph 3.1.2 stipulates that District Rural Roads 
Plan (DRRP) shall be prepared as compendium of the existing and proposed road 
network system in the District. It forms the basis for selection of road works under 
PMGSY . Further, Paragraph 4.6 of PMGSY guidelines states that no road work may be 
proposed under the PMGSY for New connectivity or Upgradation (where permitted) 
unless it forms part of the Core Network.  Furthermore, Paragraph 6.4 of the guidelines 
envisaged that the Comprehensive New Construction Priority List (CNCPL) shall be the 
basis of all new connectivity under PMGSY-I works.  It is thus clear that if any work is 
to be executed under PMGSY-I, it has to be included in all the above three lists DRRP, 
Core Network and CNCPL in hierarchy.
73  Formation Cutting: Earth work in excavation in ordinary soil soft rock and hard rock.
74  Slope stabilization: Earth work in embankment with suitable materials including protection of slopes.
Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023 
92 
SSRDA for the purpose of PMGSY II, which should clearly demarcate through routes and link 
routes. However, SRRDA had not prepared DRRP for PMGSY II as stipulated in guidelines.  
The process flow of selection and approval of PMGSY roads is shown in Chart 3.5. 
Chart 3.5: Process flow of selection and approval of PMGSY Roads 
 
In the case of Hill States, PMGSY I works are to be executed in two stages ; Stage I-
consisting of formation cutting73, slope stabili sation74, protective works and drainage 
works and Stage II-consisting of Water Bound Macadam (WBM) layers and bituminous 
surface (Black topping) course. The second stage was to be taken up after two rainy 
seasons have elapsed to ensure adequate stabilisation of the side slopes.  
Audit observed shortcomings in the planning process as discussed in the succeeding 
paragraphs.

Plan (DRRP) shall be prepared as compendium of the existing and proposed road 
network system in the District. It forms the basis for selection of road works under

Scrutiny of DRRP, Core Network and CNCPL showed that eight road works valuing 
₹ 68.64 crore were not part of either of the three lists {DRRP (one work75), Core Network 
(one work76) and CNCPL (six works 77)}, however, these road works were executed by 
SRRDA in violation of the scheme guidelines.
On this being pointed out, RDD stated that one road was approved and sanctioned by 
MoRD and the road is included in the Core Network.   Regarding the non-inclusion of 
six roads in CNCPL, the RDD stated that these roads were considered for the sanction 
by NRIDA, MoRD on recommendations of the State Government despite not being 
included in the CNCPL.
Reply of the RDD validates the audit contention that the projects were proposed in 
violation of Paragraph 4.6 of the Scheme Guidelines. 
Thus, execution of above eight works by SRRDA in violation of PMGSY guidelines 
resulted in unwarranted execution of ineligible road works valuing ₹ 68.64 crore, despite 
having targeted and identified road works to unconnected habitations under priority list 
i.e., CNCPL awaiting to be executed. This indicated misplaced priority of works by 
SRRDA.
3.3.8.2 Connecting ineligible habitations in deviation of scheme objectives
The primary objective of the PMGSY is to provide connectivity by way of an all-weather 
road to the eligible unconnected habitation in the rural area with a population of 500 and 
above (Census 2001) in plain area and 250 in a special category state including Sikkim.
Roads, and Bridges Department (RBD) is the primary Department responsible for 
construction of roads. Alongside, Rural Development Department (RDD) is responsible 
for the implementation of PMGSY scheme.
As on 31  March 2023, the connectivity status of habitations since beginning of the 
scheme is given in Table 3.5.
Table 3.5: Status of connectivity of eligible habitations under the scheme

proposal as stipulated in PMGSY guidelines. The details are as follows:

Despite having 548 eligible unconnected habitations, the SRRDA had taken up three 
road works valuing ₹ 25.25 crore which covered ineligible habitations, which in turn 
is indicative of planning without adhering to the PMGSY guidelines and consequent 
inability to provide connectivity to eligible habitations on priority basis.
On this being pointed out, RDD stated (March 2024) that these are cases of cluster 
habitations. According to PMGSY guidelines, initially unconnected habitations having 
populations 1000 or more are selected for covering under the scheme, thereafter, 
unconnected habitations having population with 500 or more are selected. After that 
unconnected habitations having population of 250 or more are selected. Thereafter, two 
or more habitations (cluster habitations) put together having population of 250 or more 
are selected.
The justification provided by the Department is not acceptable as CNCPL data does 
not include road works regarding any cluster habitation amongst the unconnected 
habitations, as stated in the reply.
With 548 unconnected habitations having population more than 250 lacking connectivity, 
expenditure on ineligible roads not only violated the scheme guidelines but also affected 
the objectives of the scheme. 
3.3.8.3 Deficiencies in preparation of Detailed Project Reports
Paragraph 8.4 of the Scheme Guidelines, 2015 provided that at the time of preparation 
of Detailed Project Report (DPR), PIU should organise “Transect Walk”, along the 
alignment, together with the Panchayat Pradhan/ Ward, local revenue and forest officials, 
wherein issues relating to alignments, land and impact on environment and landowners 
shall be discussed. At the end of the walk, alignment shall be finalised after recording 
the issues. Copy of these minutes along with digital photographs of transect walk must 
be attached to the finalised DPR.
Paragraph 5.3.1 of PMGSY operation Manual stipulates that reconnaissance starts with a 
field inspection by walking, riding on ponies (in hills) or driving in jeeps. All information 
of value, either in design, construction, maintenance or operation of the facility should 

Chapter III: Economic Sector
89
be collected, which may include details of route, requirements of cross-drainage works, 
villages, hamlets and market centres connected, economic factors – population served, 
agricultural and economic potential of the area etc.
The estimates/DPR prepared after consideration of the above, shall reflect the true scope, 
quantum and cost of works, based on detailed surveys and investigations. SRRDA had 
prepared estimates for PMGSY roads/bridges.
In this regard, it was observed that:
	The Department had no records to indicate that transect walk was organised. Hence, 
deviations from DPRs were seen while implementing the projects such as execution 
of multiple branches as mentioned above, abandonment of execution due to  
non-availability of land (Paragraph 3.3.10.2). 
	SRRDA had not conducted preliminary survey and investigation as prescribed in 
operation manual and prepared DPR in an adhoc manner. In one road work “Upper 
Lingi 9th mile turning to Lingding”, SRRDA had not provided cross drainage 
structures due to which entire formation was washed out (Paragraph 3.3.10.3). 
Another work “40 mtr. Bridge construction work on Bhusuk Khola” was constructed 
at the end of the road and there was no road connectivity on one side and no sizable 
population at the other side of the bridge (Paragraph 3.3.10.4).
	Scrutiny of records as well as physical verification of work sites showed that out 
of 46 sampled PMGSY-I roads, 21 roads were executed in such a manner that part 
of the road works were executed at a different location as against the original work 
site sanctioned by NRIDA (Appendix 3.2). Further, audit noticed that the deviations 
were done without the required approval of NRIDA. An illustrative example of 
deviation in alignment is shown in the image below:
Image 3.1: Illustration of revised alignment of road Rumtek to Arigaon with 600 meter branch road

revised the alignment and constructed one additional branch having length of

PMGSY I, connectivity to the targeted habitations was achieved without exhausting 
sanctioned length of roads and remaining length was executed out of the savings, at 
other habitations which were already connected by another road. Due to 
non-conduct of proper planning exercise, construction of roads to the habitation 
already connected was taken up which was avoidable and in violation to scheme

Thus, it is evident from the above instances that mandated survey and investigation were

roads/ multiple branches/diversion /change in alignment of works during execution.

unconnected habitations as discussed in Paragraph 3.3.9.1.
The RDD while accepting the fact, stated (March 2024) that there are deviations of the 
road alignment from the original alignment in 21 roads due to various reasons such 
as huge time gap between initial survey and execution, land acquisition issues and 
recommendations received from public representative for connectivity to new habitation 
established. Further, RDD added that the road cannot be taken through the original 
alignment. In such cases, there are changes in the road alignment leading to decrease

91
or increase in the road length. However, it is ensured that the targeted habitation is 
invariably connected.
The reply of the RDD is not acceptable as original estimates and sanction accorded to 
the works were defective as it was not based on actual survey and investigation to be 
carried out at the initial stage. Such deviation from the original Plan/DPR was carried out 
without the approval of NRIDA in violation of guidelines. Further, as per guidelines, the 
view of public representatives should have obtained before finalization of the CNCPL. 
3.3.9 Financial Management  
SRRDA maintains three separate accounts for meeting Programme cost, administrative 
expenses and Maintenance cost as detailed below:
	Programme Account: The expenditure related to construction of new connectivity 
and upgradation work are met from this account.
	Administrative Account : The administrative and travel expenses of PIUs and 
SRRDA are met from the fund released to State as Administrative Expenses.
	Maintenance Account: This account was used for funding maintenance of PMGSY 
roads and was budgeted by the State Government solely. During the period covered 
in audit, State Government received ₹ 1,126.46 crore from GoI out of which, the 
State Government released ₹ 968.57 crore to SRRDA and kept the remaining 
funds of ₹ 157.89 crore in government accounts. Funds available and expenditure 
of Pradhan Mantri Gram Sadak Yojana (PMGSY) are given below:

3.3.9.1 Delay in transfer of funds by State Government
As per the programme guidelines, funds are released to the Consolidated Fund of the 
State with the stipulation to transfer the fund to SRRDA’s account within three days 
from the date of its receipt failing which the State government is liable to pay interest at 
12 per cent per annum for the period of delay beyond the specified period. 
Audit observed that funds released during audit period were transferred to SRRDA with 
delays ranging from 3 to 61 days. As a result, the State Government was liable to pay 
interest of ₹ 2.83 crore to SRRDA as detailed in Table 3.9.
Table 3.9: Interest on delay transfer of fund by State Govt.

Thus, due to non-release of funds within the stipulated time period, the State Government

3.3.9.2 Non-recovery of Liquidated damage 
Clause 44 of Standard Biding Document (SBD) stipulates that one per cent of the initial 
contract price per week would be the amount of Liquidated Damages (LDs) for delay in 
completion subject to a maximum of 10 per cent of the initial contract price.
Examination of records revealed that 37 delayed works were under execution during 
audit period which were sanctioned during 2007-08 to 2021-22. In all these works, RDD 
had attributed the delay to the contractors and had issued notices to them from time to 
time. However, LD at the prescribed rate amounting to ₹ 17.21 crore had not been levied 
on the contractors by invoking Clause 44 of the SBD.

Chapter III: Economic Sector
93
Thus, RDD provided undue financial benefit of ₹ 17.21 crore to the contractors and 
could not get the works completed in a time bound manner by using LD as a deterrent.
The RDD stated (March 2024) that the State witnesses heavy and prolonged monsoon 
in addition to severe winter in some areas so limited working season is available for 
execution of the work leading to delay in completion of the works.  It was further, 
stated that the works were also delayed for want of timely release of State Share, Forest 
clearance issues, non-release of land compensation. Since in most cases the delay in 
work was caused due to the above reasons and not due to the contractor’s lapses the 
liquidation damages were not imposed to the contractors. 
The RDD contention that the delays were not attributable to the contractors, is 
contradictory to the reasons for delay, as mentioned in notices issued to the contractors.
3.3.9.3 Status of equipment advances granted to contractors
Clause 45 of Standard Biding Document stipulates that on the request of the Contractor, 
the Employer will make the following advance payment to the Contractor against 
submission by the Contractor of an Unconditional Bank Guarantee from a scheduled 
Commercial bank acceptable to the Employer in amounts equal to 110 per cent of the 
amount of the advance payment being requested: 
a. Mobilisation advances up to five per cent of the initial contract price excluding the 
contract price for routine maintenance 
b. Equipment Advance up to 90 per cent of the cost of the new equipment brought to 
the site, subject to a maximum of 10 per cent of the initial contract price excluding 
the contract price for routine maintenance
The Bank Guarantee shall remain effective until the advance payment has been repaid, 
but the amount of the guarantee shall be progressively reduced by the amounts repaid 
by the contractors. Interest will not be charged on the advance payment. However, if the 
Contract is terminated due to default of the Contractor, the Mobilisation Advance and 
the Equipment Advance shall be deemed to be an interest bearing advance at the base 
rate of the State Bank of India, from the date of such advance payment. 
Further, the Contractor is to use the advance payment only to pay for Equipment, 
plant and Mobilisation expenses required specifically for execution of the Works and 
the contractor shall supply copies of invoices or other documents to the Engineer. The 
details of mobilisation and equipment advances are as follows:
Table 3.10: Details of outstanding advances
(₹ in crore)
Particulars Works Balance Total
Mobilisation advance Equipment advance
Up to Phase XI 41 1.89 3.80 5.69
Phase XII U 54 4.81 9.62 14.43
PMGSY II 6 0.21 0.59 0.80
Bridges 9 0.38 0.76 1.14
110 7.29 14.77 22.06
Source: SRRDA information
Scrutiny of records showed that SRRDA made advances of ₹14.77 crore towards 
procurement of new equipment for use in the works. 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
94
While scrutinising work files and related records, Audit noticed that the contractors did 
not provide copies of invoices or other documents to the Engineer as stipulated in the 
agreement nor did SRRDA pursue the matter with the contractor to submit the same. 
Thus, audit could not draw any assurance that the advances were used for the purpose 
for which they were requested by the contractors and that the equipment were brought 
to the worksite.
On this being pointed out, RDD stated (March 2024) that after drawing equipment/ 
machinery advances, the contractor have to bring the required machineries to the work 
site without which work cannot be executed. The PIU and field engineer ensures that the 
required machineries are present at site.
The reply is not acceptable as neither were details regarding procurement of new 
equipment available in records produced to Audit nor could the RDD provide any 
documentary evidence i.e bills in support of the reply.
3.3.10 Implementation of the scheme
After the clearance of the project proposal and technical sanction, the executing agency 
invites tenders for construction and maintenance of roads following the Standard Bidding 
Documents (SBD) prescribed by NRIDA. Issues noticed in the implementation of the 
scheme viz., delay in execution, deficiency in execution, lack of maintenance, etc. are 
discussed in the following paragraphs.
3.3.10.1 Delay in execution of works
According to Para 13.1(iii) of PMGSY guidelines, all works have to be completed within 
18 months from the date of award.
Audit also observed that, during 2017-2023, 275 works (excluding maintenance and 
PMGSY-II) were in execution which were sanctioned during 2007-08 to 2021-22, out of 
which four works were completed in time, and remaining works exceeded the scheduled 
completion date. During the audit period (2017-2023), 87 new road works (2017-18) and 
58 bridges were sanctioned. The completion timeline for the last set of works sanctioned 
in April 2021 was fixed as March 2022.  The age wise analysis of delayed works is 
provided in the table below:
Table 3.11: Age wise analysis of the delay in completion of works
Sl. No. Delayed period Number of works
1 Up to 1 year 38
2 1-5 year 205
3 5-10 year 25
4 Above 10 year 3
271
Source: SRRDA information
Further, none of the 55 sampled works were completed within the scheduled date of 
completion. It was noticed that 15 works were delayed due to the Contractor’s fault, 
another three works could not be completed in time due to delay in obtaining forest 
clearance, six works were delayed due to non-execution of the Stage I works within 
scheduled time, two works due to Covid-19 and four works were delayed due to land 
acquisition issues.

Chapter III: Economic Sector
95
It was furt her observed that the delay occurred due to non-adherence to scheme 
guidelines, non-acquisition of land, prior forest clearances, lack of monitoring of the 
progress of the work and ineffective contract management. Further, PIUs had also not 
monitored the progress of the work and extension was allowed to the contractor on 
routine basis without any justified reason. It was also observed that no contractor was 
penalised for delay in completion of the above 15 works where works were delayed due 
to contractors fault and extension was granted to the contractor without any justification. 
Cases of delayed completion of two sampled works/projects are elucidated below:
	RCR from Lower Nandok to Lower Syari and 5 other works:  The package 
was awarded (January 2017) with scheduled date of completion in July 2018, to 
contractor Shri N.B. Dahal  with contract value of ₹ 22.52 crore (8.21 per cent  
below). Accordingly, Mobilisation Advance of ₹ 3.10 crore was released (January 
2017) for entire package of six works.
 SRRDA issued letter (18 March 2017) to the contractor to proceed with the work 
and to complete the work within the scheduled date. However, due to inordinate 
delay by the contractor, a final notice was served for delay in progress of work on 
04 March 2019. Audit found in Joint Physical verification (January 2023) that the 
work was still ongoing even after lapse of three and a half years from the scheduled 
date of completion i.e., March 2019.
	Timbong to Nung in North Sikkim (Stage-II) -The road work was awarded 
(October 2014) to a contractor for ₹ 266.71 lakh for sanctioned length of 3.26  km. 
The 3rd RA bill was paid in March 2020 with a financial progress of ₹ 215.58 lakh 
leaving an unspent balance of ₹ 51.13 lakh. After March 2020, the work was stalled 
due to unknown reasons. Joint Physical Verification of the road (January 2023) 
showed that the road formation of entire length had completely deteriorated, and all 
layers need to be reconstructed along with compaction. It was further noticed that 
only 300 mtrs road was carpeted. The pavement layers, culvert/CDs were also in 
deplorable condition as can be seen in Image below:
Image 3.2 & 3.3: Deteriorated condition of road “Timbong to Nung”

to contractors fault and extension was granted to the contractor without any 
justification.

➢ RCR from Lower Nandok to Lower Syari and 5 other works:  The package 
was awarded (January 2017) with scheduled date of completion in July 2018, to

from the scheduled date of completion i.e., March 2019. 
➢ Timbong to Nung in North Sikkim (Stage -II)-The road work was awarded 
(October 2014) to a contractor for ₹ 266.71 lakh for sanctioned length of 
3.26 km. The 3rd RA bill was paid in March 2020 with a financial progress of 
₹ 215.58 lakh leaving an unspent balance of ₹ 51.13 lakh. After March 2020, 
the work was stalled due to unknown reasons.  Joint Physical Verification of the 
road (January 2023) showed that the road formation of entire length had 
completely deteriorated, and all layers need to be reconstructed along with 
compaction. It was further noticed that only 300 mtrs road was carpeted. The 
pavement layers, culvert/CDs were also in deplorable condition as can be seen 
in Image below: 
Image 3.2 & 3.3: Deteriorated condition of road “Timbong to Nung”

Thus, due to lack of proper monitoring and supervision by SRRDA, the works were 
inordinately delayed which defeated the scheme objectives to connect the unconnected 
habitations within 18 months from award of the work.  
While accepting the audit contention, the RDD stated (March 2024) that 
(i) The work “RCR from Lower Nandok to Lower Syari and five other works” were 
initially delayed due to non -availability of land, forest clearance issues and 
landslides along various stretches of the road. However, now all the works are 
physically completed.

(ii) The work “Tingvong to Nung in North Sikkim” was delayed due to massive landslide

rise in the water level, and totally cutting off the road network of many villages 
including Tingvong. Due to the landslide and damages to the area, the vehicular 
road connectivity was cut off for almost four years.
The reply of the RDD was factually incorrect as in case of road “Lower Nandok to 
Lower Syari and five other works”, the work was delayed due to lapses by the contractor 
as stated in the RDD’s records itself and there were no records available to indicate that 
delay was due to forest clearance and land issues. 
With regard to road work “Tingvong to Nung in North Sikkim”, the due date of completion 
was April 2016; as such, the landslide in August 2016 cannot be the reason for delayed 
execution of the work.
3.3.10.2 Infructuous expenditure of ₹ 0.52 crore due to non-execution of Stage-II
Para 4.3.5 of PMGSY Operations Manual states that as work commences on preparation 
of DPRs, the PIUs will conduct the Transect Walk and prepare necessary papers to 
obtain land, forest clearance etc. so that before road work is put to tender, the land for 
the road is available. It is the responsibility of the State Government/ District Panchayat 
to oversee that land is available for taking up the proposed road works. A certificate 
that land is available, must accompany the proposal for each road work. It must be 
noted that PMGSY does not provide funds for acquisition of land. The details of land 
made available should be reflected in the local land records to avoid dispute. As far as 
possible, voluntary donations should be documented through agreements, copy of which 
should be forwarded to the local revenue officials for making necessary changes in the 
ownership/ possession record of the land.
Audit observed that in three out of 46 sampled works under PMGSY I, SRRDA had 
not taken possession of land prior to execution of the work. SRRDA started the work 
after obtaining NOC for acquisition of their land from landowners for roads. However, 
the landowners refused to part with their land during execution of work due to various 
reasons including non-payment of land compensation. As a result, SRRDA was forced 
to stall the work which ultimately resulted in delay/ abonnement of the works. It was 
noticed that execution of 580 metres of Stage II works was abandoned due to land 
dispute during execution of work as detailed below:
