was factually incorrect as the JPV conducted by audit along with PIUs engineers showed 
that the work was completed without achieving last mile connectivity.
	Non-monitoring of the land acquisition process
Audit noticed that RDD spent an amount of ₹ 12.19 crore during 2017 to 2022 towards 
land compensation and crop compensation.
Scrutiny of records showed that land compensation valuing ₹ 46.87 crore relating to 
184 works was pending for payment as on March 2023. However, the SRRDA did not 
maintain any details regarding total land acquired for the scheme, status of ownership/ 
transfer of ownership of the acquired land, granular details of how much compensation 
was paid or was pending in respect of each individual, etc. indicating laxity on the part 
of the SRRDA regarding the crucial aspect of Land acquisition.
While accepting the audit observation, the RDD stated (March 2024) that it is compiling 
data in regard to total land acquired from the individual, data for standing properties, 
house damages, etc.  The data of compensation which has been paid is also being 
compiled. Accordingly, letters will be issued to the Land & Revenue Department for 
transfer of land ownership from the land owner to the RDD. With regard to 184 numbers 
of land acquisition cases, the State Government has now constituted a committee which 
shall shortly recommend method to verify and proceed for settlement of compensation 
issues. Further, RDD stated that the relevant documents regarding land compensation 
and acquisition is with the concerned Subdivision Magistrate Office and the Land & 
Revenue Department.
3.3.10.3 Absence of All-weather Road connectivity due to damages to the roads
According to Para 3.1 of PMGSY scheme guidelines “the spirit and the objective of the 
PMGSY is to provide good all weather road connectivity to the eligible unconnected 
habitations”. Further, as per PMGSY scheme guidelines para 8.5 (VI b), the habitation 
concerned will not be counted as “connected” till the second stage is taken up. 
The Indian Road Congress (IRC) Hill Roads Manual stipulates the following: The water 
courses and high intensity of rainfall calls for effective drainage of roads which causes 
problems like soft surfaces, potholes, rutting, washout shoulders and even failure of 
complete section of roadway structures. Prevention of such failures requires good degree 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
98
of drainage (Clause 8.1.1).  Clause 8.10.1 ibid stipulates that whenever streams have to 
cross the road, cross drainage (CD) structures have to be provided. 
Further, clause 9.3.1 ibid stipulates that the masonry or RCC structures supporting the 
up hill slopes along a road are termed as Breast Wall for protection against instability at 
places from failing by slumping, sliding, toe failure.
Thus, work component regarding the pavement formation along with provision of cross 
drainage structure and protective wall should be suitably incorporated in the DPR by 
concerned PIUs based on site requirement of the rural roads to achieve the objective 
of PMGSY . Further, in ongoing works, routine maintenance work such as clearance of 
drainage should have also been carried out regularly.
Scrutiny of records and JPV of four out of 55 sampled roads (PMGSY I-46 and PMGSY 
II-9) revealed that these roads were not accessible till the last mile  and the targeted 
habitations remained unconnected even after incurring expenditure amounting to ₹ 28.66 
crore due to various reasons as detailed below:

This indicated that site survey/planning for inclusion of drainage provision, cross 
drainage structure and retaining wall/breast wall was not provided in the DPR as per site 
requirement and as a result, objective of the PMGSY to provide good all weather road 
connectivity to the eligible unconnected habitations was affected.
In reply, the RDD stated (March 2024) that due to falling in Zone IV of the Seismic 
Zonation Map of India the state is subjected to heavy rainfall, flash floods and frequent 
earthquakes, throughout the year. This factor leads to landslides, land subsidence and in 
absence of immediate restoration due to lack of fund the damages are more pronounced 
on the passage of time. The damages are due to the forces of nature which cannot be pre-
determined or anticipated and hence provision for same cannot be kept while framing the 
DPR. Further, RDD stated that construction and requirement of bridges along PMGSY 
roads are proposed on conducting detailed survey and investigation with regard to the 
catchment areas, vulnerability of the area etc. During the inspection of the audit team, 
the damages observed were due to the monsoon which now has been restored. 
The reply of the RDD is not tenable as roads were damaged due to absence of regular 
maintenance and non-providing of adequate protective works. Further, in case of 
“Lower Lamaten PWD road to Upper Lamaten via Sotakpani”, it was observed that the 
road formation was washed out due to choking of drain and not caused by any natural 
calamity. In case of “ Chuzachen to Lower Lamaten via Gangyap School (PMGSY 
II)”, the road was damaged due to insufficient protective work provided in the Roads.  
“Upper Mamley Chamgaon to Doling SPWD Road” was damaged due to non-inclusion 
of necessary retaining wall as per cite condition. The road “Upper Lingi 9th Mile Turning 
to Lingding” was damaged due to non-providing of necessary cross drainage structure.
Thus, SRRDA had not taken adequate measures like inclusion of cross drainage 
structure, protective works, cleaning of drain to avoid damages and further, SRRDA 
had not initiated necessary action to repair the damaged roads resulting in affecting 
connectivity to the targeted habitations.
3.3.10.4 Unwarranted expenditure on bridge construction
The objective of PMGSY is to provide connectivity, by way of an ‘all-weather road’ to 
the eligible unconnected habitations (Census 2001) in the rural areas with a population of 
500 persons and above in plain areas and 250 persons in hilly areas alongwith necessary 
bridges/culvert.
While scrutinising the bridge works taken up under the PMGSY scheme, audit observed 
that the 40-meter bridge construction work on “ Bhusuk Khola” was sanctioned during 
2017-18 at a cost of ₹ 3.06 crore. The work was awarded (June 2018) to contractor and 
completed within March 2022. 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
100
Scrutiny of records and JPV revealed that the bridge was constructed at the end of PMGSY 
road “Lower Syari to Lower Nandok”. The road ends at the bank of river (Bhusuk Khola) 
and there was no road connectivity on other side of the river, however, SRRDA had 
constructed bridge at the end of the road which was not justified as shown below:
Image 3.8: Bhusuk Khola Bridge
Bhusuk Khola bridge Road ending at dead end.
In reply, the RDD stated (March 2024) that prior to preparation of DPR, mandatory 
transect walk was carried out by the field Engineers, land revenue officers, office bearers 
at the village level as alignment of the road and location of the bridge is fixed after due 
consultation with the area MLA, Panchayats, and the villagers. 
Regarding the bridge constructed at the end of PMGSY road “Lower Syari to Lower 
Nandok”, it was constructed to connect the above road from Lower Nandok to the 
villages located in the opposite bank where there was also a proposal to construct a 
Sewage Treatment Plant. The bridge now serves as connectivity to a sizable population 
and is now connected by a vehicular road.
The reply is not tenable as PMGSY guidelines stipulate providing connectivity to rural 
areas with a population of 500 persons and above in plain area and 250 persons in 
hilly areas. Further, there is no provision in the guidelines to provide connectivity to 
a Sewage Treatment Plant. Subsequently, physical verification of the site (April 2024) 
revealed that no road was constructed at the other side of the bridge and RDD could not 
furnish details regarding habitation on the other side. This led to idling of expenditure 
to the tune of ₹ 3.06 crore.

eligible habitations. The implementing agency was to decide the length of the eligible 
road after survey of habitation.

out of 55 sampled roads (PMGSY I - 46 and PMGSY-II-9), it was noticed that the road 
length of 1570 meter in four roads was lesser than sanctioned length as detailed below. 
However, SRRDA had paid contractor for the full sanctioned length.

financially completed. However, due to the highway construction by NHIDCL, 
total of 600 m of completed road has been overlapped by National Highways and 
Infrastructure Development Corporation Limited (NHIDCL). Letters with regard to 
the damage compensation etc., has been issued to NHIDCL and on receipt of such 
compensation, the same shall be surrendered to the Ministry.
(d)  Regarding the work “RCR from Geyzing to Lingchom (6.72 km)” the works are 
ongoing and the field engineers are trying to achieve full length. The final bill will 
be released as per the detailed measurement. 
The contention of the Department about saving in the length of the roads being 
surrendered to the Ministry and inaccuracy in the measurement of length of the road 
using the vehicle odometer are not acceptable as despite reduction in length, payment 
had been made for the entire sanctioned length and the vehicle odometer reading was 
accepted by the Departmental officer during JPV . Further, while conducting physical 
verification of the work “ Geyzing Lingchom 2nd KM to Lower Bongten” - the road was 
found carpeted for 6.4 km only and the overlapping of 600 metre road by NHIDCL is a 
later development.

3.3.10.6 Execution of roads with deficient width
Paragraph 10.8.1 of IRC Hill Roads Manual provides the width of carriage width 79 and 
specifies that the pavement width will be arrived at based on the traffic census i.e for 
single lane- 3.75 meter, Intermediate lane- 5.5 meter and for double lane- 7 meter. 
SRRDA prepared the estimates of road works with carriage way of 3.75 meter in DPR 
and the roads were sanctioned by the Ministry accordingly.
Scrutiny of records and physical verification of 45 road works out of 55 sampled roads 
(PMGSY I-46 and PMGSY-II-9) revealed that in case of six road works, carriage width 
of the roads was found less than 3.75 meter as detailed below:

However, payment to contractors was made with carriage width of 3.75 mtr which 
indicated that payment was made without proper measurement by the field engineers.
Reduced carriage width, in effect means, reduced width for plying of vehicles. Also, 
this increases the risk of accidents, especially in hill roads. Execution of road work with 
lesser width resulted in undue favour to contractor to that extent. 
This indicates deficiency in monitoring mechanism on the part of the PIUs/SRRDA as 
well as extending undue benefit to the contractors. 
In reply, the RDD stated (March 2024) stated that the carriageway width of 3.75 mtr as 
specified by the PMGSY guidelines has been maintained. Nominal difference observed 
is due to the scouring at edges during heavy rainfall. 
Regarding the work “ Geyzing to Lingchom road”, it was taken over by PMGSY from 
Sikkim Public Work Department (SPWD) in a deteriorated condition. At places where 
sub-base and base were in good condition, only WBM80-III was provided. Widening has 
been carried out at most of the places where widening can be possible. The works are 
ongoing, and the field engineers are still trying to achieve full width wherever possible. 
The final bill will be released as per the detailed measurement of the existing length and 
width achieved.
The reply is not factually correct as the measurement was carried out during Physical 
Verification in the presence of concerned engineers and it was found that the width of 
the road at the site where measurement was taken was not as per the laid down norms 
however, payment was made to the contractor as per sanctioned width i.e 3.75 meter. 
Further, scouring by rainfall in the edges was also not noticed during JPV .
79 Carriage width-A portion of the road that allowed movement of traffic without the shoulder. It is also 
known as width of the pavement. In all weather road, carriage width defines the black top area of the 
road.

3.3.10.7 Deficiency in overall thickness of road works
Paragraph 10.4.1 of Hill Roads Manual read with Paragraph 10.1.2 ibid stipulates design 
of pavements which requires considerations of wheel load of vehicle, traffic, climate, 
terrain, sub grade condition etc. Further, para 10.15.3 ibid stipulates that the thickness of 
layers should be minimum 250 mm (Sub-base-100 mm and base-100 mm).
SRRDA prepared DPR after analysis of various factors provided by Hill Roads Manual 
and guidelines and work was awarded to different contractors. 
Scrutiny of records of SRRDA and JPV revealed that in case of four out of 55 rural 
roads, thickness of roads was less than required thickness specified in DPRs/estimate 
which ranged between 25 mm and 125 mm as detailed in table below:

ensure that the quality of work is not compromised in any way. Mandatory field tests are 
carried on during the inspection and in case of failure of the tests, the works are graded 
accordingly and until and unless the rectification works are carried out; no payments 
are made to the contractor. Field tests are conducted again in the same pit and also

randomly to verify the rectification work done and then only after the ATRs are accepted

all PMGSY works are inspected by the State Quality Monitors (SQM) and National 
Quality Monitors (NQM) on a regular basis to ensure that the quality of work is not 
compromised in any way. Mandatory field tests are carried on during the inspection and 
in case of failure of the tests, the works are graded accordingly and until and unless the 
rectification works are carried out; no payments are made to the contractor. Field tests 
are conducted again in the same pit and also randomly to verify the rectification work 
done and then only after the ATRs are accepted by NRIDA are the payments released. 
However, contrary to RDD’s reply, during joint physical verification in presence of 
SRRDA officers, it was found that the thickness of roads was less than the required 
thickness as specified in DPRs/estimate even though it was inspected by SQM and NQM.
Thus, lack of monitoring of the implementation of works by PIUs and lack of supervision 
by the field engineers not only resulted in substandard execution of PMGSY works but 
also in extending undue benefit to the contractors.
3.3.10.8 Improper execution of work
(i) Faulty construction of culvert/CDs
Clause 8.10.1 of the IRC stipulates that whenever streams have to cross the road, cross 
drainage (CD) structures have to be provided. Similarly, water from the side drain is 
taken across by this cross drain to divert the water away from the road to a water course 
or valley. Further, Para 4.3.1 of Operation Manual stipulates that in the case of Hill 
States, the estimates for new construction works may be prepared in two parts, the 
first stage will consist of formation cutting, slope stabilisation, protection works and 
drainage works. The second stage will include the WBM layers and bituminous surface 
course. The second stage may be taken up after two rainy seasons have elapsed to ensure 
adequate stabilisation of the side slopes. 
Audit noticed during JPV (January 2023) of one work, “Upper Mamley Chamgaon to 
Doling in South Sikkim”, that though Stage-II work was ongoing and the rural road was 
carpeted, the construction of culverts/CDs was being constructed beyond the carriage 
width of the road which would not serve any purpose. 
Image 3.10 & 3.11: Faulty culvert beyond carriage width
Culvert beyond carriage width at Upper Mamley 
Chamgaon to Doling
Culvert beyond carriage width at Upper Mamley 
Chamgaon to Doling
Thus, culvert/CD was being constructed after Stage-II works at the site. The alignment of 
culvert/CD was not designed properly leading to construction of CD/culvert outside the 
carriage width which did not serve any purpose and would result in wasteful expenditure.

Chapter III: Economic Sector
105
(ii)	 Inferior 	stock	material	utilised	without	Quality	Control	Certificate
Section 1000.5 of Quality Assurance Handbook for Rural Roads (equipment and 
test procedures) issued by NRIDA stipulates that all steel being used in the PMGSY 
works shall be subject to routine inspection and testing by manufacturer or supplier in 
accordance with this standard and shall be kept by manufacturer or the supplier. The 
record shall be available for inspection by the purchaser, or his representative and such 
certificate should be obtained by the quality control personnel. In case of any doubt, 
test may be performed at a standard laboratory. Further, the design and specification 
of culvert (a cross drainage structure up to 7 meter) /CDs should have been based on 
average traffic density to achieve the objective prescribed in the guidelines. 
Audit found that SRRDA neither ensured quality of the stock materials used at the site 
nor did they insist for quality certificate from the Contractors. 
During Joint Physical Verification in presence of SRRDA engineers, it was found that 
the culvert/CDs were constructed using TMT bars without ensuring quality of material 
in 12 cases as the quality certificate was not obtained. Audit noticed in seven cases out of 
65 Roads /bridge works that culvert/CDs were damaged/collapsed resulting in blockage/
hindrance in targeted connectivity in road/bridge work as detailed in Table 3.17 along 
with status of CD noticed during JPV in Image 3.12 to 3.15.

execution of sub-standard works but also hindered the connectivity to the targeted 
habitations.
In reply, the RDD stated (March 2024) that the culvert of “ Timbong to Nung, North 
Sikkim” had been choked due to the debris collected as a result of heavy rainfall. With 
regard to “ Lungchok to Ramthang Fague Tanek, North Sikkim” and “Upper Aho to 
Saramsa, East Sikkim”, RDD stated that the culverts were damaged due to the plying of 
heavily loaded trucks beyond the design load capacity. 
The reply of the SRRDA is not acceptable as it could not provide quality certificate for 
the stock material used at the site which was further corroborated by the JPV report. 
Thus, roads were constructed with inferior stock materials which coupled with poor 
maintenance led to choking of culverts/drains resulting in damage to the roads which 
affected the purpose of providing all weather road connectivity. 
3.3.10.9 Irregularities in upgradation work under PMGSY II
Para 3.6 of PMGSY-II guidelines stipulates that the roads selected under PMGSY-II 
are to be based on parameters such as: Growth Centre’s Rank and condition of the road 
in terms of Pavement Condition Index 81 (PCI) survey subject to requisite maintenance 
funds invested during the last three years on the road.
As per Hill Roads Manual (Paragraph 10.16.4), thickness of layers should be minimum 
250 mm (Sub-base-100 mm and base-150 mm) where commercial vehicles per day 
(CVD) ranged from 45 to 150 and California Bearing Ratio (CBR) is less than 20.
Further, Paragraph 10.8.1 of Hill Roads Manual provides the width of carriage way and 
therefore the pavement width will be arrived at based on the traffic census i.e., for single 
lane- 3.75 metre, Intermediate lane- 5.5 metre and for double lane- 7 metre.
Audit scrutiny of work “ Geyzing to Lingchom” (6.72 km) which is one out of nine 
sampled works under PMGSY II revealed the following observations.
	The work order was awarded (May 2019) to a contractor at a cost of ₹ 4.39 crore. 
As per detailed Project Report of the work, the CVD was 81 and CBR was 7.3 and 
the overall pavement design as well as carriage width was provided as per PMGSY 
guidelines.
81  A simple Pavement Condition Index based on visual inspection or driving speed or driving comfort 
would be used and prioritisation methodology applied for determining upgradation as well as 
maintenance priorities.

Chapter III: Economic Sector
107
	The sub-base course (100 mm) and WBM-II (75 mm) were provided for only one 
fourth of the total length and WBM-III (75 mm) was provided for full length against 
the requirement of 250 mm pavement for entire length. In addition, the SRRDA/
PIU should conduct the detailed existing road crust study along with PCI survey 
prior to preparation of estimates as envisaged in Hill Road Manual and scheme 
guidelines.
	However, audit found that SRRDA/PIU had not conducted PCI survey and existing 
crust study to ascertain the existing condition of the road such as width, pavement, 
drainage etc. 
	During JPV , it was observed that PIU provided inadequate pavement thickness in 
estimates of 75-100 mm only against the requirement of 250 mm due to which, the 
road condition was poor and in deteriorated condition. 
	The carriage width of the road test checked ranged between 3.35 meter to 3.75 meter 
at different stretches. 
	Further, it was also observed that SRRDA had executed the work limiting the scope 
to repair of the existing road instead of upgradation work as the hill side drain was 
not constructed as per specification of PMGSY . The box drain, which was earlier 
constructed by Roads and Bridges Department (RBD) was in dilapidated condition 
which resulted in water logging and damaged the pavement formation at numerous 
places. In addition, lesser carriage width against that prescribed at different stretches 
corroborates the fact that PIU had not taken up the work of widening of carriage 
width.
	The State Quality Monitor had also given unsatisfactory overall grading (March 
2021) in width and inadequate pavement, however, the SRRDA had not taken any 
follow up action in this regard. 
Had SRRDA conducted PCI survey of the road and DPR prepared accordingly prior to 
execution, the above deficiencies could have been avoided.
Thus, improper planning of work and deficiency in preparation of DPR by SRRDA in 
violation of scheme guidelines resulted in irregular expenditure as well as unfruitful 
expenditure of ₹ 4.39 crore. 
In reply RDD stated (March 2024) that the said road was taken over by PMGSY from 
RBD in a deteriorated condition. Since the carpeting was done earlier by RBD, the DPR 
for the base course was prepared as per the site condition. At places where sub-base 
and base were in good condition, only WBM-III was provided. The 1100 m stretch was 
provided with sub-base, WBM-II and WBM-III since it was severely damaged during 
handing taking. Widening has been carried out at most of the places where widening can 
be possible. The work is ongoing, and the field engineers are still trying to achieve full 
width wherever possible. The final bill will be released as per the detailed measurement 
of the existing length and width achieved.
The reply of RDD is not acceptable as during physical verification, carriage width of the 
road was found less than 3.75 meters at different stretches of the road already carpeted 
and even the roads as well as hill side drain were in deteriorated conditions at numerous 
places where work was executed. Further, the widening of the carriage width is also not 
possible at already completed stretches. Moreover, the pavement of the road had also 
deteriorated due to non-providing of WBM and GSB for required stretches.

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
108
3.3.10.10 Non-provision of insurance cover
According to Paragraph 9.3.1 of the Operations Manual and Clause 13.1 of General 
Conditions of Contract (GCC), the contractor at his cost shall provide all risk insurance 
cover in the joint name of the Employer and the Contractor on the amount of full contract 
price in the Contract Data. The insurance shall be effective from the start date, up to 
the date of actual completion of the work. The scope of the insurance shall cover fire, 
lightning, explosion, impact, flood, inundation, storm, earthquake, subsidence, landslide, 
theft, burglary, R&S and terrorist damage. Lack of insurance cover is a fundamental 
breach of the General Conditions of Contract (GCC), which entails termination of the 
contract agreement by the SRRDA. 
Further, clause 13.3(a) stipulates that  the Contractor at his cost shall also provide, in 
the joint names of the Employer and the Contractor, insurance cover from the date of 
completion to the end of Defects Liability Period, in the amounts and deductibles stated 
in the Contract Data for personal injury or death, which are due to the Contractor’s risks:
Audit scrutinised 113 sampled works (Roads-55, Bridges-24 and Maintenance-34) 
and found that the contractors did not provide the required insurance cover for work 
execution period as well as for five years (defect liability period) after completion of 
construction for all works. 
In the absence of insurance coverage by the contractors, SRRDA remained at risk of 
bearing financial burden of damages caused to roads /bridges, personal injury/death due 
to accidents during execution. 
Audit noticed that in absence of required insurance cover, SRRDA had to bear an 
expenditure of ₹ 1.32 crore towards restoration of two works in ongoing sites from the 
PMGSY programme fund as detailed below: 
	The work “Construction RCR from Gurung Gaon to Gairi Gaon via Sansaridara” 
was sanctioned for ₹5.99 crore and was awarded to Shri Kailash Rai . The road 
formation and cutting work was completed and protective wall, drainage and 
pavement work was under way. However, heavy rain on the night of 03 July 2019 
caused major landslide, slip and choking drains damaging the protective walls. 
Accordingly, the estimate was revised with additional expenditure on protective 
walls for restoration costing ₹ 95.94 lakh which was met from overall saving of the 
project. 
	The work “Construction RCR from NSH via Upper Gyer” sanctioned for ₹ 6.99 crore 
was awarded (July 2018) to Shri P.D, Denzongpa.  Heavy rain (May 2020) choked 
the drains, slips which ultimately damaged the overall Geometrics of the pavement, 
formation width. Accordingly, the estimate was revised for additional expenditure 
on pavement surface (₹1.62 lakh) and protective wall (₹  34.76 lakh) was provided 
for restoration. The additional financial implication of ₹  36.38 lakh was met from 
overall saving of the project.
Thus, inability on the part of the SRRDA to enforce insurance cover over PMGSY 
works by the contractor led to unwarranted burden of ₹ 1.32 crore on the scheme fund 
which was equivalent to the cost  of construction of one km road.
While accepting the facts, the RDD stated (March 2024) that in accordance to the Para 
9.3.1 of the OM and Clause 13 of GCC, the contractors were directed to provide all risk 
insurance cover, however, it was informed later to RDD that the insurance companies 
were reluctant to provide the insurance cover for PMGSY roads. 

Chapter III: Economic Sector
109
However, RDD had not furnished documents indicating that the insurance companies 
were not willing to provide insurance cover. 
3.3.10.11 Irregular re-awarding of rescinded works without tendering valuing 
₹ 22.51 crore
Clause 8.15 of the Operations Manual stipulates that i f the bidder who is awarded the 
work does not start the work within the time stipulated, the contract may be terminated 
as per the Condition of Contract and the entire bidding process repeated.
SRRDA awarded (January 2017) the work “RCR from Lower Nandok to Lower Syari 
and 5 other works packages with contract value of ₹ 2,251.92 lakh (8.21 per cent below) 
to contractor Shri N.B. Dahal. Accordingly, Mobilisation Advance of ₹ 3.10 crore was 
released (January 2017) for the entire package of six works. Since there was inordinate 
delay on the part of the contractor in commencing the work, SRRDA issued letter 
(18 March 2017) to the contractor to proceed with the work. Time extension was given 
till 31 March 2019 for completion of the work. However, as the progress of work was 
unsatisfactory, a final notice was served for delay in progress of work on 04 March 2019.
Audit noticed that the contractor requested (11 March 2019) SRRDA for cancellation of 
entire six works on the plea that he will be contesting the forthcoming General Election. 
Accordingly, SRRDA terminated (15 March 2019) the agreement and intimated the 
contractor.
However, the contractor subsequently requested (July 2019) SRRDA to revoke the 
cancellation of works and allow him to continue the entire  package of six works. 
SRRDA accepted (02 September 2019) the request of the contractor and allowed to 
continue with the contract.
Scrutiny of records of SRRDA revealed that at the time of rescinding of works, the 
balance MA outstanding with the contractor was ₹ 2.48 crore which was not recovered/ 
surrendered by the contractor. Further, bank guarantee had also expired (29 December 
2017) at the time of termination which was not renewed by the contractor. Thus, the 
contractor irregularly retained the advance paid to him for more than four months 
outside government account which resulted in loss of interest amounting to ₹ 6.39 lakh 
to the state exchequer.
Thus, allowing the contractor to retain the MA of ₹ 2.48 crore for more than four months 
even after termination of contract establishes that SRRDA not only could not adhere to 
PMGSY guidelines but also did not exercise financial diligence.
RDD in its reply stated (March 2024) that the matter pertaining to termination of 
contract and re-awarding of the work was intimated to MoRD, GoI and the matter was 
discussed with authorities of GoI and they have suggested that award/ cancellation of 
the work contract is the matter of the State Government. Hence, in view of the fact 
that re-tendering of the work would take substantial amount of time and would cause 
further delay of work and since certain portion of the work had already been completed 
by the contractor so in the best interest of the work and as directed and approved by the 
Government, the same contractor was allowed to resume the work. He has successfully 
completed all the works awarded to him.
The reply is not acceptable as at the time of rescinding the work, RDD should have 
recovered all the dues and the remaining Mobilisation advance from the Contractor. 
SRRDA did not follow due procedure for termination of the contract agreement in first 
place. This shows the lackadaisical approach of SRRDA and undue favour to contractor.

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
110
Further, the reply of RDD that to avoid further delay of work the work was re awarded 
to same contractor was not tenable as the work was already delayed by more than two 
years before the termination of contract and RDD had frequently served notices to the 
contractor for delayed progress of work. 
3.3.10.12 Maintenance of assets created under PMGSY
In terms of PMGSY scheme guidelines, a separate maintenance component funded by 
the State Government out of its resources was required to be provided in the DPR. In 
respect of hill roads where construction was done in two stages, interim maintenance, 
clearing of slips, etc. in the period between the first and the second stage were to be done 
departmentally. For maintenance after the second stage, initial five-year maintenance 
was to be contracted out to the same contractor at the time of awarding the work for 
the second stage (Paragraph 8.7 of guidelines). As per Paragraph 32.4 of the Standard 
Bidding Document which forms part of the agreement, contractor shall carry out monthly 
inspection of roads under maintenance, based on which, he shall submit monthly bills on 
e-Marg and e-governance solution for maintenance of rural road under PMGSY  online 
as detailed in subsequent clauses.
The routine maintenance standards shall meet the following minimum requirements: -
(a) Potholes on the road surface to be repaired soon after (but not more than 15 days 
to rectify) ( b) Road shoulders to be maintained in proper condition ( c) Cleaning of 
surface drains, culvert and other cross drainage structures (d) Maintenance of road signs, 
pavement markings etc. ( e) clearing of tree branches to keep the sight distance visible  
(f) maintenance operation required to keep the road traffic worthy at all times during the 
maintenance period.
Despite the necessity of regular maintenance and upkeep to preserve the life of the roads 
created under PMGSY and despite clear instructions by GoI, the maintenance of roads 
and bridges in the State was not adequate as the roads/bridges had not been properly 
maintained (ascertained from conduct of JPV) as required under the PMGSY guidelines 
as detailed below:
During JPV , audit had visited 21 out of 34 sampled roads under maintenance and it 
was noticed that all the PMGSY roads completed during 2017-21 were in dilapidated 
conditions. The drains were covered by shrubs, culverts and CDs were damaged and 
choked, with slips and landslides at numerous sites, surface of roads having been 
damaged etc as shown in Images below.
Image 3.16 to 3.19: Deplorable conditions of roads due to non-maintenance

While accepting the fact, RDD stated (March 2024) that the post DLP of five years 
maintenance need to be taken up under the State Plan funding. The Department has 
been proposing post DLP maintenance funds to the State Government. The maintenance 
works will be taken up as and when, funds are provided in the budget allocation. Further, 
the RDD stated that request for Maintenance fund as per OMMAS is placed with the 
government every year. However, release of fund depends on the State Government. 
There has been shortfall in the allocation. 
3.3.11 Monitoring
3.3.11.1 National Quality Monitor (NQM) and State Quality Monitor (SQM)
According to Para 15.2 of PMGSY guidelines, a three tier quality control mechanism is 
envisaged wherein the State Government would be responsible for the first two tiers of 
monitoring. Further, 15.6 stipulates that the National Quality Monitors shall inspect the 
road works with particular reference to quality who shall then cause to submit the report 
to NRIDA which shall in turn send it to the State Quality Coordinator within a specified 
period. The Project Implementing Units (PIU) shall ensure that the contractor replaces 
the materials or rectifies the workmanship in case the SQM and NQM report reveals 
unsatisfactory work. The results of NQM and SQM inspections during April 2017 to 
March 2023 are summarised in the following table:

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023

Table 3.18- Details of NQM and SQM inspection

inspections

As can be seen from the table above, NQM carried out a total 559 inspections wherein 
14 per cent  works were graded as unsatisfactory and 12 per cent  works required 
improvement. Similarly, SQM had carried out 1,507 inspections and graded four 
per cent works as unsatisfactory while two per cent works were graded as requiring 
improvement.  However, there was no record of action taken by SRRDA to rectify the 
works.
In reply, RDD stated (March 2024) that as per the Programme Guidelines, in respect of 
quality grading awarded as ‘Unsatisfactory/Satisfactory but Requires Improvement’ by 
NQMs, issues in work quality with proper rectifications will be taken by PIU and the 
Action Taken Report after re-verification by the SQMs at site will be communicated to 
NRIDA through the SQC with proper annexure documents and photographs. Accordingly, 
action as required by SRRDA is being done and the work is re-graded as Satisfactory 
after scrutiny by the Central Quality Controller (CQC), NRIDA. With regard to quality 
grading awarded by SQMs as ‘Unsatisfactory/ Satisfactory but Requires Improvement’, 
the work during assignment of the inspection is re-verified by other SQMs after 
proper rectifications have been completed by the PIUs and is regraded which is further 
examined and scrutinised by SQC and higher officials of SRRDA. There has been no 
such procedure of uploading the ATRs with respect to SQMs grading as per guidelines 
of PMGSY .
However, while a total of 232 works were graded as unsatisfactory/required improvement, 
RDD did not provide any records to prove that rectification as per NQM and SQM 
observation/recommendation was carried out. 
3.3.11.2 Quality control laboratories
Paragraph 15.1 of the Programme Guidelines envisage that a site Quality Control 
Laboratory will be set up by the contractor for each package. Para 9.4(a) of the OM 
provides that as per Clause 9 of General Conditions of Contract, the contractor is required 
to employ technical personnel and records in support of tests shall be maintained. Further, 
as per guidelines, quality control laboratories were to be set up by the contractor and 
it is mandatory to carry out contractual stipulated test which should be recorded in the 
Quality Control Register (QCR).
To assess the implementation of PMGSY , Joint Physical Verification of 86 works82 out 
of 113 sampled works (roads-55, bridges-24 and maintenance-34) in four districts was 
carried out by audit teams in the presence of staff of the implementing agencies. 
During JPV of ongoing 30 roads and 17 bridges works, it was found that the site 
laboratories maintained were deficient 83 in 28 out of 30 road works as well as in case 
of 16 out of 17 sampled bridge works. Quality control instruments along with required 
82 Roads(45), Bridge(20) and maintenance(21).
83 Required equipment were not available for testing of construction material at site laboratory.

Chapter III: Economic Sector
113
technical person was not available at worksite to ensure technical specification of the 
works. Further, the Quality Control Register for recording the test results were also not 
available.
In reply, RDD stated (March 2024) that Quality Control Labs are being maintained for 
the ongoing works. However, once the works are completed, the labs are removed by the 
contractor but are made available on event of the inspections by the QMs. With regard to 
the Quality Control Register, based on the directives of Central Quality Control (CQC), 
NRIDA, the scanned copies duly graded by the SE and State Quality Controller (SQC) 
have to be scanned and uploaded in OMMAS for ease of viewing online in lieu of hard 
copies through respective login IDs of the Quality Monitors. Since, the QCRs are now 
being maintained online and the registers are kept with the concern JEs, the same could 
not be produced when required, the same will be made available during the time of audit 
in future. 
However, the quality control laboratories were deficient as noticed during the JPV in 
the presence of SRRDA engineers. Further, quality control registers were not produced 
along with reply.  
3.3.11.3 State Level Standing Committee (SLSC) meetings 
Paragraph 2.4 of PMGSY Operations Manual stipulated that a State Level Standing 
Committee (SLSC)  set up preferably under the chairmanship of the Chief Secretary 
will be responsible for close and effective monitoring of programme and to oversee the 
timely and proper execution of works. Further, SLSC is to meet every quarter for close 
and effective monitoring of implementation of the programme. 
A State Level Standing Committee (SLSC) set up under the chairmanship of the Chief 
Secretary, was responsible for close and effective monitoring of the programme and 
to oversee the timely and proper execution of works . The Committee was to vet the 
Core Network, the CNCPL and CUCPL and clear the annual project proposals. Since 
the primary purpose of PMGSY (and indeed of rural connectivity) is to enable basic 
access to the rural hinterland and thus increase the tempo of economic activity and reach 
of social services, the SLSC’s role is crucial to exploiting the development potential 
created by the new assets.
However, no records relating to meetings held and minutes of the SLSC meetings held 
during the period 2017-2023 against the requirement of 24 meetings to be conducted 
were available with SRRDA which indicated that no SLSC meetings were held during 
the period 2017-2023. This indicated ineffective monitoring of progress of works, non-
settlement of land disputes etc. under PMGSY .
While accepting the audit contention, the RDD stated (March 2024) that there were 
deficiencies in conducting the SLSC meeting in the past. However, now the RDD is 
ensuring that SLSC meetings are conducted on regular basis.
3.3.12 Conclusion
SRRDA could not prioritise unconnected eligible habitations while identifying road 
works under PMGSY and expended resources in less priority road works. SRRDA prepare 
estimates by conducting appropriate ground studies and field surveys, which not only 
resulted in infructuous expenditure but also in undue benefit to the contractor. Inability 
of SRRDA in ensuring timely land acquisition resulted in delay in implementation of 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
114
the road works. The project implementation was plagued by various deviations which 
resulted from inadequate planning and groundwork. Also, in a few road works, suitable 
structures like Culverts were not built appropriately, to prevent damage to roads by 
rainfall and water streams, thus affecting the objective of providing all weather 
connectivity to the habitations. Inadequate provision of funds towards maintenance by 
the State Government led to poor upkeep of the roads and caused damage to them. 
SRRDA also did not take necessary rectification measures towards quality issues raised 
by the NQMs and SQMs.
3.3.13 Recommendations
It is recommended that 
	Responsibility of the officials concerned may be fixed for selecting ineligible 
habitations, preparing deficient DPRs and execution of unapproved portions of 
works.
	SRRDA may effectively coordinate with other Departments for getting land/ forest 
clearances and ensure timely land acquisition towards early execution of the 
projects.
	SRRDA may monitor the progress of road works with reference to the prescribed 
milestones and quality and stringent action be initiated against defaulting 
contractors to avoid delay in execution. 
	SRRDA may ensure that the roads built are in compliance with the quality 
specifications as specified in the guidelines and may take necessary rectificatory 
measures on the recommendations of NQM and SQM.
	SRRDA may streamline the existing mechanism for routine maintenance of PMGSY 
roads and bridges such that the roads and bridges remain serviceable through out 
their service life, without deterioration. 
COMPLIANCE AUDIT PARAGRAPH
RURAL DEVELOPMENT DEPARTMENT
3.4 Excess expenditure 
Rural Development Department erroneously paid ₹ 4.32 crore as labour cess was 
included in the estimate for the work and the rate quoted by the contractor was 
inclusive of all taxes including labour cess. Similarly, RDD had also paid ₹ 0.38 
crore (including cess) on inadmissible items, which was avoidable.
According to the Building and Other Construction Workers’ (Regulation of Employment 
and Conditions of Service) Act, 1996 (BOCW Act), the Government had implemented 
(September 2010) labour cess at the rate of one per cent on bills on construction works. 
To make Sikkim a Kutchha House free state, the Rural Development Department (RDD) 
identified 6,394 Kutchha house during 2015 and decided to construct 3000 Pucca houses 
under Chief Minister Rural Housing Scheme Phase-I (CMRHM-I) in 2016-17. After 
tendering (December 2016), the RDD awarded (March 2017) the work of construction 
of 3000 houses under CMRHM-Phase-I to the contractor at a total cost of ₹ 379.20 
crore on turnkey basis with stipulated time of completion by March 2019. All the 3,000 
houses were completed by August 2020. 

Chapter III: Economic Sector
115
Subsequently, in December 2018, RDD proposed to construct another 3000 houses 
(which was later increased to 3050 in March 2019) under CMRHM Phase-II later 
renamed Sikkim Garib Abas Yojana (SGAY) at an estimated cost of ₹ 443.49 crore. The 
Department awarded the SGAY works to the same contractor/firm in January 2019 at 
a cost of ₹ 443.49 crore as continuation of phase-I on the same terms and conditions.  
Further, the provision for Television and furniture was also added for all 3050 houses  
(March 2020) at a cost of ₹ 45.05 crore. This work was completed in June 2022. 
Scrutiny of records revealed that Clause 17 of standard conditions of contract entered 
into (February 2019) between RDD and the contractor for construction of the houses 
under SGAY (CMRHM) stipulated that “the rate quoted by the contractor shall be 
deemed to be inclusive of all taxes including labour cess that the contractor will have 
to pay” as labour cess was already included in the estimate for the work. However, in 
contravention of the terms of agreement RDD had paid the contractor as below:
	Per unit house cost - Initially, the work order was issued for construction of 3000 
houses which was later enhanced to 3050 houses (March 2019) at a cost of ₹ 
13,12,478/unit84. 
	Payment of labour cess on civil work - As per estimates, the labour cess was 
included in per unit cost. Accordingly RDD had paid ₹ 4.32 crore to the contractor 
as labour cess component.
Avoidable payment of cess on inadmissible items - As per given codal provision one 
per cent labour cess should have been collected on only construction works, however, it 
was seen that RDD had paid ₹ 0.38 crore (including cess) to the contractor, for purchase 
of TV and furniture to the newly constructed houses under SGAY , which was not 
admissible and led to excess and avoidable expenditure of ₹ 0.38 crore. 
Thus, RDD made irregular payment amounting to ₹ 4.70 crore, comprising cess at one 
per cent of (i) the civil work and (ii) purchase of TV and furniture, which resulted in 
excess expenditure to the tune of ₹ 4.70 crore. 
In reply (November 2023), the Department admitted that the quoted rate of the contractor 
was taking into consideration the labour cess provided in the estimate.
The Department should take urgent action to recover ₹4.70 crore from the contractor. 
The State Government needs to fix responsibility on the officials concerned for such a 
lapse.
84 Including 1 per cent labour cess and 43.60 per cent tender premium but excluding GST.

4.1 Functioning of State Public Sector Enterprises (SPSEs)
4.1.1 Introduction
The Public Sector Enterprises (PSEs) consist of the State Government Companies and 
Statutory Corporations. The SPSEs are established to carry out economic and commercial 
activities for the overall development of the State and its people. As on 31 March 2023, 
there were 17 SPSEs (13 working Government Companies and four working Statutory 
Corporations) under the audit purview of the Comptroller and Auditor General of India 
(C&AG). Besides, there were six non-working SPSEs for which audit entrustment had 
not been extended to the C&AG by the State Government as detailed in Paragraph 
4.1.10. The details of the SPSEs in Sikkim as on 31 March 2023 are given below:
Table 4.1: Total number of SPSEs as on 31 March 2022
Type of PSEs Working PSEs
Government Companies registered under Sikkim Registration of Companies  
Act, 1961
0985
Government Companies registered under Companies Act, 2013    0486
Statutory Corporations 04
Total 17
None of the 13 working Government companies were listed in the stock exchange. 
During the year 2022-23, no new PSE was incorporated and no existing PSE was closed 
down.

4.1.5 Accountability framework
The Companies Act, 2013 and the erstwhile Companies Act, 1956 had not been extended 
to the State of Sikkim. Out of 13 Government Companies existing in the State of Sikkim, 
only four companies were registered under the Companies Act, 1956/2013 while the 
remaining 9 were registered under the ‘Registration of Companies Act, Sikkim, 1961.
The four companies registered and governed by the Companies Act, 2013/1956 included 
Sikkim Urja Limited (SUL), Sikkim Power Transmission Limited (SPTL), Namchi 
Smart City Limited (NSCL) and Gangtok Smart City Development Limited (GSDCL).
During the year 2015-16, one State Government Company 94 acquired 51 per cent of 
equity share capital of SUL, which is the Holding company of SPTL. The other two 
companies (NSCL and GSCDL) were incorporated during 2016-17 and 2017-18 by the 
State Government under the Companies Act, 2013 with headquarters in Darjeeling, West 
Bengal. Thus, all these four companies (SUL, SPTL, NSCL and GSCDL) are covered 
under the definition of a State Government company owned and controlled (directly and 
indirectly) by the State Government.
4.1.5.1 Statutory Audit/Supplementary Audit
The accounts of nine State Government Companies registered under the ‘Registration of 
Companies Act, Sikkim, 1961’ are audited by Statutory Auditors (Chartered Accountants) 
directly appointed by the Board of Directors (BoDs) of the respective Companies. In 
addition to the statutory audit conducted by the Statutory Auditors, supplementary 
audit of these Companies is conducted by the Comptroller and Auditor General of India 
(C&AG) on request of the Governor of the State under Section 20(1) of the Comptroller 
and Auditor General’s (Duties, Powers and Conditions of Service) Act, 1971.
The accounts of four Companies registered under Companies Act, 2013/1956 are audited 
by the Statutory Auditors (Chartered Accountants) who are appointed by the C&AG. 
In addition to the statutory audit conducted by the Statutory Auditors, supplementary 
audit of these Companies is conducted by the C&AG under Section 143(6) (a) of the 
Companies Act, 2013.
Besides, there are four Statutory Corporations in the State (namely, State Bank of Sikkim, 
State Trading Corporation of Sikkim, Government Fruit Preservation Factory and Temi 
Tea Estate), established under the proclamation of the erstwhile Chogyal (King) of 
Sikkim. The accounts of these Corporations are audited by the Chartered Accountants 
directly appointed by the Board of Directors (BoDs) of the respective Corporations. 
Supplementary Audit of these Corporations was taken up by C&AG based on the 
94  Sikkim Power Investment Corporation Limited.

Chapter IV: Economic Sector (Public Sector Undertakings)
121
entrustment/request for their audit by the Governor under Section 19(3) of the C&AG’s 
(Duties, Powers and Conditions of Service) Act, 1971.
4.1.5.2 Role of Government and Legislature
The State Government exercises control over the affairs of these SPSEs through its 
administrative departments. The Government appoints the Chief Executives and 
Directors on the Board of these SPSEs.
The State Legislature also monitors the accounting and utilisation of Government 
investments in the SPSEs. For this purpose, the Annual Accounts of the State Government 
Companies together with the Statutory Auditors report and Separate Audit Reports of 
C&AG are required to be placed before the Legislature under Section 20 (1) of the 
Comptroller and Auditor General’s (Duties, Powers and Conditions of Service) Act, 
1971. The Annual Reports of four Government Companies incorporated under the 
Companies Act, 2013/1956 together with the Statutory Auditors Reports and comments 
of C&AG thereon are to be placed before the State Legislature under section 396 of 
the Companies Act, 2013.  Similarly, the Annual Reports of the Statutory Corporations 
along with the Separate Audit Reports of C&AG are required to be placed before the 
Legislature as per the stipulations made under Section 19(3) of the Comptroller and 
Auditor General’s (Duties, Powers and Conditions of Service) Act, 1971.
4.1.6 Arrears in Finalisation of accounts
In respect of four companies registered under the Companies Act, 2013/1956, the 
financial statements of the companies are required to be finalised within six months of 
the end of the financial year i.e. by September end in accordance with the provisions of 
Section 96(1) of the Companies Act, 2013. Failure to do so may attract penal provisions 
under Section 99 of Companies Act, 2013. Out of these four companies, two companies 
namely, Namchi Smart City Limited (latest accounts finalised for 2020-21) and Gangtok 
Smart City Development Limited (latest accounts finalised for 2021-22) had pendency 
of two and one year Accounts respectively as on 30 September 2023. 
As regards nine companies registered under the Registration of Companies Act, 1961 
and four Statutory Corporations, there is no stipulated timeframe for finalisation of 
financial statements in their respective governing Acts. 
Table 4.6 provides the details of progress made by SPSEs in finalisation of their accounts 
as of 30 September 2023.

Debt-Turnover Ratio
A low debt-to-turnover ratio (DTR) demonstrates a good balance between debt and 
income. Conversely, a high DTR can signal having too much of debt against the income 
of PSEs from core activities. Thus, the SPSEs having lower DTR are more likely to 
comfortably manage their debt servicing and repayments.

SPSE Debt
As can be noticed from Table 4.9, the SPSE-debts as on 31 March 2023 stood at 
₹ 13,057.73 crore, major portion (₹ 11,638.96 crore) of which pertained to three power 
sector SPSEs99. During the period of five years (2018-23), the SPSE-debt registered a 
marginal decrease of ₹ 227.16 crore (1.71 per cent) from ₹ 13,284.88 crore (2018-19) to 
₹ 13,057.73 crore (2022-23). During the same period of five years (2018-23), however, 
the SPSE-turnover had grown by 91.31 per cent (₹ 1,935.31 crore) from ₹ 2,119.51 crore 
(2018-19) to ₹ 4,054.82 crore (2022-23) mainly due to appreciation of ₹495.63 crore in 
the turnover of one power sector ( Sikkim Urja Limited). This has led to corresponding 
improvement in the DTR of SPSEs from 6.27:1 (2018-19) to 3.22:1 (2022-23).
During the last five years, the accumulated losses of SPSEs had registered an overall 
decrease of (₹ 936.85 crore) from ₹ 2,085.94 crore (2018-19) to ₹ 1,149.09 crore (2022-23). 
This reduction was mainly attributable to two power sector companies100, which started 
earning profits after commencement of their commercial operations. During 2022-23, 
the said power sector companies earned an aggregate profit of ₹ 1,333.47 crore as per 
their latest finalised accounts (2022-23).
4.1.8.2 Erosion of capital due to losses
The aggregate paid-up capital and accumulated losses of 17 working SPSEs as per their 
latest finalised accounts as on 30 September 2023 were ₹ 3,716.86 crore and (-)₹ 1,149.09 
crore respectively (Appendix 4.1). As on 31 March 2023, out of total 17 SPSEs in the 
State, 9 SPSEs had accumulated losses amounting to ₹ 1,343.70 crore and of these, six 
SPSEs incurred losses aggregating to ₹ 212.45 crore as per their latest finalised accounts 
as of 30 September 2023.
The Net Worth of six101 out of these nine SPSEs was ‘negative’ as the ‘Paid up Capital’ 
(₹ 76.00 crore) of these SPSEs had been completely eroded by their ‘Accumulated Losses’ 
(₹ 1,326.32 crore). The Net Worth of these six SPSEs was negative at (-)₹ 1,250.32 crore 
as on 31 March 2023. Out of six SPSEs whose Net Worth (Capital) had been eroded, one 
SPSE had earned aggregate profit of ₹ 3.76 crore during 2022-23. Out of the said six 
SPSEs, which had negative Net Worth, one SPSE102 had outstanding Government loans 
of ₹ 24.99 crore as on 31 March 2023. The details of SPSEs whose net worth had been 
completely eroded by their accumulated losses have been given in Table 4.10.

4.1.8.3 Return on Capital Employed
Return on Capital Employed (ROCE) is a profitability metric that measures the long-
term profitability and efficiency of the total capital employed by a company. Companies 
create value when they generate returns on the capital employed. ROCE is an important 
decision metric for long term lenders. ROCE is calculated by dividing a company’s 
earnings before interest and taxes (EBIT) by the capital employed104. 
During 2022-23 the overall Capital Employed in 17 working SPSEs as per their latest 
accounts was ₹15,630.61 crore while the ROCE of the SPSEs was 15.42 per cent. Further, 
out of 17 working SPSEs, only 12 SPSEs had positive ROCE while the ROCE of remaining 
five SPSEs105 was not workable due to the negative Capital Employed (Appendix 4.1).
4.1.9 Return on Investment on the basis of Present Value of Investment
The Rate of Real Return (RoRR) measures the profitability and efficiency with which 
equity and similar non-interest bearing capital have been employed, after adjusting them 
for their time value. To determine the RoRR on Government Investments in the State 
PSEs, the investment of State Government in the form of equity, interest free loans 
and grants / subsidies given by the State Government for operational and management 
expenses less the disinvestments has been considered and indexed to their present value 
and summated. The RoRR is then calculated by dividing the Profit After Tax (PAT) of 
the PSEs by the sum of the PV of the Government investments. 
During 2022-23, as per their latest finalised accounts, out of 15106 working SPSEs where 
State Government had made direct investment, six107 SPSEs incurred loss and remaining 
103 SHL, SPDCL, SLPDC, SABCCO, SPICL & GSCDL.
104 Capital employed =Paid up share capital+free reserves and surplus+long term loans-accumulated 
loss-defered revenue expenditure.
105 Serial no. A5, A6, A7, A10 and B17 of Appendix 4.1.
106 Excluding two SPSEs (SUL and SPTL), in which State Government had no direct investment in the 
form of equity, loans or grants, etc.
107 SHL, SPDCL, SLPDC, SABCCO, SPICL & GSCDL .

Chapter IV: Economic Sector (Public Sector Undertakings)
127
nine SPSEs earned profit. On the basis of return on historical value, the State Government 
investment eroded by 200.27 per cent during 2022-23. If the Rate of Real Return where 
the present value of investment is considered, the State Government investment eroded 
by 40.86 per cent as shown in Appendix 4.2. This difference in percentage of investment 
erosion was on account of the adjustment made in the investment amount for time value 
of money. 
4.1.10 Winding up of non-working SPSEs
There were six non-working SPSEs (five Companies108 and one Statutory Corporation109) 
for which audit entrustment to C&AG had expired between 2003-04 and 2016-17. Since 
the audit of these six non-working SPSEs has not been entrusted to C&AG, therefore the 
present report has not covered their functioning.
The Government Companies in Sikkim are registered under the Registration of 
Companies Act, 1961 while Statutory Corporations are governed by the proclamation 
of the erstwhile Chogyal (King) of Sikkim. There was no prescribed procedure for 
liquidation of Government Companies/ Statutory Corporations under their respective 
governing Act/ Statute.
As per the latest available information, the assets of five out of the said six non-working 
SPSEs (except Sikkim Mining Corporation) had been disposed of and the proceeds 
remitted (December 2012) to the Government of Sikkim.  As per the latest information 
available, the liquidation of the sixth non-working PSE (Sikkim Mining Corporation) 
was approved (October 2016) by the Department of Mines, Minerals and Geology, 
Government of Sikkim and its liabilities (₹ 6.85 crore) had also been waived (October 
2016) by the State Government.

4.1.11.1 Gist of important comments of the statutory auditors and C&AG 
State Bank of Sikkim (2021-22)
Comment on Auditors’ Report
1. Refer the positive assurance/opinion expressed by the Auditors on the true and 
correctness of the Accounts of the Bank for the year ended 31 March 2022 vide their 
Audit Report dated 20 July 2022 (refer para 14) and the ‘Disclaimer of Opinion’ 
given on adequacy of provisions maintained by the Bank against bad debts ( refer 
para (iii) - Others under Para 6-Basis for Disclaimer of Audit Opinion). 
As per the criteria prescribed under the ‘Loan Recovery Policy 2014’ (Policy) of the 
Bank, the Bank is required to classify its assets (‘Loans’ and ‘Advances’) into ‘standard 
assets’ and ‘sub-standard assets/non-performing assets (NPAs)’. The ‘Guidelines on 
Income Recognition, Asset Classification and Provisioning pertaining to Advances’ 
(Guidelines) issued by the Bank further prescribed the norms to maintain necessary 
provisions against NPAs, which ranged between 15 per cent (sub-standard assets) and 
100 per cent (loss assets). 
However, the Bank has neither classified its assets in the above categories nor maintained 
the mandatory provisions against NPAs as per the requirements of its own Policy/
Guidelines. Based on the information provided by the Bank, as against Bank’s total loan 
assets of ₹ 2,801.23 crore, the total NPAs (including bad debts and interest suspense) 
worked out to ₹ 1368.79 crore (48.86 per cent) as on 31 March 2022.
Even after considering the minimum mandatory provision of 15 per cent applicable to 
‘sub-standard assets’ (loan assets/accounts which remained NPA for less than or equal 
to 12 months), the Bank was required to maintain provisions of ₹ 205.32 crore against 
the NPAs (₹ 1,368.79 crore) as on 31 March 2022. On the contrary, however, the Bank 
has kept adhoc provisions of ₹ 22.03 crore (cumulative) towards NPAs as on 31 March 
2022. Thus, short provisioning against NPAs has resulted in understatement of ‘Other 
Liabilities & Provisions’ by ₹ 183.29 crore. 
Further, as an impact of the above comment, the ‘Profit for the year’  (₹ 37.04 crore) 
as reported in the financial statements of the Bank for the year ended 31 March 2022 
would turn into ‘Loss’ of ₹ 146.25 crore. As such, the positive assurance/opinion 
expressed by the Auditors on the ‘true and correctness’ of the Accounts of the Bank 
for the year ended 31 March 2022 vide their Audit Report dated 20 July 2022 (refer 
para 14), was not sustainable.

4.1.12.3 Compliance to Reports of Public Accounts Committee
As of October 2023, PAC had issued total three PAC Reports containing six 
recommendations relating to Audit Reports for the years 2012-13, 2013-14 and 2014-15 
which were presented in the State Legislature. Action Taken Notes (ATNs) against five 
recommendations of the PAC relating to Audit Report for the year 2013-14 and 2014-15 
had not been received from the concerned PSEs.
It is recommended that the Government may ensure: 
(a) furnishing the replies/Explanatory Notes to Paragraphs/ Performance Audits and 
ATNs on the recommendations of P AC as per the prescribed time schedule;
(b) recovery of loss/ outstanding advances/ overpayments within the prescribed period;
(c) revamping of the system of responding to audit observations.

Land Revenue:  Decrease was mainly due to less registration of land compared to 
previous year.

during 2022-23 over 2021-22 due to the lifting of concessions approved by the 
Government during the COVID period.

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
134
The respective departments reported the following reasons for variation:
Forestry and Wild Life: Increase is mainly due to payment of ecosystem services.
Interest Receipts: Increase in revenue mainly due to deposit of interest earned out 
of the state share of fund deposited under Single nodal accounts by the implementing 
departments.
Education, Sports, Art and Culture: Decrease in revenue mainly due to sale of tender 
forms & storage charge etc. which depends on the fund entrusted for various works 
which happens in lesser scale in current financial year.
Police: Increase in revenue mainly due to maximum receipt of deployment charge & 
reimbursement for maximum tourism influx, increase in number of renewal of gun 
licenses during 2022-23.
Road Transport: Increase in revenue was due to induction of new fleet of heavy 
commercial vehicles in the department fleet. Due to increase in construction activities 
post Covid in the State and subsequent increase in movement of Heavy Vehicles which 
resulted in increase in movement of Heavy Vehicles which resulted in increase in 
supervision collection and total revenue collection.
Plantation: Increase in revenue was mainly due to increased sale of tea.
Water Supply and Sanitation (PHE):  Increase in revenue mainly due to increase in 
number of consumer and realisation of arrears and increase in number of consumers in 
the mid of the financial year.
Tourism: Increase in revenue mainly due to revenue generation during 2020-21 & 2021-22 
being affected by the Covid pandemic which picked up after the pandemic settled.
Medical and Public Health: Decrease is mainly due to less tender fees collected since 
there was extension of contracts related to CMC/AMC of Bio Medical equipment & 
supply of medicine & consumable. The firm were given one year extension with due 
approval of Government. This led to decrease in collection of tender fees.
Stationery and Printing: Increase in revenue mainly due to more jobs executed being 
by the department especially printing of school text books.
State Lotteries: Increase in revenue was mainly due to payment of Bank interest.

5.4  Response of the departments/ Government towards Audit
The Accountant General (AG), Sikkim conducts periodical inspection of the Government 
departments to test check the transactions and verify the maintenance of important 
accounts and other records as prescribed in Rules and procedures.  Inspection Reports 
(IRs) incorporating irregularities detected during the inspection and not settled on 
the spot are issued to the heads of the offices inspected with copies to the next higher 
authorities for prompt corrective action. The heads of the offices/ departments are 
required to promptly comply with the observations contained in the IRs, rectify the 
defects and omissions and report compliance through initial reply to the AG within one 
month from the date of issue of the IRs.  Serious financial irregularities are reported to 
the heads of the Department and the Government.

pendency of IRs and paragraphs.

the Report of the Comptroller and Auditor General of India to the Principal Secretaries/ 
Secretaries of the Department concerned, drawing their attention to audit findings and 
requesting them to send their response within four weeks. The fact of nonreceipt of 
replies from the departments/ Government is invariably indicated at the end of such 
paragraphs included in the Audit Report.

to the heads of Departments through demi-official letter and replies in respect of three 
paragraphs were received.

5.7 Follow up on Audit Reports - summarised position
The Rules of Procedures of the Committee on Public Accounts of the Sikkim Legislative 
Assembly (internal working) lay down that after the presentation of the Report of the 
Comptroller and Auditor General of India in the Legislative Assembly, the departments 
shall initiate action on the audit paragraphs and the Government should submit the action 
taken explanatory notes within three months of tabling the Report for consideration of the 
Committee.  In spite of these provisions, the explanatory notes on the audit paragraphs 
of the Reports were being delayed inordinately.
Reports of the Comptroller and Auditor General of India of the Government of Sikkim 
for the years ended 31 March 2015, 2016, 2017, 2018, 2019 and 2020 containing 
15 paragraphs under Revenue Sector were placed before the State Legislative Assembly 
between March 2015 and March 2023.  Action taken explanatory notes in respect of 
nine paragraphs from six departments {Excise; Finance 119; Police, Urban Development 
and Rural Development} had not been received for Audit Reports for the years ending 
31 March 2015, 2016, 2017, 2018, 2019 and 2020.
During 2022-23, the PAC discussed four paragraphs and two Performance Audit Reports.

5.9 Audit Planning
The unit offices under various departments were categorised into high, medium and low 
risk units according to their revenue position, past trends of the audit observations and 
other parameters.  The annual audit plan was prepared on the basis of risk analysis which 
inter alia included critical issues in Government revenues and tax administration, i.e. 
budget speech, White Paper on State Finances, Reports of the Finance Commission (State 
and Central), recommendations of the Taxation Reforms Committee, statistical analysis 
of the revenue earnings during the past five years, factors of the tax administration, audit 
coverage and its impact during the past five years etc.
During 2022-23, there were nine auditable units, of which three units (33.33 per cent) 
were planned and audited. 
5.10 Results of audit
Test check of the records of three units under Revenue departments {Excise Department, 
Motor Vehicle Division, Transport Department, Directorate of Sikkim State Lotteries, 
Finance Department} were carried out during the year 2022-23. It revealed irregularities 
involving revenue aggregating to ₹ 123.05 crore in 20 cases.  During the course of the 
year, the departments concerned accepted 44 observations.
