Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
142
COMPLIANCE AUDIT
5.12		 Subject	 Specific	 Compliance	 Audit	 on	 Department’s	 Oversight	 on	 GST	
Payments and Returns Filing for the year 2017-18
5.12.1 Introduction
Introduction of Goods and Services Tax (GST) has replaced multiple taxes levied and 
collected by the Centre and States. GST, which came into effect from 01 July 2017, is a 
destination-based consumption tax on the supply of goods or services or both levied on 
every value addition. The Centre and States simultaneously levy GST on a common tax 
base. Central GST (CGST) and State GST/Union Territory GST (UTGST) are levied on 
intra-state supplies and Integrated GST (IGST) is levied on inter-state supplies.
Section 59 of the Sikkim Goods and Services Tax Act, 2017 (SGST Act) stipulates GST 
as a self-assessment based tax, whereby the responsibility for calculating tax liability, 
discharging the computed tax liability and filing returns is vested with the taxpayer. The 
GST returns must be filed online regularly on the common GST portal, failing which 
penalties will be payable. Even if the business has had no tax liability during a particular 
tax period, it must file a nil return mandatorily. Further, Section 61 of the SGST Act read 
with Rule 99 of Sikkim Goods and Services Tax Rules, 2017 (SGST Rules) stipulate 
that the proper officer may scrutinise the return and related particulars furnished by 
taxpayers, communicate discrepancies to the taxpayers and seek an explanation.
This Subject Specific Compliance Audit (SSCA) was taken up considering the 
significance of the control mechanism envisaged for tax compliance and the oversight 
mechanism of the Commercial Taxes Division, Finance Department, Government of 
Sikkim, (CTD) in this new tax regime.
5.12.2 Audit objectives
This audit was oriented towards providing assurance on adequacy and effectiveness of 
systems and procedures adopted by the CTD with respect to tax compliance under GST 
regime. This audit was taken up with the following audit objectives to seek an assurance 
on:
(i) Whether the rules and procedures were designed to secure an effective check on tax 
compliance and were being duly observed by taxpayers; and
(ii) Whether the scrutiny procedures, internal audit and other compliance functions of 
the Sectors were adequate and effective.
5.12.3 Audit methodology and scope
This SSCA was predominantly conducted based on data analysis, which highlighted 
risk areas and red flags pertaining to the period July 2017 to March 2018. Through data 
analysis, a set of 12 deviations were identified across the domains of input tax credit, 
discharge of tax liability, registration and return filing. Such deviations were followed 
up through a centralised audit 120, whereby these deviations were communicated to 
the relevant Sectors and action taken by the Sectors on the identified deviations was 
ascertained without involving field visits. The centralised audit was supplemented by a 
120 Centralised Audit did not involve seeking taxpayer’ s granular records such as financial statements 
related ledger accounts, invoices, agreements etc. 

Chapter V: Revenue Sector
143
detailed audit involving field visits for verification of records available with the Sectors. 
The returns and related attachments and information were accessed through the State 
CTD’s tax application (http://www.sikkimtax.gov.in/) as much as feasible to examine 
data/documents relating to taxpayers (viz. returns, tax payment, etc.). The detailed audit 
also involved accessing relevant granular records from the taxpayers such as invoices 
through the Sectors. This apart, compliance functions of the Sectors such as scrutiny of 
returns, conduct of internal audit, action on late filers/non-filers etc. were also reviewed 
in selected Sectors.
In Sikkim, the functional organisation of the CTD under GST is based on Sectors or 
nature of business (such as Manufacturer, Trader, Service Provider, etc.). Therefore, the 
audit of functions of selected Sectors was taken up. 
The review of the scrutiny of returns by the CTD and verification of taxpayers’ records 
covered the period from July 2017 to March 2018, while the audit of the functions of 
selected Sectors covered the period from July 2017 to March 2021. The SSCA covered 
only State administered taxpayers. The field audit was conducted from 1 April 2022 to 
1 February 2023.
Entry Conference of this SSCA was held on 1 April 2022 with a team of officers from the 
CTD headed by the Commissioner in which the audit objectives, sample selection, audit 
scope and methodology were discussed. The Exit Conference was held on 01  February 
2023 with a team of concerned officers from the CTD in which the audit findings were 
discussed. The views expressed by the CTD team during the Exit Conference and 
the written replies to the draft report have been suitably incorporated in the relevant 
paragraphs.
5.12.4 Audit sample 
A data-driven approach was adopted for planning, as also to determine the nature and 
extent of substantive audit. The sample for this SSCA comprised a set of deviations 
identified through data analysis for centralised audit that did not involve field visits; a 
sample of taxpayers for detailed audit that involved field visits and scrutiny of taxpayer’s 
records at the CTD’s premises; and a sample of Sectors for evaluating the compliance 
functions of the Sectors.
There were three distinct parts of this SSCA as under:
(i) Part I-Audit of Sectors: Three Sectors with jurisdiction over more than one 
selected sample of cases for detailed audit were considered as the sample of Sectors 
for evaluation of their oversight functions. 
(ii) Part II-Centralised Audit: The sample for centralised audit was selected by 
identification of high-value or high-risk deviations from rules and inconsistencies 
between returns through data analysis for evaluation of the adequacy and 
effectiveness of the scrutiny procedure of the CTD. Accordingly, 93 cases were 
selected for centralised audit under this SSCA.
(iii) Part III-Detailed Audit: It was conducted by accessing taxpayers’ records through 
Sectors for evaluation of the extent of tax compliance by taxpayers. The sample of 
taxpayers for detailed audit was selected on the basis of various risk parameters such 
as excess ITC, tax liability mismatch, disproportionate exempted turnover to total 
turnover and irregular ITC reversal. Fourteen taxpayers selected for detailed audit 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
144
comprised large121, medium 122 and small 123 strata taxpayers as well as taxpayers 
selected randomly. 
5.12.5 Audit criteria 
The source of audit criteria comprised the provisions contained in the SGST Act, IGST 
Act, and Rules made there under. In addition, the notifications and circulars issued by 
State CTD relating to filing of returns, notifying the effective dates of filing of various 
returns, extending due dates for filing returns, rates of tax on goods and services, payment 
of tax, availing and utilising ITC also formed part of the audit criteria.
5.12.6	 Audit	findings	
The audit findings are categorised into the following four categories:
1) Audit findings of Sector Audit
2) Audit findings of Centralised/ Limited Audit
3) Audit findings of Detailed Audit
4) Inadequacy of return data in State system
5.12.6.1 Audit findings of Sector Audit
For evaluation of oversight functions of Sectors, three out of total four Sectors 124 
of the CTD with jurisdiction over more than one sample cases of detailed audit 
were selected. The selected Sectors were Industries, Services and Trading.  The 
observations are brought under four broad categories: scrutiny of returns, internal audit, 
late/non-filing of returns and cancellation of registrations.
In Sector Audit, due to non-production/non-availability of the records pertaining to 
Sectoral functions, Audit could not comprehensively ascertain the effectiveness of the 
overseeing mechanism on returns filing and other functions of the Sectors.
a) Deficient monitoring mechanism on returns scrutiny
As per Section 61(1) of the SGST Act, the proper officer may scrutinise the returns and 
related particulars furnished by the registered person to verify the correctness of the 
return and inform him of the discrepancies noticed, if any, in such manner as may be 
prescribed and seek his explanation thereto.
To assess the scrutiny functions of the CTD, details 125 of the scrutiny conducted by the 
selected Sectors during the period July 2017 to March 2021 were called for in Audit. 
During Entry Conference (1 April 2022), the CTD intimated that the scrutiny function 
was done in the application itself and there is no module to record the scrutiny conducted. 
However, CBIC has formulated a Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) for scrutiny of 
returns which will act as an interim measure till such module to record scrutiny is made 
available in the system. 
121 First category strata comprising large taxpayers – top 2 per cent of taxpayers based on turnover.
122 Second category strata comprising medium taxpayers – next 8 per cent of taxpayers based on turnover.
123 Third category strata comprising small taxpayers – remaining 90 per cent of taxpayers based on 
turnover.
124 i) Industries and Banking (ii) Works Contract (iii) Services and (iv) Trading.
125 Number of taxpayers whose returns were scrutinized, Number of returns scrutinized, Number of 
repeated taxpayers, Number of cases where ASMT-10 were issued, Cases where discrepancy accepted 
by taxpayers and other follow up action taken.

Chapter V: Revenue Sector
145
-
CTD did not respond to audit queries as to whether any such SOP for scrutiny of returns 
has been formulated by them. Only one Sector (i.e. Industries) stated that four taxpayers 
were scrutinised by the CTD under Section 61 and notices were issued under ASMT10. 
No further records were produced by other Sectors to prove that scrutiny function has 
been undertaken. Therefore, Audit understands that out of total 10940 taxpayers, only 
four taxpayers were scrutinised and ASMT-10 were issued during the period from 2017-
18 to 2020-21. Further, the modalities of scrutiny adopted by the CTD, the basis for 
selection of taxpayers for scrutiny, the checks that were done in the application itself, any 
data analytics methods adopted and reports of DGARM 126 received, if any, and action 
taken thereon were called for (September 2022) in Audit, but the CTD had not responded 
till date (November 2023). Due to non-production of these records, the scrutiny function 
carried out by the Sectors on return filing could not be assessed comprehensively by 
Audit.
Recommendation 1: The CTD may put in place proper mechanism for scrutiny of 
returns to ensure compliance of rules pertaining to returns filing and tax payments by all 
taxpayers. The CTD may prepare a Standard Operating Procedure for manual scrutiny 
of returns in line with one prepared by CBIC, for use till automation of scrutiny is 
completed.
b) Lack of action on late-filers and non-filers
Section 46 of the SGST Act read with Rule 68 of SGST Rules stipulates issue of a 
notice in Form GSTR-3A requiring filing of return within fifteen days if the taxpayer 
had failed to file the return within the due date. Further, Section 62 of SGST Act read 
with Rule 100 of SGST Rule stipulates that in case, the taxpayer fails to file the returns 
even after such notice, the competent officers may proceed to assess the tax liability of 
the said person to the best of their judgement, considering all the relevant material which 
is available or gathered and issue an assessment order in Form ASMT-13. Section 29(2)
(c) of the SGST Act provides that the proper officer may cancel the registration of a 
person from such date, including any retrospective date, as he may deem fit, where, any 
registered person, other than a person specified in clause (b), has not furnished returns 
for a continuous period of six months.
Filing of returns is related to payment of tax as the due date for both the actions are 
same. This implies risk of non-payment of tax/penalty in the case of non-filers.
To assess the CTD’s action on late/non-filers of the returns, information 127 on non/late 
filers identified and action taken by three selected Sectors during July 2017 to March 
2021 were called for in Audit. Despite issue of several reminders, no information was 
furnished to the Audit till date (November 2023). In this regard, however, the CTD 
responded on 12 September 2022 that non-filers are not applicable under GST regime. 
The contention of the CTD is not acceptable as Section 29(2) (c) of the SGST Act clearly 
provides that the proper officer may cancel the registration of a person from such date, 
including any retrospective date, as he may deem fit, where any registered person, other 
than a person specified in clause (b), has not furnished returns for a continuous period 
of six months. 
126 Directorate General of Analytics and Risk Management.
127 such as number of non-filers identified, number of GSTR-3A issued, number of returns filed in 
pursuance of GSTR-3A and follow up action taken.

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
146
Further, Audit observed that out of the fourteen taxpayers selected for detailed audit, 
three128 had filed GSTR-1 return(s) after a delay of more than six months, three 129 had 
filed GSTR-3B returns after a delay of more than six months and two 130 taxpayers had 
not filed GSTR-9 and GSTR-9C131 till July 2023. Yet no action for cancellation of their 
registration was seen to have been initiated by the CTD (November 2023).
Recommendation 2: CTD may establish mechanism to regularly identify late-filers and 
non-filers and take necessary steps to recover tax, interest and late fee from them.
c)    Inadequacy of verification mechanism for cancellation of registration
Section 29(1) of SGST Act, 2017 stipulates that the proper officer may, either on his own 
motion, or on application filed by registered person or his legal heir cancel the registration 
having regard to circumstances where the business has been discontinued, change in 
constitution of business or the taxable person is no longer liable to be registered. 
Section 29(2) of the SGST Act, 2017 allows for suo moto cancellation of the registration 
of taxpayer by tax officer on the grounds of contravention of the Acts or Rules by the 
taxpayer, composition  taxpayers not filing return for three consecutive tax periods, 
normal taxpayers not filing return for continuous period of six months, registered persons 
not commencing business within six months from date of registration and registration 
obtained by means of fraud, willful misstatement or suppression of facts.
Rule 22(1) of SGST Rules, 2017 prescribes that where proper officer has reasons to 
believe that registration of a person is liable to be cancelled under Section 29, he shall 
issue a notice to such person in form GST REG-17, requiring him to show cause, within 
a period of seven working days, from the date of serving of such notice, as to why his 
registration should not be cancelled. 
To assess CTD’s action on cancellation of registration, information 132 pertaining to 
action taken for cancellation of registration by the selected Sectors during July 2017 to 
March 2021 was called for in Audit.
In response, the CTD (September 2022) furnished only a list of 577 taxpayers whose 
registrations were cancelled on voluntary basis. No information was furnished to Audit 
regarding suo-moto cancellations or cancellations initiated by the Sector officer in the 
State till date (November 2023). Therefore, in the absence of complete information 
on cancellation of registrations initiated by tax authorities and follow up action taken 
by them, the function of the Sectors on verification mechanism for cancellation of 
registration could not be assessed. 
128 (i) M/s Vodafone Mobile Services (GSTIN - 11AAACS4457Q1Z3) for the month of March 2018 (ii) M/s 
Sikkim Nationalised Transport Department (GSTIN - 11AAAGS1563B1ZX) for the month of November

Recommendation 3: CTD may establish mechanism to regularly identify taxpayers who 
have not filed GST returns for more than six months and take necessary steps to recover 
applicable tax, interest and late fee from them and cancel the registrations.
d)    Internal Audit
As per Section 65 of the SGST Act, the Commissioner or any officer authorised by 
him, by way of a general or a specific order, may undertake audit of any registered 
person for such period, at such frequency and in such manner as may be prescribed. 
Section 2 (13) of the SGST Act defines “Audit” as the examination of records, returns 
and other documents maintained or furnished by the registered person under this Act or 
the Rules made thereunder or under any other law for the time being in force to verify 
the correctness of turnover declared, taxes paid, refund claimed and input tax credit 
availed, and to assess his compliance with the provisions of this Act or the Rules made 
thereunder.
To assess the internal audit function of the CTD, information 133 on internal audit 
conducted by the selected Sectors during July 2017 to March 2021 was called for in 
Audit.
In response to audit requisitions, CTD (September 2022) furnished only a list of 10 
taxpayers134 selected for audit by the CTD under Section 65. However, details of internal 
audit undertaken in respect of these taxpayers not furnished. In the absence of requisite 
information/records, internal audit function could not be examined by Audit.
5.12.6.2  Audit findings on Centralised Audit
Audit analysed GST returns data pertaining to 2017-18 as made available by Goods 
and Services Tax Network (GSTN). Rule-based deviations and logical inconsistencies 
between GST returns filed by taxpayers were identified on a set of 12 parameters, which 
can be broadly categorised into two domains - ITC and Tax payments.
Out of the 13 prescribed GST returns 135, following basic returns that apply to normal 
taxpayers were considered for the purpose of identifying deviations, inconsistencies, 
and mismatches between GST returns/data:
	 GSTR-1: monthly return furnished by all normal and casual registered taxpayers 
making outward supplies of goods and services or both, and contains details of 
outward supplies of goods and services.
	 GSTR-3B: monthly summary return of outward supplies and input tax credit 
claimed, along with payment of tax by the taxpayer to be filed by all taxpayers except 
those specified under Section 39(1) of the Act. This is the return that populates the 
credit and debits in the Electronic Credit Ledger and debits in Electronic Cash 
Ledger.
	 GSTR-6: monthly return for Input Service Distributors providing the details of 
their distributed input tax credit and inward supplies.
133 Number of units planned for audit, Number of units covered, deviations, etc.
134  four from Industries, two from Services and four from Trading sectors.
135 GSTR-1, GSTR-3B, GSTR-4 (taxpayers under the Composition scheme), GSTR-5 (non-resident taxable 
person), GSTR-5A (Non-resident OIDAR service providers), GSTR-6 (Input service distributor), 
GSTR-7 (taxpayers deducting TDS), GSTR-8 (E-commerce operator), GSTR-9 (Annual Return), 
GSTR-10 (Final return), GSTR-11 (person having UIN and claiming a refund), CMP-08, and ITC-04 
(Statement to be filed by a principal/job-worker about details of goods sent to/received from a job-
worker).

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
148
	 GSTR-8: monthly return to be filed by the e-commerce operators who are required 
to deduct TCS (Tax collected at source) under GST, introduced in October 2018.
	 GSTR-9: annual return to be filed by all registered persons other than an Input 
Service Distributor (ISD), Tax Deductor at Source/Tax Collector at Source, Casual 
Taxable Person and Non-Resident taxpayer. This document contains the details of 
all supplies made and received under various tax heads (CGST, SGST and IGST) 
during the entire year along with turnover and audit details for the same. 
	 GSTR-9C: annual audit form for all taxpayers having a turnover above ₹ five crore 
in a particular financial year.  It is basically a reconciliation statement between 
the annual returns filed in GSTR-9 and the taxpayer’s audited annual financial 
statements.
	 GSTR-2A: a system-generated statement of inward supplies for a recipient. It 
contains the details of all B2B transactions of suppliers declared in their Form GSTR-
1/5, ISD details from GSTR-6, details from GSTR-7 and GSTR-8 respectively by 
the counterparty and import of goods from overseas on bill of entry, as received 
from ICEGATE Portal of Indian Customs.

Summary of Centralised Audit
Out of total 93 inconsistencies/mismatches pointed out by Audit, the CTD provided 
responses in case of 38 inconsistencies/mismatches amounting to ₹ 10.52 crore. Out 
of those 38 replies, Audit noticed deviations from the provisions of the SGST Act 
in 22 cases involving amount of ₹ 3.71 crore. Out of 22 compliance deviations, the 
CTD accepted audit observations and made recoveries in four cases with tax effect of 
₹ 0.24 crore and in 18 cases, the replies were not acceptable to Audit, hence, suitable 
rebuttal were provided.
Out of 38 cases, in 16 cases, the Department’s reply was acceptable to Audit, which 
includes 4 cases of data entry errors and 12 cases under other valid explanations.

(i) ITC mismatch between GSTR-3B and GSTR-2A
GSTR-2A is a purchase related dynamic tax return that is automatically generated 
for each business by the GST portal, whereas GSTR-3B is a monthly return in which 
summary of outward supplies along with ITC declared and payment of tax are self-
declared by the taxpayer. 
To analyse the veracity of ITC utilisation, relevant data were extracted from GSTR-
3B and GSTR-2A for the year 2017-18, and the ITC paid as per suppliers’ details was 
matched with the ITC credit availed by the taxpayer. 
Audit observed that in case of a taxpayer, M/s Intas Pharmaceuticals  (GSTIN - 
11AAACI5120L1Z1) under Industries Sector, ITC available as per GSTR-2A was ₹ 28.74 
crore and the ITC availed in table 4(A)(5) of GSTR-3B was ₹ 30.15 crore (including 
ITC ₹ 2.23 crore availed in the subsequent year 2018-19 from Table 8C of GSTR-9). 
This resulted in mismatch of ITC availed for ₹ 1.41 crore which was communicated to 
the CTD on 19 May 2022. In response, CTD furnished (April 2023) three justifications 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
154
for the excess ITC as follows:
(a) Total ITC for ₹ 29.82 crore and not ₹ 28.75 crore was being arrived after deducting 
import from SEZ. This conte ntion was not acceptable as the ITC availed figure 
arrived at by Audit did not include the figures of table 4(A) (1) of GSTR-3B 
(imports). 
(b) The excess ITC was further justified by furnishing the amended invoices pertaining 
to ITC of ₹ 0.97 crore. However, the original invoices in this case were not produced 
to Audit. Therefore, the Audit was unable to verify the claim of amendment of 
invoices in this case.
(c) There was ITC of ₹ 0.04 crore pertaining to purchase invoices of 2017-18 uploaded 
by the suppliers in 2018-19, this was checked in GSTR-1 of suppliers for 2018-19 
and found correct. Therefore, the observation is amended by Audit to that extent. 
Hence, excess ITC of ₹ 1.37 crore was due to the state exchequer from M/s Intas 
Pharmaceuticals.
(ii) ITC availed under RCM without payment of tax in GSTR-3B/ GSTR-9
Under the Reverse Charge Mechanism, the liability to pay tax is fixed on the recipient 
of supply of goods or services instead of the supplier or provider in respect of certain 
categories of goods or services or both under Section 9(3) or Section 9(4) of the SGST 
Act, 2017 and under sub-section (3) or sub-section (4) of Section 5 of the SGST Act, 2017.
GSTR-9 is an annual return to be filed once for each financial year, by the registered 
taxpayers who were regular taxpayers, including SEZ units and SEZ developers. The 
taxpayers are required to furnish details of purchases, sales, input tax credit or refund 
claimed, or demand created etc.
To analyse the veracity of ITC availed on tax paid under Reverse Charge Mechanism 
(RCM) for the year 2017-18, the datasets pertaining to GSTR-3B and annual return 
GSTR-9 were compared to check whether the ITC availed on RCM was restricted to the 
extent of tax paid. 
Audit observed that in case of a taxpayer, M/s ATC Telecom Infrastructure Pvt. Ltd. 
(GSTIN - 11AACCT1282E1ZW) under Service Sector, ITC available in table 3.1(d) 
of GSTR-3B was ₹ 0.03 crore and the ITC availed in table 4A (2) & (3) of GSTR-
3B was ₹ 0.31 crore resulting in mismatch of ITC availed for ₹ 0.28 crore. This was 
communicated to the CTD on 19 May 2022 followed by reminders on 19 July 2022 and 
31 August 2022. No response was received from the CTD (November 2023).
(iii) Short payment of tax under RCM vs. ITC availed in GSTR-3B/GSTR-9
The extent of availing of ITC under RCM for the year 2017-18 without discharging 
equivalent tax liability or, in other words, short payment of tax under RCM was analysed 
by comparing the datasets pertaining to GSTR-3B and annual return GSTR-9 to check 
whether the tax has been discharged fully on the activities/transactions under RCM. In 
cases where GSTR-9 was filed, the RCM payments in Table 4G 140 was compared with 
ITC availed in Table 6C, 6D and 6F. In cases, where GSTR-9 was not available, RCM 
payments in GSTR-3B Table 3.1(d) 141 was compared with GSTR-3B 4(A) (2) 142 and 
4(A) (3)143.
140 Inward supplies on which tax is to be paid on reverse charge basis.
141 Inward supplies (liable to be reverse charge).
142 Import of services.
143 Inward supplies liable to be reverse charge other than Import of Goods and Services.

Chapter V: Revenue Sector
155
Audit observed that in case of a taxpayer, M/s Anu Enterprises  (GSTIN - 
11BKSPP2027H2Z7) under Trading Sector, the RCM payments in table 4G of GSTR-
9 was nil (GSTR-3B also shows Nil RCM payment) and the ITC availed in table 
(6C+6D+6F) of GSTR-9 was ₹ 36,947. This resulted in excess availment of ITC on 
RCM without payment of tax amounting to ₹ 36,947 which was communicated to the 
CTD on 19 May 2022. No response was received from the CTD (November 2023). 
(iv) Incorrect availment of ISD credit
To analyse whether the ITC availed by the taxpayer is more than that transferred by the 
Input Service Distributor (ISD), ITC availed as declared in the returns of the taxpayer is 
compared with the ITC transferred by the ISD in their GSTR-6. 
In case of a taxpayer, M/s Alkem Laboratories Ltd. (GSTIN - 11AABCA9521E1ZA) 
under Industries Sector, Audit observed that the ITC availed in table 6G of GSTR-9 was 
₹ 1.11 crore and the ITC transferred by the ISD in table (5A+8A+9A) of GSTR-6 was ₹ 
0.55 crore. This resulted in excess availment of ITC transferred by the ISD amounting 
to ₹ 0.56 crore which was communicated to the CTD on 19 May 2022.
The CTD replied (April 2023) that the ISD transfer of ₹ 0.56 crore was not reflected in 
GSTR-6 table 5A due to technical glitch in GST portal. The reply is not acceptable as 
no documentary evidence was furnished to prove that excess ITC availed under ISD for 
₹ 0.56 crore was eligible.
(v) Mismatch of ITC availed between annual returns and books of accounts
Table 12 of GSTR-9C reconciles ITC declared in annual return (GSTR-9) with ITC 
availed as per audited annual financial statement or books of accounts. Column 12F of 
this table deals with unreconciled ITC.
The certified reconciliation statement submitted by the taxpayer as required under the 
Rule 80(3) of SGST Rules in form GSTR-9C for the year 2017-18 was analysed at data 
level to review the extent of identified mismatch in ITC declared in the Annual Return 
with the Financial Statements. 
In case of a taxpayer, M/s Yuksom Breweries Ltd. (GSTIN - 11AAACY3532C1Z0) 
under Industries Sector, unreconciled ITC of ₹ 2.19 crore declared in Table 12F of 
GSTR-9C, being ITC availed in GST returns in excess of eligible ITC based on financial 
statements was noticed and communicated to the CTD on 19 May 2022. In response to 
audit queries pertaining to GSTR-9C, the CTD intimated (September 2022) that data 
pertaining to GSTR-9C was in the process of integration with State system and the 
above query would be addressed after completion of the integration. No further reply 
was received from the CTD (November 2023).
(vi)	 Reconciliation	between	ITC	availed	in	annual	returns	with	expenses	in	financial	
statements
Table 14 of GSTR-9C reconciles ITC declared in annual return (GSTR-9) with ITC 
availed on expenses as per audited annual financial statement or books of accounts. 
Column 14T of this table deals with unreconciled ITC.
Certified reconciliation statement submitted by the taxpayer as required under the Rule 
80(3) of SGST Rules in form GSTR-9C for the year 2017-18 was analysed at data level 
to review the extent of identified mismatch in ITC declared in the Annual Return with 
the expenses reported in the Financial Statements.

156
In case of a taxpayer, M/s Goodwill Enterprises (GSTIN - 11ALHPP0553F1ZJ) under 
Trading Sector, unreconciled ITC of ₹ 3.12 crore declared in Table 14T of GSTR-9C, 
being ITC availed in GST returns in excess of eligible ITC based on expenses reported 
in financial statements was noticed and communicated to the CTD on 19 May 2022 
followed by reminders on 19 July 2022 and 31 August 2022. No response was received 
from the CTD (November 2023).
(vii)  Mismatch in turnover declared in GSTR-9C Table 5R
Table 5 of GSTR-9C is the reconciliation of turnover declared in audited annual financial 
statement with turnover declared in annual turnover (GSTR-9). Column 5R of this table 
captures the unreconciled turnover between the annual return GSTR-9, and that declared 
in the Financial Statement for the year after the requisite adjustments. 
The certified reconciliation statement submitted by the taxpayer as required under Rule 
80(3) of SGST Rules in form GSTR-9C for the year 2017-18 was analysed at data level 
to review the extent of identified mismatch in turnover reported in the Annual Return 
vis-à-vis the Financial Statements. The unreconciled amount in cases where the turnover 
declared in GSTR-9 is less than the financial statement indicates non-reporting, under-
reporting, short-reporting, omission, error in reporting of supplies leading to evasion 
or short payment of tax. It could also be a case of non-reporting of both taxable and 
exempted supplies. 
Audit query on unreconciled turnover in Table 5R of GSTR-9C for ₹ 6.36 crore was 
issued in respect of a taxpayer, M/s Sikkim University (GSTIN - 11AAABS2047P1ZH) 
under Service Sector and communicated to the CTD on 19 May 2022 followed by 
reminders on 19 July 2022 and 31 August 2022. No response was received from the 
CTD (November 2023).
(viii) Mismatch in taxable turnover declared in GSTR-9C Table 7G
Table 7 of GSTR-9C is the reconciliation of taxable turnover. Column 7G of this table 
captures the unreconciled taxable turnover between the annual return GSTR-9 and that 
declared in the financial statement for the year after the requisite adjustments.
The certified reconciliation statement submitted by the taxpayer as required under the 
Rule 80(3) of SGST Rules in Form GSTR-9C for the year 2017-18 was analysed at 
data level to review the extent of identified mismatch in taxable turnover reported in 
the Annual Return vis-à-vis the Financial Statements. The unreconciled amount in cases 
where the taxable turnover in GSTR-9 is less than the financial statement indicates 
non-reporting, under-reporting, short-reporting, omission, error in reporting of taxable 
supplies. It could also be on account of non-reporting of both taxable and exempted 
supplies.
Audit query on unreconciled taxable turnover in Table 7G of GSTR-9C for ₹ 85.44 crore 
was issued to the CTD in respect of a taxpayer, M/s E-cool Gaming Solutions (GSTIN 
-11AABCE3886A1Z6) under Service Sector on 19 May 2022 followed by reminders on 
19 July 2022 and 31 August 2022. No response was received from the CTD (November 
2023).
(ix)  Mismatch in tax paid between books of accounts and returns 
The certified reconciliation statement submitted by the taxpayer as required under Rule 
80(3) of SGST Rules in form GSTR-9C for the year 2017-18 was analysed at data 

Chapter V: Revenue Sector
157
level to review the extent of identified mismatch in tax paid between the Annual Return 
and the boo ks of accounts. Table 9 of the form 9C attempts to reconcile the tax paid 
by segregating the turnover rate-wise and comparing it with the tax discharged as per 
annual return GSTR-9. 
The unreconciled amounts could potentially indicate tax levied at incorrect rates, incorrect 
depiction of taxable turnover as exempt or vice versa or incorrect levy of CGST/SGST/
IGST. There can also be situations wherein supplies/tax declared are reduced through 
amendments (net of debit notes/credit notes) in respect of the 2017-18 transactions 
carried out in the subsequent year from April to September 2018. Consequential interest 
payments - both short payments and payments under incorrect heads also need to be

(x) Unsettled liabilities
GSTR-1 depicts the monthly details of outward supplies of Goods or Services. These 
details also assessed by the taxpayer and mentioned in annual return GSTR-9 in the 
relevant columns. Further, taxable value and tax paid thereof also shown in GSTR-3B. 
To analyse the undischarged tax liability, relevant data were extracted from GSTR-1 and 
GSTR-9 for the year 2017-18 and the tax payable in these returns was compared with 
the tax paid as declared in GSTR-9. Where GSTR-9 was not available, a comparison 
of tax payable between GSTR-1 and GSTR-3B was resorted to. The amendments and 
advance adjustments declared in GSTR-1 and 9 were also considered for this purpose. 
During Audit, it was observed that in case of a taxpayer, M/s Annapurna Trading 
Company (GSTIN - 11ABBFA0749A1ZE) under Service Sector, the tax payable in 
Table 4 to 11 of GSTR-1 was ₹ 1.86 crore and the tax payable declared in tables 3.1(a) 
& 3.1 (b) of GSTR-3B was ₹ 0.95 crore. This resulted in mismatch of tax liability of 
₹ 0.91 crore between GSTR-1 and GSTR-3B which was communicated to the CTD on 
19 May 2022 followed by reminders on 19 July 2022 and 31 August 2022. No response 
was received from the CTD (November 2023).
(xi)	 GSTR-3B	not	filed	but	GSTR-1	available
GSTR-3B return under Rule 61(5) of SGST Rule is the only instrument through which 
the tax liability is offset and ITC is availed.
Effort was made through data analysis to identify those taxpayers who had not filed 
GSTR-3B but had filed GSTR-1 or whose GSTR-2A was available. The very availability 
of GSTR-1 and 2A coupled with non-filing of GSTR-3B indicates that the taxpayers had 
undertaken/carried on the business during the period but had not discharged their tax 
liability. It may also include cases of irregular passing on of ITC.
One taxpayer M/s Punnya Raj Shakya (GSTIN - 11EFHPS3962D1ZI) under Industries 
Sector had filed GSTR-1 with tax liability of ₹ 3,024 but did not file GSTR-3B during 
2017-18. As GSTR-3B is the return through which liability is discharged. Therefore, 
availability of GSTR-1 and non-filing of GSTR-3B indicated possibility of non-payment 
of tax by the taxpayer.

This was intimated to the CTD on 19 May 2022 and the reasons for the inconsistencies 
and the details of action taken, if any, under Section 61 of the SGST Act was called for in 
Audit. In response, the CTD stated that the taxpayer’s registration was already cancelled 
by Tax authority on 29 October 2021 and further stated that the taxpayer had discharged 
the liability along with interest totalling ₹ 5,776 (tax ₹ 3,024 + interest ₹  2,752) on 8 
September 2022. However, it was only at the instance of Audit, action was taken and 
taxpayer had discharged the liability. 
(xii) Short/Non-payment of interest
Section 50 of the SGST Act stipulates that every person liable to pay tax in accordance 
with the provisions of this Act or the Rules made there under but fails to pay the tax or 
any part thereof to the Government within the period prescribed, shall for the period for 
which the tax or any part thereof remains unpaid, pay interest at the rate notified.
The extent of short/non-payment of interest on account of delayed remittance of tax 
during 2017-18 was identified using the tax paid details in GSTR-3B and the date of 
filing of the GSTR-3B. Only the net tax liability (cash component) had been considered 
to work out the interest payable.
Audit observed that M/s Prahlad Gurung (GSTIN- 11AYCPG8739N2Z5) under Service 
Sector filed returns (GSTR-3B) with delay which were pertaining to the tax period 2017-
18 involving tax liability amounting to ₹ 1.21 crore but no interest was paid on such 
delayed payment of tax. This resulted in non-payment of interest of ₹  0.53 crore. The 
issue was communicated to the CTD on 19 May 2022 followed by reminders on 19 July 
2022 and 31 August 2022. No response was received from the CTD (November 2023).

5.12.6.3  Detailed Audit 
In a self-assessment regime, the onus of compliance with the law is on the taxpayer. The 
role of the CTD is to establish and maintain an efficient tax administration mechanism 
to provide oversight. With a finite level of resources, for an effective tax administration, 
to ensure compliance with law and collection of revenue, an efficient governance 
mechanism is essential. An IT driven compliance model enables maintaining a non-
discretionary regime of governance on scale and facilitates a targeted approach to 
enforce compliance.
From an external audit perspective, Audit also focused on a data-driven risk-based 
approach. Thus, apart from identifying inconsistencies/deviations in GST returns 
through data analysis, a detailed audit of GST returns was also conducted as a part of 
this review. A risk-based sample of 14 taxpayers was selected for this part of the review. 
The methodology adopted was to initially conduct a desk review of GST returns and 
financial statements filed by the taxpayers as part of the GSTR-9C and other records 
available in the back-end system to identify potential risk areas, inconsistencies/
deviations, and red flags. Desk Review was carried out at the office of the Accountant 
General (Audit), Sikkim. Based on desk review results, detailed audit was conducted 
in the office of the Commissioner, CTD, Government of Sikkim, by requisitioning 
corresponding granular records of taxpayers such as purchase invoices, sales invoices, 
and other relevant documents to identify causative factors of the identified risks and to 
evaluate compliance by taxpayers. The details of risks identified are given in Appendix 
5.6 and 5.7.
In reply to audit requisitions for information/documents like annual financial statements 
and purchase/sales invoices required for detailed audit, the Department furnished records 
only in case of one taxpayer out of 14 selected taxpayers.
In seven cases, no information/records were furnished by the CTD as required by the 
Audit. The details are in Appendix 5.8 (a) and the top three cases are listed in Table 5.21. 

Chapter V: Revenue Sector

In case of six out of 14 taxpayers, records were partially produced. The details of the 
partial production are given in Appendix 5.8 (b)  and the top three cases are listed in 
Table 5.22. Consequently, in these partially produced cases, Audit was restricted to the 
information available in the returns filed by the taxpayers. Thus, Audit could not assess 
eligibility of ITC claimed and correctness of classification of goods and rates at which 
GST was paid, which constituted a significant scope limitation.

2.04
Audit	findings	-	Detailed	Audit
As brought out in the previous paragraphs, detailed audit involved a desk review of GST 
returns and other basic records to identify risks and red flags, which were followed up 
by field audit to identify the extent of non-compliance by taxpayers and action taken by 
the CTD. Noncompliance by taxpayers at various stages ultimately impacts the veracity 
of returns filed, utilisation of ITC and discharge of tax payments. As such, the audit 
findings are categorised under: (a) Returns (b) Utilisation of ITC (c) Discharge of tax 
liability and (d) Other findings.
 (a)  Audit findings related to GST Returns
The audit observations pertaining to filing of returns – delay in filing, non-payment of 
interest and late fee, etc. are brought out below:
(i)  Non-payment of interest taxpayers
Section 39(1) of SGST Act 2017 read with Rule 61 of SGST Rules provides that the 
return of inward and outward supplies of goods or services or both, input tax credit 
availed, tax payable, tax paid and such other particulars as may be prescribed are required 
to be filed on or before the twentieth day of the month succeeding such calendar month 
or part thereof.

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
162
Further, as per Section 50(1) of SGST Act, the rate of interest on delayed payment of 
tax is 18 per cent.
Audit observed that in nine out of 14 sample cases, taxpayers had filed returns with 
delays, but did not pay the interest liability of ₹ 1.05 crore for such delayed payment 
of tax. Cases of non-payment of interest is detailed in Appendix 5.9 and the details of 
calculation is given in Appendix 5.9(a).
Out of the nine cases intimated to the CTD on 14 July 2022, in five cases144, no response 
was furnished by the CTD (November 2023); in two cases 145, the CTD stated that 
the taxpayer had discharged the interest liability of ₹ 44,595.12 and ₹ 7,783.64, but 
no supporting documents were furnished to Audit and in remaining two cases 146, the 
taxpayers paid the interest of ₹ 2,051and ₹ 4,645 at the instance of Audit.
(ii) Non-payment of Late fee by taxpayers 
GSTR-1: Section 37 of SGST Act read with Rule 59 of SGST Rules provides for filing 
of monthly return of outward supplies in GSTR-1 by the regular taxpayer and the returns 
are to be filed on or before the 10 th day of the month succeeding such calendar month 
or part thereof. Section 47(1) of the SGST Act provides that any registered person who 
fails to furnish the details of outward or inward supplies required under section 37 or 
section 38 or returns required under section 39 or section 45 by the due date shall pay a 
late fee of one hundred rupees for every day during which such failure continues subject 
to a maximum amount of five thousand rupees.
Further as per Notification No. 74/2019, GST/Sikkim, dated 26th December 2019, the 
amount of late fee payable shall stand waived for the taxpayers who failed to furnish the 
details of outward supplies in form GSTR-1 for the period from July 2017 to November 
2019 by the due date but furnishes the said details in form GSTR-1 between the period 
19 December 2019 to 10 January 2020.
GSTR-3B: Section 39(1) of SGST Act read with Rule 61 of SGST Rules provides 
that the return of inward and outward supplies of goods or services or both, input tax 
credit availed, tax payable, tax paid and such other particulars as may be prescribed are 
required to be filed on or before the twentieth day of the month succeeding such calendar 
month or part thereof.
As per CTD’s notification no. 76/2018, dated 31-12-2018, the amount of late fee payable 
under Section 47 of the SGST Act shall stand waived for the registered persons who 
failed to furnish the return in FORM GSTR-3B for the months of July 2017 to September 
2018 by the due date but furnishes the said return between the period from 22 December 
2018 to 31 March 2019.
As per CTD’s notification no. 19/2021, dated 1 June 2021, for the registered persons 
who failed to furnish the return in FORM GSTR-3B for the months/quarter of July 
2017 to April 2021, by the due date but furnished the said return between the period 
from the 1 st day of June 2021 to the 31 st day of August 2021, the total amount of late 
fee under section 47 of the said Act shall stand waived which is in excess of five 
hundred rupees.
144 (i) M/s Sikkim Organics (ii) M/s Sikkim Agrochem (iii) M/s Sikkim Nationalised (iv) M/s Punj Lloyd 
under and (v) M/s Rajeev Electronic.
145 (i) M/s Golden gaming and (ii) M/s Scorpion Containers.
146 (i) M/s SIMFED and (ii) M/s Vikky Enterprise.

Audit observed that out of 14 sample taxpayers, three had filed GSTR-1/3B/9 with delays, 
but late fee payable ₹ 0.02 crore was not discharged by them. Cases of nonpayment of 
late fee given in Appendix 5.10 and the calculation of late fee in respect of GSTR-3B, 1 
and 9 are given in Appendix 5.9(a), 5.11 (a) and 5.11 (b) respectively.
The issue was intimated to the CTD on 10 February 2023. In response, the CTD replied 
(6 April 2023) that Notification No. 75/2018147 dated 31 December 2018 had waived the 
late fee under Section 47 of SGST Act 2017 for the taxpayers who failed to furnish the 
details of outward supplies in form GSTR-1 for the period from July 2017 to September 
2018 by the due date but furnishes the said details in form GSTR-1 between the period 
19 December 2018 to 31 March 2019. 
The reply is not acceptable as the late fee pointed out by Audit was calculated as per the 
latest notification No.74/2019-GST/Sikkim dated 26th December 2019. The late fees 
were calculated where taxpayers had filed GSTR-1 after due date of 10 January 2020. 
No further response was received from the Division in case of late fee on delay in filing 
of GSTR-3B and GSTR-9 (November 2023).
(iii)     Non-filing of Return
As per CTD’s Notification 47/2019 dated 9 October 2019, every registered person 
whose aggregate turnover in a financial year does not exceed two crore rupees shall 
have the option to furnish the annual returns in GSTR-9 under Section 44(1) read with 
Rule 80(1). 
As per SGST Rule 80(3), every registered person whose aggregate turnover during a 
financial year exceeds two crore rupees shall get his accounts audited as specified under 
sub-section (5) of section 35 and he shall furnish a copy of audited annual accounts and 
a reconciliation statement, duly certified, in FORM GSTR-9C, electronically through 
the common portal either directly or through a Facilitation Centre notified by the 
Commissioner. 
Audit observed that two out of total 14 sample taxpayers - M/s Sikkim Nationalised 
Transport (GSTN - 11AAAGS1563B1ZX) under Service Sector and M/s Sikkim 
Organics (GSTIN - 11ABBFS1390P1Z2) under Industries Sector with turnover of 
₹ 16.29 crore and ₹ 33.13 crore respectively did not file GSTR-9 (Annual return) and 
GSTR-9C (reconciliation statement).
The matter was intimated to the CTD on 14 July 2022 and the reasons for the non-filing 
of returns and the details of action taken, if any, by the Division under Section 61 of the 
SGST Act was also called for. 
In response, the CTD intimated (12 September 2022) that audit under Section 65 had 
been initiated and further reply would be provided after completion of audit. No further 
response was received from the CTD (November 2023).
(iv)     Filing of returns without showing proper tax liability
Section 39(1) of SGST Act read with Rule 61 of SGST Rules provides that the return 
of inward and outward supplies of goods or services or both, input tax credit availed, 
tax payable, tax paid and such other particulars as may be prescribed are required to be 
filed on or before the twentieth day of the month succeeding such calendar month or part 
thereof.
147 Corresponding notification issued by the CTD is not available.

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
164
Further, as per Section 13 of the SGST Act 2017, the liability to pay tax on services arise 
at the time of supply of the services which is determined as earliest of: 
a)  the date of issue of invoice by the supplier, if the invoice is issued within the period 
prescribed under sub-section (2) of Section 31 or the date of receipt of payment, 
whichever is earlier; or
(b)  the date of provision of service, if the invoice is not issued within the period 
prescribed under sub-section (2) of Section 31 or the date of receipt of payment, 
whichever is earlier; or
(c)  the date on which the recipient shows the receipt of services in his books of account, 
in a case where the provisions of clause (a) or clause (b) do not apply:
Scrutiny of GSTR-3B revealed that one taxpayer viz. M/s Sikkim Nationalised Transport 
(GSTN-11AAAGS1563B1ZX), a transport service provider whose transaction takes 
place on the daily basis, had declared tax liability only in March 2018 and filed nil 
returns during the period from November 2017 to February 2018.
Further, as per the records furnished (25 January 2023), it was noticed that the taxpayer 
had generated 1523 invoices from November 2017 to March 2018 with monthly totals 
ranging from ₹ 2.29 crore to ₹ 6.42 crore as detailed in Appendix 5.12.
On this being pointed out (July 2022), the CTD stated (6 April 2023) that M/s Sikkim 
Nationalised Transport’s appeal was sub-judice in the appellate tribunal regarding GST 
registration during the transition period from Service tax to GST. There was no clarity 
whether SNT was liable to pay SGST as goods transport agency and rent a cab. The 
Tribunal ruled and issued the order dated 01 September 2017, but the sealed order was 
received by SNT only on February 2018.
The reply is not acceptable as SNT had been discharging tax liability only in March even 
in the subsequent years (2018-19 and 2019-20) and in 2020-21, the tax liability was not 
discharged at all, even in March 2021.
(v) Inconsistency between CGST and SGST rate in returns 
The CGST and the SGST rates are levied at equal rates as per schedules notified by the 
Central and the State governments as per provisions contained in Section 9 of the CGST 
Act 2017 and State GST Act 2017 respectively. 
Audit checked the GST liability figures of 14 sample taxpayers and observed that the 
SGST liability figures were more than the CGST liability figures by ₹ 46,035 in case of 
one taxpayer viz. M/s V odafone Mobile services (GSTIN - 11AAACS4457Q1Z3) under 
Service Sector (Details given in Appendix 5.13).
This was intimated to the CTD (4 January 2023) with request to furnish the reasons for 
inconsistency and action taken by the CTD, if any, under Section 61 of the SGST Act. 
No response was received from the CTD till date (November 2023). 
(b)  Audit findings relating to Utilisation of Input Tax Credit 
Input Tax Credit (ITC) means the Goods and Services Tax (GST) paid by a taxable 
person on purchase of goods and/or services that are used in the course or furtherance of 
business. To avoid cascading effect of taxes, credit of taxes paid on input supplies can 
be used to set-off for payment of taxes on outward supplies.

Chapter V: Revenue Sector
165
The audit observations pertaining to utilisation of ITC are as follows:
(i)  ITC mismatch between GSTR-2A and GSTR-3B
Section 16(2) of the SGST Act prescribes the conditions for availing ITC. The 
prerequisites for availing ITC are:
•	 Taxpayer should be in possession of tax invoice or any other specified tax paying 
document
•	 Taxpayer has received the goods or services
•	 Tax has actually been paid by the supplier 
•	 Taxpayer has furnished the return to avail the ITC 
•	 The value of the goods or services along with the tax should have been paid to the 
supplier within 180 days from the date of issue of invoice. 
Rule 36 of SGST Rules prescribe the documentary requirements for claiming ITC. A 
taxpayer can avail ITC based on (a) Invoice issued by a supplier of goods or services or 
both, (b) Invoice issued by recipient along with proof of payment of tax, (c) A debit note 
issued by supplier, (d) Bill of entry or similar document prescribed under Customs Act, 
(e) Revised invoice and (f) Document issued by Input Service Distributor. No ITC shall 
be availed beyond September of the following financial year to which invoice pertains 
or date of filing of annual return, whichever is earlier.
To check the correctness of the ITC claim, Audit compared the ITC claimed by the 
taxpayers with the ITC available in GSTR-2A. Out of total 14 sample cases, mismatch 
were observed in 11 cases where taxpayers had availed excess ITC of ₹ 13.90 crore. 
(Details given in Appendix 5.14)
To check the veracity of the ITC claim, Audit called for purchase invoices of selected 
months from the CTD on 29 June 2022. The same was not furnished to the Audit till 
July 2023. 
Out of 11 cases, no response was furnished by the CTD (November 2023) in six cases148, 
however, clarifications were furnished (6 April 2023) by the CTD in respect of five 
taxpayers. None of the replies furnished were acceptable to Audit as summarised in 
Table 5.23 and further observation were made as detailed in Appendix 5.14 (a).

(ii)  ITC availed under RCM without payment of tax in GSTR-3B/GSTR-9
Section 9(4) of the SGST Act, 2017 stipulates that the State tax in respect of the supply 
of taxable goods or services or both by a supplier, who is not registered, to a registered 
person shall be paid by such person on reverse charge basis as the recipient and all the 
provisions of this Act shall apply to such recipient as if he is the person liable for paying 
the tax in relation to the supply of such goods or services or both. Further Rule 36(b) of 
the SGST Rules 2017, provides that ITC under Reverse Charge Mechanism (RCM) can 
be claimed subject to the payment of tax. 
To check the veracity of ITC claim under RCM, Audit checked the ITC availed under 
RCM declared in Tables 6C, 6D and 6F of Annual return GSTR-9 and compared that 
with tax paid under RCM figure of GSTR-3B. Out of total 14 sample cases, mismatches 
were observed in two cases where taxpayers had availed excess ITC under RCM for 
₹ 0.02 crore along with interest of ₹ 0.02 crore149. (Details given in Appendix 5.15)
149 calculated at 24 percent for four years (2018-19, 2019-20, 2020-21 and 2021-22).

on 31 August 2022.Out of the two cases, no response was furnished in one case ( M/s 
Punj Lloyd). In case of M/s Sikkim Agrochem (GSTIN: 11AAMCS5267J1Z3), the CTD 
responded (6 April 2023) that the excess ITC under RCM availed in July 2017 was 
reversed in September 2018 which was not disclosed in GSTR 9 by mistake. Although 
reversal of ₹ 0.45 crore was made in September 2018, no documentary evidence was 
furnished to support that the said reversal included the excess RCM ITC of 2017-18. 
(iii)  Incorrect availment of ISD credit
Section 2(61) of the SGST Act defines “Input Service Distributor” (ISD) as an office of 
the supplier of goods or services or both which receives tax invoices towards the receipt 
of input services and issues a prescribed document for the purposes of distributing 
the ITC to a supplier of taxable goods or services or both having the same Permanent 
Account Number (PAN) as that of the said office. Section 20 of the Act prescribes the 
manner of distribution of credit by ISD. As per Section 20(2), ISD may distribute the 
credit available for distribution in the same month in which it is availed. ISD shall issue 
invoice in accordance with the provisions made under Rule 39(1) of the SGST Rules.
In this regard, Rule 54 (1A)(a) of the SGST Rules provides for a registered person, 
having the same PAN and State code as an ISD, to issue an invoice or, as the case may 
be, a credit or debit note to transfer the credit of common input services to the ISD.
Out of 14 sample taxpayers, Audit observed mismatch in case of one taxpayer i.e. M/s 
Alembic Pharmaceuticals (GSTIN - 11AAICA5591M1ZG) for ₹ 0.17 crore. The taxpayer 
received ITC of ₹ 1.27 crore from ISD through GSTR-6 whereas the taxpayer had declared 
an amount of ₹ 1.44 crore as ITC received from ISD in Table 6G of GSTR9. This indicated 
excess availing of ITC under ISD by ₹ 0.17 crore along with the interest of ₹ 0.17 crore150.
This was intimated to CTD on 14 July 2022 and the reasons for inconsistencies and details 
of action taken, if any, under Section 61 of the SGST Act was also called for. In response, 
the CTD intimated (12 September 2022) that as GSTR-6 of the ISD was not integrated 
with the State GST portal, data would be obtained from the concerned taxpayer and 
manual verification would be conducted. The CTD further added that similar verification 
shall be conducted for other taxpayers who have availed credit from ISDs. The report of 
verification would be forwarded to Audit. It was further replied (6 April 2023) and clarified 
that in case of M/s Alembic Pharmaceuticals, the difference amount of ₹ 0.173 crore was 
due to ISD invoice of ₹ 0.169 crore and that there was only difference of ₹ 0.004 crore. 
The reply is not acceptable as the taxpayer had furnished ISD invoice of ₹ 0.169 crore 
only which was not sufficient to establish veracity of the entire ITC of ₹ 1.44 crore 
availed under ISD. In absence of adequate document ary evidence for the entire ITC of 
₹ 1.44 crore, Audit could not vouchsafe that the ITC of ₹ 0.169 crore was in addition to 
the ITC amount of ₹ 1.27 crore received through GSTR-6.
(iv) Reconciliation between ITC availed between Annual Return with expenses in 
financial statement

declared in their Annual Financial Statements (AFS), Audit examined the Table 12F 
and 14T of GSTR-9C filed by the taxpayer. The figures in table 12 and 14 of GSTR-9C

(v) Short reversal of ITC
Section 17(2) of the SGST Act, 2017 stipulates that where the goods or services or 
both are used by the registered person partly for effecting taxable supplies including 
zero-rated supplies under this Act or under the Integrated Goods and Services Tax Act 
and partly for effecting exempt supplies under the said Acts, the amount of credit shall 
be restricted to so much of the input tax as is attributable to the said taxable supplies 
including zero-rated supplies.
 Rule 42 of SGST Rules 2017, provides for the manner to determine input tax credit in 
respect of inputs or input services, which attract the provisions of sub-section (1) or sub-
section (2) of section 17.
Rule 42 (i) of the SGST Rules 2017 stipulates that ‘the amount of input tax credit 
attributable towards exempt supplies, denoted as D1 and calculated as D1= (E÷F) × 
C2 where, ‘E’ is the aggregate value of exempt supplies during the tax period, ‘F’ is the 
total turnover in the State of the registered person during the tax period and C2 is the 
common credit. 
151 (i) M/s SIMFED and (ii) M/s STCS.

Chapter V: Revenue Sector
169
Rule 43 of SGST Rules 2017 provides for the manner to determine input tax credit in 
respect of capital goods, which attract the provisions of sub-section (1) or sub-section 
(2) of section 17 and Rule 43(g) provides that the amount of common credit attributable 
towards exempted supplies, be denoted as Te, and calculated as Te= (E÷ F) x Tr where, 
E is the aggregate value of exempt supplies, made, during the tax period, and F is the 
total turnover of the registered person during the tax period. ‘Tr’ is the amount of input 
tax credit, at the beginning of a tax period, on common capital goods.
Audit observed that out of 14 sample taxpayers, one taxpayer i.e. M/s Zydus Healthcare 
(GSTIN - 11AAACG1895Q1ZB) under Industries Sector did not reverse ITC of  
₹ 0.11 crore pertaining to common inputs/input services/capital goods under Rule 42 
and 43 used for effecting Nil/Exempted supplies. The details are as given below:

(c)  Audit findings relating to discharge of tax liability
The audit findings pertaining to undischarged tax liability emanating from tax liabilities 
compared between returns (GSTR-1 and GSTR-9/GSTR-3B) and examination of the 
reconciliation statement (GSTR-9C) were as follows:
(i)  Unsettled liabilities
To check if there were any unsettled liabilities, the tax liability were calculated on 
the basis of details contained in GSTR-1 (outward supplies) and was compared with 
the GSTR-9 tax liability. Where GSTR-9 was not filed, the GSTR-1 tax liability was 
compared with the GSTR-3B tax liability. Then to identify the short payment of tax, the 
greater of the tax liabilities as per GSTR-1 and GSTR-9/GSTR-3B was compared with 
the tax payment declared in GSTR-9.
Audit observed that out of 14 sample cases, in eight cases tax paid were less than the tax 
liability. The total difference was ₹ 0.57 crore. (Details given in Appendix 5.17, 5.17(a) 
and 5.17 (b))
The discrepancies were communicated to the CTD on 14 July 2022. Out of the eight 
cases intimated to CTD, no response was furnished in six cases (November 2023) and in 
case of two taxpayers viz. M/s State Trading Corporation of Sikkim and M/s SIMFED, 
the CTD intimated (5 December 2022) that they had paid the excess liability of ₹ 0.12 
crore (on 22 November 2022) and ₹ 14,164 (on 2 September 2022) through DRC-03. 
Thus, the amount was paid on being pointed out by Audit.
(ii) Mismatch of turnover declared in GSTR-9C table 5R and 7G of ₹ 0.11 crore 
and mismatch of tax paid between book of accounts and returns (GSTR-9C 
table 9R) 
The certified reconciliation statement submitted by the taxpayer as required under the 
Rule 80(3) of SGST Rules in form GSTR-9C for the year 2017-18 was analysed at data 
level to review the extent of identified mismatch in total turnover, taxable turnover and 
taxes paid reported in the Annual Return vis-à-vis the Financial Statements. 
Table 5R of the Form 9C captures the unreconciled turnover, Table 7G of the Form 
9C captures the unreconciled taxable turnover and Table 9 of the Form 9C attempts 
to reconcile the tax paid between the annual return GSTR-9 and that declared in the 
financial statement for the year after the requisite adjustments. 
To check if the taxpayers had any unreconciled turnover and tax payments, the Audit 
examined the relevant Tables- 5, 7 and 9 of GSTR-9C. Audit observed that there was 
unreconciled turnover in case of two taxpayers of ₹ 0.11 crore and unreconciled tax 
payment in case of two taxpayers of ₹ 0.16 crore. Details given in Appendix 5.18(a) and 
5.18 (b).
Out of the four cases mentioned above (two for unreconciled turnover and two 
for unreconciled tax payment), reply was furnished (6 April 2023) in respect of 
only one taxpayer. As per the reply, M/s Civil Engineers Enterprises (GSTIN - 
11AABCC0354P1ZW) had paid the unreconciled tax of ₹ 0.07 crore at the instance of 
Audit on 20 March 2023.

(d) Other findings
(i)  Cross verification of Annual Financial Statement with GST records.
To check the facts and figures contained in the GST Returns of 14 sample taxpayers in 
comparison with their Annual Financial Statements (AFS), Audit called for AFS of 14 
taxpayers from the CTD (23 February 2022) followed by reminders on 11 May 2022, 29 
June 2022, 19 July 2022, and 31 August 2022. Out of 14, only four AFSs were furnished 
by the CTD (12 September 2022).
The Audit compared the total sale figures of four taxpayers for 2017-18 as contained in 
their GST and V AT returns with those as declared in their Annual Financial Statements 
and found difference of ₹ 44.57 crore in case of two taxpayers as detailed below.
M/s Sikkim Organic (GSTIN - 11ABBFS1390P1Z2): As per AFS 2017-18, total Sales 
figures amounts to ₹ 73.31 crore. However, as per GSTR-1, the gross turnover was ₹ 
32.33 crore (for the period from July 2017 to March 2018) and the total sales under 
V AT was ₹ 14.39 crore. Hence, the total taxable sales reported in the GST and V AT 
returns were ₹ 46.72 crore. Hence, cross-verification of AFS with GST and V AT returns 
revealed suppression of sales of ₹ 26.60 crore. As the goods were taxed at the rate of 
18 percent under GST, there was short payment of tax of ₹ 4.79 crore. The matter was 
intimated (28 September 2022) to the CTD. No reply was furnished by the CTD till date 
(November 2023). 
M/s SIMFED (GSTIN - 11AADAS4451M1ZI):   Total sales as per the AFS was 
₹ 72.28154 crore but as per the GSTR-9 total sales was ₹ 42.71 crore (of which taxable 
value was ₹ 31.73 crore and exempted and nil rated value was ₹ 10.98 crore), in respect 
of the period from July 2017 to March 2018. Scrutiny of V AT returns revealed that 
during 1st Quarter of 2017-18, total taxable value was ₹ 11.60 crore. The total sales 
reported in GST and V AT returns was ₹ 54.31 crore (₹ 42.71 crore + ₹ 11.60 crore). 
Hence cross-verification of sales figure of AFS with V AT and GST returns revealed 
that there was suppression of sales of ₹ 17.97 crore (₹ 72.28 crore - ₹ 54.31 crore) in 
the returns. As per the GSTR-9 figure, when the total sales was ₹ 42.71 crore (which 
included both taxable and exempted/nil rated goods) the tax liability was ₹ 4.39 crore 
hence proportionately the tax liability on suppressed sales of ₹ 17.97 crore works out to 
₹ 2.07 crore. The matter was intimated (28 September 2022) to the CTD. No reply was 
furnished by the CTD (November 2023). 
Recommendation 6: The department may initiate remedial action for all the compliance 
deviations pointed out through this report before they get time-barred.
5.12.6.4  Inadequacy of return data in the State System: 
Although Audit was given access to the State system, it was found that certain data/
information as detailed below was not available in the State GST portal.
GSTR-9C: GSTR-9C along with the attachments was not available in the State system. 
It may be mentioned that checking GSTR-9C was one of the means of establishing the 
veracity of data in GSTR-9 as it reflects any differences with the AFS. Even after five 
years of implementation of the GST Act, the State system had not integrated the data 
of GSTR-9C. At the instance of Audit, the CTD intimated (12 September 2022 and 5 
December 2022) that GSTR-9C was in the process of integration with State system; and 
154 As per the AFS, total sales was ₹ 72.28 crore. However, the turnover as per AFS reported in GSTR 9C 
was ₹ 43.24 crore.

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
172
the observation made by Audit pertaining to GSTR-9C would be addressed after the 
integration.
It was only in May 2023, the GSTR-9C had been integrated with the State GST system.
GSTR-2A: GSTR-2A is an auto populated return which is supposed to capture ITC data 
received by the taxpayer from various sources such as supplies from registered taxpayers 
along with the debit/credit note issued and amendments (GSTR-1 data of the suppliers), 
ISD credit received (GSTR-6 of ISD), TCS/TDS (GSTR-7/8 of deductor or collector) 
etc. However, in the State system under GSTR-2A, Table no. 4 (B2B Inward supplies 
on reverse charge), Table no. 5 (Debit/credit note including amendments), Table no. 6 
(ISD credit), Table no. 7 (TDS and TCS) were not available, only data pertaining to B2B 
invoices were available. Hence even though Audit was given access to the State system, 
complete data, or information of GSTR-2A for comprehensive scrutiny of data was not 
available in the system. 
This was intimated to the CTD on 21 October 2022. No reply was furnished by the CTD 
(November 2023).
5.12.7 Conclusion
The Subject Specific Compliance Audit on Department’s Oversight on GST Payments 
and Returns Filing was undertaken with an objective of assessing the adequacy of 
the system in monitoring returns filing and tax payments by the taxpayers, extent of 
compliance by the taxpayers and other departmental oversight functions. The audit was 
predominantly based on data analysis, which highlighted risk areas, red flags and in 
some cases, rule-based deviations and logical inconsistencies in GST returns filed for 
2017-18 by the taxpayers.
The department was unable to provide records pertaining to scrutiny of returns, action 
taken on late/non-filers, cancellation of registrations as well as on internal audit. This 
indicated that the oversight functions of Sectors were not done as envisaged by the 
GST Act and Rules. Audit also observed that the CTD had not formulated any Standard 
Operating Procedure (SOP) for scrutiny functions as done by the CBIC. Therefore, 
due to absence of an institutional mechanism in the Sectors to establish and maintain 
effective oversight on returns filing, taxpayers’ compliance and tax payments, cases of 
non-compliance by taxpayers were observed during the Audit. 
Out of total 93 inconsistencies/mismatches identified during Centralised Audit, in 
55 cases Department did not furnish any response to audit queries. Upon analysing the 
responses of the Department received in remaining 38 cases, 22 cases involving amount 
of ₹ 3.71 crore turned out to be compliance deficiencies. A relatively higher rate of 
deficiencies was noticed in mismatch of ITC availed between annual returns and books 
of accounts, excess RCM ITC availed, mismatch in tax paid between books of accounts 
and returns and incorrect availment of ISD credit. In four cases, the CTD recovered the 
amount of ₹ 0.24 crore from the taxpayers on compliance deviations being pointed out 
by the Audit. The data entry errors caused the inconsistencies in four cases and 12 cases 
had valid explanations. 
As regards Detailed Audit, out of samples of 14 taxpayers in seven cases other granular 
records were not produced at all and in six cases partial records were produced, 
which significantly limited the scope for Detailed Audit. Detailed Audit among others 
also pointed towards non-compliance on five major issues with tax implication of 

Chapter V: Revenue Sector
173
₹ 30.78 crore: (i) Non-payment of interest by nine taxpayers of ₹ 1.05 crore (ii) mismatch 
of tax liability of ₹ 0.57 crore involving eight taxpayers, (iii) mismatch between returns 
in availing ITC of ₹ 13.90 crore by 11 taxpayers, (iv) unreconciled ITC of ₹ 15.11 crore 
involving four taxpayers (v) unreconciled taxes paid of ₹ 0.16 crore by two taxpayers. In 
five cases, tax amount of ₹ 0.19 crore was paid by the taxpayers at the instance of Audit.
5.12.8 Summary of Recommendations
After evaluation of records furnished and analysis of data available in the State system, 
the following recommendations are made:
1. The CTD may put in place proper mechanism for scrutiny of returns to ensure 
compliance of rules pertaining to returns filing and tax payments by all taxpayers. 
The CTD may prepare a Standard Operating Procedure for manual scrutiny of 
returns in line with one prepared by CBIC, for use till automation of scrutiny is 
completed.
2. CTD may establish mechanism to regularly identify late-filers and non-filers and 
take necessary steps to recover tax, interest and late fee from them.
3. CTD may establish mechanism to regularly identify taxpayers who have not filed 
GST returns for more than six months and take necessary steps to recover applicable 
tax, interest and late fee from them and cancel the registrations.
4. CTD may urgently pursue the 55 inconsistencies/deviations pointed out by Audit 
for which responses have not been provided and intimate the results there-of.
5. The CTD may conduct similar data analysis (as conducted by Audit for Centralised/ 
limited Audit) of GST returns and other related data of all the taxpayers under State 
jurisdiction to identify inconsistencies and take necessary steps to recover dues 
from the taxpayers wherever applicable. 
6. The department may initiate remedial action for all the compliance deviations 
pointed out through this report before they get time-barred.

6.2 Planning and conduct of audit
Audit process starts with the assessment of risks faced by various departments of the 
Government based on expenditure incurred, criticality/complexity of activities, level of 
delegated financial powers, assessment of overall internal controls, etc.
After completion of audit of each unit on a test-check basis, Inspection Reports (IRs) 
containing audit observations are issued to the heads of the departments.  The departments 
are required to furnish replies to the audit findings within one month of receipt of the IRs.  
Whenever replies are received, audit findings are either settled based on reply/action 
taken or the audited entities are required to take further action for compliance.  Some of 
the important audit observations arising out of these IRs are processed for inclusion in 
the Audit Reports of the Comptroller and Auditor General of India.  These Audit Reports 
are submitted to the Governor of the State under Article 151 of the Constitution of India 
for laying on the table of the Legislature for taking further appropriate action.
Test audits were conducted involving expenditure of ₹ 1,327.43 crore (including 
expenditure of ₹ 885.89 crore of previous years) during 2022-23 of the State Government 
under General Sector. The details of year-wise break-up are given in Appendix 6.1.
This chapter contains one Subject Specific Compliance Audit.
COMPLIANCE AUDIT
TOURISM AND CIVIL A VIATION DEPARTMENT
6.3		 Subject	Specific	Compliance	 Audit	on	Swadesh Darshan Scheme
6.3.1  Introduction
Swadesh Darshan is the flagship scheme of Ministry of Tourism (MoT), Government 
of India (GoI) for development of tourism infrastructure in the country. MoT launched 
this Central Sector Scheme in January 2015. The objective of the scheme was to develop 
tourist circuits and promote the cultural and heritage value of the country. Further, the 
scheme also aims to generate livelihood and employment opportunities through active 

Chapter VI: General Sector
177
involvement of local communities. Under the scheme, 15 circuits155 have been identified 
in India for development. 
Tourist Circuit is defined as a route on which at least three major tourist destinations 
falling in different towns, villages or cities are located but not separated by a long 
distance which should have well-defined entry and exit points. A tourist who enters 
should be motivated to visit all the places identified on the circuit.
The MoT approved two projects Swadesh Darshan-I (SD-I) and Swadesh Darshan-II 
(SD-II) under North-East Circuit for the state of Sikkim. The entry point was defined in 
SD- I at Rangpo and exit point at Singtam. In respect of Swadesh Darshan-II the entry 
point was at Singtam and exit point at Melli. The details of both the circuits have been 
shown in Image 6.1 and 6.2.
SD-I SD-II
Image 6.1: Rangpo (Entry point) Rorathang, 
Aritar, Gangtok, Makha, Singhik, Tingbong, 
Lachen, Thangu, Singtam (Exit point)
Image 6.2:Singtam (Entry point), Makha, Temi, 
Bermoik Tokal, Phongla, Namchi, Jorethang, 
Sombaria, Daramdin, Jorethang, Melli (Exit point)
The MoT sanctioned 24 and 21 components of works under Swadesh Darshan-I and II 
respectively such as construction of Tourism Information/Interpretation centre, Wayside 
Amenities, Construction of approach roads, Parking facilities, Construction of craft haats/
bazaars/souvenir shops/cafeteria, and Toilet, Cloak room facilities and waiting rooms. 
6.3.2 Organisational arrangement
Tourism and Civil Aviation Department (TCAD), the nodal Department in Sikkim for 
implementation of the Scheme, is headed by the Principal Secretary, who is assisted 
by the Secretary, Principal Chief Engineer, Chief Engineer, Additional Chief Engineer, 
Director (Accounts), Additional Secretary and other officers.
6.3.3 Financial Overview
According to the Scheme Guidelines (2015), the funds would be released to the State 
Government as under:-
1. 1 st instalment – 20 per cent of the project cost on approval/sanction of the project.
2. 2 nd instalment - 60 per cent of the project cost after receipt of utilisation certificate 
for the first instalment.
155 Himalayan Circuit, North East Circuit, Krishna Circuit, Buddhist Circuit, Coastal Circuit, Desert

Circuit, Heritage Circuit, Tirthankar Circuit and Sufi Circuit.

3. 3rd instalment- Remaining 20 per cent of the project cost on completion of work and 
receipt of the utilisation certificate.
For the Himalayan and NE States, different funding pattern and duration of projects may 
be determined by the Mission Directorate keeping in view the difficulties in these areas. 
As per Scheme Guidelines 2017, funding pattern was revised from three instalments 
to five instalments. Audit observed that the funds were released in five instalments by

6.3.4 Audit Objectives 
The Subject Specific Compliance Audit was conducted to ascertain whether:
i) the tourist circuits were identified, prioritised, and planned properly so as to 
increase tourist potential at the sites.
ii) identified projects were executed in a manner so as to achieve integrated development 
of quality (world class) infrastructure, and
iii) proper monitoring mechanisms were established towards impact assessment of 
the Scheme and projects, in terms of achievement of respectively stated objectives 
of sustainable tourism, enhancement of tourist potential, employment generation, 
generating livelihoods, community-based development etc.
6.3.5 Audit Scope, sampling and methodology  
The Audit covered a period of seven years from 2014-15 to 2022-23. The scope of audit 
included two circuits viz. Swadesh Darshan-I and Swadesh Darshan-II. SD-I and II had 
a total of 13 and eight destinations points respectively.
