Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
180
Audit	findings
6.3.8					Identification	and	Selection	of	circuits
6.3.8.1   Improper selection of Circuits/ Destinations
Identification of the tourist circuit and the destinations is a vital component of the 
scheme. The tourist circuits/destinations were to be identified by MoT in consultation 
with the stakeholders and States based on a pre-dominant tourism theme, considering 
factors such as current tourist traffic, connectivity potential and significance attached to 
the sites, holistic tourist experience etc. 
However, audit observed that the DPR prepared by the department did not have any 
reference to such consultations, indicating absence of the same. Neither was the basis for 
selection of projects/components specified in the Cabinet Memos nor did the Department 
conduct any feasibility study before implementing the scheme.
Such deficiencies in planning affected the overall execution and implementation of the 
projects as audit noticed numerous cases of delay in completion of the projects, non-
utilisation of infrastructure, improper utilisation of infrastructure created as elucidated 
in succeeding paragraphs.
In reply, the Department stated (March 2024) that meetings were held at different 
occasions with both internally as well as with various stakeholders to discuss and 
identify the requirements of tourism facilities along with tourist circuit to be undertaken 
under SD schemes however, minutes of these meeting could not be traced. The reply of 
the department was not acceptable as department  had neither produced any document 
in support of holding of meetings with stakeholders nor was any reference made in the 
DPR regarding consultation with various stakeholders.
6.3.8.2   Deficiencies in preparation of DPR  
As per the scheme guidelines, a comprehensive DPR should be prepared for each circuit 
after consultation with the respective State Government/UT Administration, Local 
Bodies, other stake holders for the components to be funded by public funding. The 
DPR shall be prepared as per toolkit of Ministry of Tourism. A toolkit was prepared 
by Ministry of Tourism to help State Governments/Department of Tourism/Tourism 
Development Corporation in preparation of Detailed Project Reports to be submitted 
for funding under Swadesh Darshan Scheme to Ministry of Tourism, Government of 
India. Further, according to Para 4(a) of Part B of toolkit, Destination wise Assessment 
of Tourist profiling consisting of Category of tourists, Footfall of tourists, Carrying 
capacity of the destination/site, duration of stay, etc., have to be included in the DPR. 
However, audit noticed that instead of destination-wise information, State-wide 
information was provided in the DPRs. Further, information regarding profiling of tourista 
and socio-economic benefits required to be included in the DPR were not included. 
In the absence of such vital data, performance in respect of important objectives of 
the scheme such as increase in tourist traffic in the identified circuits and employment 
generation could not be evaluated or assessed.
In reply the department accepted (March 2024) that destination-wise data was not 
provided and overall state data were taken in the DPR. 

Chapter VI: General Sector
181
6.3.8.3 Deviation from the DPR
According to para 4.6 of the revised scheme guidelines 2017, if there is any escalation/
variation from approved DPR, SGs/UT Administrations must inform MoT and seek its 
approval for the same before commencing work on the component. Further, Para 7 of 
sanction letter (30 June 2015) of MoT Government of India stipulated that funds will be 
utilised only for the purpose for which they are released.
However, deviations from DPR with respect to cost, changes in scope of work and 
execution of works not included in DPR were noticed in nine cases. The details of the 
deviation are furnished in Appendix 6.2.
The above deviations were done without the approval of the GoI in violation of the 
scheme guidelines. They also indicate lack of proper site survey, planning and feasibility 
study, on the part of the department.
In reply, Department accepted (March 2024) that certain revision of items for befitting 
the requirements as per site condition and necessity were undertaken in the best interest 
of the project. 
Reply was not acceptable as the Department had neither nor informed MoT nor taken 
approval from MoT before commencing the deviation, in contrast to the guidelines. 
Further, requirement and site conditions were to be brought to the notice of MoT before 
implementing the deviations.
6.3.9 Project execution
The department carried out 127 works under 35 selected components, out of which 126 
works were handed over to the Assets cell of the Department by the Engineering cell 
after completion of the works for leasing out and one asset (Development of Adventure 
Tourism with International Standard Zipline from Hathipailay to Aritar Lake) was not 
handed over to asset cell. 
Further audit scrutiny revealed that out of 127 assets, only 19 assets 159 were being 
utilised for the purposes for which they were created and 77 were lying unutilised till 
the date of audit and 31 were being used for other unintended purposes. In other words, 
only 19 assets had been leased out till the date of audit (April 2024) and 31 of them 
were constructed within the premises of Monasteries/Temples which were handed over 
to the concerned Monasteries/Temples. The Engineering cell of the Department took 3 
days to 3 years to handover the constructed works to the Assets cell after completion of 
construction.
Details are furnished in Appendix 6.3 . The reasons for non-utilisation of completed 
projects was attributed to non-availability of suitable bidders at the base price.

The Construction of Rain shelter (₹21.63 lakh) and Development of

It is evident from the above-mentioned facts that the Department focused only on 
construction of these assets as it had not initiated necessary steps to make these assets 
operational.  As a result of which, the objectives of generating revenue, employment and 
promotion of tourism in the identified circuit remained unfulfilled.
Thus, due to improper utilisation or non-utilisation, the assets created could not generate 
revenue or promote culture & heritage of the State as envisaged under the scheme. This 
led to infructuous expenditure of ₹ 17.68 crore and idling of assets valuing ₹ 18.98 crore.
In reply, though, the department stated (March 2024) that all the assets created are leased 
out to private parties or handed over to the monasteries and are now actively serving 
the tourists and earning revenue to the government. However, supporting documents for 
evidencing the leased out property of only 19 assets were furnished to audit.
6.3.9.1 Infrastructures created in Monastery and Temple land
According to Para 8.3 of Scheme guidelines issued (2015) and para 4.3 of revised 
guidelines 2017, improvement/investment in assets/structures owned by the private 
entities were not admissible under the schemes. In this connection, TCAD submitted 
the land availability certificate along with the DPR to MoT stating that all lands were 
free from encumbrance to accommodate all the features of the project under Swadesh 
Darshan and the lands were in possession of the Department.
Scrutiny of records revealed that tourist infrastructure in nine destinations were 
constructed in the land belonging to either Monastery or temple as detailed in Appendix 
6.4, which resulted in affecting the State’s ownership of the project/assets as well as its 
access to revenue from the project.
In reply, Department stated (March 2024) that the temples and monasteries serve as 
significant tourist attractions and vital sources of livelihood within the state. Monastic 
tourism plays a central role in promoting tourism. It’s essential to involve these religious 
sites in tourism circuits to enrich the overall experience. The reply of the department 
was not tenable, since creation of infrastructure in private land was not admissible as 
per the scheme guidelines.
6.3.9.2 Delay in award of work/project 
According to the guidelines of the Swadesh Darshan Scheme, invitation and finalisation 
of all tenders for the project were to be completed within three months from the date of 
sanction of the project. 
Scrutiny of records revealed that projects under Swadesh Darshan -I and II were 
sanctioned by the GoI on 30.06.2015 and 16.09.2016 respectively. However, audit 
noticed that there was an inordinate delay in awarding the tenders for the SD-I and II. 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
186
The delay in finalisation of tender ranged from 139 days to 419 days (SD-I) and 90 days 
to 243 days (SD-II) from the due date of award of tender (three months from the date 
of sanction) due to belated process for tendering, retendering due to non-fulfilment of 
tendered conditions, etc. as detailed in Appendix 6.5. Two such cases of inordinate delay 
are highlighted below:
➢	 Award of tender in case of Development of Zipline and Angling Infrastructure at 
Menglee, Yangang under SD–II was delayed by 243 days mainly due to delay in 
initiation of tendering process (as due date of finalisation of tender was December 
2016 i.e., three months from the date of sanction of the project. However, tender 
was invited only in February 2017).  One bidder out of three participants failed to 
submit valid tender documents due to which the tendering process was cancelled 
as minimum three bidders were required in the process. Further, retendering was 
floated (July 2017) only after five months. 
➢	 Development of tourist Infrastructure at Ani Gumpa under SD-I (Gangtok) was 
delayed by 419 days mainly due to delay in initiation of tender process as due 
date of finalisation of tender was September 2015  i.e., three months from the date 
of sanction of the project. However, first tender was called only in March 2016. 
Subsequently, retendering was resorted to twice (May and September 2016). 
The reasons for re-tendering were due to insufficient valid tender documents and 
insufficient bidders. 
Due to delay in awarding of works, 21 works were delayed ranging from four months 
to 37 months from the stipulated date of completion which affected early generation of 
employment. 
In reply, department stated (March 2024) that the Project involved a large Circuit 
with many sites and many components in each site, a fair amount of time was spent in 
formulation of packages for tendering. Many works also had to be re-tendered causing 
time overruns. 
6.3.9.3 Delay in completion of work
According to sanction letter of MoT, GoI, the State Government will execute the project 
through the Engineer Wing, Department of Tourism, GoS which shall have to commence 
the work immediately to ensure timely utilisation of funds and to avoid escalation of 
cost. The project should be commissioned within the maximum period of 24 months 
from the date issue of sanction.
Audit noticed that none of the 35 components under 21 destinations (SD-I: 13 and 
SDII: 8) selected by audit were completed within the stipulated time schedule. Works 
were delayed ranging from four months to 37 months from the stipulated date of 
completion as detailed in Appendix 6.6. 
Further, out of 21 works test checked by audit, four works were delayed due to blockage 
of road during monsoon season, two works160 were delayed due to non-obtaining of No 
Objection Certificate (NOC) from landowner and Forest Department, two works were 
delayed due to non-availability of stock materials in the store of the Department, and 
in two works due to Covid-19 restrictions. In all 21 cases, time extension was allowed 
by the Department on the plea of the contractors. Audit noticed that the time extension 
granted by the department in eight out of 21 works on the basis of reasons cited by the 
contractors was not justifiable as detailed below:
160  Development of Tourist Infrastructure at Gangtok and  Development of Tourist Infrastructure at 
Tumin Lingee.

Chapter VI: General Sector
187
➢	 In the case of Development of tourist infrastructure at Singhik, North Sikkim and 
Makha, East Sikkim completion of work was delayed by one year and ten months, 
and one year and nine months respectively from the scheduled date of completion 
due to non-availability of stock materials at store. Non-availability of stock is not 
a valid reason for the delay as the department had allowed the contractors to self-
procure the stock material.
➢	 The delay in completion of four works161 ranged from four months to 16 months was 
stated to be due to obstruction of work by heavy rainfall during monsoon season . 
However, heavy rainfall during monsoon season is a regular feature in Sikkim and 
should have been factored accordingly at the time of awarding the work. 
➢	 In the work Development of Tourist infrastructure at Tumin Lingee, there was a 
delay of 11 months due to non-obtaining of NOC from Forest Department and in 
the work Development of Tourist Infrastructure at Gangtok, there was a delay of 11 
months due to delay in release of land compensation to the land owner. 
Thus, due to avoidable reasons, works under SD-I and SD-II were considerably delayed 
indicating ineffective implementation and monitoring of the scheme by the Department.
In reply, Department stated (March 2024) that due to unpredictable and untimely 
rains, disruptions in road connectivity and extended monsoons, excavation and site 
development works cannot be carried out which seriously hamper the progress of work. 
The reply was not tenable since d epartment was aware about the weather condition 
while implementing the scheme. 
6.3.9.4 Splitting of work
As per para 12 of the scheme guidelines (January 2015) and Para 4.6 of revised guidelines 
2017, the implementing agency shall follow all codal formalities while awarding 
contracts for work/material/equipment procurement and ensure complete transparency 
in its transactions. E-tendering/e-procurement will be compulsorily followed for all 
tenders. Further, the Road & Bridges Department (R&BD), GoS vide Notification (July 
2015) stipulated that Call of Tender for civil work value above ₹  two crore and up to 
₹ five crore shall be done within the district through e-tender under the jurisdiction of 
Divisional Engineer/Superintending Engineer.
Scrutiny of records revealed that the Government of India, Ministry of Tourism 
sanctioned fund amounting to ₹ 5.78 crore for “Development of Eco Log Huts (6 Nos) 
at Aritar” under Aritar-Rhenock-Rongli component under Swadesh Darshan Scheme. 
The Department divided the works in three parts i.e. (i) Construction of 3 Nos of Log 
Huts valuing of ₹ 2.63 crore, (ii) Construction of 2 Nos Log Huts valuing of ₹ 1.75 crore 
and (iii) Construction of 1No Log Hut & Staff accommodation valuing of ₹ 1.45 crore 
and tendered the work within the territorial Jurisdiction of Gram Panchayat. The Gram 
Panchayat Unit (GPU) awarded the entire work to M/s Aritar B, Educated Unemployed 
Co-operative Society Ltd. in April 2016 in three parts at the rate of 22 per cent above 
SoR 2012 with stipulated time of 24 months for completion of the work. The works were 
completed in December 2018. 
However, the work was awarded to contractor on GPU level instead of e-tendering within 
the district against the stipulations in the government notification of July 2015 which 
161 i) Development of Tourist Infrastructure at Rhenock-Aritar-Rongli, East Sikkim ii) Development of 
Tourist Infrastructure at Lachen, North Sikkim iii) Development of Tourist Infrastructure at Tingvong, 
North Sikkim iv) Development of Tourist Infrastructure at Dikchu,East Sikkim.

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
188
restricted the possibility of more bidders from participating, to ensure competitive price 
discovery.
In reply (March 2024), Department decided to split the works and tender them separately 
at GPU level for ease of execution as well as to ensure speedy progress of work. Reply 
was not tenable because splitting of work was against the above codal provision and 
there was delay in completion of work by nine months.
6.3.9.5 Expenditure on Information, Education and Communication (IEC)
As per the scheme Guidelines, 10 per cent of the project cost was earmarked for IEC 
for capacity development like workshops, seminars, publication, stake holder outreach, 
skill development etc. and professional and administrative services.
Scrutiny of records revealed that the department neither kept provision for IEC in the 
DPR submitted to the Government of India nor had GoI provided any fund for the IEC 
programme. Thus, no major IEC activities were conducted by the Department to create 
awareness about the tourist destinations.
In reply, Department accepted (March 2024) that the IEC funds were not sanctioned 
under the Project, and hence no IEC activities could be carried out from the Project 
funds. However, such activities were carried out through the regular programme out of 
the State budget. However, the department had not furnished any supporting documents 
in respect of the activities carried out from the State budget.
6.3.9.6 Damaged Signages 
As per Detailed Project Report approved by the MoT, the State Government had kept 
one per cent provision for the signages stating that the provision has been incorporated 
as per standard procedure and practice. 
Scrutiny of records revealed that the lowest bidder Maark Pictures Production & 
Advertisement, Development Area, Gangtok was shortlisted for supply of stainless steel 
signage, Tourism Logo Signage, Map signage, hanging signage, Interpretation signage, 
Granite Carved signage, Acrylic Letter signage, Toilet signage, Parking signage, Book 
Style signage and QR signage (with short clip) vide supply order of March 2020. 
Audit noticed that the work for installation of 80 signages commenced in March 2020 
and installation was completed on 08.02.2021. The total amount paid to Maark Picture 
for supply and installation of signages was ₹ 43.68 lakh. However, during physical 
verification (January 2022) along with the officers of TCAD, sixteen signages valuing ₹ 
8.62 lakh were not available at the site and two signages were found damaged as shown 
in the images below.

This indicates inability of the department to ensure that the assets created were being

6.3.9.7 Lack of information/publicity
Under Swadesh Darshan-I scheme, the Department had mapped Rangpo as the entry 
point and Singtam as the exit point covering Rorathang, Aritar, Gangtok, Makha, 
Singhik, Tingbong, Lachen, and Thangu  as tourist destinations between the entry and 
exit points. Similarly, under Swadesh Darshan-II scheme, the entry point was Singtam 
and exit point was Melli and covered the tourist destinations Makha, Temi, Bermoik 
Tokal, Phongla, Namchi, Jorethang, Sombaria, Daramdin and Jorethang. 
Rangpo: Rangpo is a border town which has all the facilities for tourists whereas lacks 
space of tourist facilities such as recreation centre, reception centre, and interpretation 
centre. 
Rorathang: Wayside Amenity is located nearby Pakyong Airport and alternative route to 
Gangtok. Rhenock -Aritar is surrounded by Lake, monasteries, Sanskrit Pathsala.
Audit noticed during physical verification of entry points viz. Rangpo (SD-I) and 
Singtam (SD-II) that important information like tourist map was not available at the 
entry point i.e. Rangpo and Singtam. There was no information on departmental the 
website regarding the circuit or the destinations under it. Thus, the department did not 
provide vital information about the circuits thereby affecting the objective of the scheme 
to motivate the tourists to visit all places identified under a circuit. 
In reply Department assured (March 24) that all new literature, fliers, brochures will be 
updated to include such newly created assets and website will be updated. 
6.3.9.8 Undue benefit to contractors
(i) Non deduction of storage charges ₹ 0.73 crore 
Sub-section 20.12 of Section 20 of Sikkim Public Works Manual (SPWM) 2009 
envisaged that the Department should recover 5 per cent  storage charges that have 
been incorporated in the Analysis of Rate (AOR) over and above the market rate while 
finalising the rates in Schedule of Rates and credit to the appropriate Revenue Receipts 
Head of the Department. Further, during the approval of self-procurement by the 
Department, it was also mentioned that the department may recover 5 per cent storage 
charges as incorporated in Analysis of relevant Schedule of Rate.
Audit noticed that the Estimate for SD I and II was prepared on the basis of the AOR 
2012 in which the five per cent  storage charges were incorporated.  However, for 
the construction of various infrastructure under the Swadesh Darshan Scheme I, the 
Department issued stock materials valuing ₹ 6.11 crore (SD-I: ₹ 2.03 crore and SD-II: ₹ 
4.08 crore) and allowed self-procurement of materials valuing ₹ 8.52 (SD-I: ₹ 3.07 crore 
and SD-II : ₹ 5.45 crore). However, the Department had not deducted five per cent  
storage charge from the Running Account (RA) Bills/Final Bills of any of the contractors 
till date of Audit (February 2023). Details are in Appendix 6.7.
Thus, non-deduction of five per cent storage charge from the RA bills/Final Bills of the 
contractors not only led to undue benefit to the contractor but also loss of government 
revenue to the tune of ₹ 0.73 crore. 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
190
In reply, Department stated (March 2024) that there is no handling of Stores by the 
Department and the same is being managed by the contractors from their own resources, 
Storage Charges were not deducted from the contractors. Reply was not tenable because 
storage charges as incorporated in the AoR 2012 should be recovered from contractor 
bills. Further, during the approval of self-procurement, it was also mentioned that the 
Department may recover five per cent storage charges as incorporated in Analysis of 
relevant Schedule of Rate.
(ii) Irregular grant of mobilisation Advance
State Government Order dated 22.02.2016 (amendment to Sikkim Public Works Code 
& Manual 2009) issued by Finance, Revenue & Expenditure Department (FRED), 
Government of Sikkim stipulates that the practice of providing mobilisation advances to 
the Contractors should be withdrawn with immediate effect.
The Department, however, granted mobilisation advances amounting to ₹ 11.56 crore 
(ranging from ₹ 0.32 crore to ₹ 2.06 crore) @ five per cent compound interest during 
the period from 31.03.2016 to 03.05.2018 to 14 contractors under Swadesh Darshan 
scheme I & II after the issue of aforesaid order. The details of MA granted are shown in 
Appendix 6.8.
The granting of mobilisation advances clearly violated the Government order and hence 
was not regular. 
In reply, Department stated (March 24) that the provisions relating to grant of mobilisation 
advance have been temporarily suspended by the Government. The Department had 
released Mobilisation Advance in some unavoidable cases.  However, reasons for grant 
of mobilisation advance in violation of Government order were neither specified in the 
reply nor recorded in the files produced to Audit.  
6.3.10 Financial Management
6.3.10.1 Non-segregation of interest component between SD-I and SD-II
According to the sanction letter dated 30 June 2015 of MoT Government of India, 
the State Government of Sikkim will not keep the amount released by the Central 
Government unutilised for more than six months. In case the funds cannot be utilised by 
such time, the same will have to be surrendered to Central Government along with the 
interest earned. State Implementing Agencies must open a Separate Account for each 
project and interest earned shall be refunded to the Ministry of Tourism at the end of the 
financial year. 
First instalment of ₹19.61 crore for SD-I was received in the Finance Department, 
Government of Sikkim in June 2015 and was credited to the State Government account 
in July 2015. Subsequent instalments of both the circuits- SD I and II were deposited 
in a single savings bank account opened during September 2016. But separate bank 
accounts for SD I and II were opened only in June 2017. The first instalment for SD- I of 
₹ 19.61 crore was released (30 June 2015) by GoI. As per the records only ₹ 0.49 crore 
was utilised by December 2015 i.e. within six months by the Department.  However, the 
Department neither took approval of GoI for revalidation/utilisation after stipulated time 
nor surrendered the amount to GoI in violation of scheme guidelines.
Audit also noticed that total interest earned from the single account as well as separate 
accounts of SD-I and SD-II was ₹ 7.91 crore, but only ₹ 4.57 crore was segregated 

Chapter VI: General Sector
191
between SD-I (₹1.64 crore) and SD-II (₹2.93 crore) as required under the guidelines and 
the remaining interest amounting to ₹ 3.34 crore was not segregated. 
In reply, Department stated (March 24) that the segregation of interest between the two 
Circuits under Swadesh Darshan was not found necessary, since no such requirement 
was communicated. Reply was not acceptable as interest was needed to be segregated 
since bank accounts were opened separately.
6.3.10.2 Irregular expenditure from accrued interest 
According to Additional guidelines for processing of projects sanctioned before 2017, 
State Implementing Agencies must open a Separate Account for each project and interest 
earned shall be refunded to the Ministry of Tourism at the end of the financial year. 
Scrutiny of records revealed that the Department earned interest amounting to 
₹ 7.91 crore from the fund received from GoI for implementation of Swadesh Darshan 
(SD) However, instead of refunding this amount back to Ministry of Tourism, GoI, 
Department utilised ₹ 5.95 crore for various purposes such as supply of tourist map, 
restoration, and maintenance of infrastructures, payment of MR wages, payment of 
supply of garden benches, development of approach road, protective work, and payment 
to conducting of audit, etc., and only ₹ 1.34 crore was returned to GoI in August 2020 
while remaining amount is still lying with the Department as of September 2023.  
In reply, Department stated (March 2024) that a part of the interest earned from SD 
Scheme was utilised to support the gap of infrastructure under the said scheme which 
includes improvement of connectivity to SD Scheme site, mitigation of flood damage 
to SD Scheme site, value addition an d promotion work etc. to ensure best utility of 
these tourism assets. However, interest was to be refunded to the MoT at the end of the 
financial year.
6.3.10.3 Irregular expenditure for repair and maintenance of assets.
As per the undertaking given (June 2015) by the State Government, it would be solely 
responsible for sustainable operation and maintenance of the identified/developed 
projects under the Scheme. It will have necessary arrangements/agreements with the 
public/private agencies for operation/maintenance of the assets created under the 
scheme. Further, according to scheme guidelines the components inadmissible under 
the schemes were land acquisition, resettlement & rehabilitation package, operation, 
maintenance and management of the assets created etc.
Scrutiny of records revealed that TCAD had incurred (November 2021) expenditure 
amounting to ₹ 0.56 crore on restoration and maintenance of Tourist Infrastructure at 
12 sites under Swadesh Darshan Scheme from the interest accrued from (June 2015 to 
March 2023) SD fund in deviation to the guidelines. Details are in Appendix 6.9. 
In reply Department stated (March 2024) that no such fund was provided and the 
Department did face difficulty in supporting such important feature of works deemed 
necessary in view of technical requirements to bring best of its utility of the created 
assets. Accordingly, a part of the interest earned from SD Scheme was utilised to 
support the gap of infrastructure under the said scheme which includes improvement of 
connectivity to SD Scheme site, mitigation of flood damage to SD Scheme site, value 
addition and promotion work etc. to ensure best utility of these tourism assets. Reply of 
the Department was not tenable, since operation and maintenance of the developed sites 
were to be borne by State Government and accrued interest was needed to be refunded 
to the Ministry of Tourism.

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
192
6.3.10.4 Irregular utilisation of funds 
According to Paragraph 2 (20) of the Sikkim Public Works Code 2009, Contingencies 
(Works) when used in respect of the accounts of a work, indicate incidental expenses 
of a sundry nature, which cannot be classified under any Sub-head or sub-work but 
pertain to the work. Further, as per Sikkim Public Works Manual 2009, the contractor 
shall provide at his own cost all materials (except such special materials, if any, as 
may in accordance with the contract be supplied by the Engineer-in-Charge’s stores), 
plant, tools, appliances, implements, ladders, cordage, tackle, scaffolding, altered or 
substituted and whether included in the specifications or other documents forming part 
of the contract.
Audit noticed that the department had kept a three per cent provision for contingency 
in the DPRs of both SD-I and SD-II which was later bifurcated into two per cent  
contingency and one per cent tools and plants charges. Audit observed that the provision 
given for plant and tools was utilised for procurement of ten vehicles amounting to ₹ 
1.28 crore during the period 2016-17 to 2018-19, which was inadmissible. 
In addition, an amount of ₹ 0.40 crore was utilised for the payment of House rent for 
Tourist Information Centre (TIC) Namchi, MR bill for the work construction of Trekking 
Trails, Log bridges and supply of furniture cum furnishing for Khamsel ceremony at 
Tholung, Construction of Trekking Trails, Log bridges and supply of furniture cum 
furnishing for khamsel ceremony at Tholung under Dzongu in North Sikkim, which were 
not related to the schemes/works under SD. Besides during 2022-23, an amount of ₹ 
17.36 lakh was incurred on site footage with drone for Bhaleydunga Ropeway and other 
activities which were not related to Swadesh Darshan. 
Hence, expenditure to the extent of ₹ 1.85 crore was irregular. 
In reply, Department stated (March 2024) that, it was found necessary to procure 
utility vehicles or inspection vehicles for the technical officials for regular and effective 
monitoring of the Schemes. Reply was not acceptable because procurement of vehicles 
was not admissible as per scheme guidelines nor did they form part of incidental 
expenses of a sundry nature.  
6.3.10.5 Inadmissible expenditure
According to para 8.3 of guidelines, operation, maintenance and management of the 
assets is not permissible under the schemes. Further, as per the undertaking given by 
the State Government, it would be solely responsible for sustainable operation and 
maintenance of the identified developed project under the Scheme and it will bear 
the expenditure on account of construction of approach road, external electrifications, 
external water supply, sewerage lines and other items. 
Audit noticed that the Department incurred ₹ 0.78 crore on external electrification, 
external water supply, sewerage, furniture and other items from SD fund which were 
inadmissible as detailed in Appendix 6.10.
Further, it was observed that nine approach roads were constructed under the 
subcomponent ‘construction of approach road’ for ₹ 6.26 crore 162 in contravention of 
the undertaking given by the State Government. The details are given in Appendix 6.11. 
162 1. Development of Tourist Infrastructure at Rangpo (₹ 10.48 lskh), 2. Development of Tourist 
Infrastructure at Rhenock-Aritar-Rongli.

Chapter VI: General Sector
193
Subsequently, it was also observed that  the construction of approach road at Dikchu 
at a cost of ₹ 14.14 lakh was not approved by the GoI as it is not reflected in the DPR 
submitted to GoI and hence not authorised.
Thus, an expenditure of ₹ 7.04 crore was incurred in violation of the scheme guidelines 
and in deviation of the undertaking submitted by the State Government to GoI. 
In reply, Department accepted (March 2024) that the basic needs of construction of 
approach road, external electrification, external water supply, sewerage, etc. are to 
be provided by the State. However, in select sites it was found that provision for such 
facilities through State schemes would take a long time to materialise, and immediate 
measures had to be taken to get such facilities in place. Justification given by the 
Department was not acceptable as expenditure on such facilities was to be from State 
fund as per the guidelines.
6.3.10.6 Diversion of scheme fund: ₹ 41.91 lakh
Para 7 of the sanction letter of scheme guidelines stipulated that the Central Financial 
Assistance (CFA) will be utilised for the purpose for which funds are released.
Scrutiny of records revealed that department had prepared DPR for the project 
Development of tourist circuit linking Singtam-Makha-Temi-Bermoik-Phongla-
Namchi-Jorethang-Okharey-Melli (Exit) under SD II at a cost of ₹ 90.09 crore. This 
included a provision of ₹ 57.70 lakh for cost of signages (Site specific) kept @ one per 
cent, similar to the provisions made in the DPR for SD-I.  Subsequently, it was observed 
that the department added ₹ one crore to the project cost as cost of signages (Signages 
along the Highway, Direction to the site signages and Site specific signages) in the DPR. 
It was seen that out of ₹ one crore, ₹ 50.00 lakh was for site specific signages which was 
redundant as it was already included in the project cost (one per cent).  
Subsequently, it was observed that an expenditure of ₹ 41.91 lakh (₹ 22.60 lakh + ₹ 19.31 
lakh) was incurred for the work “design and fabrication and installation of signages for 
SD-I” from SD-II fund which was to be recouped from the SD-I project itself. However, 
this amount was not recouped even after a lapse of one and a half years till date of audit. 
Details are in Appendix 6.12.
Thus, the excess amount which was kept as provision for signages was diverted from 
SD-II to SD-I project which was in contravention to the sanction order.
In reply, Department stated (March 2024) that direction signage and site-specific 
signages were approved by MoT and signage components were executed as per the 
sanction component of the DPR. However, reply was not tenable as department had 
already included the signage cost twice @ one per cent component-wise while estimating 
the cost of project and ₹ 50.00 lakh separately in the overall cost of the project. Further, 
Department stated that expenditure of ₹ 41.91 lakh earlier booked under SD-II was later 
recouped and booked under SD-I after the receipt of fund from the Ministry under SD-I 
scheme. However, department had not furnished the supporting documents regarding 
recoupment of fund.   
6.3.10.7 Irregular expenditure towards provision for Wi-Fi: ₹ 20.65 lakh
Para 4.2 of scheme guidelines stipulated that Wi-Fi connectivity and development, 
informative push- apps for tourism destinations would be eligible for Central Financial 
Assistance and para 4.4 stipulated that Safety, security and internet connectivity measures 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
194
shall be taken into consideration during preparation of DPR. Further, para 8 of sanction 
letter dated 16.09.2016 stipulated that the Cent ral Financial Assistance (CFA) will be 
utilised for the purpose for which funds are released.
Scrutiny of records revealed that fund amounting to ₹ 25.90 lakh was sanctioned for Wi-
Fi connection at sites. However, no Wi-Fi connection was available at any site as found 
during the physical verification of sites. Instead the department had incurred ₹  20.65 
lakh on internet connection for the Department head office at Gangtok, purchase of 
computers and other accessories. Details are in Appendix 6.13.
Thus, ₹ 20.65 Lakh provided by MoT, GoI to provide internet facility to tourists at the 
destination was diverted for internet connection at the Department office, purchase of 
computers and other accessories in contravention of the scheme guidelines.
In reply, Department stated (March 2024) that Computers were purchased with the 
objective of upgrading and enhancing the tourist handling capacity of Tourist Information 
Centers (TICs) and Permit Cells. This investment aimed to modernise the facilities and 
streamline operations within these centers. Reply was not tenable, since funds were 
sanctioned by the MoT for Wi-Fi connection at sites under Swadesh Darshan. 
6.3.11    Monitoring and impact assessment
6.3.11.1 Formation of State Level Monitoring Committee
As per revised guidelines of the scheme, the States UT Administration shall appoint 
a State Level Monitoring Committee for timely implementation of the project. The 
purpose of the committee is to provide feedback on the progress of implementation to the 
GoI regularly. On the basis of this, the Department constituted a monitoring committee 
consisting of six members headed by Chief Engineer as Chairman on 10.02.2016 after a 
lapse of seven months of issue of sanctioned letter.  
Scrutiny of records revealed that no records existed in the department to indicate that 
SLMC met regularly after its formation. This is particularly important since audit has 
observed numerous cases of delays in implementation of the projects as indicated in 
paragraph 6.3.9.3. Since monitoring of the overall progress of the scheme was one 
of the objectives of formation of SLMC, the monitoring mechanism envisaged by the 
scheme guidelines was rendered ineffective as no meetings were held by the SLMC. In 
absence of effective functioning of the monitoring mechanism, delay in completion of 
works ranging from four months to 37 months could not be avoided. 
In reply, Department stated (March 2024) that State Level Monitoring Committee 
headed by the Chief Engineer has been constantly monitoring the Schemes right from 
conceptualisation till completion, but proceedings of the meetings have not been 
recorded. Reply was not tenable in the absence of proceeding of the meetings.
6.3.11.2 Analysis of Monthly Progress Reports (MPR)/Quarterly Progress Reports 
(QPR) of the project 
As per the standard procedures for processing projects sanctioned before October 2017 
under Swadesh Darshan Scheme, all the States shall mandatorily submit MPRs in the 
prescribed formats giving physical and financial progress to the ministry so that ministry 
can review the progress of the projects.
During scrutiny of the MPR (October 2021) submitted to MoT by Department of 
Tourism, Government of Sikkim, it was seen in the MPR that the components approved 

Chapter VI: General Sector
195
in DPR were shown with the sanctioned amount. However, while implementing the 
project (Development of Tourist Infrastructure at Singhik/Mangan), the components had 
been revised (two log huts were constructed instead of four projected in DPR) and the 
sanctioned amount was also revised (such as ₹ 56.42 lakh incurred on wayside amenity 
at Singhik against sanctioned amount of ₹ 215.65 lakh) which indicate that the MPRs 
were not based on actual facts and figures.
The absence of correct figures relating to financial and physical progress would mislead 
the GoI regarding the execution of the scheme and actual expenditure incurred against it.
While accepting, the Department stated (March 2024) that the several changes have 
occurred at site during the course of execution necessitating minor to major deviations 
from the original estimates in almost all sites. Such deviations may not have been 
correctly reflected during reporting in many cases.
6.3.11.3 Assessment mechanisms
As per paragraph 15 of scheme guidelines, the outcome of the Integrated Development 
of the tourist circuit in mission mode would be measured in terms of increase in tourist 
traffic, employment generation, and increase in revenue generation, etc. in the identified 
circuits. The outcome would be regularly evaluated and measured through survey by 
independent agency. Further, according to Para 4(a) of Part B of toolkit, Destination 
wise assessment of Tourist profiling consisted of Category of tourist, Footfall of tourists, 
carrying capacity of the destination/site, duration of stay, etc., which had to be included 
in the DPR.
However, Audit noticed that the Department did not maintain vital data such as tourist 
traffic data either for the individual destination or for the circuit. Further, it was seen 
that the Department neither spelt out the figures of proposed employment generation 
in DPR nor maintained any records regarding the employment generated under the 
schemes. Besides, 77 assets were lying idle during the audit period affecting fulfilment 
of objective of the SDS.
Thus, due to absence of such vital information, achievement of the scheme with respect 
to increase in tourist traffic, employment generation, increase in revenue generation, 
etc., could not be assessed by the department.
In reply, Department accepted (March 24) and stated that the it is in the process of 
devising a method for maintenance of such data both through conventional means as 
well as through digital intervention.
6.3.12  Conclusion
1. Basis for selection of projects/components had not been specified in the Cabinet 
memos and the Projects to be implemented were selected without proper feasibility 
study, which was against the provisions of the scheme guidelines. Audit also noticed 
cases of significant deviations from approved DPR’s indicating lack of proper 
implementation of the scheme.
2. Execution of the works under the scheme were impaired with significant delay, 
both in terms of tendering as well as execution of the work.
3. Physical verification of assets created under the scheme revealed that much of the 
infrastructure created were either lying idle or were being used for purposes other 
than those envisaged under the scheme. SLMC, which was stipulated to monitor 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
196
the progress of implementation of the scheme was ineffective as no meetings were 
held during audit period. 
6.3.13 Recommendations
1. Department should ensure that before selection of a project, proper survey, 
feasibility studies and consultation with shareholder are ensured. DPR should be 
able to spell out clearly the reasons for selection of a project. No deviation from 
approved DPR shall be permitted. 
2. Department shall make every effort to ensure that the assets created under Swadesh 
Darshan I and Swadesh Darshan II are utilised for the purpose for which they were 
created.
3. Department shall ensure that a proper monitoring system may be developed to 
capture key data pertaining to tourist footfall, employment generation  for effective 
implementation of the scheme.
4. The expenditure incurred on procurement of vehicles from contingencies should be 
recouped from State Consolidated Fund.
5. The issue of construction of infrastructure at Monastery and Temple premises may 
be taken up with GoI for regularisation.

7.1 Functioning of the Panchayati Raj Institutions (PRIs) in the State
7.1.1 Introduction 
The 73rd Constitutional Amendment Act, 1993 gave a constitutional status to Panchayati 
Raj Institutions (PRIs) and provided for a uniform structure, holding regular elections, 
regular flow of funds through Finance Commissions, etc. 
Consequently, Government of Sikkim (GoS) enacted Sikkim Panchayat Act (SPA), 
1993. A two-tier system of PRIs viz., Gram Panchayat (GPs) at village level and Zilla 
Panchayat (ZP) at district level was established under this Act . As on March 2023, 
there were six ZPs 163 consisting of 124 Territorial Constituencies (TCs) and 199 GPs 
comprising 1,149 wards in the State. Out of 199 GPs, there are two traditional institutions 
of self-governance at Lachung and Lachen in North Sikkim, also known as the Dzumsas 
deemed to be Gram Panchayat Units (GPU). GoS promulgated Sikkim Zilla Panchayat 
(Financial) Rules, 2001; Sikkim Zilla Panchayat (Financial) Amendment Rules, 
2004; Sikkim Gram Panchayat (Financial) Rules, 2003; and Sikkim Gram Panchayat 
(Financial) Amendment Rules, 2004. 
7.1.2 Allocation of Fund to PRI
The main sources of funds of PRIs include grants from Central and State Government . 
The details of grants from Central and State Government in respect of GPs and ZPs 
during the period from 2020-21 to 2022-23 are tabulated in Table 7.1.

for settlement.
7.2 Functioning of the Urban Local Bodies (ULBs) in the State
7.2.1 Introduction
Consequent upon the 74 th Constitutional Amendment Act, 1993, the Urban Local 
Bodies (ULBs) were to be made full -fledged institutions of Local Self-governance and 
with significant increase in responsibilities with greater powers and distinct sharing 
of resources with the State Government . GoS enacted the Sikkim Municipalities Act, 
2007 empowering ULBs to function as institutions of Self-governance and to accelerate 
economic development in urban areas . Though the Sikkim Municipalities Act was 
enacted in March 2007, the ULBs (viz., Municipal Corporation, Municipal Council and 
Nagar Panchayats) were formed only during 2010-11.
As of March 2023, there were seven ULBs (Municipal Corporation: 1, Municipal 
Council: 1 and Nagar Panchayats: 5) in the State.
7.2.2  Financial position
The sources of funds of the ULBs comprise own revenues generated by the ULBs 
from different sources 165 and State Government grants-in-aid (GIA) released through 
the Director, Urban Affairs Department for maintenance & development purposes. The 
details of own revenue and State Government GIA of all the seven ULBs during the

URBAN DEVELOPMENT DEPARTMENT
7.3 Performance Audit on Solid Waste Management in Urban Local Bodies
Government of Sikkim (GoS), in the State Policy on Solid Waste Management Strategy 
notified in April 2019 has envisioned to establish a ‘Zero Waste System’ for Solid Waste 
Management in the state by the year 2030 with a mission to enable the community to 
visualise experience and own up utilisation of resources along with conservation of 
environment for sustainable development.
The performance audit of Solid Waste Management (SWM) in Urban Local Bodies 
(ULBs) for the period 2018-23 was carried out (August- September 2022 and May-June 
2023) with the objective to assess the efficacy of SWM including collection, segregation, 
storage, transportation and disposal of waste. The ULBs stated to have collected entire 
quantity of solid waste generated (68.9 tonnes per day (tpd) during 2022-23, of which 
23.78 tpd was processed and remaining 45.12 tpd was dumped at landfill/ dumpsite. While 
the State Government formulated (April 2019) ‘State Policy on Solid Waste Management 
Strategy and Action plan for SWM’, by laws, etc. and successfully implemented green 
protocol for waste reduction through ban on use of plastic, polystyrene, ethens, etc,; 
the ULBs had neither prepared SWM Plan nor complied with the action plan circulated 
by the State Government in respect of Cluster Plan for Municipal Solid Waste (MSW) 
management, disposal of waste at cluster level, stringent law for source segregation and 
resource recovery. While the segregation of waste was not in practice by majority of 
the households, transportation of waste was done by the ULBs in uncovered vehicles 
without any partition thereby mixing various types of waste. Processing of waste was 
minimal ranging between 17 and 34.51  per cent due to underutilisation of processing 
facilities and not setting up Material Recovery Facility (MRF) and waste to energy 
plants, resulting in significant per cent  of waste going to the landfill/dumpsite. The 
landfill/dumpsites were located near the river and were overburdened due to dumping 
of unprocessed mixed waste, thereby reducing the shelf life. Though the Information 
Education and Communication (IEC) activities were carried out, behavioural changes 
had not come about as the dumping of waste at jhoras167 continued. 
Highlights
The data on waste generated, submitted by ULBs was based on estimation without 
assessing the quantity and composition of waste generated through sample collection.
(Paragraph 7.3.9.3)
Test checked ULBs had not carried out assessment of the financial viability of SWM 
services on a stand-alone basis to ensure their sustainability. While user charges were 
not realised in full by two ULBs (GyNP and MNP), the ULBs were dependent on 
government grants for SWM services to the extent of 35 to 94 per cent as only 6 to 
65 per cent of expenditure on SWM services was met from user charges/ outsourced 
contract fees. 
(Paragraph 7.3.10.2 &7.3.10.3)
167 Jhoras are Seasonal stream of water.

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
200
Two ULBs (GMC and NJNP) had outsourced the services of door-to-door collection 
of waste and transportation up to landfill/ dumpsite to garbage contractors. The 
garbage contractors had not complied with the terms and conditions of the contract 
towards bearing cost of fuel, minor repair and maintenance of garbage truck 
provided to contractors for rendering SWM services (collection and transportation). 
Resultantly, GMC and NJNP incurred expenditure of ₹ 1.30 crore towards fuel, repair 
and maintenance of garbage trucks during 2022-23, leading to avoidable expenditure 
and undue favour to the contractors.
(Paragraph 7.3.10.4 & 7.3.10.5)
Joint physical verification showed that source segregation of waste was not being 
carried out by majority of the households. The households bring in waste in single bin 
without segregating, dump the unsegregated waste in the garbage truck, which did not 
have separate compartment for dry and wet waste, thereby indicating lack of efforts 
at segregation.
(Paragraph 7.3.11.3)
The performance of the ULBs regarding processing of waste was very minimal, 
ranging between 17 and 34.51 per cent of waste generation , due to limited number 
of processing units (51.80 tpd capacity), majority (50 tpd) of which (at Martam and 
Gangtok) were non-functional from November 2021 to January 2023 and again from 
June 2023. 
(Paragraph 7.3.11.6)
The only sanitary landfill (SLF) at Martam, initially designed for 25 years (up to 
2040) for catering to only one ULB (Gangtok MC), was catering to four ULBs as of 
March 2023. Due to excessive dumping of mixed waste, coupled with very limited 
processing of waste, the shelf life of landfill would end within two years (2025), 
requiring urgent action by the State Government.
(Paragraph 7.3.12.1)
The monitoring by UDD and ULB was lax as direction of State Level Committee 
(SLC) and National Green Tribunal (NGT) was not acted upon as of June 2023 with 
regards to source segregation and safe disposal as unsegregated waste (mixed waste) 
was collected and transported by the garbage vehicles and the landfill/ dumpsite 
continued to receive mixed waste instead of inert waste. 
(Paragraph 7.3.14.1 & 7.3.14.2)
7.3.1 Introduction 
Municipal Solid Waste Management (MSWM) in urban areas has emerged as one of 
the biggest challenges that our country faces today. The situation is aggravated by rapid 
urbanisation. Inadequate management of waste has significant negative externalities in 
terms of public health and environmental outcomes. Besides, it has an adverse impact 
on the aesthetic appearance of the municipal areas.
For effective management of solid waste, GoI formulated the Municipal Solid Waste 
(Management and Handling) Rules, 2000, the Solid Waste Management Rules, 2016 
and respective Manuals, etc. The Urban Local Bodies (ULBs) are responsible for 
implementation of the Solid Waste Management (SWM) Rules, 2016 within their

7.3.3 Audit objectives 
The objectives of the Performance audit were to assess whether:
	Strategy and planning of SWM in ULBs was commensurate with the solid waste 
generated and concurrent with the prevailing legal framework;
	Financial management for discharging SWM functions were efficient and effective;
	Municipal tasks associated with SWM including collection, segregation, storage, 
transportation, disposal and social inclusion of informal waste workers were 
effective, efficient and economical; and
	Monitoring and evaluation of SWM was adequate and effective.
7.3.4 Audit criteria 
The criteria for evaluating the performance of SWM were derived from: 
	The Municipal Solid Waste (Management and Handling) Rules, 2000 and SWM 
Rules, 2016 and respective Manuals (Ministry of Urban Development);
	Guidelines of Swatch Bharat Mission, 2017;
	Sikkim State Policy on Solid Waste Management Strategy, 2019;
	Sikkim By-laws on Solid Waste Management, 2019;
	State Action plan on Solid Waste Management, 2019; and
	Central Pollution Control Board (CPCB)/Sikkim Pollution Control Board (SPCB) 
and National Green Tribunal (NGT) orders and guidelines.
7.3.5 Scope of Audit
The Performance Audit on SWM, covering a period of five years (2018-23), was 
conducted through scrutiny of records in UDD, Mission Director of Swatch Bharat 
Mission, Sikkim Pollution Control Board (SPCB) and four sampled ULBs as shown 
below.

Simple Random Sampling (SRS) method was adopted to select the sample of four 168 
(out of seven) ULBs. The selected ULBs (60 per cent) accounted for approximately  
82 per cent of waste generated in the State during the review period.
7.3.6 Audit methodology
An entry conference was held on 17 August 2022 with the Secretary, Urban Development 
Department (UDD); Member Secretary, State Pollution Control Board (SPCB); and 
Municipal Commissioner, Gangtok Municipal Corporation (GMC) in which the audit 
methodology, scope, objectives, and criteria were explained. The audit methodology 
involved document analysis, responses to audit queries, joint physical verifications 
(JPV) with municipal staff and collection of photographic evidence. While framing 
the conclusions and recommendations, good practices regarding waste management in 
Sikkim have also been quoted. The results of the performance audit were discussed 
with the Secretary, UDD and the Member Secretary, SPCB in an exit conference on 04 
December 2023. Replies from the State Government, wherever received, were suitably 
incorporated.
7.3.7 Acknowledgement
Audit acknowledges the cooperation and assistance extended by the officers and staff of 
UDD, all the four ULBs and SPCB in conducting the performance audit. 
7.3.8	 Audit	findings
Audit findings are organised into five processes (generation, segregation, collection, 
transportation, processing and disposal) as per waste management cycle including 
planning and policy, and financial management which are discussed in the subsequent 
paragraphs.
7.3.9 Strategy and planning
Solid Waste Management is an integral component of municipal services delivered by 
ULBs, which are responsible for the safe and healthy environment of a city. Therefore, 
preparation and implementation of a strategic and detailed Municipal Solid Waste 
Management Plan is essential169.
7.3.9.1  State Policy and Strategy on Solid Waste Management
Rule 11(1)(A) of SWM Rules, 2016 (notified on 08 April 2016) stipulated that the 
State Government should prepare a State Policy and Strategy on SWM within one 
year of notification of the Rules. The policy should emphasise waste reduction, reuse, 
recycling, recovery and optimum utilisation of various components of solid waste to 
ensure minimisation of waste going to the landfill and minimise impact of solid waste 
on human health and environment.
168 GMC, MNP , NJNP and GyNP .
169 SBM2-Solid Waste Management Plan (para-1).

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
204
Audit observed that the State Gove rnment brought out (April 2019) State Policy on 
Solid Waste Management (SWM) Strategy in 2019, after a delay of two years. The 
Policy (paragraph 9.1) emphasised waste reduction by promoting responsible resource 
use and eco-friendly practices (green protocol), segregation at source, decentralised 
solid waste management and disposal, etc. The State Government issued green protocol 
during April 2019 as shown in the box placed alongside.
Audit further observed that policy initiative was taken for segregation of waste at source 
vide the State Policy ( paragraph 9.2), making it mandatory for all to follow minimum 
of two bin segregation at source and disposal of segregated discards through biological 
methods ( vermi-composting, aerobic composting or anaerobic digestion). Non-
biodegradable discards were to be sent for further segregation and recovery at waste 
pickers / scrap dealers’ facility or Material 
recovery facility. However, in practice, 
source segregation was not adopted by most 
of the households as discussed in Paragraph 
7.3.11.2.
Similarly, the State Policy on SWM Strategy 
(paragraph 9.3) stipulated decentralised 
mode of waste management considering tough 
and undulating terrain, scarce availability of 
land for SWM, effort, cost and infrastructure 
required to transport the waste to a common/
centralised facility. 
Audit observed that even four years after the 
policy initiative was approved in 2019, as of 
June 2023, only two (out of seven) ULBs had 
decentralised solid waste processing facilities 
at Mangan Nagar Panchayat (MNP) and Gangtok Municipal Corporation (GMC) as 
discussed in Paragraph 7.3.11.6 (a). 
Thus, due to inability of ULBs to ensure segregation of waste at source coupled with 
inadequate decentralised processing units, minimisation of waste going to landfill/ 
dumpsites waste was not adequately ensured to reduce negative impact on human health 
and environment. Conversely, a large percentage of waste was dumped at landfill/ 
dumpsites as discussed in Paragraph 7.3.11.6.
7.3.9.2  Municipal Solid waste management plan
The SWM Rules ( Paragraph 15) stipulated that the ULBs shall prepare solid waste 
management plan (SWMP) as per State Policy and Strategy on SWM within six months 
from date of notification of State Policy and Strategy for submission to State Government. 
Further, MSWM Manual (Part-II), 2016 (Section 1.4.5 and 1.4.6) emphasised the need 
for ULBs to prepare a detailed SWM plan, short term (one to five years) and long-term 
(20-25 years) action plans. Each short-term plan should be reviewed by the ULBs every 
two to three years to ensure institutional strengthening, community mobilisation, waste 
minimisation initiatives, waste collection and transportation, treatment and disposal, 
and financial outlay. The planning process is as shown in Chart 7.4:

MSWM Manuals, 2016 (Section 5.4 read with Section 4.5.1.9) also stipulated that ULBs 
should prepare contingency plans for appropriate storage of waste to tide over situations 
of non-performance of processing/treatment/disposal facilities and to control surface 
water impacts resulting from the production of leachate 168169
170 in a quantity greater than 
expected or with a quality worse than expected.
Audit also observed that the State Government prepared an action plan as per State 
Policy and Strategy on SWM in April 2019. The action plan envisaged preparation of 
Cluster Plan for SWM by ULB’s, disposal of waste at cluster level, stringent law for not 
adopting source segregation and initiation of maximum resource recovery.
Audit observed that the ULBs had neither prepared SWMP as required under the Rules 
as of June 2023 nor prepared short-term/long-term action plans during 2018-23 as 
mandated by SWM Rules, 2016. Even the contingency plan had not been prepared by 
the ULBs as of June 2023 as mandated by the MSWM manual, 2016.
MNP stated (March 2023) that the SWMP would be drawn up immediately in 
consultation with SPCB and SBM Team of UDD; the GMC informed (March 2023) that 
the draft action plan was under process for approval from State High Power Committee. 
With regard to contingency plans, the UDD informed (January 2023) that due to non-
availability of land, the ULBs were not able to frame contingency plan for processing, 
storage, treatment and disposal of waste generated. The UDD added that the Department 
had taken up the matter seriously and submitted proposal for allotment of lands for the 
purpose. 
After being pointed out by audit, the UDD stated (December 2023) that all seven 
ULBs had adopted (November 2023) the action plan of the State. Thus, the ULBs had 
not formulated the action plan as of October 2023, recording a delay of four years  
(from October 2019 to October 2023).

169 
170 Leachate -The liquid that seeps through solid waste and has extracts of dissolved or suspended 
material from it.

7.3.9.3 Unreliable assessment of quantity and composition of waste generated.
According to MSW Manual, 2016 Part II (Clause 1.4.3.3), ULBs should assess the 
quantity and composition of waste generated to plan for and design MSWM systems 
effectively. The quantity and composition of MSW generated in the ULB determine 
collection, processing, and disposal options that could be adopted. Further, the Manual 
also prescribes the methodology of assessment of waste generation (clause 1.4.3.3.1 
& 1.4.3.3.2). Audit scrutinised the data with respect to waste generation, collection, 
treatment and disposal. Observations in this regard are discussed below:
Table 7.4: Details of waste generated by all ULBs during 2018-23
(in Tonnes per day)
Year 2018-19 2019-20 2020-21 2021-22 2022-23*
Generation 75.10 74.70 71.90 65.50 68.90
Collection 67.10 74.60 71.90 65.50 68.90

Quantification of waste not reliable 
Audit noticed that the data was arrived based on estimation and the same was submitted 
(in Form-IV) to the SPCB. The quantification was not reliable in absence of supporting 
evidence, absence of weighing equipment at disposal points to measure the waste (except 
for one weighbridge 171 at Martam landfill site, catering to the need of GMC) during 
2018-23. Thus, the data on waste generation, treatment, etc. provided by the ULBs was 
not authentic.
This was evident by the fact that the data for 2018-19 did not add up to the total collection 
of waste (67.10 TPD)172.
The UDD, while accepting that the methodology of assessment might not be as per the 
guidelines, stated (January 2023) that assessment of waste was being done by the ULBs 
and waste collected were weighed at landfill on daily basis and assessment report was 
submitted every month. The reply is not acceptable as the weighing equipment was 
available only at Martam landfill, which caters to GMC and not to NJNP, MNP and 
GyNP. Thus, daily weighing at landfill was not factually correct. 
During exit conference, the Department stated (December 2023) that the quantification 
is based on number of trips of tipping vehicle carrying garbage. The reply is not tenable 
as the quantification did not include waste deposited at unauthorised places, waste 
recovered by informal waste pickers, Kabadi system, quantity of waste in the tipping 
vehicle, etc.   
Composition of waste
According to SWM Manual 2016 Part II (Clause 1.4.3.3.1), for the purpose of long-
term planning, the average amount of waste disposed by a specific class of generator 
should be estimated by averaging data from several samples. Audit observed that sample 
collection of waste was not done as of June 2023. In reply, the UDD stated (January 2023) 
171  Installed in February 2020. During January 2023 a total of 73.56 to 50.56 tpd of waste received at 
Martam landfill.
172 Collection = Treatment+ Disposal.

Chapter VII: Local Bodies
207
that the sample could not be collected in absence of technical expert and environmental 
scientist.
Thus, absence of reliable data has constrained UDD and the ULBs to plan and design 
MSWM systems effectively.
The State Government brought out (April 2019) State Policy on Solid Waste Management 
(SWM) Strategy and also an action plan. The ULBs had neither prepared MSWMP , 
short term and long-term plan, contingency plan nor maintained authentic data for 
waste generation, treatment and disposal etc. for effective management of solid waste.
7.3.10 Financial Management  
Financial management refers to management of funds economically, efficiently and 
effectively. It also includes budget management, both receipt and payment and assets 
and liabilities. The position of financial management in ULBs is discussed below.
7.3.10.1 Receipts and expenditure
According to Clause 15(x) of SWM Rules 2016, ULBs were required to make adequate 
provisions of funds for capital investment as well as operation and maintenance of SWM 
services in annual budget ensuring that funds for discretionary functions of local bodies 
were allocated only after meeting the requirement of necessary funds for SWM and 
other obligatory functions. The details of receipt and expenditure of four test checked 
ULBs for 2018-23 are given in the table below:

in the subsequent paragraphs.
7.3.10.2  Assessment of requirement of funds
According to SWM Manual 2016 Part II (u/s 1.4.5.6.2), SWM services are sustainable 
only if they are financially viable on a stand-alone basis. Therefore, the assessment of 
financial viability is an important step in planning SWM system. 
Audit scrutiny revealed that assessment of financial viability of SWM services on a 
stand-alone basis and their sustainability was not carried out by any of the ULBs test

checked. Though the ULBs were primarily dependent on government grants for SWM, 
ranging from 35 to 94 per cent of the total expenditure, none of the test checked ULBs 
had either assessed financial viability of SWM services nor prepared Detailed Project 
Reports (DPRs) for SWM during 2018-23 for obtaining funds under SBM for improved 
solid waste management, especially relating to processing and treatment of solid waste. 
This had resulted in inadequate infrastructure for the management of the waste generated 
in the selected ULBs.
In reply (January 2023), the UDD stated that the Department had sought support from

While accepting that the user fee is not sufficient to carry out waste management services, 
UDD stated (Jan 2023) that the Department was considering revising user fee and fee

However as of June 2023 the user charges were not revised. Further, the user charges 
were realised only for 20 to 40 per cent of households in test checked ULBs MNP and 
GyNP as pointed out in succeeding Paragraph 7.3.10.3. Had the ULBs collected the 
user fees for all the households the operational loss would have been marginally reduced.
7.3.10.3 Levy and collection of user charges for solid waste management
Clause 15(f) of SWM Rules, 2016 provides for levy of SWM user charges for purpose 
of collection, transportation and disposal of solid waste. As per the notification (August 
2019) issued by UDD, user charges @ ₹ 30 per month per household should be levied. 
For commercial establishments, the user charges ranged from ₹30 to ₹ 35,000 per month 
depending on nature of business, which was to be paid along with renewal fee of trade 
license.
Audit observed that the user charges were collected by deputing ULB staff in GyNP and 
MNP. In case of GMC and NJNP, user charges were collected by contractors, to whom 
the task of handling of solid waste had been outsourced. 

Chapter VII: Local Bodies
209
Audit observed that the GMC and NJNP did not mention the number of households from 
whom the user fees were to be collected for waste collection services by the contractor. 
The actual amount of user fee collected by these contractors from the households could 
not be ascertained in Audit as they were not required to submit the details of collection 
to the ULBs. 
In absence of these records, audit could not ascertain whether the annual fee charged by 
the ULB to contractor was sufficient or not.
Similarly, in case of MNP and GyNP, user charges were collected by the ULBs by 
deputing their staff as fee collectors. It was observed that the ULBs had not maintained 
the database of ward-wise number of households. Further, ULBs also could not produce 
ward-wise monthly challans for the period of audit. Thus, the possibilities of leakage of 
revenue cannot be ruled out. 
Analysis of available documents revealed that regular collection of user fee was not 
ensured. The user charges realised ranged from 20 to 30 per cent households in case of 
MNP and GyNP respectively as against the target of 90 per cent collection efficiency 
stipulated in the SLB set by the State Government, even after considering the total 
households as per 2011 census.
7.3.10.4 Extra expenditure by GMC towards garbage vehicles 
GMC had outsourced its waste collection and transportation activities to 18 contractors 
who were L1 in 20 wards of the city as of 2022-23 based on open tender.
The agreement drawn between the outsourced contractor and GMC inter-alia provided 
that (i) the contractor shall collect garbage and municipal waste 6-6.30 AM daily and 
do all the waste management in compliance with MSW Rules, 2016 till its disposal at 
landfill; (ii) One driver and two safaikaramcharis will be provided by GMC to each 
truck and salary of them will be borne by GMC, (ii) fuel in garbage truck to be provided 
by the contractor; (iii) all the minor maintenance and repair of garbage truck provided 
to the contractors will be borne by contractor; (iv) user fee will be collected by the 
contractor from the household as per the latest notification of UDD without revenue 
sharing with GMC, etc. 
The contract was liable to be terminated in case of violation of terms and conditions of 
the contract and forfeiture of the contract amount deposited by the contractor. 
Audit scrutiny revealed that the contractors were not adhering to the terms of contract 
relating to cost of fuel and minor repair and maintenance of garbage truck to be borne 
by the Contractors. Instead, the cost of fuel and repair and maintenance of garbage truck 
was borne by GMC in contravention of the agreement. It was also noticed that GMC did 
not maintain data for minor and major repair and maintenance separately.
As a result, GMC had to incur additional expenditure of ₹ 82.78 lakh towards 
providing fuel during 2022-23, leading to avoidable expenditure and undue favour to 
the contractors. Further an amount of ₹ 36.61 lakh was incurred towards repair and 
maintenance of Garbage trucks during 2022-23. However, in absence of segregation of 
expenditure between minor and major repair and maintenance, Audit could not ascertain 
the actual amount of additional expenditure towards minor repair and maintenance of 
vehicle which should have been borne by the contractor.

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
210
GMC however, had not initiated any action against the defaulting contractors to terminate 
the contract and effect forfeiture of contract amount deposited by the contractor.
Not only this, the ULBs had neither attempted to assess the surplus revenue earned by 
these agencies in collection and transportation of waste nor explored possibilities of 
revenue sharing model with these agencies.
During exit conference (04 December 2023), the Department stated that the ULBs had 
to bear the expenditure towards minor repair and maintenance and fuel as the Garbage 
contractors have expressed their inability to bear this expenditure in view of high 
operational cost.
The reply is not tenable as the contractors were liable to bear the expenditure as per the 
terms and condition of the contract. Hence, either the contract amount should be forefeited 
from the contractors or GMC should ensure that the cost of cost and minor repairs are 
borne by the contractor in compliance to the conditions of contract immediately.
7.3.10.5  Extra expenditure by NJNP towards garbage vehicles 
Similarly, NJNP had also outsourced its waste collection and transportation activities to 
one contractor during 2022-23 at ₹ seven lakh per annum.
The agreement provided that (i) the contractor shall collect garbage and municipal waste 
before 9:00 AM daily and transfer the same to Sipchu dumpsite; (ii) the contractor shall 
collect specified user charges from households and commercial establishments; (ii) token 
fee of motor vehicles to be borne by the contractor; (iii) the contractor shall maintain the 
garbage vehicles provided to them such as expenditure towards repair of tyres, etc.; (iv) 
salary of drivers and safaikarmacharis to be borne by the contractor. 
The contract was liable to be terminated in case of violation of terms and conditions 
of the contract and forfeiture of the contract amount deposited by the contractor. The 
schedule appended with the contract agreement specified a rate ranging between ₹80 
(for Readymade cloth shop) to ₹ 1,500 per month (for hotels star category).
Audit scrutiny revealed that while the contractor levied the user fee as per the schedule 
appended with the contract agreement but higher than the rates specified in the state 
government notification (November 2019) as pointed out in Paragraph 7.3.10.3. 
However, the contractor was not adhering to the terms of contract relating to repair and 
maintenance of garbage truck and salary of driver and safaikramcharis as required under 
the agreement. As a result, NJNP had to incur additional expenditure of ₹  47.37 lakh 
towards minor repair and maintenance (₹1.59 lakh) of the four Garbage vehicles and 
salary of drivers and safaikarmacharis (₹ 45.78 lakh) engaged in garbage vehicles 
during 2022-23, leading to avoidable expenditure in one year alone and undue favour to 
the contractors. 
NJNP had not initiated any action against the defaulting contractors to terminate the 
contract and for forfeiture of the contract amount deposited by the contractor as of 
November 2023.
7.3.10.6  Infructuous expenditure on procurement of Eco-bins 
The State Government (UDD) proposed (October 2018) to procure 8,728 numbers of 
compost eco bins at a cost of ₹ one crore with an objective to convert waste generated 
into compost at household level for use as manure in the terrace for organic produce. 
The State Cabinet approved (27.11.2018) the proposal of UDD vide Cabinet memo No. 

Chapter VII: Local Bodies
211
55/Secy/SBM/UDHD/GoS/15-16 dated 27.11.2018 to procure eco-bins and identify the 
beneficiaries by the UDD before procurement of the bins.
Audit observed that the objective of procurement of eco-bins to convert waste into 
compost at household level for use as manure was not achieved as the households were 
not comfortable with continued use of eco-bins as it was a difficult and time-consuming 
process.
Based on Cabinet approval, UDD placed the order (December 2018) with SIMFED for 
supply of compost bin (eco bin) at Gangtok (6,982 nos.), Jorethang (1,571 nos.), and 
Mangan (175 nos.). The Department released 100 per cent advance payment of (₹ one 
crore for eco-bins) to SIMFED (December 2018) against supply of 8,728 compost eco 
bins.

available for verification.

additional four ULBs (RNP, SNP, GyNP and NMC) by diverting the eco-bins 
initially allotted to NJNP and GMC for further distribution to beneficiaries.

years (November 2023).

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
212
During exit conference (December 2023), the Department stated that due to high altitude 
and cold weather, the composting was a difficult and time-consuming process and thus 
some of the households were not comfortable with continued use of eco-bins. The 
Department, however, assured that the beneficiaries would be persuaded to use compost 
bins. 
The reply is not tenable as the department (UDD) had not considered the suitability 
of compost bins for hilly terrain such as Sikkim where composting is difficult, before 
the procurement of eco-bins. The reply is also silent on reasons for non-distribution of 
2000 ecobins by GMC and the status of utilisation of 5,506 eco-bins at NMC, GyNP, 
GMC, and NJNP which were purchased for minimising waste going to landfill.
Thus the objective of the procurement of eco-bins to convert waste into compost at 
household level for use as manure was not achieved despite incurring ₹ one crore due to 
inability of UDD to devise the criteria for identification of beneficiary, coverage of only 
17 to 31 per cent households and non-assessment of the suitability of compost bins to 
the State.

7.3.11  Management of solid waste
Solid waste management refers to all the activities associated with handling of discarded 
solid materials, from generation, prevention, treatment, handling, reuse and ultimate 
residual disposition of solid waste 177. The position of solid waste management in the 
ULBs of Sikkim is discussed below.
7.3.11.1  Information, Education and Communication (IEC)
SWM Rules, 2016 (Clause 15 (zg)) and Manuals on MSWM, 2016 (Section 1.4.5.13) 
stipulated undertaking of IEC activities by the ULBs to create public awareness and 
educate waste generators to achieve the overall objectives of SWM. The IEC campaign 
should target households, shops, and commercial and institutional premises as well 
as other stakeholders such as municipal officials, elected representatives, schools, 
NonGovernment Organisations (NGOs), the informal sector, media, etc., to ensure 
behavioural changes to adopt effective management of municipal waste.

of Urban Affairs in January 2015.

7.3.11.2  Generation of waste  
The total solid waste generation by the seven ULBs of the State was stated to be 68.8 tpd 
for 2022-23. The four-test checked ULBs accounted for 56.6 tpd of waste generated 
during 2022-23. The trend of solid waste generation in respect of GMC is shown in table 
below:
