Audit analysis revealed that the per capita waste generation in case of GMC had 
decreased marginally during 2018-19 (from 283 gm/day) to 2022-23 (219 gm/day) but 
was still high as against the national average per capita waste generation (98 to 117 gm/ 
day) as reflected in the CPCB report. In absence of weighing equipment, data of waste 
generation in other three test checked ULBs (MNP, GyNP and NJNP) was not reliable. 
Hence, audit could not ascertain the trend of waste generation in these ULBs.
7.3.11.3  Segregation of waste  
Segregation of solid waste is required for separating recyclable material, organic waste 
for processing and residual inert material for disposal. MSW Manuals, 2000 (Section 
8.10.1(a) and SWM rules 2016 (Section 4.1(a)) stipulate that ULBs must accord highest 
priority for segregation of waste at source. 

Chapter VII: Local Bodies
215
The State Policy on Solid Waste Management Strategy (April 2019) emphasised 
(paragraph 9.2) segregation of waste at source for efficient and safe handling of 
discards and made it mandatory for all to follow minimum of two bin segregation at 
source. While setting the target under Service Level Benchmark (2016), the ULBs had 
set the target of 100 per cent source segregation of solid waste.
The State Government reiterated (November 2022) compulsory segregation of waste 
by the waste generators to reduce burden to landfill and scientific disposal of waste, 
failing which violators would be penalised as per rules.
All the four test checked ULBs claimed that solid waste was stored at source in 
segregated form by the households/ commercial establishments and 89 to 100 per cent 
of premises were segregating the waste at source.
Joint physical verification by Audit along with municipal staff, however, revealed 
(April 2023) that majority of households were not adopting source segregation, as 
shown in images.
Image 7.7: Gangtok
 Image 7.8: Mangan
 Image 7.9: Gyalshing
Thus, segregation of waste by households, as reported by ULBs was not in practice, 
despite reiteration (November 2022) by the State Government. The ULBs also had not 
initiated stringent measures such as refusal of unsegregated waste from households 
during collection of waste by garbage vehicle. The violators were neither identified by 
the ULBs nor penalised as envisaged in the rules as of June 2023. 
The UDD stated (January 2023) that compulsory source segregation of municipal 
waste in urban areas has been notified from the office of Chief Secretary and also by 
the ULBs. The Department added that waste processing plant and MRF has also been 
proposed to be installed at landfill and ULBs for segregation of waste. Training for 
Self Help Groups (SHGs) and NGOs in source segregation was also proposed. 
However, source segregation was not being carried out and waste processing plant and 
MRF were yet to be installed and operationalised as of June 2023.
	En-route segregation of waste
Audit observed that instead of adopting segregation at source, limited quantity of 
segregation was done enroute by the safaikarmachari  deployed in the garbage 
vehicle, while transporting the waste to landfill. The recyclable items such as plastic, 
glass, hard board, etc. were separated enroute by Safaikarmacharis  (as shown in 
images) and stored in separate sacks for handing ove r to scrap dealer by these staff at  
landfill site. 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
216
Image 7.10: Gyalshing
 Image 7.11: Mangan
However, the exact quantity of such items disposed to scrap dealers was not verifiable 
in the absence of documentary evidence. The contractor, engaged for overseeing the 
Sipchu landfill site, was remitting ₹20,000 per month towards sale of recyclable items to 
NJNP. The contractor had not maintained any documents despite direction (May 2022) 
from NJNP to maintain logbook to keep track of volume and type of waste accumulated 
at dumpsite. Even the agreement (April 2021 and April 2022) with the contractor for 
overseeing the Sipchu landfill site provided for proper quantification of segregated waste.
Similarly, for Martam landfill, a contractor was engaged to lift recyclable items for 
sale to scrap dealer on payment of annual fee of ₹ 50,000 to GMC. However, no 
details regarding the amount of recycled waste received/processed at the landfill were 
maintained by him too. 
The ULBs did not accord due priority to segregation of waste at source. Even the limited 
quantity of waste segregated enroute was also not quantifiable in absence of proper 
records relating to quantity of re-cyclable waste segregated and sold to scrap dealers 
during 2018-23.
7.3.11.4 Collection of waste
Manual on MSW, 2000 (u/s 10.3 and 10.4) envisaged upon ULBs to arrange for collection 
of all types of solid waste 178 from doorstep or community bins regularly to ensure that 
waste stored at source is not disposed of on the streets, drains, water bodies, etc.
The Handbook of service level benchmarking, 2018 (paragraph2.3.1) also emphasised 
doorstep level collection as an essential and critical starting point in the entire chain 
of scientific SWM services. Waste-free clean roads and drains, scientific treatment of 
waste, recycling and disposal can all be achieved in a sustainable manner only if door-
to-door collection of waste is sustained.
Audit observed that door-to-door collection was done in all the four test checked ULBs 
through motorised vehicles. While door-to-door collection of waste was done by ULBs 
in GyNP and MNP, the activity was outsourced to GMC (to NGOs) and NJNP (private 
individual). Garbage trucks were deployed early in the morning and bell ring system was 
adopted to alert the households to bring their waste for collection at a pre-determined 
point in the locality. The truck collected waste from these points from households 
and institutions and then transported to landfill/ dumpsite. The coverage of door-to-
door collection was 100 per cent  for residential and non-residential buildings through 
motorised vehicles as of March 2023. The effort of ULBs in this regard was appreciable. 
178 domestic, trade and institutional, food/biodegradable waste, recyclable waste material/non-
biodegradable waste.

Chapter VII: Local Bodies
217
Audit, however, observed that the vehicles used were uncovered (open top) and non- 
compartmentalised (no separate compartment for carrying wet and dry waste) as 
discussed in Paragraph 7.3.11.5. Further, staff deployed for door-to-door collection 
were not found to be using protective gears as mentioned in subsequent paragraphs.
	Street sweeping and cleaning on daily basis
MSW Manual, 2016 (u/s 2.4.1 & 2.4.2) stipulated well-planned, time-bound daily 
system of street sweeping including adequate staff and equipment. Street sweepers 
should report daily for duty at designated locations having provisions for storing street 
sweeping equipment and wear proper protective gear such as boots, gloves, etc. 
Audit observed that all the four-test checked ULBs ensured daily sweeping of entire stretch 
(38.5 km) of streets under their respective jurisdiction manually by safaikarmacharis, 
engaged by the ULBs, through long broom and hand cart.
However, ULBs had not adopted or deployed mechanised vehicle for sweeping as being 
practised in other parts of the country.
	Use of personal protection equipment not ensured
SWM Rules, 2016 (clause 15 (zd)) envisaged upon ULBs to ensure provisioning of 
personal protection equipment including uniform, hand gloves, raincoats, appropriate 
footwear and masks to all workers handling solid waste and their use by workforce. 
Audit observed during physical verification (April-June 2023) that work force involved 
in manual handling of waste used protective equipment partially, in test-checked ULBs 
(NJNP, MNP and GMC at Martam). While boots were used, hand gloves were not used 
in some cases as shown in the following image.
Image 7.12: Martam (Under GMC)
 Image 7.13: Sipchu (Under NJNP)
There was no improvement on this count despite being pointed out by SPCB in their 
inspection (November 2020) report of Martam landfill about employees working without 
wearing safety gear. The ULBs may conduct awareness program through IEC activities 
to encourage the workers engaged in SWM activities to use protective gears to ensure 
safe disposal of municipal waste.
Non-utilisation of protective equipment is risky and may lead to serious health hazards. 
Efforts taken for enforcing use of protective gears by the workers on the part of the 
ULBs were not available for audit verification.  
The effort of the ULBs about 100 per cent door to door collection of waste was appreciable, 
street sweeping was carried out on daily basis manually by the safaikarmacharis, without 
exploring possibilities of deploying mechanical means, as was being done in other parts 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023

of the country. The work force involved in manual handling of waste continued to use 
protective gear partially even after being pointed out by SPCB in November 2020. 
7.3.11.5  Transportation of waste 
The transportation of solid waste from source of generation to the authorised destination 
is important to ensure its proper disposal. 
MSW Rule 2000 (clause 4 of Schedule II) and MSW Manual, 2016 ( paragraph 2.3.2 
and 2.3.12.1) stipulated that vehicles used for transportation of waste shall be covered 
and fitted with GPS, duly synchronised with the GIS, to monitor and track waste 
transportation vehicles and identify any irregularities in waste movement. Waste should 
not be visible to public, nor exposed to open environment preventing their scattering. 
Byelaws (paragraph 6.18) of Solid Waste (Management & Handling) Cleanliness and 
Sanitation for Urban sector of Sikkim (April 2019) also emphasised that ULB shall 
ensure to transport the collected waste to the final disposal place in closed vehicles.
A total of 30 vehicles (Tipping truck-18; non-tipping truck-5; compactors-4; JCB 
loader-1 and others-2) were used for handling of waste by four test checked ULBs 
during 2022-23. 
Audit noticed that none of the tipping vehicles used were covered (closed top type) 
having separate compartment for carrying segregated waste (bio-degradable and 
recyclable) and fitted with GPS, which was in violation of Rules as stated above. 
The ULBs stated (April-May 2023) that although the vehicles were open type, waste 
was covered before transportation to landfill/ dumpsite.
Physical verification (June 2023) of 10 vehicles at Martam landfill conducted by Audit 
along with Municipal staff revealed that all the ten vehicles (checked at Martam) 
transported waste in uncovered vehicles without compartment as shown in image:
Image 7.14: Weighbridge at Martam
 Image 7.15: Weighbridge at Martam
Image 7.16: Sipchu

Chapter VII: Local Bodies
219
In reply, UDD (January 2023) stated that instruction were issued to all ULBs to adopt 
hygienic methods while transporting waste to landfill.
However, the waste continued to be transported in uncovered vehicles without partition 
for carrying dry and wet waste separately as of June 2023.
The ULBs transported waste in uncovered vehicles without partition for carrying dry and 
wet waste separately, making waste visible to public and exposed to open environment. 
The ULBs were not able to monitor and track the waste transportation vehicles to identify 
any irregularities in waste movement in absence of GPS in the vehicles.
7.3.11.6 Processing of Municipal Solid Waste
According to the CPCB Guidelines (paragraph 4.1) for Disposal of Legacy Waste (Old 
Municipal Solid Waste), processing means any scientific process by which segregated 
solid waste is handled for the purpose of reuse, recycling or transformation into new 
products (paragraph 2(xvii)). 
MSW Rules 2016 (clause 15(v) (a) & (b)) envisaged upon Secretary, UDD and ULB 
authorities to lay emphasis on waste reduction by installing processing facilities and 
associated infrastructure to ensure minimisation of waste going to the landfill thereby 
minimising impact of solid waste on human health and environment.
Though the State Policy on SWM Strategy (April 2019) laid emphasis on reduction, 
reuse and recycle, major portion of waste generated still reached the landfill/ dump site 
during 2018-22 due to limited processing as discussed below:
a)  Underutilisation of processing facilities
The State Policy on Solid Waste Management Strategy 2019 envisaged (Para-4.1 and 
13.1) zero waste policy and recommended incentivising and having a delineated chain 
of command for implementation of Zero Waste systems, which included Secretary, 
UDD and others. 
Audit observed that as against the generation of 68.9 tpd of waste (2022-23), the available 
processing facilities in the State accounted for only 51.8 tpd, leading to shortage of 25 
per cent in terms of capacity. Even these available facilities were not functional for 
major part of the review period. 
Audit observed that only two (out of seven) ULBs had decentralised solid waste 
processing facilities (aggregating to 51.80 tpd) at Mangan and Gangtok as of June 2023. 
Out of the total 51.80 tpd processing capacity of the State, 51 tpd was under GMC, 
and 0.8 tpd was with MNP. The remaining ULBs did not have any waste processing 
infrastructure.
Audit also observed that the waste processing facility (50 tpd) at Martam landfill site 
was non-functional since 13 November 2021 to 23 February 2023 and after working for 
a brief period of four months (from 24 February to 31 May 2022), went nonfunctional 
again from June 2023. Thus, due to underutilisation of these facilities, processing of

in the table below.

b)  Inadequate infrastructure for processing 
As per Clause 15(v) of SWM Rule-2016, ULBs should facilitate construction, operation 
and maintenance of solid waste processing facilities and preference shall be given to 
decentralised processing to minimise transportation cost and environmental impacts 
such as bio -methanation, micro composting, vermin composting, anaerobic digestion 
or any other appropriate processing for bio-stabilisation of bio-degradable wastes. 
The State Policy on Solid Waste Management Strategy ( paragraph 9.3) stipulated 
decentralised mode of waste management considering tough and undulating terrain, 
scarce availability of land for SWM, effort, cost and infrastructure required to transport 
the waste to a common/centralised facility.

b)  Inadequate infrastructure for processing
As per Clause 15(v) of SWM Rule-2016, ULBs should facilitate construction, operation 
and maintenance of solid waste processing facilities and preference shall be given to 
decentralised processing to minimise transportation cost and environmental impacts 
such as bio-methanation, micro composting, vermin composting, anaerobic digestion or

The State Policy on Solid Waste Management Strategy ( paragraph 9.3) stipulated 
decentralised mode of waste management considering tough and undulating terrain, 
scarce availability of land for SWM, effort, cost and infrastructure required to transport 
the waste to a common/centralised facility.  
Audit observations in this regard are discussed below. 
	Composting centres
Composting179 is a controlled process involving microbial decomposition of organic 
matter. Only two ULBs (MNP -0.80 tpd and GMC-51 tpd) possessed composting plants. 
While the compost plant of 50 tpd was set up at Martam Sanitary landfill field (SLF), 
another 1 tpd was set up at Kanchanjunga market complex at Gangtok by GMC. Details 
of processing are reflected in Paragraph 7.3.11.6(a). The compost plant at Mangan was 
functional and processed waste was sold to market and also utilised by ULB.
	Material Recovery Facility (MRF)
Materials Recovery Facility (MRF) means a facility where non-compostable solid waste 
can be temporarily stored by the local body or any person/ agency authorised by ULBs 
to facilitate segregation, sorting, recycling and/or recovery of recyclables from various 
components of waste by authorised informal sector of waste pickers, informal recyclers 
or any other work force engaged by the local body before the waste is delivered or taken 
up for its processing or disposal180.
The State Government sanctioned (May 2022) construction of seven MRFs, one in each 
ULB and simultaneously released fund of ₹ 105.63 lakh (@ ₹ 15.09 lakh /MRF). Only 
two MRFs (NJNP and GMC) were completed as of June 2023, of which the facility at 
NJNP was not functional.
Joint physical verification of MRF at NJNP by Audit along with municipal staff  showed 
(May 2023) that the MRF, though complete by 22.11.2022, was not functional as machinery 
(such as shredder, compressor, conveyor belt, weigh bridge, etc.) were not installed. No 
action was initiated by the ULB to start the process of obtaining these machineries 
and installing the same to make the MRF functional, for the last seven months. As a 
result, not only was the fund 
of ₹ 30.85 lakh 181, incurred 
towards construction not 
yielding value for money but 
five tpd waste could not be 
processed. 
While the MRF was under 
construction in case of MNP; 
GyNP had not commenced 
construction for want of 
suitable land as of June 2023. 
It was also observed that existing MRF of GyNP was not functional since April 2022 
owing to damage caused by landslide as shown in the image.

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
222
Audit further observed that the other two MRFs completed at NJNP and GMC were 
completed within one and a half years of sanction, while the remaining five MRF (MNP, 
SNP, RNP, GyNP and NMC) were yet to be completed even after two years of sanction 
of the project. Even the scheduled date of completion was not found on record. 
	Bio mining of waste
Biomining refers 182 to the excavation of old dumped waste and making windrow of 
legacy waste thereafter stabilisation of the waste through bio-remediation i.e. exposure 
of all the waste to air along with use of composting bio-cultures, i.e. screening of the 
stabilised waste to recover all valuable resources (like organic fines, bricks, stones, 
plastics, metals, clothes, rags etc.) followed by its sustainable management through 
recycling, co-processing, road making etc.
The SWM Rules, 2016 (clause15 (zj and zk) envisaged upon ULBs to investigate and 
analyse all old open dumpsites and existing operational dumpsites for their potential of 
bio-mining and bioremediation and wherever feasible, take necessary action to bio-mine 
or bio-remediate the sites. 
Audit observed that the ULBs had not initiated necessary action for bio-mining and 
bioremediation of old and open dumpsites as of June 2023, except once by inviting 
expression of interest (EoI) in August 2022 for preparation of DPR for Martam landfill. 
Thereafter, no follow up was done to commence bio-mining and bioremediation as of 
June 2023.
	Waste to Energy plants
As per Clause 21 of SWM Rule 2016, non-recyclable waste having calorific value of 
1,500 kilocalorie per kilogram (kcl/kg) or more shall not be disposed of to landfill and 
shall only be utilised for generating energy either through refuse derived fuel or by 
giving away as feed stock for preparing refuse derived fuel. High calorific wastes shall 
be used for co-processing in cement or thermal power plants. The ULBs should propose 
to set up waste to energy processing plant of more than five TPD. 
A total of 20.2 (out of 56.55) TPD was processed during 2021-22 by four test checked 
ULBs. However, no facility to convert waste to energy by applying technology such as 
incineration, gasification, pyrolysis, or any other technology was available with ULBs. 
Waste to energy plant was not set up in any of test checked ULBs during 2018-23.
Inadequate emphasis by UDD and ULBs on waste reduction by non-installation 
of processing facilities such as bio remediation, waste to energy plants, etc. and 
underutilisation of compost plant (at Martam), led to a large percentage of waste (40 to 
100 per cent) reaching the landfill/ dumpsite during 2018-23 thereby impacting human 
health and environment.
7.3.12 Disposal of solid waste
According to CPCB guidelines ( paragraph 2 ) for Disposal of Legacy Waste (Old 
Municipal Solid Waste), disposal of solid waste means the final and safe disposal of 
post-processed residual solid waste, inert street sweepings and silt from surface drains 
on land as specified in Schedule I of SWM Rules, 2016 to prevent contamination of 
ground water, surface water, ambient air and attraction of animals or birds. 
Schedule-I of the MSW Rules, 2000 provided the time schedule of December 2001 or 
earlier for improvement of existing Sanitary Land Fills (SLF) and December 2002 or earlier 
for identification of landfill sites for future use and making site(s) ready for operation.
182  According to CPCB Guidelines (para- 4.1) for Disposal of Legacy Waste (Old Municipal Solid Waste) 
issued in February 2019.

Chapter VII: Local Bodies
223
Clause 11 (f) and 12 (a) of MSW Rules, 2016 also provides that the State and District 
authorities shall facilitate identification and allocation of suitable land for sanitary 
landfill for setting up solid waste processing and disposal facilities to local authorities 
within one year from the date of notification of the Rules. 
Audit noticed that ULBs did not have sanitary landfill sites for disposal of solid waste, 
except for Gangtok town, operated by GMC. Three (out of 7) ULBs (NNP, NJNP and 
GyNP) used dumping yard at Sipchu in West Sikkim for disposal of waste. Another 
four ULBs (GMC, RNP, SNP and MNP) used landfill site at Martam as of March 2023.  
MNP was dumping waste at Chandey dumping yard but due to public complaints, the 
Chandey site was closed from March 2023. Thereafter, MNP had been dumping their 
waste at Martam, which is located at a distance of nearly 100 km from Mangan.
One site at Ringdam in North Sikkim has been identified (April 2017) for developing a 
landfill. However, construction work had not commenced as of March 2023 for want of 
environment clearance. 
Thus, two ULBs (Rangpo and Singtam) were yet to identify land for setting up of sanitary 
landfills and another ULB ( Mangan) though identified the site, had not been able to 
develop the same even after 21 years of stipulated time frame (since December 2002).
Audit observations on the functioning of these landfills/dump sites are given below. 
7.3.12.1 Martam Sanitary landfill
Martam SLF was handed over to GMC in 2017 by UDD having a capacity of 50 tpd for 
waste treatment. It is located 18 km from Gangtok and 0.5 km from main road. The SLF 
is managed by GMC and caters to waste disposal of three ULBs (Rangpo, Singtam and 
Managan) besides GMC. Important points noticed during audit are discussed below.
	Land fill near river
Schedule I of SWM Rule 2016 (A) (vii) stipulated that the landfill site shall be 100 meter 
away from river, 200 meter from highways, habitations and water supply wells. It was 
noticed that the landfill was situated near river Ranikhola, at a distance of hardly 10 to 
25 meters at various stretches, on the hill slope, as shown in  Images 7.18 and 7.19.
Further, the area occupied by solid waste dumping increased from 0.99 ha in May 2017 
to 1.44 ha in December 2022 and height of waste accumulation also increased as shown 
in satellite Images 7.18 and 7.19.
Image	7.18:	Satellite	Image	at	the	time	of	completion	of	landfill	(November	2017)

	Lifespan of landfill sites
The Martam SLF was designed for 25 
years (up to 2040) to cater to GMC. It 
was, however, observed that lifespan 
of SLF was depleting fast primarily 
owing to dumping of mixed waste, 
in violation of the MSW Rules 2000 
(Schedule II (6) and low composting 
due to underutilisation of existing 
processing facilities (50 tpd plant at 
Martam) and inadequate initiatives by 
ULBs to install processing facilities 
through Refused derived fuel (RDF), 
waste to energy, etc. 
The State Pollution Control Board, 
Sikkim cautioned (September 2020) that the solid waste processing sites at Martam 
and Sipchu would not last for more than five more years (up to 2025) and advised more 
scientific waste management approaches such as recycling, conversion of 100 per cent 
of biodegradable wastes to compost and non-biodegradable waste to Refuse Derived 
Fuel / Waste to Energy technology183. 
The SPCB advised setting up of an RDF Plant at Martam and Sipchu to reduce the solid 
waste load and increase the life of these two sites. 
Audit observed that this advisory was not acted upon and no follow up was done as 
of June 2023. The ULBs continued to dump a large percentage of waste to landfill/ 
dumpsites and scientific waste management approach was yet to be adopted. 
	Disposal of unprocessed waste to landfills
As mentioned in the preceding paragraph, compost plant of 50 tpd at Martam SLF was 
non-functional for more than one year. According to SWM Rule 2016 ( paragraph (iii) 
of Schedule I), waste processing facility shall be planned as an integral part of the land 
fill site.

	Legacy waste not disposed

biodegradable, non-combustible and non-reactive inert waste and pre-processing rejects 
and residues from waste processing facilities wereallowed to go to sanitary landfill to 
achieve the desired objective of zero waste going to landfill.  
The SWM Rules, 2016 (paragraph-15(zj)) directed ULBs to clear legacy waste dumps 
as well as exis ting operational dumpsite down to pre-existing ground level through a 
process called bioremediation or biomining, which refers to an environment-friendly

7.3.12.2 Sipchu dumpsite 
The landfill site was handed over to NJNP in 2019-20 which catered to South and West 
Sikkim. In one portion of the site, civil work relating to construction of processing units, 
landfill, etc. was under execution through a contractor by the UDD. Pending completion 
of sanitary landfill, a portion of the complex was used as dumping yard for dumping 
unprocessed waste, currently. Important points noticed during audit are discussed below: 
	Dumpsite near the river
Rule 20 of MSWM Rules 2016 stipulated that construction of landfill on the hill shall 
be avoided in the hilly areas. Further, Schedule I of SWM Rule 2016 (A) (vii) stipulated 
that the landfill site shall 
be 100 meter away from 
river, 200 meter from 
Highways, Habitations 
and water supply wells. 
It was noticed that the

placed alongside.
	Non-completion of landfill site at Sipchu
For catering to the needs of South and West district of ULBs and others, landfill site 
at Sipchu, near Jorethang was identified. Accordingly, work order for construction of 
‘Regional facility for solid waste treatment and disposal project’ at Sipchu was issued 
(October 2018) to the contractor ( Shri Karcho Bhutia) at a cost of ₹ 17.49 crore with 
stipulation to complete within eighteen months (by March 2020). The contractor had 
been paid ₹ 9.30 crore as of March 2023. During joint physical verification (May 2023) 
of the landfill, audit observed that the progress of work was very slow and as against the 
expenditure of ₹ 9.30 crore, only the work relating to site levelling and boundary wall 
was completed as of the date of JPV . 
The estimate was revised (January 2023) to ₹ 27.21 crore (from ₹ 17.49 crore) on 
the ground of change in scope of work such as strengthening of RCC structure, river 
training works to deviate the course of river, protective work at the approach road to

The UDD invited (April 2018) EoI and also issued (October 2018) work order to the 
contractor for ₹ 15.99 cr ore with stipulation to complete within 18 months (by April 
2020). The mobilisation advance of ₹ 2.30 crore was also released (August 2019) to the 
contractor against the bank guarantee.  
Audit observed that action towards environment clearance as required under the norms, 
was initiated only in September 2018, and final clearance was received in November 
2021. The project could not commence as of June 2023 even after obtaining environment 
clearance.
The solid waste, therefore, had to be transported to Martam, which is located 88 km 
away, leading to unnecessary burdening of landfill at Martam and avoidable extra 
expenditure on transportation. 
The Martam SLF and Sipchu dumpsite were situated near the river-bed in violation of 
SWM rules, unprocessed and mixed waste were being disposed of to these sites, and 
the capping layer of earth covering was not provided over waste to avoid erosion and 
collection of leachates at landfills sites. The waste was left open and exposed to open 
environment. Drainage berms were also not constructed to divert run-off away from the 
active cell of the landfill to prevent further damage to the environment. A total of 3 lakh 
tonnes of accumulated waste (legacy waste), occupying 38.22 acre of land was lying at 
these two sites without remediation as of March 2023, despite NGT’s order to remediate 
the waste on priority. The shelf life of the SLF and dumpsite had depleted fast owing 
to dumping of unprocessed and mixed waste, and would last only up to 2025, unless 
urgent action was initiated by the Government to enhance processing and dump only 
inert waste to these sites. 
7.3.13 Integration of waste pickers in SWM system
The SWM Rules, 2016 (Clause 11 (c) and 15 (c)) requires State Government to provide 
broad guidelines regarding integration of waste pickers or informal waste collectors 
with SWM system and ULBs to establish a system to recognise organisations of 
waste pickers or informal waste collectors and promote and establish a system for 
integration of these authorised waste pickers and waste collectors to facilitate their 
participation in Solid Waste Management (SWM) including door to door collection 
of waste.
Accordingly, State Policy on Solid Waste Management Strategy (April 2019) prescribed 
for integration of the informal sector (waste pickers and scrap dealers) with the State 
level Solid Waste management regime as primary partners. The informal sector would 
be represented in State Level Advisory Body. The waste pickers and scrap dealers would 
be identified and authorised to collect /receive, discards from public and government 
institutions for recycling. 
The policy also envisioned providing training to the waste pickers and provision of free 
medical check-up and insurance for them.
Audit observed that though the ULBs had identified waste pickers and scrap dealers 
they were to be yet integrated into the system as of November 2023 by ensuring their 
representation in the State Level Advisory Body. They were also not provided with 
training, free medical check-up and medical insurance as envisaged in the policy.

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
230
7.3.14 Monitoring 
A comprehensive monitoring and evaluation (M&E) system should be adopted for 
proper implementation of the MSWM plan and for assessing progress towards meeting 
the targets of the plan 184185186
187. Position of monitoring of MSWM services in the ULBs is 
given below.
7.3.14.1 Partial compliance to direction of State Level Committee (SLC) on SWM 
The State Government constituted (February 2019) a State level committee (SLC) 188, 
headed by Former Judge of High Court of Sikkim, to execute the orders of National 
Green Tribunal (NGT) relating to implementation of Municipal Solid Waste Management 
Rules, 2016. The Member Secretary, SPCB is the Member Secretary to the Committee. 
The Committee met from time to time and directed.
i. Preparation of state waste management policy (February 2019), action plan for urban 
areas, to hold inter departmental meetings so as to converge the line department for 
efficient implementation of waste management rules;
ii. Framing of by-laws for solid waste management (March 2019);
iii. UDD to upload information about ULBs on their website on status of municipal 
solid waste for information to general public (April 2019); 
iv. Forest Department to reconstitute state environment impact assessment authority 
(SEIAA) urgently for expediting environmental clearance to Ringdam waste 
processing facilities at Mangan (December 2019).
Audit observed that direction of the Committee relating to formulation of state waste 
management policy, action plan for urban areas, by-laws for solid waste management, 
etc. was complied with and notified by the State Government. 
However, direction relating to inter departmental meetings to converge the line department 
for efficient implementation of waste management rules, uploading of information 
on status of MSW, and reconstitution of SEIAA was not acted upon as of June 2023. 
Absence of efficient implementation of waste management rules, especially relating to 
scientific disposal of waste was fraught with the risk of leeching and contamination 
of water. This was compounded by lack of facilities for assessing status of ambient 
air, ground water, leachate quality, compost quality, etc. in waste processing/ landfill 
sites in Sikkim 189. The SPCB although had advised (September 2020) adopting more 
scientific waste management approaches by ULBs, had not followed up and ensured 
their compliance.
7.3.14.2 Inadequate compliance to NGT order 
The NGT vide OA no. 606/ 2018  had raised a number of issues pertaining to SWM 
in Sikkim. The NGT in its recent hearing ordered (03 November 2022) that the State  
Government should take time bound measures for effective management of waste 
generated on day-to-day basis as well as ensuring necessary remedial actions on waste 
accumulated over the years at landfill sites. 
184 
185 
186 
187 A comprehensive monitoring and evaluation (M&E) system should be adopted for proper 
implementation of the MSWM plan and for assessing progress towards meeting the targets of the plan.
188 vide notification no. 12/ Home/ 2019 dated 08.02.2019,
189 MSW Annual Report 2023-24 of SPCB sent to CPCB vide letter no. 430/SPCB/664 dated 29.07.2024. 

Chapter VII: Local Bodies
231
In compliance, the State Government issued (30 November 2022) directions for 
compliance by all concerned including ULBs, which inter-alia included (i) compulsory 
source segregation at household level; (ii) only segregated waste to be collected by 
garbage vehicles of ULBs; (iii) ULBs to be responsible for ensuring implementation of 
collection and scientific disposal of solid waste as per norms; (iv) landfill shall henceforth 
accept only inert waste; etc. Any violation was to be viewed seriously and actions to be 
initiated as per rules.
Audit observed that these directions were not acted upon in full as of June 2023 which 
can be seen that the deficiency in source segregation continued at household level 
(Paragraph 7.3.11.3), unsegregated waste (mixed waste) was collected and transported 
by the garbage vehicles ( Paragraph 7.3.11.5), (iii) the landfill continued to receive 
mixed waste instead of inert waste ( Paragraph 7.3.12). The ULBs and UDD had not 
taken any action against the defaulters as of June 2023.
Audit observed that this advisory was not acted upon and no follow up was conducted 
as of June 2023. The ULBs continued to dump a large percentage of waste to landfill/ 
dumpsites and scientific waste management approach was yet to be adopted.
Non-disposal of waste in a scientific manner in accordance with norms stated in SWM 
Rules was fraught with the risk of leeching and contamination of water as mentioned in 
the preceding (Paragraph 7.3.12.1).
7.3.14.3 Non-compliance to Inspection report by SPCB
According to SWM Rules, 2016 (Clause 16(1) (b), the SPCB shall monitor environmental 
standards and adherence to conditions as specified under schedule-I of the Rules for 
waste processing and disposal sites which stipulated the specifications for Sanitary 
Landfills.
Audit observed that SPCB had conducted inspection (27.11.2020) of Martam landfill 
site and noted that (i) e-waste, plastic waste and other solid waste were mixed with 
MSW; (ii) segregated waste were not being brought by ULBs; (iii) criteria for landfill 
disposal laid down in SWM rules 2016 had not been complied with as sanitary landfill 
was filled with unsegregated and pre filled waste; (iv) landfill was generating foul smell 
and polluting gases (methane, H2S, etc.) due to dumping of bio degradable waste along 
with other waste; (v) ETP plant for treatment of leachate being generated from sanitary 
landfill had not been completed. Similarly, SPCB had also observed (18.06.2018) that 
(i) safety gears such as gloves and masks were not being used by employees during 
segregation of waste.  
Audit noted that, the deficiencies pointed out by SPCB persisted as of June 2023 as 
confirmed during physical verification (June 2023) of landfill site at Martam by Audit in 
presence of GMC officials (as mentioned in Paragraph 7.3.12.1). 
7.3.14.4 Environment and health impact assessment (EHIA)
SWM manual 2000 (paragraph 22.2.1) stipulated EHIA of MSW to identify and predict 
the impact of SWM activities, suggest appropriate preventive measures to safeguard 
environment and human he alth and wellbeing, and to interpret and communicate 
information about the impact. 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
232
Audit observed that EHIA had not been conducted as of June 2023. As a result, 
identification of environmental and health hazards, interpretation of environmental and 
health risk, and preventive measu res for environmental and health risk could not be 
initiated. This was despite SPCB pointing out (June 2018) about non assessment of 
health and environment hazard.
UDD stated that (January 2023) a thorough study in coordination with SPCB will be 
carried out for assessment of damage to environment and human health posed by the 
waste. In June 2023 the local residents of the surrounding area of Martam landfill site 
raised this issue and sought immediate redressal. 
However, assessment of damage to environment and human health posed by waste was 
not carried out by UDD as of August 2023.
7.3.14.5 Management Information System
According to SWM Manual 2016 (6.1.1 and 6.1.3), a management information system 
(MIS) should be set up to record and monitor all information or data on MSWM and 
was one of the best way to ensure achievement of target through a computerised MIS. 
Audit observed that no such MIS was developed by the ULBs nor any efforts taken to 
implement the same for the last four years since adoption of the rules in April 2019. In 
the absence of MIS, online monitoring of SWM activities by the ULBs was not possible 
to assess the extent of success/ shortfall in SWM services. 
7.3.14.6 Ward level committee not formed
According to MSW Manual 2016 (Section 6.1), ward level committees should be 
constituted for ensuring and monitoring SWM services including segregation, collection, 
transportation, street sweeping, drain cleaning, and prohibition of littering.
However, in test-checked ULBs, ward level committees were not yet constituted till 
the date of audit nor timeline prescribed though State Government made a provision 
for it through State policy on solid waste management strategy notified in 2019. This 
indicated deficiencies in monitoring of SWM activities. 
7.3.14.7 Data regarding waste not displayed
The Bye-laws (April 2019) of Solid Waste (Management & Handling) Cleanliness 
and Sanitation for Urban sector of Sikkim ( paragraph 6.70) envisaged upon ULBs to 
release monthly data about waste received at landfill and waste processing sites. Such 
information was to be maintained at the ULB Office and displayed on their website.
However, it was observed that ULBs had not maintained the required data.
7.3.14.8 Designated officers and periodic reports
The Bye-laws of Solid Waste (Management & Handling) Cleanliness and Sanitation for 
Urban sector of Sikkim ( paragraph 6.15) envisaged upon ULBs to designate officers 
responsible for implementing the obligatory responsibilities specified under these Rules/
Bye-laws by ULBs. The specific plans and time schedules and achievements against the 
same along with reasons for shortfalls, if any, were to be shared publicly by the official/
authority of the ULBs and their website. 

Chapter VII: Local Bodies
233
Audit observed that though officers were designated for SWM activities, however, 
specific plans, time schedules and achievements there against along with reasons for 
shortfalls, were not shared publicly by the ULBs.
7.3.14.9 Service level benchmarks 
Ministry of Urban Development (MoUD), Government of India, launched (2008) the 
Service Level Benchmarking (SLB) initiative covering water supply, waste water, and 
SWM and storm water drainage. The 14 th FCs and SBM also endorsed the principle 
of benchmarking and included SLB as one of the conditions for the allocation of 
performance-based grants to ULBs. 
Audit observed that State Government (UDD) initially fixed target (September 2013) of 
SLB for Gangtok Municipal Corporation (GMC) only. This was subsequently revised 
(April 2016) and the target was fixed for all the ULBs.
A comparison of target prescribed by GoI vis-à-vis target fixed by the State Government 
disclosed that while the target in respect of efficiency in collection of user charges was 
not set, target in respect of collection efficiency, extent of segregation, scientific disposal 
was below the target in case of GMC and GyNP and cost recovery was below the target 
for GMC, GyNP and MNP as shown below: 
Table 7.13: Prescribed performance indicator vis-à-vis target set of SLB
(in per cent)

However, the assessment of achievement by ULBs against the above target was not 
carried out as of June 2023 by the UDD to determine the extent of achievement and 
shortfall in providing basic services to citizens.
7.3.15 Conclusion
The performance audit disclosed that the ULBs had not formulated any MSWM plan 
for  appropriate handling of waste. The door-to-door collection of waste was largely 
done by deploying garbage trucks at the roadside (garbage collection point) and entire 
waste generated was being collected, as indicated by the records provided. The source 
segregation of waste was not being adopted by most of the households. The households 
bring in waste in single bins without segregating. The municipal staff segregate waste 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
234
enroute during transportation and recyclable items were collected and sold to scrap 
dealers of the neighbouring state of West Bengal. 
The performance of the ULBs regarding processing of waste was very minimal as there 
were only two decentralised processing units and one centralised processing unit at 
Martam landfill, aggregating to total capacity of only 51.80 tpd. These processing units 
(at Martam of 50 tpd) also remained non-functional from November 2021 to January 
2023. The only SLF at Martam, initially designed to cater to one ULB (GMC), was 
catering to four ULBs as of June 2023.
This SLF was designed for 25 years (up to 2040), however, due to excessive dumping of 
mixed waste, coupled with very limited processing of waste, the shelf life was expected 
to end within two years (2025). 
The situation would be very alarming, unless urgent action was initiated well in time, 
as the SLF would cease to be operational soon. While the legacy waste of three lakh 
tonnes was yet to be remediated despite advisory of NGT, leachate treatment was also 
not initiated. 
Though the air and water quality were found to be satisfactory, the advisory and counsel 
by State Advisory Board and SPCB was only partially acted upon as of June 2023.   
7.3.16  Recommendations
Based on the findings as reflected in the report, following recommendations are made 
for further strengthening of SWM in the ULBs in Sikkim:
	The Municipal Solid Waste Management Plan should be drawn up duly assessing 
the quantity and composition of waste for scientific management of increased 
quantum of waste.
	The department should take immediate steps to increase the user fee and ensure 
collection of user fee from all concerned to augment the revenue base of ULB.
	The source segregation should be strictly implemented and vehicles having 
compartment must be used for waste collection and transportation to ensure that 
the segregated waste is not mixed up with other waste.
	The processing of waste should be increased to ensure only inert waste (up to  
20 per cent of total waste generated) reaches the landfill to avoid unnecessary 
burden on landfill.
	The legacy waste should be immediately remediated as recommended by NGT.
	Urgent action should be initiated to locate alternative site for landfill for Gangtok 
and Pakyong district, expeditious establishment of landfill at Ringdang for Mangan 
district and completion of Sipchu landfill expeditiously.
	Monitoring should be strengthened to ensure full compliances to NGT orders, 
advisories issued by State Level Adv isory Board (SLAB) and SPCB to ensure 
efficacious implementation of SWM Rules.

COMPLIANCE AUDIT

7.4	 Subject	 Specific	Compliance	Audit	 on	 Efficacy	of	 implementation	 of	 74th 
Constitutional Amendment Act in Sikkim
7.4.1 Introduction
The Constitution (74th Amendment) Act (CAA), 1992 which came into effect on 01 June 
1993, provided a constitutional status to Urban Local Bodies (ULBs). The CAA added 
the 12 th schedule in the Constitution of India. The 12 th Schedule of the Constitution 
enumerates 18 specific functions (listed in  Appendix 7.2)  to be devolved to ULBs . 
Accordingly, Government of Sikkim (GoS) enacted the Sikkim Municipalities Act, 
2007 to give effect to these provisions of the Constitution. Sikkim has seven ULBs 
(Municipal Corporation - one; Municipal Councils - one and Nagar Panchayat - five). 
All ULBs have an elected body comprising Councilors.
The Audit was taken up with the objective to assess whether the provisions of 74th CAA 
have been implemented effectively in the State to enable the ULBs to function as an 
effective institution of local self-governance.
7.4.2  Organisational set-up of Urban Local Bodies
The Secretary, Urban Development Department (UDD) is the overall in charge of 
ULBs in the State. The Municipal Commissioner is the executive head of the Gangtok 
Municipal Corporation (GMC) while the other Municipal Councils /Nagar Panchayats 
(NP) are headed by the Municipal Executive Officers.
The organisational structure with respect to functioning of ULBs in the State is as 
follows:
Chart 7.6: Administrative Body

7.4.4 Empowerment of ULBs 
The 74 th CAA introduced Articles 243Q to 243ZG relating to municipalities. State 
Governments were required to incorporate these provisions in the respective 
Municipalities Acts. Scrutiny of the Sikkim Municipalities Act (SMA), 2007 revealed 
that the provisions of the 74 th CAA had been appropriately incorporated in the SMA, 
2007 by the State Legislature. The details are shown in Appendix 7.3 and Appendix 7.4.
It was however observed in Audit that the legal provisions were not backed by decisive 
actions leading to compromise in attaining the spirit of the 74thCAA as discussed below:
7.4.4.1 Devolution of functions to the ULBs
Government of Sikkim through amendments to ‘the Sikkim Municipalities Act, 2007’ 
transferred (June 2010)190 six (out of 18) functions to ULBs in 2012 as shown below: -
Table 7.17: Status of devolution

Audit observed that in practice, the ULBs were solely responsible for one function 191, 
had limited role in five functions 192 and had no role in the remaining 12 functions 193.

in full with respect to 17 subjects specified in the 12th Schedule.

240
slum development etc.), these functions continued to be executed by various departments 
of the State Government without involving ULBs as shown in Appendix 7.5. 
Analysis of records pertaining to Solid Waste Management which is one of the functions 
devolved fully to ULBs showed that the ULBs faced financial constraints in dealing 
with waste management functions. The revenue collection through user charges was 
not sufficient to meet the expenditure towards waste collection and disposal for the four 
ULBs test checked in audit. It is pertinent to mention that the user charge for solid waste 
management is being fixed by UDD even though the function is fully devolved. 
Thus, non-devolution of functions to the ULBs undermined the spirit of decentralisation 
and the spirit of 74th CAA. Furthermore, even in fully devolved functions such as Solid 
waste management, audit noticed that full autonomy to impose appropriate fees/ user 
charges to make up the entire expenditure on this activity was not available with the 
ULBs. 
The Department accepted the facts and offered no comment on the above para.
7.4.4.2 Effectiveness of institutional framework for ULBs
The objective of the 74thCAA was to entrust delivery of major civic functions to ULBs 
so as to enable them become institutions of self-government. 
Accordingly, the State Government was required to establish and empower appropriate 
institutions (such as State Election Commission, State Finance Commission, District 
Planning Committee, etc.). Position of their constitution and effectiveness is discussed 
below:
a) State Election Commission
Article 243ZAand243K stipulate that the State Election Commission (SEC) should be 
constituted and vested with powers of superintendence, direction, and control of the 
preparation of electoral rolls for, and the conduct of elections in respect of Panchayats 
and ULBs.
It was observed in audit that rules for conducting municipal election were framed in 
2007 and first-ever municipal election was held in 2010. The SEC has so far conducted 
three elections (April 2010; October 2015; and March 2021) in respect of ULBs. There 
were delays in holding of elections in 2015 by five months and in 2021 by 16 months.
The Department accepted the facts and offered no comment.
b) State Finance Commission 
According to Article 243-I of the Constitution, the State Government has to constitute 
State Finance Commission (SFC) within one year of the commencement of the 74 th 
CAA and thereafter on expiry of every five years with a mandate to review the financial 
position of the local bodies and to make recommendations to the Government for 
devolution of funds to these bodies.
Audit observed that the State Government had accepted 13 (out of 20) recommendations 
pertaining to 3rd to 5th SFCs for the ULBs. The recommendations not accepted by the State 
Government are given in Appendix 7.6. Out of 13 accepted recommendations, no action 
was initiated on 8 recommendations as of November 2023 as detailed in Appendix 7.7. 

Chapter VII: Local Bodies
241
Audit noticed that the Third SFC recommended (February 2010) a levy of property tax 
by ULBs to generate revenue.  
Audit observed that ULBs were not given adequate powers with respect to collection 
of the property tax, which is a major source of revenue for ULBs. The Thirteenth 
Central Finance Commission (TFC) had recommended (December 2009) for setting 
up of Property Tax Board and levy of Property Tax on lands and buildings in urban 
areas. The Fourth State Finance Commission (4 th SFC) report envisaged (May 2013) 
revenue generation of ₹ 3.08 crore during 2019-20 from Property Tax. The Government 
accepted (November 2014) the recommendation of the Commission and directed the 
administrative department i.e., UDD to devise an appropriate methodology to impose 
and collect the revenue. However, as of November 2023, no such methodology was 
finalised and consequently property tax could not be levied. Thus, the ULBs in Sikkim 
were constrained to become institutions of self-Government through deficient financial 
empowerment.
Audit observed that due to non-compliance of accepted recommendations, the ULBs 
could not enhance their revenue base, especially the revenue from property tax. 
The department accepted the facts and offered no comment.
c)  Delay in release of 15th Finance Commission grants to ULBs 
According to Operational Guidelines for implementation of the recommendations of 
the Fifteenth Finance Commission (FC-XV) on Urban Local Bodies (ULB), State 
Government was required to release Grants to ULBs within 10 working days of receipt 
of fund, failing which penal interest at the rate of market borrowing would be paid to 
ULBs.
Audit noticed that the State Government delayed the release of XV FC fund by 27 and 
88 days in two cases amounting to ₹ 30 crore as shown below:
Table 7.18: Delay in release of XV FC fund
SI 
No.
Fund Received on Amount
(₹ in crore)
Delay in 
transfer  
(in days)
Penalty

(in	₹)
1. 05.11.2020 (1st Instalment Tied Grant) 15.00 88 1,98,356
19.05.2020 (1st Instalment) 6,74,411
2 28.07.2022 (2nd Instalment of United Grant) 15.00 27 1,56,452
Total 10,29,219
As would be seen, the State Government had to release a penal interest of ₹10.29 lakh 
to ULBs due to delayed release of fund. The delay was due to Covid-19 lockdown in  
2020-21 and delayed release of resources by Finance Department during 2021-22.
d) District Planning Committee
In pursuance of Article 243ZD of the Constitution of India and Section 127 of the 
Sikkim Panchayat (SP) Act, 1993, the State Government constituted (July 2008) District 
Planning Committee (DPC) in each district. The Committee was headed by Adhyaksha, 
Zilla Panchayat and the members comprised MP/MLA from the District, elected head 
of ULBs, etc. The Committee, in terms of reference of DPC, was assigned the role and 
responsibility of consolidating plans prepared by the ZPs, GPs, Nagar Panchayats and 

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
242
Municipal Corporation in the District and preparing a draft development plan for the 
district as a whole.
Audit observed that the DPCs finalised the Annual District Development Plans (ADDPs) 
pertaining to Zilla Panchayat  alone. It had neither obtained input from various line 
departments nor forwarded the ADDP to the State Government for integration with the 
state plan.
Thus, the DPC was not discharging its full responsibility of preparation of draft 
development plan for the district as a whole as plan for ULBs was not included therein. 
The Department accepted the facts and offered no comment.
7.4.5 Human resources of ULBs
Adequate and qualified manpower is essential for the empowerment of ULBs. According 
to the 4th SFC recommendation (May 2013), the ULBs should have discretionary powers 
to recruit the human resources required for functioning, which was accepted (November 
2014) by the Government. The SMA, 2007 Act (Section 29), however, empowered the 
State Government to transfer officer/ staff of ULBs to any other ULBs or any Government 
department and vice-versa. The powers to promote officials, initiate disciplinary action, 
impose penalties, etc., in respect of the officials of ULBs, had also not been delegated 
to ULBs.
Audit observed that the State Government posted staff to ULBs without any consultation 
with ULBs as to the actual requirement. Not only were the powers not vested with 
ULBs for assessment, recruitment, promotion of staff of ULBs; the staff posted were in 
excess of sanctioned strength in Gangtok Municipal Corporation. The State Government 
had also not formulated municipal cadre in the State as of November 2023, even after 
accepting the recommendations in November 2014. 
Thus, the ULBs were not independent as far as manpower assessment, recruitment, 
mobilisation and incentivisation of human resources is concerned.
The department accepted the facts and offered no comment.
7.4.6 Conclusion 
Audit observed that the State Government had appropriately constituted SFC, SEC and 
DPC to give effect to the 74th CAA and to empower the ULBs to function as institutions 
of self-government. The empowerment of ULBs was however compromised as only the 
function of Urban Property Alleviation has been entirely devolved to the ULBs. Five 
of the functions viz. (i) Regulation of land-use and construction of building.; (ii) Public 
health, sanitation conservancy and solid waste management; (iii) Provision of urban 
amenities and facilities such as parks, gardens, playgrounds; (iv)Vital Statics including 
registration of birth & death and (v) Public amenities including street lighting, parking 
lots, bus stops and public conveniences were devolved partially. The remaining twelve 
functions are yet to be devolved.
The partial transfer of functions placed constrained on the functions of the ULBs as 
envisaged by the Constitution of India.

Chapter VII: Local Bodies
243
The ULBs were not able to generate sufficient revenue due to delays in finalisation 
of property tax by the State Government and were heavily dependent on Government 
grants and thus were not able to function as institutions of self-Government.
Even after a lapse of almost 30 years since the 74 th Constitutional amendment in 1992, 
concrete actions were not initiated by the State Government to fully empower the ULBs 
to function as institutions of self-government by transferring all the 18 functions as well 
as requisite autonomy to recruit and promote manpower, levy and collect property tax, 
etc.
7.4.7 Recommendations
	State Government should empower ULBs to function as institutions of self-government 
by devolving all the 18 functions enlisted in the 12 th Schedule of the Constitution 
urgently.
	The State Government should empower the ULBs to generate maximum possible 
revenue, especially through early finalisation of modalities of property tax and levy 
of other fees/taxes independently by ULBs to augment the resource base of ULBs and 
enable them to function as institutions of self-governance.
	The State Government should initiate efforts to empower the ULBs by ensuring 
compliance to the accepted recommendations of various SFCs.

CHAPTER VIII

8.1 Follow up action on earlier Audit Reports
Audit observations on financial irregularities and deficiencies in maintenance of initial 
accounts noticed during local audit and not settled on the spot are communicated to the 
audited departments and to the higher authorities through Inspection Reports (IRs).
Serious irregularities noticed in audit are included in the Report of the Comptroller 
and Auditor General of India and presented to the State Legislature. According to the 
instructions issued by the Finance Department, Government of Sikkim, all the concerned 
administrative departments were required to furnish suo moto explanatory notes on the 
paragraphs/Performance Audits included in the Audit Reports within one month from 
the date of issue of the Audit Reports.
It was, however, noticed that as of March 2023, in 21 per cent cases, the concerned 
administrative departments had not submitted explanatory notes on the paragraphs/ 
Performance Audits included in the Audit Report pertaining to the year 2013-14. In 
respect of Audit Reports for 2014-15, 2015-16, 2016-17, 2017-18 and 2018-19, 
explanatory notes had not been submitted by concerned departments in 16, 25, 33, 56 
and 100 per cent cases respectively. The position of suo moto explanatory notes not 
received as on 31 March 2023 is shown in the table below:

8.2 Response of departments to recommendations of Public Accounts Committee
The Finance, Revenue and Expenditure Department (FRED) issued instructions to all 
departments to submit Action Taken Notes (ATNs) on various suggestions, observations 
and recommendations made by the Public Accounts Committee (PAC) for their 
consideration within 15 days of presentation of the PAC’s Reports to the Legislature. 
The PAC’s Reports/recommendations are the principal medium by which the Legislature 
enforces financial accountability of the Executives to the Legislature and it is appropriate 
that they elicit timely response from the departments in the form of ATNs.
As on March 2023, the PAC had completed discussions of Audit Reports for the year 
up to 2014-15 and discussions on Audit Report for the year 2015-16 were in progress. 
Recommendations on the Audit Reports for the year up to 2014-15 had been issued 
by the PAC.  As of March 2023, ATNs had been received in respect of 629 out of 
667 recommendations194 of the PAC, made for the Audit Reports for the years between 
1990-91 and 2014-15.
194  38 (666-628) ATNs relates to Audit Report 2011-12 (1 ATN), 2012-13 (22 ATNs), 2013-14 (07 ATNs) 
and 2014-15 (8 ATNs).

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2023
246
8.3 Monitoring
The following Committees had been formed at the Government level to monitor the 
follow up action on Audit related matters:
Departmental Audit and Accounts Committee : Departmental Audit and Accounts 
Committee (DAAC) had been formed (November 2010) by all departments of the 
Government under the Chairmanship of the Departmental Secretary/Head of Department 
to monitor the follow up action on Audit related matters. The DAAC’s function was to 
monitor the response and corrective action on findings reported in the IRs issued by the 
Accountant General (AG). It was to hold meetings once in three months and to send 
quarterly action taken report on the issues to the State Audit and Accounts Committee. 
During 2022-23, no DAAC meeting was held.
State Audit and Accounts Committee: State Audit and Accounts Committee (SAAC) 
had been formed (June 2010) at the State level under the Chairmanship of the Chief 
Secretary. This was to monitor the response and corrective action on the findings reported 
by Audit to review and oversee the working of DAAC and also to hold meetings once in 
three months. The information in this regard was not furnished, though called for.
After formation of the SAAC by the State Government, not a single meeting was held.
8.4 Outstanding Inspection Reports
The AG conducts periodical inspection of the Government departments to test check 
the transactions and verify maintenance of important accounts and other records as 
prescribed in the rules and procedures. These inspections are followed up by issuing 
IRs on irregularities detected during the inspection and not settled on the spot, to the 
Heads of the Offices inspected, with copies to the higher authorities for taking prompt 
corrective action. The Heads of the Offices are required to promptly comply with the 
observations contained in IRs, rectify the defects and omissions and report compliance 
through initial reply to the AG within one month from the date of the issue of the IRs. 
Serious irregularities are reported to the Heads of the departments and the Government.
The position of outstanding IRs pertaining to Civil (Expenditure audit including that 
of Works, Forest and Autonomous Bodies), Revenue (Audit of Revenue departments) 
and Commercial (Audit of State Public Sector Undertakings) audit as of March 2023 is 
shown below:

Appendix 2.2
Methodology of Sampling
(Reference: Paragraph 2.3.4)
1. Selection of Districts:
The State of Sikkim has four districts namely, East, West, North &South during the 
period covered under Performance Audit (2017-18 to 2021-22). The State Building 
and Other Construction Workers Welfare Board (SBOCWWB) had not maintained the 
data of district wise collection of Labour Cess. Thus, selection of district is made by 
considering the district wise population of the State (census data 2011) and district wise 
GDP of the State obtained from the State department 195. Based on these two criteria, 
East and South districts have been selected for the Performance audit.
2. Selection of Schemes: Under SBOCWWB vide notification no. 06/DL dated 
12/03/2015, 22welfare schemes196 were created for the SBOCWWB workers. In addition 
to this, the State vide notification no. 22DL dated 14 June 2018 promulgated eight 
schemes specified for Financial Assistance for the SBOCWWB workers viz., i) Maternity 
(ii) Marriage (iii) Minor injury sustained in the course of employment (iv) Funeral (v) 
Medical (vi) Pension (vii) Partial disability sustained in the course of employment (viii) 
Permanent disability/death occurred in the course of employment. Further, one-time 
Financial Assistance vide. GOI notification no. -11017/09/2019-RW dated 27.03.2020 
was also released by the State Government to registered SBOCWWB workers.
For selection of schemes, the criteria of the PA guidelines stipulated as under:
Total 10 welfare schemes were to be selected based on Stratified Random Sampling. 
The schemes will be stratified into three strata stratified based on amount of assistance 
disbursed as below:
(i) five schemes with maximum amount of financial assistance.
(ii) three schemes with moderate amount of financial assistance.
(iii) two schemes having zero beneficiaries, if applicable.
Considering the expenditure involved in 22 schemes, it was noticed that only nine 
schemes were being actively implemented by the SBOCWW Board. The expenditures 
incurred during 2017-22 were as depicted in tabled below: 
195 Directorate of Economics, Statistics, Monitoring and Evaluation, Govt. of Sikkim.
196 1. Vocational training to the family members of the workers, 2. Free legal aid facilities, 3. Library cum 
reading room, 4. AwarenessProgramme on HIV/AIDs and other Social issues, 5.Organising seminars/
Workshops for creating awareness about the right of the workers and other social issues, 6. Cultural 
Programmes, 7. Blood donation camp, 8. Medical Camps and health checkup melas, 9. Creche, 10. 
Facilities for informal and non-formal education of workers, 11. Periodic awareness Programme for 
sanitation & basic health awareness, 12. Benefit for accident victims rendered physically handicapped 
for alternative employment, 13. Special awareness scheme for female workers, 14. Marriage of 
female workers and their female children (Kanyadan), 16 Ex-gratia compensation, hospital treatment 
and funeral expenses of the workers and their family members, 17. Distribution of tools & other 
related materials, 18. Death Compensation and Permanent Disablement Compensation as approved 
by the Welfare Board, 19. Provision of school for children of registered workers and educational 
infrastructure etc., 20. Provision of accommodation for offices of Welfare Board, Hostels, Training 
Centers etc., 21. Skill Development of the registered workers and their dependents and 22. Any other 
schemes, initiatives and welfare measures including initiatives to build capacity, skill of labours and 
their dependents.

In addition to above, other SBOCWWB schemes having high expenditures e.g., 
Distribution of Welfare items, School/Education infrastructure development and 
Financial Assistance during COVID-19 etc. as depicted in Table 2 were also scrutinised 
separately.
3. Selection	of	Beneficiaries:
From the above FA schemes (Table 3), during 2017-22 only 61 nos. of beneficiaries from 
all four districts197had availed the assistance and accordingly, the Board had disbursed 
a total amount of ₹14.08 lakh to these beneficiaries. Thus, considering the maximum 
amount of disbursal from each district East and South districts were selected for the 
beneficiary survey. 
District wise selection of beneficiary survey for 100 nos. at each district is as under:
A) East District: Out of 100 beneficiaries to be selected for sample survey, all 57 
beneficiaries who had availed the FA schemes were covered.  28 workers were 
selected by random sampling basis from the latest list of registered workers 
who had not availed any FA by their name in alphabetical order. Remaining 15 
unregistered workers were covered at the time of joint physical inspection of 
unregistered establishments.
B) South District: Similarly, out of 100 beneficiaries to be covered in survey, two 
beneficiary who had availed FA schemes were selected.  68 registered workers 
were selected similar in line of East District and remaining 30 unregistered workers 
were covered at the time of joint physical inspection of unregistered establishment.
4. Selection of 4 Registered and 4 Unregistered Establishment for joint inspection:
(i) Registered Establishment:
As per the list provided to audit as of date there were only 70 active registered 
establishments in East (34) & South (36) Districts. 
Thus, four registered establishment from each district was selected on random Sampling 
basis. 
(ii) Selection of Unregistered establishment: Based on the information (latest 
Monthly Progress Report) collected from the Works Departments and list of registered 
Establishments maintained by SBOCWWB, four unregistered establishments from each 
district were selected by using PPSWOR sampling method based on Estimated cost of 
the works/establishments for joint physical verification.
(iii) Selection of accident and incident cases: The Board had not maintained any 
records of accident/incident cases. Neither any site inspections records were available 
with Board for reporting such cases. However, during scrutiny of FA related records, 
audit and JPV of establishment 6 cases of incident/accident were found which were 
analysed and comments were incorporated draft. 
197 North: Beneficiaries (03); Expenditure (₹ 0.18 lakh), South: Beneficiaries (02); Expenditure (₹ 0.20 lakh), East: 
Beneficiaries (57); Expenditure(₹ 13.60lakh),West: Beneficiary (01); Expenditure (₹ 0.10 lakh).

NB:
1. Earnings before Interest and Tax (EBIT) have been derived by adding back the Interest and Tax Expenses to the ‘Net Profit/Loss for the year’ as reported in the Profit and 
Loss Account.
2. Networth means the sum total of the paid-up share capital and free reserves and surplus less accumulated loss and deferred revenue expenditure. Free reserves mean all 
reserves created out of profits but do not include reserves created out of revaluation of assets and write back of depreciation provision.
3. Capital Employed represents Shareholders’ Fund plus Long-Term Borrowings where, Shareholders Fund represents ‘Paid up Share Capital plus Free Reserves and Surplus 
minus Accumulated Loss minus Deferred Revenue Expenditure.
4. Return on Capital Employed has been depicted the EBIT as a percentage of the Capital Employed.
5. The paid up capital of SPSEs at serial no. 10 was negligible (₹ 70.00 only) while in case of other three SPSEs (serial no 5,16 & 17), there was no paid up equity capital. 
Hence, the paid up capital in respect of said four SPSEs has been taken as ‘zero’.

Appendix 5.3
Limited/Centralised Audit – Replies not accepted by Audit
(Reference: Paragraph 5.12.6.2 b, c(i))
Sl. 
No.
GSTIN Dimension Amount	in	₹ Audit rebuttal
1. 11AAACI5120L1Z1 1:ITC Mismatch 
between GSTR 3B 
and GSTR 2A
1,37,18,109202 Excess ITC ₹ 1,41,07,002 revised to ₹ 1,37,18,109 on the basis of reply: The CTD furnished three 
justifications for excess ITC out of which one was accepted which is as follows: - There was ITC 
of ₹ 3,88,893 pertaining to purchase invoices of 2017-18 uploaded by the suppliers in 2018-19, 
this was checked in GSTR 2A of 2018-19 and found correct. Therefore, the observation amount is 
reduced by ₹ 3,88,893. Hence excess ITC revised to ₹ 1,37,18,109.
2. 11AAMCA7281L1ZF 1:ITC Mismatch 
between GSTR 3B 
and GSTR 2A
25,96,784 As per the reply (6 April 2023) excess ITC of ₹ 25.97 lakh was due to bills involving tax/ITC of 
₹ 2.55 lakh shown in 2018-19 and invoices involving tax/ITC of ₹ 22.82 lakh not uploaded by the 
suppliers and the balance of ₹ 5.49 lakh was paid vide challan. The reply is not acceptable because 
only the list of five invoices involving tax/ITC of ₹ 2.55 lakh had been submitted without the copies 
of invoices. Moreover, the challan of ₹ 5.49 lakh was paid vide cash which got credited in the 
cash ledger which can be utillised by the taxpayer for discharge of tax liability hence it cannot be 
considered as payment of excess ITC.
3. 11AAXCS7643D1Z5 2: ITC availed 
under RCM without 
payment of tax in 
GSTR-3B/ GSTR-9
1,25,873 As per the reply, the excess RCM ITC availed during 2017-18 had been reversed in the month of 
March 2018. 
Examination revealed that the total reversal in March 2018 was ₹ 9.27 lakh. In absence of any 
credible evidence, Audit could not vouchsafe that the excess ITC of RCM of ₹ 1.26 lakh was 
included in the reversal of March 2018.
4. 11ARJPC1527C1ZO 2: ITC availed 
under RCM without 
payment of tax in 
GSTR-3B/ GSTR-9
2,34,331 As per reply (6 April 2023) the taxpayer applied for cancellation and was duly approved by the 
cancellation authority on 4 February 2022. Now, that the taxpayer’s registration is cancelled, the 
excess ITC of ₹ 2.34 lakh is a loss to the state exchequer, this indicated inadequacy of scrutiny of 
taxpayers returns by the tax authority even at the time of approving the cancellation of registration.  
202 The excess ITC revised from ₹ 1,41,07,002 to ₹ 1,37,18,109 on the basis of reply furnished by the CTD.

6. 11AAACI5120L1Z1 4:Incorrect 
availment of ISD 
credit
39,72,001 To justify the excess ITC availed under ISD of ₹ 39.72 lakh following were stated: (i) the taxpayer 
had taken ISD of ₹ 14.62 lakh manually in January 2018 for which letter was submitted to GSTN, 
(ii) had wrongly taken credit under ISD instead of normal credit of ₹ 14.76 lakh, the head office was 
informed and ISD credit of June 2018 was reduced by the same amount and (iii) ISD credit of ₹ 
9.88 lakh was taken manually in December 2017 as GSTR 6 was operational only from June 2018. 
The reply is not acceptable because supporting documents to establish their claim was not furnished 
to Audit, only two invoices involving tax of ₹ 48,961 and ₹ 60,595 was furnished to Audit.
7. 11AABCA9521E1ZA 4:Incorrect 
availment of ISD 
credit
55,61,655 As per the reply ISD transfer of ₹ 55.61 lakh was not getting reflected in GSTR 6 table 5A due to 
technical glitch in GST portal. The reply is not acceptable as no documentary evidence was furnished 
to support excess ITC availed under ISD amounting to ₹ 55, 61, 655 were actually eligible.
8. 11ARDPP2143B1ZL 5:Mismatch of ITC 
availed between 
annual returns and 
books of accounts
33,94,084 As per the reply balance sheet is prepared for contract business only whereas ITC was availed for 
procurement of Hotel business also. Further the reason for the unreconciled ITC as mentioned in 
GSTR 9C table 13 A also states that balance sheet is for Contract business where as the taxpayer is 
also involved in supply of services (hotel was under construction stage) for which ITC was claimed 
but not recorded in the books of account for Contract business. 
The reply is not acceptable because Section 17 (5) (d) of the SGST Act 2017 provides that: 
Notwithstanding anything contained in sub-section (1) of section 16 and subsection (1) of section 
18, input tax credit shall not be available in respect of goods or services or both received by a 
taxable person for construction of an immovable property (other than plant or machinery) on his 
own account including when such goods or services or both are used in the course or furtherance of 
business.

279
Sl. 
No.
GSTIN Dimension Amount	in	₹ Audit rebuttal
9. 11AAACT5131A1ZA 5:Mismatch of ITC 
availed between 
annual returns and 
books of accounts
5,01,270 As per the reply, the payment of unreconciled ITC has been made through DRC -03. Payment of ₹ 
7.43 lakh was done on 5 April 2021. The reply is not acceptable because the payment made under 
said DRC-03 was for supply of goods and not for excess or unreconciled ITC.
10. 11AABCO6063D1ZZ 5:Mismatch of ITC 
availed between 
annual returns and 
books of accounts
2,73,838 As per the reply, the difference of ITC was due to input related to cross charge distributed by the 
corporate head. The reply is not acceptable as under cross charge the ITC is not distributed, instead 
invoice is raised by corporate head for the supply of goods or services amongst distinct persons; and 
no evidence was furnished to support that the invoices raised by the corporate head was not included 
in their annual financial statement.
11. 11BTFPB1791L1ZV 5:Mismatch of ITC 
availed between 
annual returns and 
books of accounts
5,45,029 As per the reply, the excess claim of 2017-18 had been adjusted by claiming less ITC during financial 
year 2018-19. The reply is not acceptable as there was no evidence to support that the excess ITC of 
₹ 5.45 lakh made during 2017-18 had been adjusted in the subsequent year.
12. 11AAFCS1221B1ZD 7-Mismatch in 
turnover declared in 
GSTR-9C Table 5R
82,41,468 To justify the difference of ₹ 82.41 lakh the taxpayer stated the following:  
(i) ₹ 28.94 lakh was the insurance claim. (ii) ₹ 53.47 lakh was the interest income.  
Reply is not acceptable as no documentary evidence was furnished.
13. 11ARDPP2143B1ZL 8-Mismatch in 
taxable turnover 
declared in GSTR-
9C Table 7G
3,88,76,124 As per the reply the Financial statement is prepared on accrual basis and uncertified bill has been 
accounted for. One bill (of ₹ 3.60 crore) was raised by one contractee (M/s Bridge and Roof Limited) 
in March 2018 whereas the payment was made in May 2018 hence it was not reflected in the annual 
returns in 2017-18. 
The reply is not acceptable because as per Section 13 of SGST Act 2017 the time of supply should be 
earliest of the (a) the date of issue of invoice by the supplier, if the invoice is issued within the period 
prescribed under sub-section (2) of section 31 or the date of receipt of payment, whichever is earlier; 
or (b) the date of provision of service, if the invoice is not issued within the period prescribed under 
sub-section (2) of section 31 or the date of receipt of payment, whichever is earlier.
Besides no supporting documents (i.e. copy of the particular bill) was furnished to Audit to justify 
the unreconciled amount of ₹ 3.89 crore.

was ₹  5.83 lakh (not ₹  12.78 lakh). It 
was further stated that the excess ITC

DRC 03.

(28.50 + 6.94 = 35.44) lakh. Moreover, evidence of the reversal of ₹ 1.30 lakh was neither 
furnished nor found in the State system.
